# CHAPTER LXXVI. Exeunt Omnes (that’s fancy old stage-speak for every last character, extra, and random stagehand shuffles off the set, no encores allowed)
My Dear Doctor,

Let me paint you the scene from thirteen whole months back: this rambling, silly story I’d been churning out page by page looked dead certain to wrap up permanently, and I was flat on my sickbed half-convinced I’d be checking out before I even got to write the final chapter for Pendennis. Then a good mate of ours turned up dragging you through my bedroom door, and I swear on all my half-empty inkwells and dog-eared notebook stacks, I’d never have dragged myself upright and back to my desk if it weren’t for your nonstop, hawk-like watchfulness and your clever, brilliant medical tricks.

I still love thinking back to all that kindness back then — not just yours, but all the little random, over-the-top acts of friendship and sympathy from folks I’d never have expected to care half that much, turning up with jellies and bad jokes at the exact time I was so wobbly and miserable that even the tiniest bit of warmth felt like a fresh blanket straight from the fire.

And the best, most ridiculous part? You refused every actual coin I tried to hand over as payment, said the only fee you’d accept was a plain old thank-you. Fair enough! I’m stamping that thank-you right here in ink, for me, for all my chaotic household, and even for Pendennis himself (who you basically gifted his happy ending, let’s be real).

Yours most sincerely, and more grateful than you could ever fit inside your entire doctor’s leather satchel,

Look, let’s get this straight from the start: the two years’ worth of scribbling that’s landed in your hands today is never going to win awards for fancy, polished, perfect art. That’s a given, and I’m not pretending otherwise. But it’s got one huge advantage over all those over-labored, pretentious tomes: it’s unapologetically honest.

When a writer’s yammering directly at you, page after page, no fancy middlemen, he’s bound to slip into being frank, just blurting out whatever’s bouncing around his head no filter. Flipping back through these pages now, I spot a dozen typos, half a dozen lines I banged out in a huff at 2 a.m that I’d give a pound to take back. This whole thing is just a long, rambling chat between you and me, no more, no less. Of course there will be dull bits! Of course the story will drag sometimes! Anyone who nags at you for hours is going to accidentally let slip their dumb obsessions, petty vanities, and weird little quirks eventually, right?

You don’t judge your best mate after two years of hanging out only on that one stupid drunk speech he made at your cousin’s wedding, right? You judge him on the whole of him – is he a stand-up bloke? Does he mostly tell the truth? Is he trying not to lie to you? Or is he a phony hack putting on a whole dramatic act to make you clap and buy him a pint, pulling cheap little tricks to make you like him? I’m not daft, I know I’ve gotten far more readers than I ever could have daydreamed about, and I’m not about to yell at you that you’re forbidden to think my art is garbage, or that you’re not allowed to nod off halfway through a chapter. All I ask? Believe that I’m trying my best to tell you the truth. If there’s no truth in these pages, there’s nothing at all.

For all you excitement addicts who only pick up books for the drama, let me tell you a secret: this one had a completely different, wildly chaotic original plot that I tossed in the bin. You were going to get the most blood-pumping thrill ride imaginable, and me and my publisher were going to get so rich we’d both buy new top hats. What’s more exciting than a roguish St Giles ruffian (who was secretly very noble, of course) getting regular secret visits from a posh Belgravia heiress? Contrasts! Slang mixed with fancy aristocratic banter! Fist fights! Narrow escapes! *Murder!* Heck, up until 9 o’clock this very morning, poor old Colonel Altamont was all lined up to be hanged – he was already standing on the trapdoor, halfway out the jail window – before I felt too sorry for him and called the whole execution off.

I scrapped the entire “super exciting murder book” plan (my publishers were so polite about ditching their big payday, bless them) because I quickly realized I had no clue what I was writing about. I’ve never once been best mates with a convict! I don’t know how actual jailbirds and street ruffians talk! I couldn’t go compete with M. Eugène Sue and all his gritty crime stories, that would be like me trying to run the Grand National with no pants on, I’d faceplant in the mud before the first hurdle. And if I *did* write a real, unfiltered rascal, he’d be so hideously awful you’d hurl the book straight across the room. If an artist can’t paint a villain fairly, full warts and all, he’s got no right to show him off at all.

Heck, we can’t even draw normal, regular educated men as they really are these days! This book is about one of those guys, no better and no worse than most of the posh blokes you meet at dinner parties – but I’m not allowed to show you all his real dumb foibles, his selfish little habits, all the messy stuff he gets up to. Ever since the author of *Tom Jones* died, no fiction writer in this country has been allowed to draw a full, unfiltered, actual whole MAN. We all have to drape them in fancy polite cloth, stick a fake conventional little simper on their faces. Society won’t let us show the unvarnished real thing in art! A whole crowd of very proper ladies wrote me angry notes and cancelled their subscriptions, all because I wrote a young man who struggled with temptation, who had the passions to feel the pull of bad choices, and the decency to fight and overcome them. You’ll never hear the unvarnished truth about what really goes on in the clubs, the colleges, the army mess halls – what your sons actually talk about when they think no grown-ups are listening. I tried to be a little more frank than the average prissy Victorian novel here, I swear I had no bad intentions, and I promise it won’t do any of you a bit of harm.

Truth doesn’t always taste like fancy sugarplums, but it’s always better than the fake, cloying nonsense everyone else is peddling – whether it’s a stuffy learned man on a stage giving you a lecture, or a worn-out old storyteller wrapping up his work to say farewell to all his kind readers.

KENSINGTON, Nov. 26th, 1850.

It was peak London social season, and Major Arthur Pendennis trundled over from his fancy lodgings to his usual Pall Mall club, where he was less a member and more the institution’s unofficial reigning monarch. As one of England’s undisputed top three wine snobs, and a man with a knack for running every group he joined, he’d been elected to the club committee years back, and the stewards and waiters bowed to him lower than they would to a visiting duke or field marshal.

At exactly 10:15 every morning, the Major made his entrance in boots shined so bright you could use them as a shaving mirror, a crisp check morning cravat that never once rumpled before dinner, a buff waistcoat studded with buttons bearing the royal crest, and linen so spotless that even Beau Brummell once cornered him outside a tailor’s shop begging for his laundress’s address — right before Brummell had to do a runner to France to escape his gambling debts. His coat, white gloves, neatly waxed whiskers, and even his carved walking stick were all perfectly tuned to the vibe of a retired military gentleman. From the back, you’d swear he was barely 30. Get a foot closer, though, and you’d spot that his lush rich brown “hair” was a very well-made wig, and faint crow’s feet fanned out around his slightly faded eyes on his handsome, mottled face. He had a nose straight out of the Duke of Wellington’s playbook, long milky-white hands, gold cuff links gifted to him by the Duke of York, and several fancy rings, the biggest of which blazed with the famous Pendennis family crest he polished every single night before bed.

He always claimed the exact same corner table, near the fire and within a foot of the window, and no one even thought of challenging him for it these days. A pair of drunken silly young lads had tried twice in years past to steal his spot for a joke, but the Major had simply sat at the next table, rested his chin on his hand, and stared at them so calmly they got the creeps and scarpered mid-bite of their toast. Now his post was as sacred as a royal throne, and the waiters laid his letters out on the table before he even walked in. Half the young dandies about town would gawk at his stack of mail, covered in fancy noble seals and franked by members of parliament. If anyone in the club had a question about etiquette, society gossip, who was secretly separated from their spouse, or how old the Duke of So-and-so really was, they all came running to Pendennis. Marchionesses would pull up outside the club in their carriages just to leave him notes, or drag him out for a stroll in Hyde Park. The young men loved walking with him down Pall Mall, too — he tipped his hat to every second person they passed, and half of them were lords.

So the Major sat down at his sacred table, waved the waiters off to fetch his buttered toast and fresh hot newspaper, and peered at his stack of letters through his fancy gold lorgnette (which he insisted was a fashionable novelty, not a pair of reading glasses). He sorted them one by one: there were three thick formal dinner cards promising heavy conversation and three courses of overcooked meat, gushy little private notes from society ladies begging him to bring that famous bottle of claret he kept locked in his cellar, a thick note on official paper from the Marquis of Steyne inviting him to a tiny, French-speaking party at Richmond’s Star and Garter, and a request from the Bishop of Ealing and his wife for the Major to come to dinner at their house. Glowry, the grumpy Scottish surgeon eating breakfast across from him, glowered the whole time, seething that no one ever invited him to any fancy fun parties.

Once he’d read all the notes, the Major pulled out his pocket datebook to juggle his social calendar. He dumped boring old Cutler the East India Director’s dinner without a second thought to go to Steyne’s fancy French party, accepted the bishop’s invite (the port there was worth sitting through two hours of theological small talk), and sorted the rest of his engagements to suit his own amusement and social clout. He ate his kipper, flipped through the Gazette to double check his name was listed correctly on the guest list for a lord’s recent fete, and chatted cheerfully to all his friends passing by the table.

Tucked all the way at the bottom of his mail pile, all alone, was one sad little letter with a country postmark and a cheap, homely seal. The handwriting was a delicate, wobbly lady’s scrawl, marked *IMMEDIATE* with a shaky underline, but the Major had ignored it. A country letter wasn’t about to cut the queue ahead of all the marchionesses and marquises lined up for his attention. Finally, curiosity got the better of him, and he broke the seal engraved with the name “Fairoaks”, postmarked Clavering St. Mary’s. It was from his sister Helen Pendennis.

“My dearest Major,” she wrote, “I beg and implore you to come to me *immediately*. I am in the deepest grief and perplexity. My darling boy Arthur, who has been everything a fond mother could wish, has broken my heart. He has formed — I can barely write it — a passion, an infatuation, for an actress performing locally. She is at least twelve years older than Arthur, who will not even turn eighteen until next February, and the wretched boy insists he is going to marry her.”

The Major gaped so wide that Glowry, across the table, fumbled for the lancet he kept in his card case, convinced his respected friend was about to have an apoplectic fit. Tucked inside was a second letter, written in the messy, scrawly handwriting of his teenage nephew Arthur, the family seal splattered all over with extra wax like the boy was shaking so bad he could barely hold the sealing wax stick. The kid ranted on for pages about how his beloved Miss Costigan (stage name Miss Fotheringay, late of Drury Lane) was descended from ancient Irish royalty, that her ancestors were kings of Ireland the same year their own Pendennis ancestor landed in Ireland with Richard II, that their difference in age was totally irrelevant, that his love was true and forever, that no stuffy old society prejudices were going to stop him from marrying the woman of his dreams, and please would his dear uncle the man of the world come down to Fairoaks at once to calm his silly old mom down.

When the Major finished that letter, his face turned so purple Glowry was half out of his seat ready to yell for a doctor. The heir of the Pendennises marrying a second-rate tragedy queen ten years his senior? The whole of London would laugh at him so hard he’d never dare show his face at a club again. Worse — he was going to have to bail on Lord Steyne’s fabulous Richmond party. He was going to spend all night jolting around in a drafty mail coach instead of sipping excellent claret with the most fun, exclusive crowd in England. He stomped over to the club writing room, fuming, scribbled regretful notes to every single one of his upcoming hosts, ordered his valet to book two seats on that night’s mail coach, and promptly charged the full cost of the trip to his sister Helen’s household account. The little romantic idiot and his tearful mom could pay for the chaos they’d caused.

Back in the early days of the Regency of George the Magnificent—you know, the portly fellow who spent more on waistcoats than most aristocrats spent on their entire estates—there lived in the tiny west-of-England town of Clavering a gentleman by the name of Pendennis. Old locals still swore they could remember seeing his name painted on a board hung beneath a gilded pestle and mortar, over the door of a dinky little Bath shop where Mr. Pendennis worked as an apothecary and surgeon. He’d attend to sick gentlemen in their bedrooms, help ladies through those “most interesting periods” no one dared name aloud, and still stoop across the counter to sell a brown-paper plaster to a farmer’s wife, or flog toothbrushes, hair powder, and fancy London perfume to anyone who could spare the pennies. The good folk of Clavering had memories far stickier than their metropolis counterparts, so none of these very humble details ever quite got swept under the genteel carpet later.

The kicker? That same apothecary who doled out penny salts and overpriced Windsor soap to stray customers was a university-educated gentleman, with a Somersetshire family tree so old it stretched back to the Druids—who knows, maybe even further, to the blokes who first invented mud? His lot only deigned to marry Normans *very* late in their family history, thank you very much, and they were distantly related to every half-decent aristocratic clan in Wales and Brittany. He’d nearly finished his degree at Cambridge too, and would have gone on to great things, if his father hadn’t dropped dead insolvent halfway through his second year, forcing poor Pen to strap on an apron and pick up the pestle. He loathed the trade with every fiber of his being, and only took the job at the urging of his mother’s brother, a coarse, low-born London apothecary whose class his father had apparently demeaned himself to marry.

No sooner was his apprenticeship up than he fled his crass relative and set up that little Bath shop on his own. Poverty nipped at his heels for years; he could barely afford to keep the gilt pestle from tarnishing and his bedridden mother fed and comfortable. Then fate intervened in the silliest way possible: Lady Ribstone was being carried to the Bath Rooms by a very drunk Irish chairman, who stumbled straight into Pendennis’s doorpost, rammed the pole of his chair right through the shiniest pink glass bottle in the shop window. Her ladyship screamed loud enough to rouse every stray cat on Milsom Street, Pendennis hauled her inside, plied her with cinnamon and sal-volatile, and so the tide turned.

His manners were so impossibly soft and gentlemanly that Sir Pepin Ribstone, of Codlingbury, Somerset, instantly named him the personal apothecary to his entire sprawling household. When young Master Ribstone came home from Eton for Christmas, overate himself into a fever, and Pendennis nursed him back to health with endless patience, he was made for life. Bath’s high society adopted him wholesale, the ladies adored him, and step by step he climbed the social ladder: first his scruffy little shop got a fancy remodel, then he banned all sales of toothbrushes and perfume as unworthy of a man of his ancient Druid lineage, then he shut the shop entirely and kept only a tiny surgery run by a genteel young assistant, then he bought a gig with a driver, and finally he splashed out on a one-horse closed carriage, with the full Pendennis family crest blazoned all over the panels. His poor old bedridden mom got wheeled to her bedroom window to see him step into it, and blubbered “What would Arthur say now? That boy never once came to see my dearest Johnny through all his years of starving and scraping!”
“Captain Pendennis is with his regiment in India, mother,” John would mutter, mortified, darting a glance at his young assistant Mr. Parkins. “And I’d much rather you didn’t call me Johnny in front of the lad, if you please.”

It wasn’t long before his mother stopped calling him Johnny, or any other pet name at all, and the house felt awfully quiet without her grumbly but loving voice. He moved his night bell straight into her old bedroom, dragged the great big four-poster bed in there, and slept there himself. He was over forty by then, before the Napoleonic Wars ended, before George the Magnificent took the throne, long before our story even starts. And what’s a newly minted gentleman without his pedigree? He dragged his own long-forgotten family tree out of a trunk, just like Sterne’s old soldier fetching his sword, had it framed and glazed, and hung it in his drawing-room between prints of Codlingbury House and his old Cambridge college.

Around the time his mother died, one of his regular patients, the virtuous old Lady Pontypool, kicked the bucket too. That lady was the daughter of the 12th Earl of Bareacres, great-great aunt to the current earl, widow of both the 2nd Lord Pontypool and the Reverend Jonas Wales of Clifton’s Armageddon Chapel. For five years before her death, she’d been waited on hand and foot by Miss Helen Thistlewood, a distant poor relation of the Bareacres clan, whose father Lieutenant Thistlewood had died fighting at the Battle of Copenhagen. Helen got free board and lodging under Lady Pontypool’s roof, sure—but she also got five full years of the kind of petty, vicious tyranny only old noblewomen can inflict on a helpless orphan. Pendennis, who popped in to see his patient twice a day, couldn’t help but notice how sweet and gentle the girl was, even when the old bat screamed at her for trivial nonsense. It was while they were all trundling to Bath Abbey in the fourth mourning coach to drop Lady Pontypool’s remains off that he looked at her pale, sweet face and decided he had to propose—so much so that his pulse spiked to at least 90 beats a minute.

He was over twenty years older than her, and never the most fiery of men. Maybe he’d had a young love he’d had to snuff out, the way you drown a litter of unwanted blind kittens, all the best romances of the poor having to be disposed of sooner or later. At 43, he was a quiet, neat little gentleman in black stockings, with a bald spot on his head. A few days after the funeral he called on her, pretended to take her pulse, held onto her hand, and asked her where she’d go now that the Pontypools were boxing up every plate, chair, and teacup in the house, locking everything in green baize chests under three keys, and carting it all away right under her nose.
She teared up and said she had no clue. The old lady had left her a thousand pounds, she said, but she’d probably have to move into a boarding house or work at a school. She had no other plans.
Still holding her cold little hand, Pendennis told her she could come live with him. He was old, he admitted, way too old for such a blooming young lady, but he was well-born, honourable, even-tempered, his fortune was growing every year, and he was all alone in the world—he’d spend the rest of his days making her happy, if she’d have him. The whole speech he’d composed in bed that morning, and rehearsed word for word in his carriage on the ride over.
Helen probably could have dreamed up a more romantic match, if she’d had a choice: a stuffy little man who rapped his teeth when he was nervous, who bowed extra hard to the butler and simpered at the lady’s maid the same way he used to back when he was a mere apothecary running errands for rich patrons. But she also knew how good he’d been to his mother, how honourable and prudent he was, so she blushed bright red, curtsied as low as her knees would let her, and asked if she could please, please think the very kind proposal over.

They got married in the dead, dull Bath season, when all the fancy people were in London. Pendennis, through a friend who was a Member of the Royal College of Surgeons, booked them lodgings in Holles Street, Cavendish Square, brought her up in a chaise and pair, took her to the theatres, the parks, and the Chapel Royal, showed her the crowds arriving for a royal drawing-room, and gave her every fancy London pleasure she could possibly imagine. He left calling cards on Lord Pontypool, the Earl of Bareacres, and his oldest patrons Sir Pepin and Lady Ribstone. Bareacres ignored the card entirely. Pontypool called on them, complimented Helen, and said Lady Pontypool would come visit soon—though all that ever came was her footman, who dropped off her mistress’s card and an invitation to a concert five weeks later. The Ribstones, though, actually invited them to a grand dinner, a story Pendennis bragged about right up till the day he died.

His entire life’s secret ambition was to be seen as a proper gentleman, no strings attached. It took him years of careful saving, but fortune smiled on him: he dumped a pile of cash into buying a little house and estate right next to the village of Clavering, and he was so proud the day he walked his own acres that he could barely speak, never mind admit to anyone how giddy he was. He also bought shares in a wildly successful copper mine that doubled his fortune, sold his Bath apothecary business to Mr. Parkins for a fat pile of cash and a lifetime annuity, and retired forever from pestles, pills, and plasters.

His son Arthur was eight years old when he moved to Fairoaks Park, which explains why little Pen barely remembered the Bath shop existing at all—his dad never once mentioned it. He called in the local Clavering doctor to treat his own family, retired his old black medical breeches and stockings forever, started wearing bottle-green coats with brass buttons and drab gaiters to market and quarter sessions, and acted like he’d been a landed gentleman his whole entire life. He stood at his lodge gate waving gravely at the passing coachmen and guards, founded the Clavering Book Club, set up the Samaritan Soup and Blanket Society, and even successfully lobbied to move the local mail route away from the nearby dumpy village of Cacklefield and through Clavering instead. At church he was an active vestryman, every Thursday he trundled round the market stalls feeling oats, punching geese in the breast, chatting with the farmers like he’d been doing it his whole life, and if anyone dared call him “Doctor” he got huffy. The only title he’d answer to was Squire.

Before anyone could blink, half a dozen “ancestor portraits” turned up hung round his oak dining room. He swore blind every last one of them was a genuine Lely or Van Dyck, and if anyone asked who the figures were, he’d vaguely say they were old Pendennises, don’t you know? Roger Pendennis who fought at Agincourt, Arthur Pendennis who charged at Crecy, General Pendennis who won glory at Blenheim and Oudenarde. His wife Helen clearly thought these stories were utter rubbish, and always tried to change the subject whenever he started going on about them—but little Arthur believed every single word. Those long-dead Pendennis warriors were as real to him as Robinson Crusoe, Peter Wilkins, or the Seven Champions of Christendom he read about in his nursery books.

His total fortune never topped 800 pounds a year, so he couldn’t afford to hang out with the very top tier of county nobility, but he had a perfectly comfortable little set of B-list gentry friends, the sort of people who lived close enough to the roses to smell the posh scent, even if they weren’t blooming themselves. They dined each other twice a year, travelling a dozen miles across the shire to show off their silver and eat overcooked roast beef till midnight. The only downside was the hordes of Clavering townsfolk who kept poking their noses into the house: nosy Mrs. Pybus was always snooping in Helen’s conservatories, messing up her soup ticket distributions, and swaggering Captain Glanders of the 50th Dragoon Guards was forever hanging round the stables trying to rope Pendennis into his endless petty feuds with the vicar, the postmaster, and the grammar school headmaster for beating his son too hard. The Pendennises thanked their lucky stars Fairoaks House was a full mile outside the village, or they’d never have had five minutes of peace.

The Fairoaks lawn sloped straight down to the tiny little river Brawl, and across the water were the overgrown woods of Clavering Park, Sir Francis Clavering’s estate. The big Clavering house was all shuttered up, a grand freestone palace with huge staircases, marble statues, and a grand portico that got a full page spread in *The Beauties of England and Wales*. Sir Francis’s grandfather had gone broke building the ridiculous palace, his son had finished the family ruin by living in it, and the current Sir Francis was off gallivanting abroad somewhere, no one rich enough to rent the massive mouldy pile. Little Arthur spent hours wandering its empty, creaking halls as a boy, half-terrified he’d run into a ghost. At sunset, the view from Fairoaks lawn was stunning: the grass and the trees turned bright gold, the upper windows of the great empty house flamed so bright you had to squint, the river slid quietly west into the dark woods, and the purple towers of old Clavering Abbey rose up behind the treeline. Little Arthur and his mother would stand there, their long blue shadows stretching across the grass, reciting Milton’s lines about “These are thy glorious works, Parent of Good” in soft voices, to Helen’s utter delight. Their walks usually ended in a whole heap of hugs, for loving and praying were Helen’s favourite pastimes. Little Arthur even grew up claiming he was guaranteed a spot in heaven, because his mother would never agree to go there without him.

As for John Pendennis the Squire, everyone respected him like he was the Medes and the Persians rolled into one. His hat was always the best brushed hat in the whole county, every meal was served exactly at the same minute, woe betide little Pen if he turned up late. Prayers, letter reading, business, stable inspections, hen house and pigsty checks, all happened on a rigid schedule. After his exactly 6pm dinner, he always napped in his armchair with the *Globe* newspaper on his knee and his yellow bandanna handkerchief spread over his face—those handkerchiefs were a gift from his brother the Major, who’d helped John buy his army majority years back, so they were great friends now. By the time that pretty sunset view rolled round at half past seven, he was still snoring away inside, completely oblivious to all the poetry and mother-son affection happening out on the lawn.

As soon as he walked back into the drawing-room, newspaper under his arm, Helen and Pen instantly hushed, no more giggling or gushing. While Pen curled up in a great big armchair gobbling up every book he could get his hands on, the Squire wrote his little gardening articles for the *Gardener’s Gazette*, or played a very serious game of picquet with Helen, or the occasional friend from the village.

Pendennis always scheduled his grandest dinner parties for the biennial visits of his brother Major Pendennis, who’d sold out of the army after his regiment came back from India and New South Wales, and lived comfortably on half-pay. “My brother the Major” was Pendennis’s favourite conversation opener at the reading rooms, at quarter sessions, all across the county. The Major was their personal hotline to fancy London high society: he always turned up with the latest gossip about lords and dukes, dined with nobility every other week, and talked about them all with perfect soldierly decorum. “Lord Bareacres was good enough to invite me up to Bareacres for the pheasant shoot,” he’d say, or “Lord Steyne was kind enough to ask me down to Stillbrook for Easter.” His fame as a fashionable man about town was so widespread that gentry drove ten miles out of their way to call on him when he stayed at Fairoaks. Everyone was shocked when he turned down a marriage proposal from Miss Hunkle, the attorney’s daughter, who had 1500 pounds a year of her own. “As a bachelor,” he explained, “no one cares how poor I am. I move in circles where a few thousand pounds a year doesn’t make a difference in how people see you. Miss Hunkle is perfectly respectable, but she doesn’t have the breeding or the manners to fit into that world. I’ll die an old bachelor, and she’ll find a far better husband than a worn out half-pay soldier.” He turned out to be right: Miss Hunkle married a wild young French nobleman a year later, got titled Baroness de Carambole, and dumped her useless husband barely six months after the wedding.

The Major was everyone’s favourite at Fairoaks: he was as good-natured as he was well-bred, and adored Helen, whom he swore was one of the finest ladies in all of England. Helen’s quiet beauty, her natural sweetness, and that simple dignity that only perfect purity and innocence can give a woman made the praise entirely deserved. Arthur thought of her as less of a human and more of a literal angel when he was a boy—all wisdom, kindness, and grace. When John drove her to the county assize balls and concerts, he’d walk in with her on his arm, and stare down all the rich lords like he was daring any of them to show him a wife half as good as his. Of course, that made half the local county ladies, who had three times her fortune, seethe with jealousy: Miss Pybus said she was cold and haughty, Miss Pierce said she was far too proud for the wife of a former doctor, and the parson’s wife Mrs. Wapshot insisted she got to go first through every door, since Helen was only a “medical man’s wife.” Helen didn’t notice any of the drama at all. She just went about her days saying her prayers, loving her family, helping her neighbours, and doing her duty, completely unbothered.

Arthur Pendennis was just turned sixteen, fresh off dethroning his schoolmasters to reign as unchallenged lord of Fairoaks estate, and to hear his doting mother tell it, he was the spitting image of a Greek god in miniature. Any neutral onlooker would have called him dumpy as a sack of new potatoes, but Helen’s golden-eyed assessment was “neat, compact, and distinguished.” His brown hair glinted like spun gold in the sun, his round rosy face was dotted with freckles, and he was so perpetually ready to laugh that his crinkly honest blue eyes made every stranger want to like him instantly. The faint reddish stubble he stared at in the mirror for 20 minutes every morning, praying it would turn into proper whiskers, was a family miracle as far as his mother was concerned. Between 16 and 18 he shot up three whole inches to 5 foot 8, a feat Helen refused to believe was even possible, given that his late revered father had been barely 5 foot 5. She was half-convinced he’d been sneaking growth tonic in his lemonade when no one was looking.

There was zero chance of him ever going back to Greyfriars School. The masters’ strict rules about Greek verb conjugations and cold porridge at 6 a.m. did not fit his new royal schedule of lazing on the lawn reading novels. His uncle the Major and the family doctor both insisted he needed to cram a full Greek tragedy under his belt if he ever wanted to get anywhere in life, but Pen very casually dropped a line to Helen over tea about how Greyfriars was full of unruly lads who swapped pocket pistols and stole pies off the headmaster’s windowsill, and Helen panicked so fast she agreed he could stay home indefinitely, no further questions asked.

Next, the Major offered to pull strings with the Commander-in-Chief to get Pen a fancy commission in the Foot Guards, and Pen’s heart leaped so high it nearly bounced out of his chest. He still remembered his old schoolmate Tom Ricketts, the sad little fourth-former who used to wear trousers so tight he could barely sit down without squeaking, who every older boy used as a human target for rubber erasers. Now he’d seen Tom parading in crimson and gold uniform, balancing a giant bearskin cap on his head, hefting regimental colours, and giving Pen a patronising little nod like he was a lowly stable boy. Pen would have traded every last one of his penny dreadfuls to have that uniform, to march at the centre of a crowd of bayonets and Waterloo medals, to have the band blast trumpets for him wherever he went.

But when he brought the news to Helen, she turned pale as a sheet. She said no proper Christian gentleman made the army his profession, and his dear departed dad would rise straight out of his grave to scold him if he even thought about it. Pen, who would sooner cut off his own nose than make his mom cry, immediately surrendered his imaginary red coat and epaulettes no questions asked. Helen thought he was the noblest creature to walk the face of the earth. The Major, meanwhile, fired off a huffy curt letter back calling his nephew a soft-headed spooney who’d thrown away the best possible career for no reason.

That Christmas, when the Major came down to visit, he softened a little when he saw Pen ride out hunting. Pen had a lovely bay mare named Rebecca, and he rode with such cool, unshowy pluck he cleared every fence in the shire without a single wobble. He immediately started bragging about his brand new top boots to all his old school friends, and even Helen had to admit he’d look devastatingly handsome in a hunting scarlet. The catch was, every time he rode out, she paced the parlour for four hours straight, fully expecting him to be carried home on a shutter after a bad fall.

Don’t go thinking he neglected his studies entirely, though. He devoured every single book in a 10 mile radius that wasn’t on the school’s required reading list: old plays, horse care manuals, a dusty crate of very racy French novels he dug out of the back of the Clavering bookshop, and all the old heavy folios in Dr Portman the vicar’s library, which he’d read perched on the top rung of the library step for hours like a very determined pirate scaling a mast. He and Dr Portman bonded hard over glasses of port after dinner, and the only thing Mrs Portman sighed over was that her daughter Minny was two full years older than Pen’s own mother, and weighed as much as Pen and Helen put together. No chance of that little romance ever taking off.

To keep him from turning into a total layabout, they hired Mr Smirke, the vicar’s curate, to tutor him daily for a very generous salary. Smirke was a perfectly nice man who never spilled tea on the tablecloth, wore one perfectly preened curl on his fair forehead, and tied his cravat with the somber grace of a man attending a funeral for a favourite goldfish. He rode his pony so badly his toes stuck out so far at the ankle you could hang a sheet over them. No teen boy with a working sense of humour could possibly respect that. Pen nearly gave Smirke a full heart attack when he plopped him on Rebecca and took him out across a common right where the local foxhounds were running. Smirke rode straight into the middle of the pack, lamed one of the hounds, and got yelled at by the huntsman so loud and so colourfully Smirke thought he’d be struck by lightning right there in the mud.

Smirke was an absolutely useless tutor, it turned out. Instead of drilling Pen on algebra, he shared all his terrible Latin and English poetry with him, and they galloped through Homer and the Greek tragedies at such a chaotic, breakneck pace Pen remembered almost nothing later except three random quotes he could drop at parties to sound well-read, exactly like every posh Oxford man of the era. Pen was a diehard Byron stan, knew all his lines by heart, and would drag little Laura, his mom’s tiny ward, over to the window to recite dramatic Corsair monologues at her until she stared so wide-eyed you’d think he was speaking alien. When he read Mrs Hemans and Bishop Heber out loud to Helen, she’d dissolve into tears so fast she was immediately sobbing into her linen handkerchief.

It wasn’t long before Pen started getting his terrible poetry published in the County Chronicle, all signed “NEP”: verses about a tear, a rant denouncing Popery on St Bartholomew’s Day, an ode to Madame Caradori singing at the assizes, and Helen kept every crumpled clipping tucked in her jewellery box right next to his baby socks and his first ever haircut. He wrote a full-length tragedy where 16 people died before the end of act two, and Helen laughed so hard at it Pen threw the whole manuscript in the fire in a huff. He even rode out electioneering for the local Blue Tory candidate, got a little too carried away with the election Madeira, and stumbled home at 10 p.m. with six yelling farmer voters trailing behind him, only for Helen to step out in her white nightgown holding a single candle. The voters scattered like naughty mice before she could even scold them.

By this point, 18-year-old Pen had caught the love bug full bore. He was dying for a grand, consuming, Byronic passion, a fair lady to write all his poems for, instead of wasting verses on fictional Zuleikas and Ianthes he’d read about in books. What he didn’t know was that Smirke the tutor was also madly in love – not with some pretty young lady his own age, but with Helen Pendennis, Pen’s mother. He was so smitten he gave Pen his favourite Elzevir Horace, a silver pencil case, and let him skip every single math lesson he wanted, just to hang around the estate near Helen. He never washed the glass Helen drank tea from once, and kept it on a special sacred shelf above his desk. He thought Pen was the luckiest boy alive, getting to live in the same house as the most beautiful woman in the whole shire.

Helen, meanwhile, was pinching pennies left and right, convinced Pen was going to grow up to be either Archbishop of Canterbury or Lord Chancellor, no less. There was just one tiny problem: there was literally no single girl Pen’s age within 15 miles. The dairymaid? Far too low for a Pendennis to court. The admiral’s daughters? Thought Pen was a snot-nosed kid. The three Glanders girls? Still in pinafores with their braids tied up in dirty pink ribbon. So Pen rode out every day, craning his neck to stare into every passing carriage, half-convinced his mysterious perfect Dulcinea would be sitting in the back of some random yellow post-chaise. Every single time, it was either a 70-year-old snuffy old dowager or three girls who were no prettier than the turnips growing by the side of the road. Helen, for her part, had already mapped out his whole romantic future: he’d marry little Laura, when he was 26 and she was 18, fresh back from his grand tour, settled in a rose-covered country parsonage preaching the finest sermons the shire had ever heard.

It was on one such trip, when Pen rode into Chatteris to drop off his new epic poem for the County Chronicle, that he was leaning against the stable wall chatting to the hostler about Rebecca, when a grand black tandem carriage with bright scarlet wheels rattled into the yard. A giant figure wrapped in three overcoats and a wide-brimmed hat leaned out the window and yelled, “Hallo, Pendennis! Is that you?” It was none other than his long-lost old schoolfellow, Mr Foker.

Back at the George Inn, Mr. Foker and his new chum Pen settled into a slap-up feast in the coffee room, where waiter Mr. Rincer toted in the first dish and bowed so low he nearly tipped his own wig off, as if he was waiting on the Lord Lieutenant himself rather than two giddy young chaps with more appetite than sense. Foker tore into the turtle soup and venison with the same ravenous glee he’d shown a year prior, when the fanciest feast he could scrape together was stolen polonies and fizzing ginger beer that tasted vaguely like dish soap. Pen couldn’t help but respect his connoisseurship when he took one sip of champagne, made a face, and announced it was “barely fermented gooseberry they’re trying to pass off as French,” then winked one eye at the glass of port, prodded it like it owed him money, and declared it the genuine good stuff, warning the waiting staff not to even try pulling a fast one on him.

He knew every single inn employee by their first name, asked after their wives’ bad knees and their cats’ unexpected litters, and every time a London stagecoach rumbled up to the door (the old kind that set off bright and early from the George back then), he’d fling the coffee room window wide open, yell at the guards and coachmen by name, and do a dead-on impression of their horn toots so loud the old half-blind parrot hanging by the bar squawked and fell straight off its perch.
“Bottle of sherry, bottle of that so-called champagne I just roasted, bottle of port, and a coffee strong enough to strip paint off a boot,” Foker said, once they’d worked their way through all the delicacies and a mountain of nuts and overripe fruit, “Reckon it’s toddle o’clock, innit, Pen?”
Pen sprang up with bright, wine-flushed eyes, and the pair trundled over to the theatre, where they paid their entry fee to the wheezy old woman napping in the ticket booth. “That’s Mrs. Dropsicum, Bingley the manager’s mother-in-law,” Foker whispered, “Once got a standing ovation for Lady Macbeth so dramatic she tripped over the witches’ cauldron mid-soliloquy.”

The theatre was emptier than a pauper’s purse, even though Bingley’s playbills had been screaming about “universal bursts of electric delight” and “galvanic thrills for all the family.” Twenty or so bored souls dotted the pit benches, a handful of drunks kicked and whistled in the galleries, and a dozen freeloaders on free passes occupied the boxes right next to Pen and Foker. Lieutenants Rodgers and Podgers, plus young Cornet Tidmus of the local Dragoons, were camped out in a private box, yelling at the actors like they were old mates down the pub and cheering them by name mid-scene.
Bingley the manager, who hogged all the best tragic and comic roles for himself, only stepping aside occasionally for visiting London stars, was currently chewing the scenery as the Stranger. He was decked out in skin-tight pantaloons and Hessian boots that made his calves look like overstuffed sausages, swaddled in a giant cloak, with a hearse-feather-draped beaver hat drooping over his caked-on stage makeup, half-obscuring his scratchy, slipping brown buckled wig. He’d raided the theatre’s costume jewellery chest for the shiniest, fakest diamond ring he could find, and waggled it ostentatiously on his little finger right at the pit, so every single patron could admire its gaudy glitter. He’d told every new young actor in the company the ring was a sacred stage heirloom: it had belonged to legendary actor George Frederick Cooke, who got it from Quin, who almost certainly paid a whole shilling for it at a market stall. Bingley was fully convinced the entire world was spellbound by its sparkle.
He was reading from that weird, tattered stage book no normal human would ever hold at that absurd angle, pointing at random lines and wagging his head like he was getting direct, dramatic divine revelations from a very bored angel. The second he spotted Pen and Foker in their box, he decided they were his new personal audience, staring right past Foker (who was just happily sucking the top of his walking stick, unbothered) to fix his gaze dead on Pen. When he moaned that overripe line about Hope’s cradle being the grave, he sounded like a bassoon getting stuck in a drain, staring so hard Pen was convinced the whole theatre was looking at him. Every time Pen glanced down to escape the gaze, then looked up again, Bingley’s eyes were still glued to him like a determined leech.
When the scene finally wrapped, Foker whacked his cane against the box rail and yelled, “Bravo, Bingley! Give the bloke a hand, Pen, everyone loves a bit of applause!” So Pen clapped along snorting with laughter, and the Dragoons across the way hooted so loud their gloves split at the seams.

The set shifted suddenly to a grand chamber in Wintersen Castle, and Foker leaned in to whisper, “That’s bit-part player Hicks and Miss Thackthwaite over there, she’s pretty alright innit—wait HURRAY, BRAVO, HERE’S FOTHERINGAY!”
The whole pit thumped their umbrellas against the floor, the galleries fired off a volley of whoops, and the Dragoons and Foker clapped so hard their hands turned bright pink. Peering out from the wings, you could see Mr Costigan, her scruffy red-faced dad, grinning like he’d just won the lottery. Pen’s eyes blew wide as saucers as Mrs Haller swept on stage, head bowed, then lifted her gaze to thank the cheering crowd, folded her hands over her chest, and sank into a magnificent curtsy that earned even more whoops and umbrella thumps. Pen, giddy on wine and instant adoration, yelled “BRAVO” louder than literally everyone else in the building. The little old first fiddler in the orchestra, Bows, who had a crutch propped against his music stand, looked up from his sheet music and smiled at the boy’s obvious starstruck enthusiasm.
Anyone who only ever met Miss Fotheringay later, after she married and became a fancy London society lady, had no clue how breathtakingly beautiful she was back then, at 26 (though she swore to every single person who asked she was only 19). Tall, broad-foreheaded, with soft natural rippling black hair that none of the crimping-iron-obsessed younger misses of the day could replicate, braided back over a neck as smooth and perfect as the Venus de Milo’s. Her eyes, when she lifted them to look at you before dropping their thick purple-fringed lids, shone with soft, unfathomable tenderness, like she already knew every single one of your most embarrassing childhood secrets. She never laughed (her teeth were nothing to write home about, truth be told) but a soft, sweet, endless sad smile played over her lips, dimpling her cheeks and chin. Her ears were little pearly shells, and the theatre’s very best (still fake) diamond earrings only looked tacky next to them. She swept across the stage in long, flowing black gowns that moved like water around her, only the tips of her large, elegant sandals peeking out of the fabric, which Pen immediately decided were Cinderella’s lost slippers. But it was her hands and arms that were truly unbeatable: when she folded them over her chest in resignation, flung them out in silent agony, or waved them soft and light before her like snowy doves, the whole theatre held its breath.
She delivered her big monologue about her lost little William and his sisters, wailing about how her fancy kept torturing her with visions of them sick and crying for their mother, with such gushing, smothered pathos that she made every single person in the theatre feel like she was crying her heart out directly onto their shoulder. Old Bows buried his face in his blue cotton handkerchief, blubbered “BRAVO”, Foker was sobbing so hard into his giant yellow bandanna he looked like he’d spent an hour chopping raw onions, and Pen was completely gone. He followed her every movement around the stage, when she walked off the rest of the theatre vanished into a meaningless blur of lights and red-uniformed dragoons. When she flung herself on Bingley’s shoulder for the big reconciliation scene, with the child actors clinging to their knees and the rest of the cast clustered around, Pen’s hot, foggy eyes couldn’t see anyone but her. The curtain fell, and it felt like a soft heavy pall dropping over the whole room.
He didn’t hear a single word Bingley said when he came out to announce the next night’s show, and almost missed the whole theatre screaming for a curtain call for Miss Fotheringay, not him. When Bingley finally got the hint, grinned, and dragged her back out on stage, Pen’s eyes nearly fell out of his head: her braid had slipped loose, the dragoons were throwing her wildflowers, she tucked them to her chest, brushed her hair back from her face, and her eyes locked straight onto Pen’s for two whole, perfect seconds before the curtain fell again.
He didn’t hear a single note of the Dragoons’ brass band playing the post-show overture. “She’s an absolute crusher, ain’t she now?” Foker gushed. Pen just babbled vague nonsense, he couldn’t explain the overwhelming, delicious, maddening feeling surging through his chest, like he’d drunk three bottles of champagne in one go. Foker quickly got fully absorbed in the silly double hornpipe dance that followed, but Pen couldn’t have cared less. The whole stage was a mist, with only her face visible in the middle of it. At the end of the dance he checked his watch, panic spiking: he had a long ride home.
“Hang on, stay for The Bravo of the Battle-Axe!” Foker begged, “Bingley’s brilliant in it, he wears red tights and tries to carry Mrs Bingley over a fake cataract bridge and nearly drops her into the painted water! It’s total chaos, you’ll love it!”
Pen scanned the playbill lingeringly, hoping against hope Fotheringay’s name was somewhere in the afterpiece cast list, but it wasn’t. He had to leave. He squeezed Foker’s hand so tight it nearly ached, choked out a goodbye he couldn’t even properly say, and wandered aimlessly around the streets of Chatteris in a daze for what felt like hours before he remembered his horse waiting at the George. He galloped home, and the Clavering town clock bonged 1am exactly as he stumbled into the Fairoaks courtyard, dashed into his bedroom, and faceplanted under the covers.
He slept like a log, no tossing and turning, until the rooks started cawing from the woods outside his window at dawn. The very second his eyes popped open, her face was the first thing in his head. It felt exactly like that day three years prior, when his dad had given him a fancy new gold watch for getting into the fifth form: he’d hidden in every empty corner of the house to rub it, polish it, and listen to it tick, checking every five minutes to make sure it was still safely in his pocket. (That watch was a total piece of garbage, by the way, never kept time right, and he eventually traded it away for a decent workmanlike one, but right now this new, all-consuming crush felt like the shiniest, most perfect treasure in the whole world.)
He felt years older than he had the day before, put on his fanciest waistcoat and his best new stickpin, strutted down to breakfast acting impossibly haughty, patronising his mum Helen and little Laura, who’d been plucking away at her piano lesson for hours already. After he rattled through the household prayers without listening to a single syllable he was saying, Laura gawked at his fancy getup and asked him what the play was about. Pen laughed and refused to tell her (it was definitely for the best, given some of the more dramatic, sappy bits). When she asked why he was dressed like he was off to meet the king, he blushed and told his mum he was planning to ride over to Baymouth to visit his old schoolfellow who was studying there with a very learned tutor, to suss out their reading list to prep for his own upcoming time at university. Helen’s stomach twisted with that vague, uneasy worry she’d had ever since the farmer had turned up last night with news Pen wouldn’t be home for dinner, but she told herself Pen had never lied to her before. He waved them off, trotted his horse slow at first, then galloped like a madman the second he was out of earshot of the house.
He ran straight into his tutor Mr Smirke, who was ambling over to Fairoaks to give Pen his usual three hours of lessons, and Pen shot past so fast the little man’s pony shied, flinging Smirke headfirst straight into a hedge full of stinging nettles. Pen cackled, yelled that he was heading for Baymouth, and vanished half a mile down the road before Smirke could even pick grass out of his hair. Sitting there in the nettles, stinging all over, Smirke decided there was no point chasing his pupil now. He might as well mosey over to Fairoaks, ask Mrs Pendennis when Arthur would be back, and make little Laura recite her Watts’s Catechism to pass the time.
And that, dear readers, is what love does: it turns giddy teen boys into galloping, brainless idiots, and mild-mannered little curates into nettle-stung hedge victims, all before 9am.

Rebecca the mare didn’t slacken pace for a second, galloping full tilt all the way to Baymouth like she had a personal grudge against the cobblestones, until Pen dumped her at the inn stables and bolted straight for Mr. Foker’s digs— the address he’d scribbled down the other day, above a chemist’s shop that didn’t do half as much business selling cough syrup as it did peddling cheap cigars and lukewarm soda water to the horde of rowdy young university students living on the floor above.

When Pen burst through the door, the only person home was Mr. Spavin, Foker’s old mate and co-owner of that tandem they’d careened into Chatteris with the day before, who was half-asleep smoking a cigar and trying to teach a scruffy little terrier to do tricks with a crumb of biscuit. Pen’s bright pink, wind-burned gallop-fresh face looked ridiculous next to Spavin’s waxy, overindulged, three-nights-no-sleep features, and Spavin noted the contrast immediately. “Who’s that bloke?” he thought. “Looks as fresh as a newly picked pea. I’d lay five to one his hand doesn’t shake even before his first morning beer.”

Foker was nowhere to be found. Disaster! Spavin couldn’t say when he’d roll up— sometimes he vanished for a day, sometimes a whole week, no questions asked. Which college was Pen from? Did he want a pint of perfectly decent ale? He fished Pen’s calling card off the table (Pen was absurdly proud of having a proper calling card back then, for the record), the two exchanged a couple of awkward pleasantries, and Pen beat a hasty retreat.

He trundled down the cliff to the beach, pacing up and down the sand gnawing his nails while the sea roared and crashed in the background, bright and endless, blue waves foaming and thundering at the shore. Pen stared right through them, too wrapped up in his own whirring, overemotional teen brain to notice a single bit of the view. There was a whole tidal wave of nonsense and infatuation flooding through his head, and he hadn’t a hope in hell of stopping it. He chucked stone after stone into the surf, but it didn’t make the smallest dent in his mood. He was furious he couldn’t find Foker. He needed to see Foker. He had to see Foker. “What if I just ride a little ways down the Chatteris road, just to chance running into him?” he thought. Half an hour later, Rebecca was saddled up again, galloping along the grass verge.

Four miles out of Baymouth, the Clavering turnoff branched off, as every local knows, and Rebecca naturally tried to swerve for it (she knew the way home to Pen’s stables, where the hay was far better than anything she’d get on the Chatteris road) but Pen whacked her over the shoulder and kept going all the way to the turnpike, not a single sign of Foker’s black tandem or bright red wheels anywhere.

Well, he was already at the turnpike, so he might as well keep going, right? That was perfectly logical. So he rode straight to the George, where the hostler cackled and confirmed Foker was definitely inside. “He were making a right racket last night, sir, drinking and singing and threatening to knock Tom the postboy’s block off! Though I reckon Tom would’ve wiped the floor with him, if it came to it.” He winked super sarcastically at Foker’s footman Stoopid, who trundled out of the yard carrying his master’s immaculately brushed clothes. “Go on then, show Mr. Pendennis up to his lordship,” and Pen trailed after him up the stairs to the bedroom, where Harry Foker lay buried under a mountain of featherbeds.

The pillows and duvets swelled up all around him so all you could see of the little bloke was his sallow face and pointy red silk nightcap peeking over the top edge.

“Oy, who goes there? Halt and give the countersign, brother!” Foker yelled out groggily from the pile of bedding. “Wait, Pendennis? Did your mum know you snuck out last night? Did you join us for supper? Wait no— who *did* we have at supper last night, Stoopid?”

“Three officers, Mr. Bingley, and Mr. Costigan, sir,” Stoopid said, completely deadpan, not cracking a single smile.

“Ah right! The cup went round, the jokes were flying, we all chanted songs. I remember I tried to fight a postboy. Did I win, Stoopid?”

“No sir. Fight never happened, sir.” Stoopid replied, still utterly straight-faced, while he arranged Foker’s fancy dressing case — a huge silver-mounted trunk his doting mum had gifted him, that he never travelled anywhere without. It was stuffed full of fancy silver trinkets: a silver dish, silver mug, little silver jars for every possible fancy perfume, and a whole set of razors all sharp and ready, for the hypothetical future day Foker finally grew a beard.

“Ah well, save it for another day,” Foker yawned, throwing his lanky little arms over his head. “No fight, but *plenty* of singing. Bingley sang, I sang, the General — that’s Costigan — sang. Have you ever heard him do *The Little Pig Under The Bed*, Pen? It’s a masterpiece.”

“That man we met yesterday,” Pen stammered, already trembling, “the father of —”

“Of the Fotheringay! Exactly! Ain’t she a straight-up Venus, Pen?”

“Beg pardon, sir,” Stoopid cut in. “Mr. Costigan’s in the sitting room, sir. You invited him for breakfast, sir. He’s been knocking five times, but we weren’t to wake you, and he’s been sat there since eleven o’clock, sir.”

“What time is it now?”

“One, sir.”

“Can you imagine what my dear old mum would say,” Foker groaned, “if she saw me lazing in bed at one in the afternoon? She sent me down here with a tutor, you know. She wants me to ‘nurture my neglected genius’ — snort — this is a damn sight better than seven o’clock Latin lessons, isn’t it, old pal?” he cackled, then waved Pen off. “Go on in and chat to the General while I get dressed. And for God’s sake ask him to sing *The Little Pig Under The Bed*, it’s hilarious.” Pen, all jittery and flustered, trundled off to meet Costigan while Foker started his two hour toilet routine.

For context: one of Foker’s grandfathers was a self-made brewer who left him a massive fortune, the other was an earl who gave him the world’s most doting, overprotective mum. The Fokers had gone to the same Cistercian school for generations, where young Harry’s name was painted right over the playground pub sign, under the words “Foker’s Entire”. He got bullied unmercifully for that, for his funny face, for being useless at schoolwork, for being a total slob, for gorging himself on tuck. Anyone who’s ever seen a quiet, bullied kid skulk around school doing everything he can not to draw attention to himself knows exactly what happens the second he walks out the school gates: within months, shy little Harry bloomed into the sarcastic, quick-witted, absurdly dandyish Foker we all know. He was still a total dunce, of course — you don’t suddenly learn Latin just cause you leave school and sign up as a wealthy fellow-commoner at university — but by the time he walked into the sitting room to join his guests, he was doused in cologne, decked out in immaculate linen, and looking positively splendid.

Captain Costigan — he preferred “Captain” to “General”, for the record — was perched on the windowsill, holding the newspaper at full arm’s length. His eyes were a bit wonky and bloodshot, so he mouthed every word he read, like most gents who don’t get much practice reading. His hat was tilted so far over one ear it was practically slipping off, and anyone paying attention would’ve noticed his boots were so big and scuffed and falling apart it was clear times were not exactly flush for him. Poverty always attacks the extremities first, right? Your hat, your boots, your gloves are the first things to go when you’re skint, and every single one of Costigan’s was extra ragged and shabby. The second he saw Pen he hopped off the windowsill, saluted him with two fingers sticking out of his broken black glove, then whipped his hat right off his head to bow. He was half bald, but he’d combed a whole mess of lank iron-grey hair over his scalp, with two wispy little tufts hanging down on either side of his face. Decades of whisky had turned what was once a handsome complexion a rich coppery red. He wore a high stiff neckerchief covered in stains, and a dress coat buttoned tight across his chest where the remaining buttons hadn’t fallen off.

“The young gentleman I had the honour of being introjuiced to yesterday in the cathedral yard!” he boomed, doing an over-the-top dramatic bow. “Hope I see you well, sir! I spotted ye in the theatre last night during my daughter’s perfawrumance, and ye were gone by the time I walked her home — Jack Costigan’s poor, but he’s a gentleman, after all! I came back to the house to drink with our young friend Mr. Foker, and we had such a night of it, sir — him, three gallant dragoons, and your humble servant. Gad, it reminded me of the old days, when I was serving His Majesty in the Foighting Hundred and Third!” He pulled out a beat-up old snuff box and offered it to Pen with the utmost stately dignity.

Pen was so flustered he could barely speak. This shabby, ragged old buck was her father! The Captain reeked of last night’s cigars, and twisted the little tuft of hair on his chin as jauntily as any 20 year old dandy about town.

“I — I hope Miss Fotheringay, Miss Costigan is well, sir!” Pen blurted out, bright red in the face. “She — she gave me more pleasure than I’ve ever had at a play. I think, sir, I think she’s the finest actress in the whole world!”

“Shake my hand, young man! Ye speak from the heart!” Costigan yelled, clapping him on the shoulder. “Thank ye, sir, an old soldier and fond father thanks ye. She *is* the finest actress in the world. I’ve seen the great Siddons, I’ve seen O’Neill — they were grand, but what were they next to my daughter? I don’t let her use our real family name on the stage, ye see. The Costigans of Costiganstown are proud folk, and they’d say an old soldier who served the crown was disgracing himself letting his daughter earn his daily bread.”

“Surely there’s no more honourable work in the world,” Pen stammered.

“Honourable? Bedad, sir, I’d like to see the man who’d say Jack Costigan would do anything dishonourable! I’ve got a heart, sir, even if I’m poor. I can spot a man with a heart the second I see him, and you’ve got one — I read it in your honest face and your steady eye. And would ye believe it,” he leaned in to whisper, all tragic and mournful, “that that rogue Bingley, who’s made a fortune off my child, only pays her two guineas a week? She’s got to pay for all her own costumes out of that, and that, plus my own tiny little income, is all we’ve got to live on!”

Costigan’s income was so small it was basically non-existent, but nobody knows how shabby Irish gents manage to stay afloat, somehow, scraping together whisky and water every single day even when they haven’t got a penny to their name. If he’d ever written his life story it would’ve been a wildly entertaining moral fable, but he never would’ve told it even if he could, and he couldn’t have told it straight anyway — fact and fiction were all muddled together in his whisky-softened old brain. He’d started life pretty grand, with a commission, a handsome face, and one of the best singing voices in the whole army. Right up until the day he died he sang those wild, sad, hilarious Irish ballads that made everyone cry, even himself. Poor old Cos! Brave and soppy, funny and daft, endlessly good-natured, almost honest half the time. He’d drink with anyone, he’d back anyone’s bill, and he died in a spunging house where even the sheriff’s officer that locked him up was fond of him.

Within half an hour of chatting to Pen, Costigan had smoothly relieved him of his last two sovereigns, in exchange for tickets for his daughter’s upcoming benefit night. It wasn’t the dodgy old arrangement from the year before, when Miss Fotheringay had lost fifteen shillings on the whole show — this was a deal with the theatre manager where she got to sell a set number of tickets and keep most of the cash herself. Pen only had two pounds in his whole purse, but he handed it straight over. He was too scared to offer more, in case he offended the old captain’s delicate honour. Costigan scrawled him a ticket order for a box, slipped the two sovereigns into his waistcoat pocket, patted the spot with a big grin like the coins were warming his chest.

“Faith, sir, gold’s a damn sight scarcer for me these days than it used to be, same as many a good fellow,” he said. “I won six hundred of ’em in one single night, sir, when my great friend the Duke of Kent was stationed in Gibraltar!” And off he went, reeling off endless stories about the garrison, the races, the bets, the mountains of claret they all drank, keeping Pen completely entertained until Foker finally trundled in ready for breakfast.

Breakfast was a glorious spread of devilled turkey and mutton chops, and Costigan’s spirits rose higher and higher the more he ate. He chattered nonstop about his old army days, the lords and generals and lord lieutenants he’d rubbed shoulders with, the tragic death of his dear late wife Bessie, the duel he’d challenged a Captain Shanty Clancy to for looking rude at his daughter when she was out driving in Phoenix Park, and how Clancy apologized, bought them all a dinner at Kildare Street, and the six of them drank 21 bottles of claret between them. By the time he’d finished his second glass of curaçao, he was so happy and sentimental he started crying, saying he couldn’t think of a greater joy than hanging out with two such noble, generous young gentlemen. He wasn’t exactly the most upstanding role model for impressionable young lads, but there are far worse, richer, more dishonest men walking around in fine positions in life that never got up to half the harmless silly roguery old Cos did.

The three of them trundled out onto the street, Costigan linking an arm through each of theirs, grinning and winking at every shopkeeper they passed that he owed money to, as if to say “look at the fancy rich company I’m with, you’ll definitely get your money soon mate”. They parted ways with Foker outside a billiards hall, where he had a pre-planned game with some of Colonel Swallowtail’s regiment’s officers.

Pen and the shabby old captain wandered on together, Costigan slyly grilling him about how rich Foker was, what his family was like. Pen told him Foker’s dad was the famous brewer, and his mum was Lady Agnes Milton, daughter of Lord Rosherville. Costigan immediately burst into the most over-the-top stream of compliments about Foker, gushing about his “native aristocracy you can spot at a hundred paces, paired with a brilliant mind and a heart made of pure gold” — not a single word of which he actually believed.

Pen just wandered along, listening to him ramble, amused and confused and starry-eyed. He was far too earnest and honest a kid to ever assume anyone would lie to him, so he took every single one of Costigan’s tall tales at face value. Costigan had never had a more grateful, attentive listener, and he was chuffed to bits with the boy.

He liked Pen so much — he seemed so sweet, artless, cheerful — that he finally offered him an invitation he almost never gave to random young men: “Would ye do me the honour, sir, of popping back to my little humble lodgings just around the corner? I’d love to introjuice my young friend to my daughther, Miss Fotheringay.”

Pen was so shocked and over the moon he almost went limp on Costigan’s arm. He thought he was going to collapse right there on the pavement, and trembled so hard he was sure the old captain would notice. He stammered out a bunch of incoherent, flustered words saying he’d be more than delighted, he was already her biggest fan, and he followed Costigan up the street barely even noticing where he was going. He was going to see her! He was going to see her at home! She was the entire centre of the universe as far as he was concerned. The day before he met her felt like a lifetime ago. A whole new world was about to start.

Costigan led him down to quiet little Prior’s Lane in Chatteris, tucked in the church quarter right next to Dean’s Green and the canons’ houses, overshadowed by the huge looming cathedral towers. He lived on the first floor of a low old gabled house, with a brass plate on the door reading “Creed, Tailor and Robe-maker”. Old Mr. Creed was long dead, his widow worked as a pew-opener in the cathedral, their eldest son was a little scamp choirboy who spent all day playing toss-halfpenny and leading his little brothers into mischief, with a voice so sweet it could’ve been an angel’s. Two of the smaller Creed boys were sat on the front doorstep, and they jumped up the second they spotted the captain, lunging straight for the tails of his dress coat. Costigan was a soft touch, and whenever he had a bit of spare cash he’d bring them apples or gingerbread, which meant the widow Creed never chased him for rent when he was short. “That way, the widdy never presses me for the rent when

For weeks after Pen got bit by the love bug, Cicero and Euripides didn’t stand a chance. Poor Mr. Smirke, his tutor, had the easiest stretch of work he’d had in his entire curate career—half the lessons Pen just stared out the window, and the other half he’d already snuck out the door.
The real victim of Pen’s lovesick chaos was his mare Rebecca. Whenever she went lame, Pen threw a bigger tantrum than Richard III after his horse got killed under him at Bosworth. He pored over farrier manuals from the Chatteris livery stable until his eyes crossed, dropped so much cash on vet bills and rental horses he was on first-name terms with every stable boy within 10 miles, just so he could gallop over to Prior’s Lane at a moment’s notice.
Half his trips he made up a story to his mom about going to Chatteris for fencing lessons, the other half he lied through his teeth to Smirke, saying he wanted to read a Greek play together, then bolted to catch the Competitor down coach. He’d hang out for two hours with Emily Fotheringay, then hop the 10pm Rival coach back home so no one noticed he was gone. He almost blew his whole secret once, when Mrs. Pendennis asked Smirke if they’d had a productive study night the day before. Smirke’s mouth was already open to admit he hadn’t seen Pen at all, when Pen ground his boot heel so hard onto the poor curate’s toe under the table it made him yelp, and shut his mouth faster than a clam at low tide.
Naturally, two guys who get tangled up in the same love adjacent mess end up talking about it, and there’s no better sport than listening to two besotted fools ramble on about their crush (as long as you’re not the one doing the rambling). Smirke made Pen swear a solemn secret oath to tell him he wasn’t mixed up in some sketchy, improper entanglement—because if he was, the poor flustered curate was convinced he’d have to break his vow and tell Mrs. Pendennis, which would ruin the quiet, secretly pining little life he’d built for himself admiring Pen’s mom from afar.
“Unworthy? Unlawful?” Pen squawked, indignant. “She’s purer than she is beautiful, and I’d never look at another woman. The only reason I haven’t told my family yet is—well, there are heavy, secret reasons I can’t share. And any man who dares say a rude word against her? That’s an insult to me, and dammit, I won’t stand for it.”
Smirke gave a wobbly little laugh. “Whoa, easy, Arthur, don’t challenge me to a duel. You know I can’t fight, I’ve never even held a pistol that wasn’t used for firing off birthday toffees.” That compromise just left the poor curate even more wrapped around Pen’s finger, and the Greek verbs and math problems gathered more dust than ever.
If Smirke had bothered flipping through the Poet’s Corner of the weekly County Chronicle that arrived in Wednesday’s mail, he’d have noticed a gushy new poem popping up every week: lines to Miss Fotheringay of the Theatre Royal, all dramatic, passionate, signed not with Pen’s old “NEP” signature, but a mysterious “EROS”. Even Helen, who clipped every single one of Pen’s amateur verses out of the paper to save in a scrapbook, didn’t connect the two. She just cooed at Pen one day, “This Eros guy writes sort of like you, dear, but yours is far better. Have you met this Miss Fotheringay he’s fawning over?”
Pen said he had, that he’d seen her play Mrs. Haller in The Stranger a few nights prior, and hey, fun fact—she was doing Ophelia for her benefit show next week. Why not all go? It’s Shakespeare, after all! Little Laura jumped up and down so fast her ringlets bounced, begging to come see her first ever play. Pen made sure to drop the Shakespeare name extra heavily, because his late dad had spent his whole life ranting about how the Bard was better than every poet in Johnson’s collection put together, and had always sworn he’d take Helen and Pen to a proper Shakespeare play as soon as Pen was old enough.
Tears welled up in Helen’s eyes as she remembered her husband’s old speeches. She kissed Pen’s forehead and agreed to go. Then on a whim, she invited Smirke along too. The poor curate had been raised by strict Clapham parents who’d banned theatres entirely, so he’d never set foot in one in his life. But Shakespeare! And a whole night riding in Mrs. Pendennis’s carriage, sitting right next to her? He couldn’t say no. He stammered out a messy speech about “temptation and gratitude” while making such a moon-eyed face at Helen that she started feeling distinctly uncomfortable. The man was getting creepier by the day, and she was already half-regretting the invite.
We don’t need to drag you through every second of Pen’s courtship of Emily—you’ve already got a pretty good idea of how it went. He’d sit with her for hours, spilling every dumb 18-year-old thought out of his brain: every line of Byron he’d memorized, every half-baked poem he’d scribbled down, every grand plan he had for their future, rambling on for 20 minutes straight without stopping. Emily never had to do much work at all, except nod prettily, bat her eyes, and gasp “Oh, that’s so beautiful! Say those lines again!” while she thought about something way more practical: how to stretch her old black satin dress into a new gown, what they’d have for dinner that night (hashed mutton, probably), if she could turn her old scarf into a bonnet just like the fancy one Miss Thackthwaite down the street had bought.
It didn’t take long for Emily and her dad to catch onto how head over heels Pen was. The second time he left their house, Captain Costigan winked at his daughter over his glass of grog and cackled, “Faith, Milly darling, you’ve hooked that rich boy for good.”
“Pfft, he’s just a baby, papa,” Milly shrugged. “He’s barely even grown up.” Little did she know, Pen was galloping home on Rebecca at that exact moment, screaming her name at the top of his lungs and grinning so wide his face hurt, and he’d have been thrilled to hear she called him a “child”.
“Baby or not, he’s a great catch,” the Captain pushed on. “I asked the bartender at the George and the grocer where his mom shops—she’s loaded. Drives her own chariot, owns Fairoaks Park, he gets every bit of the property the second he turns 21. You could hunt far longer and not find a better fish than that, Miss Fotheringay.”
“Boys his age are all talk,” Milly said, serious. “Remember back in Dublin when you carried on about Lord Poldoody? I’ve got a whole desk full of verses he wrote me at Trinity, and then his mom shipped him abroad and married him off to some random English heiress.”
“Lord Poldoody was a nobleman, that’s how they are,” her dad huffed. “You’re way better set up now, Milly. But don’t lead the boy on too much, I won’t have anyone trifling with my daughter.”
“Neither will his daughter, you can be sure of that, Captain,” Milly said, pouring him another sip of punch. “I’m old enough to take care of myself, don’t you worry.”
So Pen kept coming back, day after day, rushing in the door all breathless, rushing out again, getting more obsessed every single visit. Half the time Captain Costigan would make a silly excuse to leave the two of them alone, cock his hat over one eye, and strut off to go hang out at the pub, leaving them in the low wainscoted drawing-room with the big window looking out onto the Dean’s garden. Pen would ramble, Emily would sit there looking stunningly pretty doing her needlework, the cathedral bell would boom every hour, the rooks would caw in the elms at sunset, and Pen never wanted to leave. For the record, every Sunday Emily and her dad turned up to church, the Captain bellowing the responses in his thick rich Irish brogue, behaving so perfectly the vicar would have never guessed what their day jobs were.
Little Bows, the crippled acting coach who’d spent three years drilling every single gesture, line, and perfectly timed sob into Emily after she’d flopped horribly in London years prior, was furious. He’d worked himself hoarse screaming at her to nail every part, had mapped out exactly which foot she should stand on, exactly when to roll her eyes, exactly which syllable to cry on to make the crowd weep, and now this teenage squire was wandering in thinking he was gonna marry his star pupil right before a London manager was supposed to discover her and make her a household name? It was a travesty. Emily wasn’t even particularly grateful for Bows’ work—she was just… stupid. But Pen? Pen was so madly in love he couldn’t see a single flaw in her.
The post horses from the Clavering Arms showed up right on time, and the whole crew piled in the carriage to the Chatteris theatre. The audience was packed. Pen spotted his friend Foker and his buddy Spavin in a fancy box, all dolled up in their fanciest evening wear, waving wildly. They approved of the group too: little Laura was round-cheeked and pretty with her pile of shining brown ringlets, Mrs. Pendennis looked absolutely regal in her black velvet gown and the diamond cross she only wore on special occasions, and Smirke sat behind them, his white tie perfectly tied, blushing bright red because he’d never been in a theatre before.
