The coachman Samuel was posted in the pit, alongside a bunch of dragoon non-commissioned officers whose band was playing in the orchestra, and their colonel, the portly war hero Swallowtail with his Waterloo medal, was sat in a box with his young officers.
“Who’s that odd man in the white gloves bowing at you, Arthur?” Helen asked. Pen blushed bright red. “That’s Captain Costigan, ma’am. A Peninsular war officer.” He didn’t elaborate further, and Helen had no reason to guess the man was Miss Fotheringay’s father.
The night’s Hamlet was a visiting London actor named Mr. Hornbull, and the theatre manager Mr. Bingley was playing Horatio, saving his best performance for the second play of the night, *Black-Eyed Susan*.
The Hamlet performance was fine, but Ophelia? She was stunning. Her dark hair tumbled over her shoulders as she raved through her mad scene, waving her gorgeous white arms, flinging her flowers around, making the whole crowd weep. She was the prettiest corpse the stage had ever seen. When Hamlet and Laertes were fighting in her grave, she was peeking out from backstage, curious to get a look at Pen’s little group in their box.
Everyone was obsessed with her. Helen gushed nonstop about how beautiful she was, Laura cried so hard at the tragic ending her nose ran, Smirke cheered so loud his voice went hoarse. Pen was over the moon his mom liked her so much, it was almost perfect. The whole house roared with applause when the curtain fell, the dragoons were stamping their feet, Colonel Swallowtail waved his cap over his head, and Pen was yelling “Fotheringay! Fotheringay!” louder than anyone else. Even Helen was waving her handkerchief, little Laura was bouncing up and down clapping.
When Emily was led out on stage to take her bows, Pen spotted the little locket he’d bought for her with his last shilling plus a pound he’d borrowed from Smirke glinting on her chest, and he nearly blurted out the whole secret to Helen right then and there. Foker tossed a giant bouquet of roses up at her, Smirke tossed a shy little rose that landed in the pit, and Emily went through her whole practiced routine: glancing gratefully at the whole crowd, pretending to tremble with emotion, pressing the flowers to her chest like she was about to faint.
Then *Black-Eyed Susan* started, and Emily looked just as lovely in her simple russet gown and pink ribbon, making the whole crowd sigh over her sweet, sad story. No one wanted the night to end.
Pen would have spilled his whole secret to his mom on the ride home if they’d been alone in the carriage, but he ended up sitting up on the box next to Smirke, smoking a cigar while Smirke wrapped his comforter tight around himself against the cool night air. Halfway home, Foker’s fast tandem carriage zoomed past them, and Spavin blasted a very wobbly rendition of *Rule Britannia* on his key bugle, making Laura cackle.
Two days later, the Dean of Chatteris was hosting a dinner for his clerical friends. They drank excellent port, gossiped about the bishop over dessert, and Doctor Portman, Pen’s old mentor, snuck out of the dining room doors to go chat with Mrs. Dean, who was walking on the lawn with her kids, pink parasol over her head. The sun had just sunk behind the giant cathedral towers, and the little crescent moon was glowing bright in the golden summer sky.
At the end of the Dean’s lawn, right next to the old open window of the first-floor room in the house that backed onto the garden, were two people: a 26-year-old actress with her eyes wide open, and an 18-year-old boy so blind in love he couldn’t see straight. Poor Pen had just done it—he’d trembled, cried a little, and poured his whole heart out on the floor at Emily’s feet, proposing to her, kissing her hand over and over like his life depended on it.
“Does your mother know about this, Arthur?” Emily said slow, not pulling her hand away. She was already thinking about that pretty diamond cross Helen had worn to the play, and the 200 acres of Fairoaks property, and figured this arrangement could work out very nicely for her.
Pen babbled that his mom had already seen her and loved her, that of course she’d be happy they were getting married, that no one on earth could not adore her. Emily patted his messy hair, gave him a slow pitying little smile, and let him keep kissing her hand.
Down on the lawn, little Master Ridley Roset, the Dean’s 7-year-old son, tugged at the back of his mom’s dress and pointed up. “Ma! Look up there!”
What a sight it was. There was Pen, all dopey and lovesick, kissing Emily’s hand like it was made of solid gold, and Emily sitting there completely calm, perfectly composed. Mrs. Dean’s jaw dropped, little Rosa her younger daughter gaped, and Doctor Portman stared up at the window, frozen, half furious half bewildered, because he’d just caught his prize pupil, the boy he’d spent years mentoring, head over heels tangled up with the local theatre actress, right over the Dean’s rose bushes.

Our old pal Major Arthur Pendennis trundled up to Fairoaks right on schedule, fresh off the worst 24 hours of his gout-prone life, courtesy of the Victorian mail coach. Some great oaf of a passenger, swaddled in so many overcoats he looked like a walking down duvet, had wedged him into the corner so tight his ankle ached for hours, and snored so loud it could have roused a whole parish of dead churchgoers. Across from him, a widow lady had sealed every window shut so tight no fresh air could sneak in, and was steadily guzzling Jamaica rum punch out of a secret bottle stashed in her reticule, until the whole carriage reeked of spiced booze. Any time the Major even started to doze, he’d get jolted awake by the turnpike horn blaring right outside the window, his giant neighbor shoving him even further into the woodwork, or the widow digging her pointy boot heels into his already sore toes. Ah, the good old days of 19th century travel, huh? Eight whole miles an hour for 20+ hours, hard seats, grumbling coachmen mad you didn’t tip them enough, passengers who brought their own private rum supplies—who among us hasn’t suffered through that grand old adventure? And somehow people still managed to show up smiling back then, go figure.

Up on the roof next to the Major’s valet sat two schoolboys, bound home for midsummer holidays. The Major nearly choked on his sad watery tea at the Bagshot inn stop when he watched those two tuck into a mountain of ham, eggs, pickled pie, boiled beef, tea, and coffee that could have fed a whole cricket team, while he moped thinking Lord Steyne was tucking into a seven-course fancy dinner back in London right that very minute. The lads’ unhinged vacation appetite was charming enough, though, and the Major perked right up when he learned one of the boys riding inside with him was a lord’s son—someone he’d naturally rubbed shoulders with in fancy London society circles. That little lord slept through the entire night’s squeezing, horn blaring, and rum fumes, and hopped off at the end of the journey looking fresh as a daisy, while the Major stumbled down at Fairoaks lodge gate yellow-faced, beard bristly, his wig totally out of curl, and rheumatism shooting through every limb like tiny little angry firemen. The lodge keeper and gardener’s wife bowed so deep their bonnets nearly fell off, and his valet Morgan greeted him with the reverence due a man who’d survived 24 hours of mail coach hell.

Helen Pendennis had been watching for him from the window, but she didn’t rush out to greet him. She knew the Major would never let a soul see him looking that rough before he’d done his full beauty routine—years ago, a 10-year-old Pen had gotten in so much trouble for nicking a little morocco box off the Major’s dressing table, which turned out to hold his set of false back teeth, something the Major obviously refused to wear while jolting around in a mail coach, and wouldn’t be seen in public without. Morgan treated his master’s wigs like top-secret state documents, curling them behind closed doors and smuggling them into his room like he was passing off a crown jewel. So the Major trundled straight to his bedroom, groaned, wheezed, cursed Morgan through every step of the toilet routine until his teeth were in, his wig was properly curled, his cravat was tied just right, and he descended the staircase to the drawing room looking every inch the dignified, distinguished man of fashion he was determined to be.

Pen wasn’t in the room, just Helen and little Laura, who was sewing at her feet. The Major, who never had much use for Laura anyway (he had his secret reasons, we’ll get to those later at some point), didn’t even bother to shake her hand—just stuck out a single stiff forefinger for her to touch. Laura took the wobbly offered finger, dropped it immediately, and bolted out of the room as fast as her little legs could carry her.

She found Pen out in the orchard, pacing up and down a path deep in a very heated conversation with his tutor Mr. Smirke. Pen was so wrapped up in talking he didn’t hear Laura yelling his name until Smirke tugged on his coat sleeve and pointed at her running over. She grabbed his hand and said, “C’mon Pen! Uncle Arthur’s here!”

“Is he, now?” Pen said, and Laura felt him squeeze her hand tight. He glared at Smirke like he was ready to take on his uncle, or any man who dared get between him and what he wanted. Smirke, as per usual, rolled his eyes heavenward and sighed softly. “Lead on, Laura,” Pen said, half fierce, half trying not to look nervous. “I’ll go pay my respects to my uncle.” He was laughing to cover up how anxious he was, though—he knew this was the big showdown, the moment he had to defend his grand great love.

See, Pen had spilled every single detail about Miss Emily Fotheringay, the actress, to Smirke over the last 48 hours, nonstop. Smirke, who was a little bit in love himself with the idea of grand romance and had never even been to a play before, ate every word up, and was half convinced that Pen’s plan to marry the 30-something actress at 18 was totally sensible. They could stick her drunk old dad Captain Costigan in the spare yellow bedroom forever, right? How hard could that be? Even better, Pen had nearly talked his own soft mom Helen into going along with it too! He’d parried every one of her objections with that ridiculous indignant “I know better than you” logic only a teen boy can pull off, and Helen was halfway to believing that if the stars were aligned for the match, why fight it? All she cared about was that her boy was happy, and she was dreading the Major turning up and blowing the whole thing up, for all the usual reasons two people look at marriage from totally different points of view: the London fancy set thinks you need a Belgravia house, a tiny dinner party spread, a brougham to drive in Hyde Park, and money stashed away for all the future kids before you even think about marrying, while soft country Helen thought two people in love could make it work on nothing but hope and hard work.

Helen, the soft romantic, had spent years daydreaming about Pen bringing a wife home, and giving up the best bedroom and the head of the table so he could be happy—she thought any woman alive would be lucky enough to marry her perfect boy, even if she was a nobody. But an actress? A woman who’d spent years performing on stage under the gaze of hundreds of strangers, who probably hung around with all kinds of loud, ill-bred theatre folk? That was a hard pill to swallow, but Pen had talked her around so much that she was ready to do almost anything to keep him from being sad. He’d spent one whole evening at Chatteris bragging to Emily about how much influence he had over his mom, and the other night scribbling the most dramatic, over-the-top poetry ever written for her, vowing like the great Montrose that he’d make her famous with his sword and his pen, that no man had ever loved a woman half as much as he loved her.

That midnight, Helen had crept past his bedroom door, seen a light under the crack, and heard him tossing around muttering verses. She snuck in to check on him, like she’d done a hundred times when he was a sick little kid, and found him surrounded by scattered paper, chewing on a pencil, full of Byron, Moore, and every over-the-top romantic poem he’d ever read, fully in the throes of teen dramatic frenzy. He nearly jumped out of his skin when she spoke, hid all his poems under his pillow, and then burst into tears hugging her neck, yelling that he loved Emily more than anything in the world. Of course she coddled him, what else could a soft mom do? Emily didn’t even understand the half of his fancy poetry, but her dad Costigan winked and told her to tuck all the letters away, said Pen’s “pomes” were better than anything ever written by that old fool Poldoody.

So when the Major finally made his grand appearance in the drawing room, Helen grabbed his sleeve immediately, begging him to be gentle with Pen. “He’s got a very high spirit, he won’t stand unkind words!” she said. “Dr. Portman yelled at him the other night, and Pen got so mad it frightened me! He thinks he’s a grown man now, please be careful.”

The Major gallantly kissed her hand, but inside he was rolling his eyes so hard he almost saw his own brain. Good grief, the boy had managed to wrap his own mother right around his little finger! She’d let him have a pet elephant if he cried loud enough for it. “Don’t you worry, my dear,” he said out loud, “I’ll be perfectly gentle. But that boy is not marrying a strolling stage actress. No Pendennis head of the house is going to run off and marry someone from a travelling fair booth, ma’am.”

Helen panicked, said if he broke the news too suddenly Pen would be heartbroken, maybe do something stupid. The Major waved her off, launched into his own tragic life story about how when he was a young penniless lieutenant he’d been in love with a girl with 15 thousand pounds, whose parents made them break up, he’d gone off to India, and they’d both gotten over it just fine. She was now a baronet’s wife with 13 grown kids! He rambled on and on about all the high society horror stories he’d seen: Lord Brouncker got cut by everyone for marrying a druggist’s daughter, Lady Snapperton refused to invite her to a fancy ball, Lord Clodworthy’s actress wife looked old enough to be his mother, Lord Willowbank’s wife kept trying to hang herself out of jealousy of a dancer. Why on earth would you suffer that lifelong embarrassment when you could just break a silly little teen romance off quick?

Helen sighed, agreeing that young unequal marriages were the worst thing in the world, and kept alluding again and again to that sad old family tragedy of Laura’s dad that she couldn’t stop thinking about. Since we’ve brought Laura up so many times, and you’re all probably dying to know who she actually is, we’ll clear that all up nice and easy in the next chapter.

Once upon a very long vacation, a strapping young Cambridge tutor named the Reverend Francis Bell rolled up to his aunt’s tiny village cottage, where his cousin Helen Thistlewood (whose dad had copped it at Copenhagen, poor lieutenant) lived on a pension so small you could tuck it behind a teacup. Bell was a hotshot college fellow, famous for churning out prize-winning students faster than the village dairy churned butter, and he’d brought three or four pupils along to camp out in the spare rooms for the summer.

Everybody figured out within 48 hours that the reverend lad was thoroughly trapped in an engagement he’d stumbled into as a gawky 17-year-old. Picture it: he was helping Miss Martha Coacher, daughter of his old childhood tutor, pick peas one sunny afternoon, got so flustered he dropped his basket, and flopped straight onto his knees in the dirt to swear eternal undying devotion. Martha was 12 years his senior, and her love life up to that point read like a list of every bad 18th century romance trope: three of her dad’s pupils had trifled with her affections and vanished, the local apothecary had dumped her via a note left on his shop counter, and a dragoon officer she’d danced with every night at Bath had ridden off one morning mid-flirt, never to be seen again. No wonder she jumped at the chance to lock down a earnest, clever boy who actually seemed to mean what he said.

She sent him back a lock of her auburn hair tied with blue ribbon as her answer – never mind she’d snipped off three identical locks for the three prior faithless lovers, the hair grew back, after all. Young Bell was so over the moon he decided he’d grind his way to top Wrangler, secure a fat college living, and make her his wife no matter what. Term after term he brought home honours, sent her prize books and silver declamation trophies, corresponded constantly, and truly believed their love was the stuff of legend.

By the time he turned 26, though? The pea garden magic had well and truly worn off. Martha was 34, sharp-tempered, coarse-mannered, and forever picking fights. Bell realized he was bound by hundreds of gushy old love letters to a woman he couldn’t stand the sight of. So rather than spend the summer at her father’s parsonage, he legged it down to his aunt’s village, where he hadn’t set foot since little Helen was small enough to sit on his knee.

Helen was now a 20-something beauty, and for four months straight they walked at sunset, read from the same book while old Mrs. Thistlewood dozed by the candles, and slowly fell head over heels for each other. She knew all about his messy engagement, even saw the hard, snappish letters Martha sent him. But both of them were far too noble for their own good. They decided duty came first: Bell would honour his silly teenage promise, they’d go their separate ways, and they’d both be miserable for the rest of their lives. That’s 19th century romance for you.

The living fell in far too soon. Bell was already grey and worn out by the time he got installed as parson. Helen sent him a polite “My dear Cousin” note for his wedding, returned all his old letters and the tiny lock of his hair she’d kept tucked in her desk, and married Arthur Pendennis senior soon after. Then Bell finagled a faraway chaplainship on the godforsaken Coventry Island, dragged Martha out there with him, and she died of tropical fever a few months later, still cackling about how she’d stopped him from running off with Helen. Bell married a nice colonial lady for a bit, but she died in childbirth too, and the poor man gave up on joy entirely. He shipped his tiny newborn daughter back to Helen, with a note begging her to raise the girl for him, then kicked the bucket himself not long after.

Little Helen Laura showed up at Fairoaks dressed in black, clinging to her nurse’s hand and sobbing her eyes out, and Helen Pendennis took her in instantly as a piece of the old love she’d never forgotten. Her new husband John Pendennis, though, was a moody old stick-in-the-mud who hated the very sight of her – she reminded him of all that old romantic history he’d rather pretend never happened – so he insisted they skip the “Helen” name and just call her Laura. Only once he was in the ground did Helen dare shower the little girl with all the affection she’d been holding back. That’s the origin story of your little Laura: two hundred pounds in the bank, no fancy noble relatives, and a whole heap of quiet softness that neither grumpy John Pendennis nor his snobby brother the Major thought was worth a tuppence.

Alright, enough of that. Let’s let Pen back in, the poor lad who’s been stood hovering outside the door rehearsing his dramatic duel speech for ten whole minutes.

He’d puffed out his chest, practiced his most stern, battle-ready scowl, and convinced himself he was about to have a screaming knock-down drag-out fight with his terrifying uncle, defending his great romantic love like a knight of old. He flung the door open so hard it banged against the wall, marched in with his “lance couched and plumes flying” expression, ready to snap “come on then, I’m ready.”

Major Pendennis, that old smooth operator, surveyed the boy’s ridiculous pompous posturing and almost snort-laughed out loud. He’d already sussed out that Helen was half ready to give Pen everything he wanted, so yelling and grand speeches would do no good at all. So he dropped all his stern guardian act, beamed the friendliest smile you ever saw, held out both his hands, shook Pen’s limp, confused hand gaily, and said “Well Pen my boy, tell us all about it.”

Poor Pen was gutted. He’d geared up for full Greek tragedy, and the script had gotten swapped for a friendly tea party. His big grand entrance was completely wasted. He blushed bright red, fumbled for words, and nearly burst into tears. “I – I didn’t know you’d arrived till just now,” he stammered. “Is – is London very busy, then?”

The Major had to turn away to hide his cackle, darting a comical little glance at Helen, who was equally half-amused half-melted at the sight of her son flustered. She stepped forward and kissed his forehead, and Pen’s last scrap of tragic hero dignity dissolved entirely.

“What a pair of soft silly things they are,” the Major thought to himself. “If I’d waited one more day, Helen would have hitched up the carriage to go and beg blessing on this nonsense marriage herself.”

“Come on, no need for all the over-sentimental stuff,” he said, still grinning. “Pen, old chap, spill the whole story.”

Pen immediately snapped back into his tragic heroic pose. “The story is sir, that I’ve met the most beautiful, virtuous woman of noble birth who’s fallen on hard times. My whole life’s happiness is tied up in her. I know there’s a gap in our ages and all sorts of obstacles, but I’m ready to surmount them all. She’s agreed to marry me, to share my life and my fortune.”

“Eh, how much fortune is that exactly, lad?” the Major said, raising an eyebrow. “Last I checked, you don’t own a single penny in your own right.”

“Every penny he has is mine, and I’d give it all to him,” Helen burst out immediately.

The Major bit back a very sharp “for heaven’s woman, shut up!” and kept his temper. “Right, right, of course you would. Everyone knows you’d do anything for him. But at the end of the day, that fortune you’re offering the young lady is your mother’s, Pen, and you’re only 18. Why on earth didn’t you have the decency to consult the lady who runs the house, pays the bills, and feeds you three meals a day before you went and pledged all her assets to a stranger? I’m not yelling at you, mind – the law and your dad’s will give me full authority over you for another three years, but I’m talking man to man here. Isn’t it a bit selfish to make all these huge decisions for the woman who’d give you the shirt off her back, without even asking her opinion?”

Pen’s head dropped. All that supposed grand, selfless romantic gesture he’d been so proud of suddenly looked an awful lot like a selfish, headstrong bit of silliness. “I did it in a fit of passion,” he mumbled. “But I said it now, and I stand by it. I won’t go back on my word, not for anything. I’ll die first. I’ll work for her! I’ll go on the stage and act with her, she says I’d be great at it.”

“Fair enough,” the Major said, utterly unflappable. “But no gentleman breaks his word when he’s given it freely. We’re not asking you to jilt her. But you owe your mother a little courtesy, don’t you? Your family, me as your father’s representative?”

“Oh of course,” Pen said, already half-relieved.

“Great. Then give us your word on one other thing, Arthur. No sneaking off for a private marriage. No trips to Gretna Green in the dead of night, you understand?”

“Arthur would never lie to his mother!” Helen said, shocked.

Pen’s face burned with shame. He’d already been sneaking around behind his mother’s back this whole time, hadn’t he? He held his hand out to his uncle. “On my word of honour as a gentleman, sir, I will never marry without my mother’s full consent.” He gave Helen a bright, loving smile, and trotted off to his study.

“He’s an absolute angel!” Helen gushed.

“Good stock, that boy, on both sides,” the Major said, very pleased with how easily his little trick had worked. He even bowed over the tip of her glove, and slipped into that posh drawl he only used when he was feeling extra clever with himself. “My dear, I’m very glad I came down. That last little promise I got him? Learned that trick from Lady Ferrybridge, three years back. Her son had been secretly married to a Scotch clergyman’s daughter for three whole months before Lord Ferrybridge found out, all because he tried to yell and threaten the boy instead of getting his word of honour. That’s why I wasn’t taking any chances.”

“Arthur would never do that,” Helen said, completely unshaken.

The Major didn’t press the boy any harder that day. He knew time was on his side, and Pen’s over-inflated romantic honour was the perfect lever to work him with. Over dinner, when they were sipping the good claret, he chatted away casually, pointing out how Pen’s marriage would shut every fancy possible career path he could have walked right into: no diplomatic postings to Pumpernickel, no army commission to follow their ancestor who fought at Ramillies, no entry into good society, no bright future at the bar outside the most low-rent circles. “You’ll be stuck down here in Fairoaks forever, my boy,” he said, sighing fondly at the thought of his beloved Pall Mall clubs. “No county gentry will call on you, and even I’ll be too embarrassed to come visit. You’ll spend your whole life drinking rum punch with grumpy farmer gents, dragging your poor mother down into your new wife’s low social circle. It’s no skin off my nose, of course, but it’s a crying shame for you and her. But of course, you’re a man of honour, you’ve given your word, you’ll do the right thing by her. Just promise us there’ll be no sneaking around, you’ll keep up your studies, and you’ll write to her openly, with your mother’s full knowledge, alright? You do write to her a fair bit, I suppose?”

Pen blushed bright crimson, and stammered out that yes, he had written quite a lot.

Let anyone who’s ever had the unadulterated joy of rubbing shoulders with London’s fanciest, most exclusive drawing rooms admit it: Major Pendennis pulled off a sacrifice so grand, it practically ought to have a marble plaque in his club’s smoking room. He abandoned London in *May*, for heaven’s sake—his morning papers, his afternoons of drifting between club couches and sipping sherry with widowed ladies who thought he was the wittiest man alive, his trots around Rotten Row, his opera stall where every lord and duke nodded at him so reliably his name was guaranteed a line in the *Morning Post* the next day, those secret little 8-person dinner parties that were too select for even the Post to hear about. All of it, he tossed aside to lock himself up in a draughty little country house with a soft-hearted widow, a starry-eyed teenage son, a whiny curate, and a 10-year-old girl who still picked daisies in the front garden.

Nobody but the Major realized how monumental this self-denial was, of course. He’d get his pre-franked letters from town, heave a world-weary sigh, and show Helen the piles of gilded party invitations like a martyr displaying the nails from his cross. Helen just smiled politely, not getting the whole overblown pathos of the situation, but young Pen gaped at the fancy peerage names scrawled on the envelopes and hung on every one of his uncle’s gossipy stories about high society like they were holy writ.

The Major’s memory was a walking archive of aristocratic tea and scandal, and he poured it all into Pen’s eager ears nonstop. “My dear boy,” he’d say, deadly serious, “you can’t start studying Debrett soon enough. Don’t bother much with the fake historical bits, half those families made up their royal descents last Tuesday—our family’s the only one with a perfectly clean, unbroken line, mark my words. Study the *alliances*. Who’s married to whose cousin, who hates whose son-in-law. I’ve seen a man’s whole career get blown up because he was too thick to learn this one simple rule. Just last month, at Lord Hobanob’s dinner, that new scribbler Mr. Suckling decided to crack a whole set of jokes about Admiral Bowser switching political sides. Turns out the lady right next to him was Bowser’s daughter, and the man across the table was Bowser’s son-in-law. By the end of his bit the whole room was so quiet you could hear a crumb fall off the tablecloth, and Suckling was still snickering like he’d told the funniest joke ever. He will never darken a South Street doorway again. I promise you that.”

He rambled on like this for hours on their two-hour daily terrace walks, or after dinner over their port, mourning that Sir Francis Clavering still hadn’t moved into the local park to give the gentry someone to socialize with, wailing that Lord Eyrie was out of town so he couldn’t drag Pen over to introduce him to the nobleman’s two pretty daughters. “Who knows? You could’ve married Lady Emily or Lady Barbara Trehawk, had a title and a fortune to boot. But those dreams are gone, my lad. You’ve made your bed, now you’ve got to lie in it.”

Pen ate it all up. It was far more thrilling than any schoolbook he’d ever read, hearing stories about the Prince Regent, famous beauties, and all the behind-the-scenes silly business of the rich and powerful, even if it bored his mother Helen half to death. The Major’s morality wasn’t exactly built to get anyone a spot in heaven, but it was perfectly calibrated to make sure you never got snubbed at a party, never got stuck with the worst table at a restaurant, and never missed an invitation to the year’s most exclusive picnic. He fully believed his way was the only *correct* way, and he was determined to pry his naive, lovestruck nephew’s eyes wide open to the real world.

For instance, no man in the whole parish went to church more regularly than the old bachelor. “Doesn’t matter so much in town, Pen,” he’d say, “the men there all skip and no one notices. But down on your own land, a gentleman’s got to set an example. If I could carry a tune I’d even sing out of the family pew—Duke of Saint David’s does it every Sunday, and the villagers stare at him like he’s a saint come down from heaven. As long as the Claverings are away, you’re the most important man in the parish. You could run for Parliament one day, if you play your cards right. Your dad would’ve done it, if he’d lived. You can too—*if* you don’t go and marry some girl the whole neighborhood refuses to call on, no matter how nice she is. Well, well, painful subject. Let’s change it.” He’d change it, then bring it back up again 17 times before dinner was over, the moral of every story being: Pen was throwing his whole life down the drain over this actress.

It didn’t take much to make a soft teenaged boy think he was a very important young man, so Pen lapped up all his uncle’s advice. Suddenly Captain Costigan’s sloshed, punch-reeking company wasn’t fun anymore. He couldn’t imagine dragging that unshaven, perpetually half-drunk old man home to meet his mother, and every time the Major prodded him about his beloved Miss Fotheringay he stammered. “Is she accomplished?” No, not really. “Clever?” Well… average, definitely not clever. “Show me her letters.” Pen had to admit he only had three silly little notes from her, all just inviting him over for tea.

“Cautious little thing,” the Major said dryly. “She’s older than you, my poor boy.” Then he immediately apologized, all soft and humble, said he was just a silly old uncle who only cared about the family’s honor, because Pen would blow up instantly if anyone so much as breathed a rude word about Emily, swearing he’d never part with her, never let anyone talk about her lightly.

He swore the same thing over at Chatteris, too, in front of Emily, her friend the actress Miss Rouncy, and Costigan himself. The Captain pointed to the old fencing foils hung up on his wall, grumbled that he wouldn’t advise any man to go messing with his daughter’s affections, then hugged Pen so tight he could smell the whisky on his breath, wiped a fake tear out of his eye with a dirty paw, and called him his own dear son. Pen shuddered in his grasp, thinking of his spotless, respectable mother back home. He’d even written Costigan a letter recently, slipped a sovereign inside because the old boy was broke, and signed it “your affectionate son Arthur Pendennis.” He could not get out of Chatteris fast enough that day, riding his horse Rebecca back home, grumbling to himself that Emily had no idea what huge sacrifices he was making for her, the poor girl.

What Pen didn’t know was that the Major had a whole surveillance operation running. No matter how sneaky he thought he was being, sneaking over to Chatteris to see Emily, the Major found out every time without fail. He never stopped Pen, never forbade him from going, but the constant awareness that his uncle knew exactly where he’d been made Pen far less eager to go gallivanting off than he used to. Once he snuck half a mile down to the Fairoaks Inn to catch the coach to Chatteris, only to find his uncle’s valet Mr. Morgan perched on the coach roof, apparently running a message for the Major. Morgan rode all the way to Chatteris and all the way back with him, not saying a single word. Pen had full run of the house, every bit of “decent liberty” he could ask for, but he always felt like someone was watching him, even when he was sitting at Emily’s feet.

Morgan, it turned out, was a master spy. He’d made multiple trips to Chatteris already, interviewing every waiter, ostler, and barmaid at The George inn, and found out no one there had ever seen Costigan pay for a single drink. He’d gotten himself chummy with the barracks’ servants, who told him the Captain had been banned from the officers’ mess for getting too drunk and trying to duel a 19-year-old cornet over a bad hand of cards. He sat backstage with the Chatteris theatre’s supporting actors, buying them cigars and punch, and every last one of them agreed Costigan was broke, drunk, and perpetually in debt, but no one had a single bad word to say about Emily. She never went anywhere without her father, even when he was half blackout, he’d still stand guard outside her dressing room. Morgan even went to see her perform, and admitted privately to the Major she was a spectacularly pretty woman.

Doctor Portman even grilled the pew-opener Mrs. Creed after church one Sunday, threatening her with all the wrath of the church if she didn’t tell him everything. Mrs. Creed said Emily never let Pen be alone with her for even a minute—she always made Mrs. Creed, or one of her sons, or Emily’s old piano teacher, hang around the whole time. Pen would even turn up during Sunday church services, hoping everyone else was out, but Emily still dragged a chaperone out of the kitchen to sit with them.

The Major and Doctor Portman groaned together when they heard the news. They couldn’t dig up a single speck of dirt on the girl’s reputation! The Major sighed, “If this was just a casual fling, we could let it slide. Boys have to sow their wild oats, after all. But a *virtuous* attachment? That’s the worst possible thing. All that daft romantic nonsense they pick up being raised by women.”

“Now Major, you’re being far too cynical,” said the Doctor. “A virtuous attachment to a girl of his own rank and fortune would be splendid. But this infatuation? I’d tell him straight to break it off.”
“That’s exactly how you’d make him elope with her the very next day,” the Major said. “We just need to bide our time, that’s all.”
“Wait a second,” Portman said, halfway out the door. “I don’t go to the theatre normally, obviously—but why don’t we just go see her perform ourselves?”

The Major cackled. He’d been at Fairoaks two whole weeks, and he’d never thought of that. So they arranged for Pen to have a quiet dinner with his mother that night, then hopped in Portman’s chaise and drove over to Chatteris, had a very jolly bachelor dinner at The George, and trundled off to the theatre to watch Hamlet. It was the exact same Ophelia performance they’d heard about a hundred times—Emily didn’t change a single gesture, line delivery, or curtsy from one night to the next. The Major lifted his fancy opera glass, stared at her for a long minute, and muttered under his breath, “Hang it all, the little rascal didn’t even pick an ugly one.”
Portman applauded loudly. “She’s a very fine actress, Major, and undeniably very pretty.”
“Notice that young dragoon in the stage box?” the Major said, pointing to the man they’d shared the dinner room with earlier. He was clapping so hard his gloves were splitting, and Emily gave him a soft little simper in return. The Major snapped his opera glass shut, stuffed it in his pocket, and didn’t watch another minute of the show. They left before the silly afterpiece started, Portman heading back to his wife at the Deanery, and the Major wandering back to his reserved room at The George, his head already full of a dozen new scheming plots.

Major Pendennis, that seasoned old social warhorse who’d spent half his life honing a schmooze so smooth it could slip past a duchess’s chaperone without a second glance, ambled back to the George Inn at a very leisurely pace, fully prepared to toddle up to his room, blow out his candle, and put off dealing with his idiot lovesick nephew for at least another eight hours. Fate, however, had other (far more entertaining) plans.

His valet Morgan, the man who knew every secret in three counties before the gossips had even finished their first breakfast tea, was loitering by the front door like he’d been waiting to pounce. He intercepted his master mid-trudge for the stairs, bobbing his head with that infuriatingly smug air of someone holding top-tier blackmail-adjacent gossip. “Beg pardon, sir—you might want to pop into the coffee-room first. There’s a young gent in there I reckon you’d be over the moon to chat to.”

“Arthur? Did the little fool drag himself back to town at last?” the Major squawked, immediately tensing up like he was about to have to bail the boy out of some new mess.

“Not him, sir—Mr. Harry Foker, that’s. Lady Agnes Foker’s son. He ate such a massive dinner he conked out on the bench right after, only just woke up and rang for his coffee. Thought you two might hit it off,” Morgan said, yanking the coffee-room door open before the Major could protest.

The room was all but empty, save for Foker himself, rubbing bleary eyes like he’d just woken from a three-day bender, his boots propped up on the table next to a graveyard of empty decanters and half-eaten dessert leftovers. He’d meant to head to the theatre, see a play, maybe make eyes at the chorus girls, but a second helping of plum pudding and a couple bottles of claret had knocked him flat before he could even find his hat.

The Major was just mentally drafting his most charming opening line when Foker beat him to it, holding up the evening paper with the easy friendliness of a bloke who’d never met a stranger in his life. “Wanna borrow the Globe, sir?”

“Obliged to you, my boy,” the Major beamed, bowing so deep his old military coat flapped. “If my eyes don’t deceive me, you’re Harry Foker, Lady Agnes Foker’s son! I’ve had the pleasure of dining at your grandfather’s table many a time—you’ve got that classic Rosherville face, no mistaking it.”

Foker nearly blurted out that he’d pegged the Major for a traveling haberdashery salesman, but bit the comment back at the last second. “Oh, right! Pleasure to meet you, sir—who might I be addressing, exactly?”

“Arthur Pendennis’s uncle and guardian, that’s who. The boy’s talked about you so often I feel like we’re old friends already. Mind if I park my soda water at your table?”

“Sir, you do me proud!” Foker said, shifting all his half-eaten cake scraps to make room. “Arthur’s the best sort of bloke alive, if you ask me. Way cleverer than I am— I was always the dunce at school, could barely get through my Latin verses without crying. But I know a good chap when I see one, y’know?”

“Flattery and modesty both, that’s what that is!” the Major chortled, settling in. “Arthur’s told me all about what a sharp fellow you are, actually.”

“Pfft, not with books,” Foker said, wagging his head. “Couldn’t stick ’em for nuts. Arthur? He’d write half the whole school’s verses for ’em for a shilling and a glass of beer. But—look, sir, you’re his guardian, right? Hope you don’t mind me saying, but he’s a total flat when it comes to women. Proper daft.”

The Major’s ears pricked right up, like a hound that’s just spotted a fox. “A flat? How do you mean?”

Foker winked at him so boldly he’d have winked straight at the Duke of Wellington himself, no remorse required—two bottles of wine had left him with zero filters and zero fear of anyone’s rank. “Oh, c’mon, you know. He’s gaga over that actress, Miss Fotheringay. Real name’s Costigan, her dad’s that old rip they call the General, who lives on whisky and water and IOUs. We went to the theatre together a while back, and he fell for her so hard he face-planted right into her lap, practically. Proposed to her, even! Can you believe that?”

The Major nearly spilled his soda water all over his waistcoat. “He did what?”

“Proposed! We were all ribbing Sir Derby Oaks about it at mess yesterday, the other bloke who’s hanging around her, and he got so mad he almost challenged Pen to a duel. D’you know Oaks? You walked right past us smoking at the inn door the other night, before you went in to dinner. His old dad was a doctor I remember, back before he got made a baronet.”

“Wait a second—Oaks is another admirer?” the Major leaned forward, suddenly giddy.

“Mate, they’ve got a literal schedule! Sir Derby gets Mondays, Wednesdays, Fridays. Pen gets Tuesdays, Thursdays, Saturdays. Miss Fotheringay’s not daft, she’s playing ’em off against each other like a fiddle. Two strings to her bow, and all that.”

“Seems like you’re pretty wide awake to the whole game yourself, eh, Harry?” the Major snickered.

“Aw, I’m not clever or nothing, but I know what o’clock it is, yeah,” Foker shrugged, unphased. “Can I help you out with this whole mess? Seems obvious your lot ain’t exactly chuffed that your nephew’s about to shack up with an Irish theatre family that spends more on whisky than rent.”

“Couldn’t have put it better myself,” the Major nodded, even though half of Foker’s silly slang went right over his gray old head. So he started grilling Foker for every last scrap of tea about the Costigans, and Foker spilled it all, fair and square: the old man was a total scamp but chaperoned his daughter like a hawk, no funny business allowed, they were both holding out for a rich husband. And the kicker? All those gushy love letters Pen had been shoving in his breast pocket like they were holy scripture? They weren’t written by Miss Fotheringay at all. They were penned by her former bestie, Miss Rouncy, who was twice as sharp and way better at spelling, ever since the two girls had a screaming fight over a pair of ostrich feathers.

At that the Major’s eyes nearly popped out of his head. He wheedled Foker so hard that by the end of the night, Foker had slipped him a sample of Miss Rouncy’s handwriting right into his pocketbook. Score one for the Major.

Then came the bombshell that made the old half-pay major feel like he’d won the lottery. “Wait a second,” Foker said offhand, “the General’s dead certain Pen’s got two thousand a year coming to him when he turns twenty-one, y’know? That’s why he’s not kicked him out yet.”

“Two thousand a year?” the Major shrieked so loud the whole coffee-room echoed. “His mother’s only got five hundred a year total! She’ll probably live to be eighty, and Arthur hasn’t got a single penny to his name beyond whatever pocket money she tosses him! He’s not rich at all! They’ve been barking up the wrong money tree entirely! You’ve just made me the happiest man alive, Harry!”

They shook hands so hard their knuckles ached, made plans to chat more at breakfast before Foker drove off in his drag the next morning. Meanwhile, ten miles away, Pen was probably curled up in bed kissing one of those fake love letters like it was the crown jewels, none the wiser.

Bright and early the next day, Foker handed over that little note from Miss Rouncy, just like he promised, and trundled off in his drag. The Major, grinning like he’d robbed a bank, popped over to see Doctor Portman at the Deanery, spilling every last detail of his new discoveries over breakfast. They sat by the window staring across the lawn straight at Captain Costigan’s lodgings, the very same window where they’d spotted Pen mooning around a few weeks prior. The Doctor was so mad at the landlady Mrs. Creed for hiding Sir Derby’s visits that he threatened to excommunicate her from the cathedral entirely. But the Major was already raring to go.

“Right,” he laughed, finishing his coffee and standing up. “Off I go to fight the dragon.”

“God speed, Major!” the Doctor cackled back. “I’ll hear your confession when you get back, if you come back alive.”

The Major trundled round to Mrs. Creed’s front door, climbed the stairs to Costigan’s rooms, and heard a whole racket of stamping feet and shouts of “Ha, ha!” coming from behind the door. The tiny kid leading him up the stairs piped up, “That’s Sir Derby Oaks having his fencing lesson! He comes Mondays, Wednesdays, Fridays, exactly on the dot.”

The Major knocked, and a tall gentleman popped the door open, foil in one hand, fencing mask dangling from the other. The Major bowed as polite as could be. “Captain Costigan? Major Pendennis here, Arthur Pendennis’s uncle.”

Costigan snapped his foil up into a perfect military salute, his face lighting up like he’d just spotted a long-lost drinking buddy. “Major! The honer’s moine! Deloighted to make yer acquaintance, sure and begorrah!”

Major Pendennis and Captain Costigan were two old campaigners who’d dodged everything from Waterloo musket fire to angry landladies demanding back rent, so you’d better believe they kept their cool when the Major made his grand entrance. The rest of the crew crammed into Cos’s sitting room? Not so much.
Slow, dreamy Miss Fotheringay’s usually unflappable heart suddenly thumped so hard you could see her bodice move, her cheeks flushing a bright, healthy cherry red just as Lieutenant Sir Derby Oaks glared at her like she’d intentionally lost his favorite prized foxhound. The crooked little old man, Mr. Bows, perched on the window seat had been trying to scribble theater sheet music for the past ten minutes, but the two young lads’ fencing lesson had them stamping and hopping around so hard the floorboards shook like a jelly mold. He was overjoyed to drop his quill and gape at the newcomer, who tipped his perfectly blacked boots and distributed bows so graceful you’d have thought he was greeting the entire royal court.
“Me daughter—me dear friend Mr. Bows—me gallant young pupil, Sir Derby Oaks!” Costigan announced, waving a hand like he was hosting a royal levee rather than a crumby second-floor lodging. “Give me one minute, Meejor, and I’m all yours.”
He bolted for his tiny adjacent bedroom so fast his coat tails flapped, slicked his lank hair with a ancient hairbrush older than the Battle of Trafalgar, tore off his frayed old stock, tied on the brand-new one Emily had stitched for him, and threw on the fancy new coat he’d ordered special for her benefit—all in the space of 60 seconds flat, the man had impressive speed even three sheets to the wind. Sir Derby followed him in, and waddled out a minute later squeezed into his absurdly tight “shell jacket”, stitched by a sadistic tailor who clearly hated large cavalry officers, which he, Milly, and probably even poor smitten Pen all thought was the height of masculine perfection.
The two elders made small talk about the weather before Costigan made his grand reappearance in his “new shoot”, as he insisted on calling the coat. “No need to apologize for greeting you in my shirt sleeves, Meejor,” he purred, oozing Irish charm.
“An old soldier could spend his time worse than teaching a lad how to wield a sword,” the Major replied, ever suave. “Why, I swear I remember hearing you swing a tune pretty well yourself back in the day, Captain Costigan, at Sir Richard Strachan’s table in Walcheren.”
This news made Bows look up so fast his glasses slid down his nose, utterly baffled, because he was fairly sure Costigan had never left Dublin after 1810. The Major didn’t let that little detail trip him up. “But we can hash out old war stories later. I came here today to pay my respects to Miss Fotheringay,” he said, and bowed so low and courtly he could’ve been greeting a duchess, not a provincial theater star. “My nephew Arthur raves about your performances, of course—but he’s a starry-eyed 18-year-old boy, so I had to come judge for myself. I’ve seen every top actress on the London stage, and I swear, you outshine all of them. You’re as majestic as Mrs. Siddons herself.”
“Faith, I’ve been saying that for years!” Costigan winked at his daughter, gesturing for her to pull the half-finished satin gown off the only empty chair. Milly did, and dropped a curtsy so deep you’d have thought he’d handed her a diamond tiara.
“Just as pathetic as Miss O’Neill, too,” the Major went on, “those little snippets of song you sing reminded me of Mrs. Jordan in her prime, and your bearing’s exactly like the great French tragedienne, Mars—have you ever seen her perform, Miss Fotheringay?”
“Oh, there was two Mahers over in Crow Street,” Milly said plainly. “Fanny was decent enough, but Biddy was naught to write home about.”
“Sure, the Major means the god of war, not the two Dublin lasses!” Costigan jumped in, saving everyone from the awkward beat.
“Not that Mars, though I suppose Venus herself can be forgiven for mixing them up,” the Major grinned, directing the joke right at Sir Derby, who’d just stomped back into the room in his skin-tight jacket. Neither of them got the pun, and Sir Derby just scowled harder, looking like he wanted to ask what the devil this snooty old army guy was even doing in his favorite actress’s lodgings.
The Major was delighted by the sulky look on the baronet’s face. *Oh, good*, he thought. A rival with 10 thousand a year and a cavalry commission. That’s way better than my idiot nephew.
“Hope I didn’t interrupt your fencing practice,” he said smoothly. “I’m only in Chatteris a day or two, and I was desperate to meet my old comrade Costigan, and see the lady who enchanted me so much from the stalls last night. I wasn’t the only man smitten! My reverend friend went home raving about your Ophelia, and I saw Sir Derby fling a bouquet that no actress ever deserved more. Aren’t those the very blooms sitting in that glass on the mantelpiece?”
“I’m very fond of flowers,” Milly simpered, batting her lashes hard at Sir Derby. He still looked like he’d swallowed a sour plum.
“‘Sweets to the sweet’, right? That’s the line from Hamlet, isn’t it?” the Major chirped, still being unshakeably friendly.
“Pon my life, I don’t know,” Sir Derby grunted. “Never was much for books.”
“Can that be?” the Major gasped, all mock-surprised. “You don’t take after your father, then! He was one of the finest scholars I ever met, and I knew him very well, y’know. He even saved my life once, back in the day.”
Milly’s eyes went wide, rolling first to the Major then over to Sir Derby all full of soft gratitude. The baronet, though? He looked like he wished his apothecary dad had let the Major die of the fever instead. “My dad was a good doctor, yeah,” he mumbled. “Not my line of work. Got an appointment to keep. Bye Cos, bye Miss Fotheringay.”
He stormed out before she could even beg him to stay, the clatter of his sabre thudding down the creaky stairs, and they all heard him curse little Tom Creed out in the passage and kick the boy’s peg-top so hard it sailed into the middle of the street.
“Monstrous fine looking young man, that,” the Major said to Costigan, not cracking a smile even though he was this close to snorting with laughter.
“A credit to the army, that lad!” Costigan gushed. “Refined manners, princely fortune, rides sixteen stone like he’s a featherweight.”
“Perfect champion, no doubt all the ladies adore him,” the Major nodded.
“He’s alright on a horse,” Milly said, “but he never has anything interesting to say.”
“Best seen, not heard, that one,” Bows muttered, making the Major make a mental note: this woman can bring an audience to tears on stage, but she can’t follow a basic pun about a French actress? Wild.
Costigan, ever the hospitable Irish host, immediately insisted on plying the Major with drinks. The Major, who was no more hungry than a man who’d just gorged himself at a Lord Mayor’s banquet, declared he was fainting from hunger and dying for a biscuit and a glass of wine. He knew the easiest way to soften a man up is to let him play the generous host. “Get out the old Madara, Milly love,” Costigan winked.
Milly snuck downstairs, handed little Tommy Creed sixpence to run to the Grapes pub for a pint of Madara and sixpennyworth of assorted biscuits, promising him two biscuits for himself if he ran fast. While he was gone, she sat with the landlady gossiping about how nice the Major was, how butter wouldn’t melt in his mouth, and how Sir Derby had stormed off in a jealous huff before they could figure out how to keep both him and rich little Pen happy.
Bows, who’d seen enough messy theater romances to last three lifetimes, cleared his throat and pointed out that Milly was late for rehearsal, or the manager would fine her half a day’s pay. She grabbed her bonnet and shawl, smiling pretty as ever, and the Major shook her hand so warmly it could’ve been paternal. Bows hobbled out behind her, thoroughly confused: the old Pendennis isn’t here to support the engagement? That can’t be right.
The second the door clicked shut, Costigan dove for the bottle and chugged the last of the old leftover wine, then tore into the new pint from the pub so fast his hands shook. He was already well oiled when he leaned across the table, eyes glistening. “That lad Pen’s gonna make your family a fine son-in-law, Major! When he moves into Fairoaks Park, he’ll take me with him, eh? Me poor old aching bones won’t have to live in this tiny lodging no more.”
The Major sipped his wine, making a face like it was the finest vintage from Lord Steyne’s cellar, then dropped the bombshell. “Wait a second. Pen’s not an heir to two thousand a year. His dad, my brother, was a country apothecary, and he left all his money—five hundred a year, total—to his wife. Pen doesn’t get a penny until his mother dies, and her family lives to be 90. He’s got no fortune at all.”
Costigan’s face went bright tomato red. He shot up, slamming his fist on the table. “Are you making a fool out of me? Insulting me and me daughter? I’ve got stacks of that boy’s mushy love letters! I’ll drag him to court for breach of promise! I’ll demand satisfaction, sir! I’ll fight you if you try to jilt me girl!”
The Major didn’t even flinch. He stood up, adjusted his gloves, and stared Costigan dead in the eye. “Threaten to publish a teenager’s silly love poems and challenge a man to a duel over a marriage that would leave your daughter living in a garret on 500 a year? Be my guest. I’m not scared of any Costigan in Ireland. I’m here to stop this stupid, poverty-bound match, and I will stop it.”
Costigan drew himself up to his full 5 foot 4 height, trying to look as fierce as a lion. “You said you’re staying at the George Hotel, Major? A friend of mine will call on you before you leave Chatteris. You’ll hear more from me.”
The Major stormed down the stairs, and Costigan bowed after him over the landing, as dramatic as if he’d just won a standoff against Napoleon himself. By the time the Major hit the street, he was fuming—but also grinning. He’d already derailed the whole silly romance, before his starry-eyed nephew even had a clue what was going on.

We’ve mentioned Mr. Garbetts before, of course: the theatre’s top leading tragedian, strapping as a prize fighter, famously jovial, and just famously unreliable with a bill. He and Captain Costigan were thick as thieves—joint stars of the Magpie Hotel’s after-show drinking club, who’d co-signed so many of each other’s dodgy IOUs that neither could remember who actually owed who money anymore. They were fast friends, full stop, even if Garbetts almost never popped round Costigan’s place: Milly Fotheringay had turned down his marriage proposal flat years before he married his own wife, and Mrs. Garbetts was still jealous enough of Milly to stare a hole right through her stage costume at every performance.

None of that backstory matters here, though—what counts is that the second Milly and faithful old Bows the fiddler stepped out the house, Costigan sent his tiny errand boy Tommy pelting off to fetch Garbetts, with orders to grab two extra glasses of hot whisky-and-water from the Grapes pub on his way back. Ten minutes later, the tragedian (famous for breaking pokers clean over his bicep for a laugh mid-soliloquy) and the booze turned up at the door.

Costigan didn’t spill every single detail of the Major’s visit that the reader already knows—he saved the best bits for when the spirits had loosened both their tongues, and together they drafted a very dramatic, very threatening letter addressed to Major Pendennis. It demanded the Major stop meddling in the proposed match between Arthur Pendennis and Milly, name an early wedding date, or else give Costigan the “satisfaction due to any gentleman of honour.” If the Major dared refuse? Costigan threatened to horsewhip him within an inch of his life on the main high street of Chatteris for all the county to gawk at. The letter was sealed with the Costigan family’s only remaining piece of heirloom silver: a dented crested seal that had seen so many drunks stamp it on wax it now looked more like a squashed duck than a proper noble coat of arms.

Garbetts marched off as official second, Costigan squeezing his hand and calling down blessings on his head before he shut the door. Then the Captain dragged out his ancient flintlock duelling pistols, the same ones he swore had “dispatched no less than three rascally gentlemen over honour disputes in Dublin back in ‘29” (they had in fact never been fired outside of a village fairground shooting gallery). He polished the locks, lined up all of Arthur’s gushy love letters and terrible poetry on the table beside the empty whisky glasses, and settled in to wait for Garbetts to bring back word of the duel time and location.

Twenty minutes later, Garbetts stumbled back in, face as white as a fresh pot of greasepaint.
“Ye saw the old rascal, then?” Costigan barked, already lining one of the pistols up at his own bloodshot eye to test the sightline.
“Seen him? Aye, but I didn’t dare hand over that daft letter,” Garbetts said, aghast. “I ran into that prat Tatham, the Major’s snooty lawyer, loitering on his doorstep. I’m a married family man, Captain! I’m not touching duels, my wife will hide all my stage Hamlet tights if she finds out I was mixed up in a challenge. I want nothing to do with your shooting match.”

Costigan flew into a towering rage, calling Garbetts a coward and quoting lines from the character Bob Acres (the famous duel wimp from *The Rivals*, which Costigan had played on stage and off for decades) until the two men bickered themselves into a foul mood and Garbetts stormed off, challenge letter clutched in his hand and a half-empty glass of whisky he’d forgotten to set down still in the other. The letter was never opened. The proposed shooting match was deader than a pantomime villain.

Milly turned up home soon after with Bows, fresh from rehearsal, already toting a pair of once-white satin shoes she planned to scrub clean with breadcrumbs for her Ophelia performance the following Tuesday. The whole flat reeked of hot whisky, and her father was pacing back and forth so agitated he was almost tripping over his own boot heels.
“Ye’re tipsy again, Papa,” Milly said, shoving him away when he tried to hug her and wail about her broken heart. “Ye promised no spirits before dinner.”

Costigan ranted through slurred sobs about the Pendennis clan being a pack of lying traitors, spilling the news that the Major had turned up to reveal Arthur didn’t have two thousand a year, or two hundred a year, or even twenty spare pounds a year—he was entirely dependent on his 500-a-year mother, who was young enough to remarry tomorrow and leave him completely penniless. He rapped his dented old pistol case for dramatic emphasis, but Bows had heard him yell about “defending family honour” a dozen times before over far smaller slights, so he barely paid the death-box any mind.

Milly didn’t scream, or swoon, or tear up the love letters. She just plucked a chunk of bread out of the cupboard, sat down at the table to scrub the grime off her satin shoes, and said, very matter-of-fact in her soft Irish brogue: “Sure if he’s no money, there’s no use marrying him, then, is there?”
When Costigan spluttered that Arthur had tricked them by riding a fancy horse and buying Milly a silk shawl, Milly blinked and pointed out that Arthur had never once claimed to be rich—*Costigan* was the one who’d convinced himself the boy was a secret millionaire, and spent months hyping up the “Lady Pendennis of Fairoaks Park” fantasy all on his own.

She laid out the entire plan in ten seconds flat, no dramatics, no duels, no public nose-tweaking that Costigan had been swearing he’d do to the Major on the park promenade. Send Arthur a polite, formal letter asking for a full list of his assets and income before they locked in the wedding date. He’d be forced to admit he had nothing. Then write back very politely saying the match simply couldn’t go ahead, wish him well, and send all his silly poems and love letters back. No fuss, no mess, no chance of anyone getting shot.

By the time they sat down to their beefsteak pudding dinner, Costigan had forgotten all about his grand revenge fantasy. The whisky had worn off, he was tucking into extra suet, and he was far more worried about running into Arthur and having to break off the engagement first than he was about duelling the Major. Bows snickered the whole meal through, half-admiring and half-scornful of how easily Milly had popped every last one of Costigan’s over-the-top melodramatic bubbles.

After dinner, Milly sorted through Arthur’s stack of pages: all the gushy late-night love letters, the terrible verses full of Latin she couldn’t even parse, the scribbled notes about running away to live in a cottage by the sea. She carefully tore out the newspaper clipping of Arthur’s published poem praising her performance as Imogen to tuck into her scrapbook of good reviews, tied the rest of the stack up neatly with a piece of old string like it was a pound of sugar from the grocers, then put the kettle on to make tea. She didn’t shed a single tear, didn’t sigh over the wasted romance, didn’t give a second thought to all the lonely, lovesick nights Arthur had spent writing every line of those pages. Ten miles away, Arthur was still hugging her memory to his chest and daydreaming of their grand future. Back at the Costigan flat, Milly passed around a bowl of filberts, and asked Bows if he’d stay for a second cup.

Over at Fairoaks, everyone was starting to side-eye the empty armchair Major Pendennis usually occupied at dinner. Dr. Portman and his wife popped by Helen’s lodge on their drive home to Clavering, bearing a quick scribbled note from the Major: he’d be lingering in Chatteris one more day, he claimed, to chat with the Tathams, the family solicitors, that very afternoon. Not a single word about the absolute knock-down, drag-out fight he’d already lost to Captain Costigan that very morning.

Pen, for his part, had zero urge to wander into Chatteris while his uncle was still there. The very thought of the Major lurking smugly on the Dean’s neatly mowed lawn, spying on him while he made moon eyes at Miss Costigan in her drawing room, would’ve turned a perfectly pleasant stroll (a treat he barely ever got to enjoy) sour in two seconds flat. His silly little boyish crush was far too shy to show off in public anyway, beyond the frankly overzealous ogling he did every time Emily so much as handed him a cup of tea. All giddy new love talk sounds like absolute gibberish to anyone not currently smitten, after all: it’s a vocabulary book with no key, a lamp with no flame. Go dig up that box of old love letters you hid in the attic from when you were 17, if you don’t believe me. You’ll read those gushing lines and wonder what sort of hallucination made you think that level of puling nonsense was romantic. We’re not even going to bother printing the terrible, over-the-top flowery verses and letters Pen churned out at this point in his life, purely to save him from lifelong public embarrassment. They’re far too spooney for even the softest-hearted young lady to read in cold blood. We’ve locked them up tight, tied with Emily’s sewing cotton and sealed with Captain Costigan’s giant dented silver flask cork, and no one’s getting the key.

The Major, meanwhile, had stormed out of his interview with Costigan in such a towering rage that even his own shadow would’ve crossed the street to avoid him. “That impudent bog-trotting scoundrel,” he fumed to himself, “dared threaten me? Dared say he’d let his lot of Costigans marry into the Pendennis family, and then challenge me to a duel? If he can dig up even one man who counts as a gentleman to carry that challenge, I’m half tempted to say yes—pshaw, though! Can you imagine the county gossip? Me, a respectable retired officer with polished boots and lavender cologne, dueling a tipsy hack actor’s dad over a row with a barn theatre actress?”

When Dr. Portman asked him how the fight against the dragon Costigan had gone, the Major didn’t have the heart to tell the clergyman about the drunken Irishman’s insults, just said the whole mess was ugly, far from over, and swore both the Doctor and his wife to not breathe a word of the business to Helen at Fairoaks. He sent off that tame, vague note we already mentioned, then trundled back to his hotel to take his wrath out on his valet Morgan, who later gossiped to Foker’s footman over their dinner in the servants’ hall that the Major was “dammin and cussin upstairs and downstairs like a sailor on shore leave.”

That gossip made its way straight to Foker, who was just finishing his breakfast (it was two o’clock in the afternoon, as per usual for him) and decided he was dying to know how the Major’s interview with Costigan had turned out. He traipsed down the hall in his fancy brocade dressing gown and the hand-embroidered fez his doting mother had stitched for him, and knocked on the Major’s door.
The Major was deep in consultation with old Mr. Tatham, the white-haired family solicitor who’d managed his late brother’s affairs, sorting out a new lease for Helen’s Fairoaks property. Foker, embarrassed to intrude, started to back off, but the Major waved him in. Foker doffed his fancy fez, bowed, spread his crimson dressing gown skirts over the armchair he sank into, and beamed at the two men so warmly he could’ve sweet-talked a grumpy inquisitor into a good mood.
After Foker made them all laugh about how nice his robe was, the Major spilled the whole messy story. “That Irishman, Emily’s father? Refuses to believe my nephew’s broke, called us both liars, even hinted I was a coward before he kicked me out. When you knocked, I half thought you were his second come to deliver the duel note.”

Mr. Tatham, a stern Quaker-adjacent type who never set foot inside a theatre, was horrified. He dropped a very useful little fun fact: “I know that Costigan personally. He’s a total deadbeat, wine soaked, never pays his bills. We’ve got an outstanding writ out against him and one of his actor pals, Garbetts the tragedian, for a bill they owed our local respectable Quaker grocer Mr. Skinner. Costigan even cried in the man’s shop to beg for extra time, but neither of them are worth the trouble of sending the bailiffs after.”

Right on cue, there was another knock at the door. In lumbered a burly shabby-looking man in a braided frock coat, holding a letter with a huge blotched red wax seal. “Can I speak to Major Pendennis in private? I’m bearing a message from my good friend Captain Costigan—” He stopped dead, turned paler than a ghost, locked eyes with Mr. Tatham. “Hullo, Garbetts!” Foker cackled, delighted. Garbetts didn’t say another word, gasped some unintelligible gibberish, and bolted out of the room before anyone could grab him.
All three men burst out laughing. “No duel today, Major,” Foker wheezed, already mimicking Garbetts’ horrified face. “If they tried to fight it out, Mr. Tatham here could just send the bailiffs to arrest both seconds halfway through the first shot.” The Major couldn’t have been more relieved.

Foker, being the chaotic good little rich boy who could get into any room, decided to pop over to Costigan’s lodgings later that afternoon to snoop. Costigan was already at the Magpie Hotel pub, bragging to his drinking buddies about how he was gonna blow the Major’s brains out, so only Emily was home, washing teacups while old Bows the fiddler sat across from her smoking his pipe. Foker poked his silly little head in the door, did a ridiculous Harlequin spin so fast his head looked like it was about to fall off, and made Emily laugh so hard she snort-laughed, the kind of laugh even Bows’ wittiest jokes could never drag out of her, the kind Pen’s most dramatic love speeches just confused her.
Foker spilled the whole story of Garbetts’ failed duel note delivery, told Emily straight that Pen was dead broke, not the 2,000 a year rich husband her dad had promised her. Emily’s face fell. “Oh, he’s a nice sweet boy,” she said, a little sad, “but no 2,000 a year? Sure I was an idiot for believing all my da’s silly stories. Next time I’ll pick my own husband.” The very next second, she was probably mentally picturing that handsome rich Lieutenant Sir Derby Oaks she’d danced with at the last assembly.

Then Foker dropped the master bait: he’d booked a private room back at The George, they could have lobster salad and unlimited champagne for dinner, come meet the Major there, bury the hatchet. No one has ever turned down free lobster salad and bubbly, especially not Emily, so she agreed immediately, even dragging grumpy Bows along. Foker sent his servant Stoopid down to the Magpie to fish Costigan out mid-drunk ballad, and by seven o’clock that evening, the Major found himself sat at a dinner table with the fiddler, the actress Costigan had demanded he duel over, and 10 minutes later Costigan himself stumbled in, so drunk he’d already been crying over his own sad Irish song at the pub. He grabbed the Major’s hand, squeezed it so hard his knuckles went white, and called him his dearest, oldest, most beloved friend. No one mentioned the duel. Everyone ate lobster. A grand old time was had by all.

The Major rolled back to Fairoaks the next day, acting so casual no one would’ve guessed he’d spent the previous night drinking champagne with the man who’d threatened to murder him 24 hours earlier. He was extra sweet to Pen, gave him a very emphatic “God bless you” before he went to bed, almost told Helen the whole messy story then decided not to ruin her night’s sleep.
The next morning at breakfast, the post arrived. Pen grabbed a letter, blushed bright red, recognized Costigan’s scrawled handwriting immediately, and didn’t dare open it in front of everyone. The Major sent little Laura off to play (she hated him anyway, so she went without arguing), pulled Helen aside into the hall, and told her plainly: “It’s done. The affair’s over. I dictated that breakup letter myself, got Emily to add a couple lines of her own. The engagement’s off for good.”
Helen ran back into the dining room, just in time to see Pen ripping the letter open, face gone stupefied. The note said they’d just found out Pen was far too poor to support a wife, Emily was 10 years older than him, marriage was completely impossible, and he ought to stop visiting their house at once. Emily’s tiny little added note said she agreed with her father, she was grateful for all his kindness, but they shouldn’t meet until the pain wore off.
Pen flew into a wild panic. “This is a trick! That’s not her handwriting! Someone’s playing a joke on me!” He raged, yelling that he was going to ride straight to Chatteris, confront Emily, make her tell him to his face it was over. The Major just sighed, pointed out that Emily spelled “affection” with one F, that she was ten years his senior, that her father owed every shopkeeper in three towns money, and she was never going to be a suitable companion for Helen.
Pen stormed off to Chatteris anyway, banged on Costigan’s door, got all his letters returned unopened, and eventually ran into Emily on the street walking with her father. She looked right through him, didn’t even nod. Pen pined so hard he got a full fever after he went to see her perform one night where the audience was so tiny the manager gave everyone their ticket money back. Everyone begged him to travel abroad for a while to get over her, but he flat-out refused. Heartbreak hurts, but let’s be honest—plenty of people have gotten dumped before Pen Pendennis, and none of them died of it, right?

If you’d wandered into Chatteris’s little theatre not long after our last round of chaos, you’d have found Manager Bingley hamming up his signature role of Rolla in *Pizarro* to an audience so sparse it looked like half the ticket holders had gotten lost on the walk over, and the other half had bailed to get fish and chips instead. Poor Arthur “Pen” Pendennis had the entire box section pretty much to himself, slouched over the rail with bloodshot, red-rimmed eyes, staring so hard at the stage he’d have bore a hole in the scenery if he could. None of the Spanish soldiers, sun priest virgins, or messy battle scenes registered for him—he only saw Cora, the second she stepped onto the boards. He’d admit later he was so hopped up on unrequited love, rage, and despair he’d half considered bringing a pistol to the theatre, and the only reason he didn’t do something daft enough to land him in a cell outside Chatteris gaol for the rest of his youth was his mum at home, who never pressed him on his moping but left warm tea and silent sympathy out for him every night he trundled home miserable.

Miss Fotheringay made for a very fine Cora, all in her flowing white gown, leopard-skin drapery, sun brooch on her chest, and cheap sparkly bracelets glinting on her bare arms. She delivered her handful of lines perfectly, and looked even better, but that night those eyes that had completely rotted Pen’s brain weren’t cast his way at all. He was too wrapped up in his own drama to notice her glances were fixed firmly on two guys in the box right next to his.

The whole theatre had been half-asleep up to that point. The cast yawned through their lines, chatted to each other mid-dialogue, and everyone was already halfway to agreeing to issue full refunds like they’d done the night Pen got chased out of the theatre earlier. Bingley was phoning in his performance, his wife was muttering Elvira’s lines under her breath like she was apologising to the empty seats for being there.

Then suddenly, Mrs. Bingley started bellowing her lines so loud she sounded like a bull escaped from the local fair. Bingley tossed off his apathy and started sprinting across the stage yelling like a street preacher. Garbetts, Rowkins, and Miss Rouncy all pulled out every over-the-top acting trick they knew, hamming so hard their faces were cramping. They were all performing for the two new arrivals in Box 3: one was a quiet, sharp little old man in black, the other was the most absurdly overdressed dandy you’d ever seen in Chatteris.

That was Mr. Dolphin, London’s biggest, fanciest theatre impresario, complete with a hooked nose, piles of curling brown hair and whiskers, a coat covered in more gold braid and velvet trim than a royal parade, three under-waistcoats, rings so heavy they weighed his fingers down, and a white kid-gloved hand that pulled out a yellow handkerchief smelling so strongly of musk and bergamot the fumes wafted all the way to the back of the gallery. No wonder every actor on stage was trying to suck up to him.

He barely noticed any of their efforts. Bingley yelled, Mrs. Bingley bellowed, Dolphin just took snuff out of a giant solid gold box and nodded vaguely. It was only the final scene, when Rolla (Bingley, huffing and puffing, hauling around his extremely overgrown 5-year-old son who was playing the “baby” of the play) staggered in with the kid, and Cora screamed “O God, there’s blood on him!” that Dolphin finally clapped hard and yelled “Bravo!” at the top of his lungs. He smacked his secretary Billy on the back so hard the poor old guy nearly spilled his snuff. “By Jove, Billy, she’ll do!” he crowed.

Old Billy, the sardonic secretary, squinted. “Who the hell taught her that trick? I saw her at the Olympic Theatre three years back, she couldn’t even say boo to a goose.” That was grumpy old Mr. Bows, the guy tucked away in the orchestra pit plucking at his fiddle, who’d coached her through every beat. The whole cast heard the cheer, and swarmed around Fotheringay the second the curtain fell to offer gushing congratulations, all while silently hating her guts.

Dolphin hadn’t popped up in tiny nowhere Chatteris for no reason, of course. His London Museum Theatre was this close to collapsing. The big A-list legitimate drama star had run 20 nights, and no one made a penny except him. The celebrated Mr. and Mrs. Cawdor had flopped so hard no one turned up to their run of tragedies. The lion tamer’s big animal act had gotten mauled mid-show, the Lord Chamberlain shut the whole thing down, and his grand fancy opera production had left him so deep in debt everyone in London was just waiting for his bankruptcy notice to turn up in the Gazette.

His fancy rich patron was none other than the Marquis of Steyne, who hung out in his private curtained box so the audience couldn’t see him, but every single ballet girl knew exactly when he was there—they’d all be mid-leap 3 feet off the ground, twirling like a top, and still manage to cast adoring little glances at his hidden box. One night he was in there with his two toadies Wenham and Wagg, cackling so loud the pit was yelling for silence, when Wenham pulled out a private letter from Major Pendennis. The Major was begging them to get Dolphin to travel out to Chatteris, see Miss Fotheringay, sign her to a London contract, because his young nephew was head over heels obsessed with her and swore she was better than every leading actress on the West End stage. Steyne saw right through the story, remembered his own daft teenage fling with a market gardener’s daughter back when he was at Eton, and waved a hand. “Send Dolphin down. If she’s half that good, sign her.”

That’s how Dolphin ended up in Chatteris, and before he left he sent Steyne a gushing note saying he’d signed Miss Fotheringay on the spot, and she’d be making her London debut very soon.
