Pen saw the announcement in the Chatteris local paper, the same paper he’d spent months writing gushing, star-struck reviews of her in. The editor ranted about her talent, predicted she’d take London by storm, and the theatre started slapping up posters announcing “Miss Fotheringay’s Final Nights in Chatteris!” Pen and Sir Derby Oaks showed up to every single performance: Sir Derby hung out in the stage box throwing bouquets and grinning when Fotheringay tossed him a little smirk, Pen moped all alone in his empty back box, pale, haggard, and miserable.

One night, old Mr. Bows the fiddler climbed out of the orchestra pit, trundled into Pen’s box, and held out a hand. “C’mon. Let’s go walk.” They trundled down the moonlit street together, and parked themselves on Chatteris bridge, dangling their legs over the edge, talking about *her*.

“We’re both fools for the same woman,” Bows said, puffing on a crummy cigar. “You’re not the first, you won’t be the last. And I’ve got less excuse than you, I’m old enough to know better. She hasn’t got a heart bigger than the stone you’re leaning on. If you or I fell off this bridge and drowned tonight, she wouldn’t bat an eye. She only puts up with me ‘cause she knows she can’t act for shit without me coaching her. She’ll send for me the second she gets settled in London, guaranteed. Not ‘cause she likes me. ‘Cause she needs me. No deep feelings, no big dramas, no soft spots. She likes her dinner, and she likes people clapping for her. That’s it.”

“…And you still stick around?” Pen asked, shocked out of his own self-pity.

“Habits, lad. Like snuff, or cheap whisky. I’ve put five years of work into making her what she is. Can’t quit now. One day she’ll get married, though, and dump me like I’m dumping this cigar end.” He flicked the little glowing spark into the river, and it vanished in the dark water. Pen rode home that night, and for the first time in months, he stopped thinking only about his own broken heart.

Major Pendennis had resolved to hunker down at Fairoaks like a general holding a siege until the unpleasant actress lady was fully out of the county, no exceptions. He never acted like he was hovering over Pen or laying down strict rules, of course—he just had every single servant on the estate reporting back on the boy’s every trip to the shops, every late night walk, every weird mumble over breakfast, so he had a full minute-by-minute log of his nephew’s activities whether Pen knew it or not.

I’d wager every single one of you reading this has had your heart broken at least once, for whatever silly reason fate, a lying lover, or your own dumb self-sabotage could cook up. Go dig up that cringey old sad memory of yours, and you’ll get exactly what Pen was going through. Oh, the 3 a.m. wake-ups where you’re rolling back and forth in bed like a rotisserie chicken, counting the hours toll by toll, miserable enough that you’d trade a whole year of good meals for one single text back. I’ve never heard of a man straight-up dying of heartbreak, but I’ve seen a strapping 12 stone lad waste all the way down to nine and a half after a bad breakup—so that’s a whole quarter of him basically vanishing, no small loss. Don’t panic for Pen, though: he’ll bounce back, same as the rest of us. The Major would lean in and pat Mrs. Pendennis’s hand reassuringly when she fretted, and say, “Half of a man’s pain when he gets left isn’t even about the girl—it’s about his bruised vanity. Getting dumped is no fun, of course, but look how easy we ditch the ladies ourselves, eh?”

Mrs. Pendennis, bless her sweet sheltered heart, didn’t know a single thing about that sort of cynical wisdom. She’d had one tiny heartbreak decades ago that she’d never spoken of, and aside from that all of her fierce motherly worry was focused 100% on Pen, so much so that she felt his sorrows twice as sharp as he did himself, hovering in the hallway outside his room when he was moping, sneaking extra jam on his toast to cheer him up.

The Major, for all his worldly grump, deserves credit for sticking it out at Fairoaks, which was about as exciting as watching paint dry for a man who used to pop in to three separate London drawing rooms in a single evening. His biggest highlights these days were a rare dinner with Dr. Portman or a local squire, and a dreary backgammon rubber with the widow that stretched for three hours. He’d hover by the letter bag like an excited puppy every post day, read every single line of the evening paper, and fuss over his health with his fancy medicine chest, figuring all this quiet country air would undo the damage of years of fancy London banquets. He dressed up in full formal attire twice a day for no reason, paced the terrace walk for his daily exercise, and kept himself sane by rereading his two volumes of the *Annual Register* so many times he could recite every detail of the American war and the Wyoming massacre by heart. This is what they call being adaptable, you see.

Pen sometimes joined the Major for backgammon at night, or sat and half-listened to his mom pluck out soft old ballads on the piano in the summer evenings, but he was still restless as a caged fox. He’d get up before the sun was even up, trudge over to the spooky carp pond in Clavering Park—you know the one, where a milkmaid drowned herself a hundred years back, and the locals swear her ghost haunts the rushes at midnight. He never came close to drowning himself, no matter how much his mom panicked he might. He fished there for hours, zoning out staring at his float bobbing in the eddies, and every time he caught a carp, tench, or eel he’d trudge home triumphant, and the Major would cook them up all fancy in the Continental style he’d learned on his tours.

He sat under an old gnarled tree at the edge of the pond scribbling terrible angsty poetry, which he would burn a few years later and pretend he’d never written, blushing so hard his ears turned red remembering how he rhymed “sorrow” with “borrow” and “tomorrow” no less than seven times. When you’re that deep in drafting rhymes for your heartbreak, your sorrow’s almost over already, that’s a fact. He still had his grumpy days, his random mad fits where he’d saddle up his mare Rebecca and gallop her all across the countryside, screaming the name of his lost actress love loud enough to make turnpike keepers and carters stare and wonder if he’d gone fully mad.

Harry Foker started turning up at Fairoaks nearly every other day, and his chaotic good energy was a godsend for the Major and Pen, though it gave the widow and little Laura quite the shock. His fancy tandem carriage caused absolute chaos in Clavering market square one afternoon: he knocked over a whole stall of cabbages, nicked Mrs. Pybus’s poodle’s ear right off with his wheel, and popped into the Clavering Arms to chug a full glass of raspberry bitters for the fun of it. Every old biddy in town scrambled to pull out their dog-eared copies of the peerage to look him up, and when they couldn’t find his name in their 30-year-old volumes, they decided he was even fancier than they’d first thought, the son of an actual earl’s daughter no less. Of course, the story of Pen’s disastrous actress crush spread through the same small town faster than a grease fire.

From the top of the London coach driving past Fairoaks lodge, Clavering St. Mary looks like a perfect postcard: the bright little Brawl river glinting in the sun, the old church tower peeking over treetops, soft rolling hills stretching all the way to the sea in the distance. The old coach driver Tom Smith used to point out a specific riverside tree to his passengers and tell them all the artists came there to paint the view, calling it “a proper Habby landscape, sir.” But as with most pretty things you see from far away, Clavering is a whole lot less charming up close. On non-market days the streets are so empty the clack of a woman’s wooden pattens echoes all the way down the high street, and you can hear the rusty old flag on the Clavering Arms creak before you can hear a single human voice. The Assembly Rooms haven’t hosted a ball since the old Clavering volunteer regiment threw one for their colonel 20 years prior, their stables all empty save for Thursdays when the farmers roll in with their carts and gigs to sell their turnips.

The old grey stone church looms over the market square, its ancient saint statues all broken and headless, the ones high out of reach so old even Dr. Portman doesn’t remember what they’re supposed to be of, while the local low-church pastor Simcoe rants that they’re idolatrous abominations. The rectory is a stout old brick house from Queen Anne’s reign, tucked next to the grammar school, the Finucane sisters’ girls’ academy, and an old slaughterhouse leftover from abbey days. Dr. Portman spent half his fortune buying a fancy stained-glass window all the way from the Netherlands for the church, and a cathedral-sized organ to go with it—but half the town decided the window was full of popish idols and trooped off to Simcoe’s new chapel down by the factory, leaving Dr. Portman’s wife seething, because now she has to curtsy to Simcoe’s stuck-up wife who’s the daughter of some obscure Irish peer, and half the town is calling her husband a heathen for enjoying a rubber of whist after dinner. The poor doctor told her he wouldn’t cave to the nonsense: King George III himself played whist, after all, and if the locals wanted to call him a sinner for that, they could go jump in the Brawl.

The little town was already full of more petty drama than the whole of London combined. The book club Major Pendennis set up to try and give people something neutral to do devolved into constant bickering, so the only people ever seen in the reading room were the quiet curate Smirke who read all the magazines, old Glanders who napped by the window, and nosy Mrs. Pybus who read everyone else’s letters before they could pick them up from the post.

When the story of Pen’s failed actress engagement blew through town, every single person had an opinion. The Finucane girls whispered about it over their tea, their boarding school students gossiped about it in the dorm after lights out, the grammar school boys snickered and pointed at Pen when he walked past, calling him stuck-up “Lord Pendennis” for not wearing the same cheap corduroys they did. And who was the biggest spreader of the gossip? None other than Mrs. Portman herself, who couldn’t keep a juicy story to herself if you paid her, much to her husband the doctor’s deep mortification. The old biddies of the town clucked like a flock of offended hens: “Gracious, how that widow Pendennis spoils that horrid stuck-up boy! Marrying a painted actress off the stage, of all things! If his dear father was still alive this scandal would never have happened!”

Pen had never been exactly popular in Clavering. He was a quiet, proud lad who thought all their petty small-town dignities and gossiping was deathly boring, and he didn’t bother hiding it. He only even liked Dr. Portman and the curate Smirke, and the rest of the town thought he was far too uppity for not dropping by their boring tea parties uninvited. The whole village was yelping about the Pendennises like a pack of tiny yappy terriers nipping at their heels, desperate to drag the well-off family down to their level. Dr. Portman and Glanders didn’t dare tell Mrs. Pendennis all the terrible things people were saying about her boy, but Pen found out soon enough. Someone chalked “FOTHERINGAY FOR EVER!” all over the Fairoaks gate one night, another fellow pasted a giant Chatteris playbill to the front door. Then one afternoon, as Pen walked through the churchyard on his way to church, that brute Hobnell, the 20-year-old overgrown grammar school boy who boarded with Wapshot, struck a dramatic Hamlet pose by a brand new empty grave and started cackling out Ophelia’s death speech straight at Pen, leering the whole time.

Pen flew into such a rage he shrieked out something very close to a curse, whipped his riding crop straight across Hobnell’s face, threw the whip down and dared the bully to fight him fair, and in two seconds flat had knocked the sputtering idiot straight into the empty grave that had been waiting for a recently deceased elderly parishioner. He stood there, fists clenched, face red with rage, yelling at the rest of the gawking grammar school boys to come and take a turn too—until they all scattered like scared chickens, right as Dr. Portman rounded the gate to see what the ruckus was about, and Hobnell clambered out of the grave covered in dirt and bleeding from his split lip. Pen was so mad he could barely speak, his voice cracking on the verge of tears as he said the coward had insulted him, and the kindly doctor didn’t even bother scolding him.

Major Pendennis, who was perpetually terrified of what the neighbours thought of him, was fit to be tied over the tiny silly scandal blowing up around Pen’s head, but Pen didn’t say a word about the churchyard brawl to anyone at home. He just trotted over to visit Harry Foker, who was absolutely tickled pink at the chance to act as his second. Foker drove his fancy drag straight over to the Clavering Arms, sent his servant Stoopid over to Wapshot’s grammar school with a very formal note challenging Hobnell to a gentleman’s duel. Stoopid reported back later that all the big grammar school boys had passed the note around and cackled over it, and Hobnell said he’d give his answer after afternoon school let out—right as Mr. Wapshot himself stepped out of the schoolhouse wearing his full master’s academic gown, wondering what all the shouting was about.

News of Pen’s dumb little scuffle spread through the neighbourhood faster than a bad cold at a village tea party, and the reactions were perfectly predictable: fusty old Doctor Portman turned purple with huffing rage, like someone had stolen his favourite port decanter, while wily old Major Pendennis just snickered behind his hand and went “oh, good lad, showing some spirit.” As for poor Helen Pendennis? She was this close to draping the whole household in black crepe, convinced her sweet baby boy was one step away from duelling a dozen men at dawn and landing himself in jail for murder. Every little scrap of drama tied to that actress Miss Fotheringay seemed to spit out fresh disaster, and she was more determined than ever to ship Pen off far from Chatteris, anywhere he couldn’t run into the hussy who’d turned his whole life upside down.

When Helen tearfully remonstrated with him and the Doctor yelled at him for his wild, duel-obsessed nonsense, Pen leaned into the whole “wronged gentleman” bit harder than a bad actor in a melodrama, all wide-eyed solemnity and teenaged earnestness. He insisted he’d done absolutely nothing to provoke the fight, that no man had the right to insult his honour and get away with it, then turned to his uncle with the most dignified little stare he could pull off, and demanded to know what else a proper gentleman could possibly do but challenge the lout to a fistfight (or a pistol duel, if we’re being honest about his dramatic daydreams).

“Vous allez trop vite, my good idiot,” the Major said, flustered, seeing as he’d spent the last six months filling the boy’s head with his own old army-era nonsense about honour and duels. “For fancy London men of the world, sure, go shoot each other over a snub if you want. But two overgrown schoolboys? This is ridiculous, my dear boy. Completely daft.”

“It’s wicked, and nothing like my sweet, gentle son,” Helen said, dabbing at her eyes with a handkerchief, bewildered by how stubbornly Pen was clinging to his silly honour code.

Pen gave her a very patronizing kiss on the forehead. “Women, dear mother, simply don’t understand these matters. I put myself entirely in Foker’s hands, there was no other possible honourable path I could take.”

The Major grinned and shrugged. Those two lads were turning out to be far more entertaining than he’d expected, he thought. Helen, meanwhile, declared Foker was a horrid little troublemaker who’d lead her boy straight to the gallows if they went to the same university, and threatened to cancel the whole university plan right then and there. She only held off because she remembered Pen’s late husband had always dreamed of sending their son to his old alma mater, and she couldn’t bear to break that last wish.

Everyone had already agreed Pen would head up for the October term, after all. Foker had promised to introduce him to all the right people, and the Major was dead set on that being a fantastic advantage for the boy. “Young Foker’s on familiar terms with all the very best peers’ sons at the university right now,” the Major blustered to Helen. “The eldest son of the Duke of Saint David’s is there, Lord Magnus Charters, Lord Runnymede’s boy – who’s Foker’s first cousin, by the by, Lady Runnymede was Lady Agatha Milton, you must remember her! Lady Agnes Foker will have you out to their country house Logwood in no time. Far from being worried about Pen being friends with Foker, who’s a bit odd and very funny but totally good-natured and prudent, I think it’s the luckiest thing that ever happened to him! He already bailed us out of that whole mess with the Fotheringay marriage, after all.”

Helen sighed, supposed the Major knew best. Foker had been very kind when Pen was making a fool of himself over that actress, that was true, but she couldn’t shake a bad, squirming premonition that more trouble was coming. All the brawls, the gossip, the fancy worldliness the Major kept gushing about scared the daylights out of her when she thought of her soft, romantic boy wandering off into it alone.

Doctor Portman was all for Pen going off to university too. He hoped the lad would buckle down and study, meet some nice, respectable friends, and at the very least get far away from all the Chatteris drama that was driving Helen half mad. Pen, who was half distracted from his broken heart by all the fights and fuss around him, just mumbled grumpily that he’d do as he was told.

August and September brought Chatteris’ annual assizes, races, and a flood of random out-of-town visitors traipsing through the local theatres, which meant Miss Fotheringay was dragging herself through her farewell performance run night after night. No one except our little circle of Pendennis associates seemed to care one bit she was leaving the stage forever – all the fancy county folks who’d sung her praises to the rafters in London when it was trendy to be her fan, looked at her performing on the rickety little Chatteris stage and went “eh, nothing special about that woman.” It’s a fate a thousand geniuses and a thousand frauds have suffered before and after her time. The actress herself didn’t care a whit, though; she went about eating her three square meals, acting her parts, and sleeping her eight hours a night with the unflappable calm of a person blessed with zero overactive sentiment. Turns out a healthy dose of mild, cheerful dullness is one of life’s greatest superpowers – it skips you right past 90% of the unnecessary grief, drama, and harmful overthinking that sentimental saps like Pen torture themselves over.

Pen wandered in and out of the Chatteris theatre as the mood took him that season, and his moping about over the actress drove his mother half wild with anxiety. She would’ve interfered a dozen times to ban him from going, if the Major hadn’t kept nudging her back and reassuring her the boy was already getting over it. The Major’s biggest clue that Pen’s heartbreak was fading? The kid was spouting terrible poetry everywhere. He’d yell lines of verse at the top of his lungs while stomping through the shrubbery, mutter Latin hexameters through dinner, and when the Major snooped through Pen’s study one day while he was out riding, he found a whole fat notebook full of sappy sonnets, complete with stuffy little footnotes quoting classical authors, just like they taught him at school. “He can’t be that badly heartbroken,” the Major thought, very wisely, and pointed out to Helen that Pen was coming home ravenous for dinner every night, and clearing two plates of breakfast every morning. “Gad, I wish I had that appetite,” the Major said, glumly thinking of the special digestion pills he had to take to force down half a chop. “Mark my words, the boy’s starting to sleep through the night, no more moping till three a.m. It’s cruel, but it’s true – he’s over that actress.”

Pen couldn’t vent all his silly sappy feelings to anyone else: if he told his mom about his broken heart she’d cry for three hours, the Major would tease him in that dry, worldly way that made him squirm, and Foker would just burp and offer him a cigar before changing the subject to oysters. So he latched onto poor Smirke the Curate, and decided Smirke was his Best Friend Forever. What’s a pining teen without a designated confidant to listen to all his moaning? Pen used Smirke like the old shepherd Corydon used his elm tree – he carved Miss Fotheringay’s name (metaphorically) into every conversation, filled Smirke’s ears with endless rants about his lost love. He thought he and Smirke were soulmates, because he could sigh all his griefs right into his tutor’s ears, and Smirke was only too happy to listen for his own secret reasons.

Pen’s gushy affection knew no bounds. He wrote odes to Smirke, full of Latin Sapphics and Alcaics, and gave them to him as gifts. He handed over his fancy bound favourite books, his gold watch chain, even tried to give Smirke his prized double-barrelled shotgun. He ran up to Chatteris and bought an expensive gold pencil case on credit (he still owed Smirke money for half the silly gifts he’d bought Miss Fotheringay, after all) engraved with a dramatic message about their eternal unbreakable friendship. The soft-hearted Smirke ate all the attention right up, he was delighted.

The poor Curate was absolutely devastated at the thought of Pen leaving for university. Once Arthur was gone, his whole reason for popping by Fairoaks every single day vanished. What excuse would he have, to hang around and get a little smile or kind word from Helen Pendennis, that was more precious to him than the cheap little dinner Madame Fribsby cooked up for him every night? No more “popping over to help Pen translate Homer” visits, no more weekly excuse to stop by, no more little Laura needing extra catechism lessons. He’d curled around Fairoaks like ivy on a garden wall, and the thought of getting ripped off made him ache. He debated dropping to one knee and proposing to the widow right that second, going over every little tiny nice thing she’d said to him in the last three months – she’d praised his sermon three weeks back, she’d thanked him profusely for the melon he brought over for her little dinner party, she’d said she’d always be grateful he was so kind to Arthur. But what if he proposed and she said no? He’d be banned from Fairoaks forever, and that little house was his whole entire world.

Which brings us to the universal truth, dear reader: every single person on this planet has their own private little soap opera going on, that they care about a thousand times more than anyone else’s drama. Helen Pendennis is fretting about her baby boy flying the nest for the big wide world, the Major is seething because he’s stuck in this hick village instead of hobnobbing with dukes and duchesses in London, Pen is torn between pining for his old actress crush and secretly dying of excitement to get out and see the world, and Smirke’s got his own little heartache following him around even on his pony rides. We’re all so lonely and secret, aren’t we? You and your spouse have shared the same bed for forty years and think you know each other – psha. Do you feel your partner’s gout pain? Does she lie awake tossing and turning when you have a toothache? Your sweet innocent teenage daughter, who seems to do nothing but practice piano and fawn over her mom? She’s thinking about that lieutenant she danced with at the last ball. Your honest schoolboy son is secretly tallying up the money you’ll give him next term, and all the debts he owes the village tart shop. The little old grandma dozing by the fire, who’s months from the grave? She’s not thinking about any of you – she’s daydreaming about the night she was 19, dancing a cotillon with that handsome captain before your grandpa ever asked her out. We’re all little isolated islands, each with our own whole secret universe under our own hats. But let’s get back to poor lonely Smirke.

Smirke only had one person he could talk to about his secret crush: that most unwise of gossips, his landlady Madame Fribsby. No one had any idea how she’d earned that fancy “Madame” title – she’d left Clavering as a bouncing teenaged seamstress, gone off to work for a milliner in London, popped over to Paris for a bit, and come back calling herself Madame, insisting she’d been awarded the title by the French government. No one was rude enough to point out the French king couldn’t just hand out random Madame titles to every seamstress who walked past his palace. She bought the late Mrs. Harbottle’s millinery shop for a bargain, brought her elderly deaf mother to live with her, was endlessly kind to the poor, went to church every Sunday, and had a perfect reputation. She also read every single cheap sentimental romance novel ever printed, no exceptions, to the point she was so absurdly soft-hearted she saw star-crossed lovers in every single pair of two single people who so much as said hello to each other.

The day after Helen had visited the Curate, Madame Fribsby had decided for certain that Smirke was head over heels in love with the widow, and set about meddling to push them together as hard as she could. Helen barely ever saw her, since she made all her own dresses and never bought fancy millinery, but every rare time Madame Fribsby got to visit Fairoaks, she’d gush over Smirke nonstop – how gentle he was, how studious, how dreadfully lonely, wondering out loud why no nice lady would take pity on him and marry him.

Helen laughed at her silly sentimental remarks, and teased her back: “Why don’t you take pity on him yourself, Madame?” Madame Fribsby would dramatically pat her chest, shake her fancy Madonna-style lace front wig, and spout broken French about how her own poor wounded heart belonged to a lost love in Spain, glancing up at a beat-up print of a French carbineer in a green coat and brass cuirass hanging on her wall. Helen didn’t care to dig into her weird romantic secrets, she thought of Smirke as nothing more than a respectable clergyman who was tutoring her son, no more. But Madame Fribsby was dead set that the widow was secretly pining right back.

Smirke, for his part, was more than happy to ramble on about his crush to his landlady. They’d sit in her parlour for hours, chattering away mostly in terrible French, so her deaf old mother couldn’t understand a word of what they were saying. When Major Pendennis dropped by to tell Smirke that Pen would be off to university in October, and his tutoring services wouldn’t be needed any more, the poor curate almost had a full-on panic attack. The critical moment had come – he either had to propose, or lose Fairoaks forever.

Meanwhile, the entire Clavering gossip mill was already churning out ridiculous stories about the Pendennis household. Madame Fribsby had been gossiping about Pen’s actress drama for months, the whole village of old maids and retired army officers who made up the town’s “genteel” population had heard every exaggerated detail a dozen times. There were rumours Pen had hanged himself in the Fairoaks orchard and his uncle had cut him down just in time, rumours Miss Fotheringay had dumped him, rumours the Pendennis family had paid her thousands and thousands of pounds to keep quiet about the whole affair. When Pen heard the rumours slandering his old crush’s honour, he raged like a tiny furious lion, wishing old Mrs. Pybus (who had more whiskers than most cavalrymen) was a man so he could challenge her to a duel. He glared so menacingly at the local vicar Simcoe one day from horseback that the man ran home and wrote a thundering sermon about Jezebels, bad theatre, and youth going straight to hell, that everyone in the congregation knew was a dig straight at Pen.

Pen got labelled a profligate, a brawler, and almost a murderer in the village, but that wasn’t the end of it. The gossip soon shifted over to Helen, and the new rumour that the widow was planning to marry Smirke the second Pen was safely shipped off to university. “It’s all settled!” Mrs. Pybus cackled to Mrs. Speers over tea. “He’s there every single day, anyone can see it!” The rumours spread fast, and soon even Doctor Portman heard all about it. The good rector flew into such a fit of rage he wanted to fire Smirke on the spot and kick him out of his parish. “That simpering milksop curate, thinking he’s good enough for a wonderful woman like Mrs. Pendennis?” he sputtered. “Where does impudence even end!”
“Besides, he’s far too young for her,” Mrs. Portman added. “Poor dear Helen is old enough to be his mother, for heaven’s sake!”

# CHAPTER XVII: Which Wraps Up The First Half Of Our History (With One Gaspingly Absurd Secret Reveal You Won’t See Coming)

It was a perfectly unremarkable sun-dappled afternoon at Fairoaks, the kind that makes idle local gossips decide to extend their walks for no good reason beyond getting to spill tea to a friend. Our mild-mannered curate Mr. Smirke was tucked away upstairs in Pen’s study, halfheartedly pretending to run through Latin verbs with his pupil, when Mrs. Portman, the Rector’s wife, finished her business chit-chat with Mrs. Pybus, glanced up at the cloudless sky, and decided a detour over to Fairoaks to visit her dear friend Mrs. Pendennis was non-negotiable. Over scones and tea, she dropped the juiciest piece of small-town gossip the parish had seen all quarter: quiet, meek Mr. Smirke had a two-year long secret attachment, and had been hiding his betrothal for ages.

“Wait, who on earth has he gone and fallen for?” Helen Pendennis asked, doing her best to look perfectly poised, while a little jolt of alarm zinged through her—all the local busybodies had been dropping hints lately, after all.

“My dear, when he first moved here we all thought we’d get him paired off with Myra,” Mrs. Portman laughed. “We even stopped inviting him to dinner for a bit because the whispers got so loud. Then people started saying he was smitten somewhere else, but I always shut that down immediately! I told everyone it was never you—”

“Me?” Helen squawked, scandalized. “The man was hired to tutor my Arthur! How dare anyone be so impertinent!”

“Upon my soul, that’s far too much,” Major Pendennis huffed, slamming down his newspaper and dropping his double monocle onto his lap like the gossip was a personal insult to his entire family’s dignity.

“I’ve no patience for that Mrs. Pybus spreading such nonsense,” Helen fumed.

“See? I told her that exact thing,” Mrs. Portman said, smugly. “Turns out our demure little curate has been engaged this whole time to a Miss Thompson, over on Clapham Common! I’m absolutely delighted, not least because unmarried curates wandering around your house giving your daughters piano lessons are always a recipe for awkwardness. I’m telling you this privately, obviously—don’t go breathing a word of it to anyone else, I don’t want to spoil the surprise.”

Helen was over the moon with relief, honestly. She gushed about how kind Mr. Smirke was, how he was such a good tutor for Arthur, and as soon as Mrs. Portman left, she and the Major gushed over the good news too. Helen even started kicking herself for how distant and haughty she’d been to Smirke lately, avoiding him around the house when he’d spent months running errands for her, copying music, teaching little Laura watercolors, and bringing Arthur extra books.

“Sure, be grateful,” the Major grumbled. “But don’t get familiar. He’s a paid tutor, not family. Rank has its lines, you know I think you’re far too soft on the fellow.” Helen disagreed wholeheartedly, though, and when Smirke trundled downstairs after his study session with Pen, hovering awkwardly by the hall to say goodbye, Helen walked over, blushing a little, shook his hand, and practically dragged him into the drawing room, inviting him to stay for their fancy dinner. Smirke, naturally, agreed so fast he nearly tripped over his own feet.

Free of all her old misgivings about Smirke pining after her, Helen was the picture of warmth and kindness to the poor curate all through dinner, piling his plate with roast beef and chatting to him nonstop—so much so that the Major decided to be as snooty and condescending to the tutor as humanly possible, talking to Smirke like he was a lowly footman who’d wandered into the dining room by mistake. Even Pen, who was still nursing a broken heart and fully convinced he was the most tragic young genius in all of England, snickered at his uncle’s performative snobbery.

Smirke couldn’t have cared less about the Major’s rudeness, though. He was too busy soaking up Helen’s attention, gabbing happily about the fancy bazaar, the latest missionary meeting, the bishop’s new sermon, and the gossipy bits about London high society he’d read in the newspaper that morning, pulling out every trick a well-meaning sentimental cleric could think of to charm the lady he adored. The Major yawned so wide his jaw ached as soon as Helen and little Laura left the dining room, groaning “That man is the most unbearable bore alive, how can he talk so much?”

“I’m very fond of him, Arthur adores him,” Helen said, innocently. “I wonder what Miss Thompson of Clapham is like, anyway?”

“Eh, I always thought he was pining after someone much closer to home,” the Major said.

“Towards Myra Portman?” Helen asked, confused.

“Towards you, you goose,” the Major said. Helen blushed bright pink, acting like that was the most ridiculous thing she’d ever heard, even though she’d suspected it for months, and rambled on about how happy she was for Smirke, and how she hoped Arthur wouldn’t drink too much wine with him.

Unfortunately, Arthur was already way ahead of her. Delighted he’d been given free run of the cellar for the night, he dragged out three bottles of his late father’s famous imported claret, and he and Smirke were passing the glasses around faster than you could say nunc vino pellite curas. Within half an hour the first two bottles were all but empty, Pen was sloshed enough to declare that wine was the only lover who never cheated a man, and was reciting the overwrought sad love poetry he’d written that morning, all about how his actress ex was a heartless deceiver who’d thrown away the love of a great future poet. His voice got all wobbly and emotional, his grey eyes glistened, and Smirke, ever the sentimental soft touch, grabbed Pen’s hand across the plate of half-eaten apricots and declared him a genius whose name would go down in history.

“Promise me you’ll remember your old tutor when you’re famous, Arthur!” he gushed, tears in his eyes behind his spectacles. Pen swore he would, slapping Smirke on the back and declaring him his best friend in the whole world, before yelling for a third bottle of claret, bragging that his uncle had seen Sheridan drink five bottles of the stuff plus a whole bottle of maraschino once, and breaking into a sloshed rendition of the drinking song from *Der Freischütz*. Helen, pacing on the lawn outside with Laura to watch the sunset, heard his voice and smiled, thinking her boy was finally cheering up after his broken heart.

“Go on, old man,” Pen sloshed, pouring Smirke another full glass. “Let’s toast your secret lady! Your Clapham Miss Thompson, right? Hip hip hurray, here’s to Mrs. Smirke!”

Smirke gulped the wine down, trembling all over. “Oh Arthur,” he gasped, “you have no idea—she’s not Miss Thompson! She’s not Miss Portman! She’s not anyone silly you’ve ever heard of! For two whole years, my whole heart has belonged to one perfect, angelic woman—your mother. I love Helen Pendennis! I’d settle every penny I own on her, and even leave something for you, Arthur, when I die!”

Pen went from three-bottle drunk to completely stone-cold sober faster than you could blink. He jumped to his feet, stared at Smirke like the curate had grown a second head, and yelled “My mother? Good God man, you’re mad! She’s seven years older than you!”

Smirke winced, because that was exactly the jab Pen had coming—Pen had spent months pining after an actress ten years his senior, after all. But Pen wasn’t having it. “That’s different! I’m her son! You’re my tutor! That’s the most absurd, overstepping bit of cheek I’ve ever heard!” He rang the bell for coffee, and when the old butler showed up staring at the three empty claret bottles, Pen coolly told the staff to bring Smirke’s pony around to the door. The poor curate, red-faced and mortified, trundled out to the stables alone, Pen trailing behind him to help tighten his saddle girths.

“Hey, I’m sorry I snapped,” Pen mumbled, a little guilty, as Smirke climbed onto his pony. “You’ve always been great to me. But this can’t happen, mate. Be well. God bless.” Smirke nodded silently, and trotted off down the lane, his shoulders hunched in defeat. Helen was waiting for Pen on the lawn, frowning a little because Smirke had left without even having tea, and Pen just laughed, put his arm around her waist, and walked her around the lawn for a bit, lighter than he’d felt in months. Later that night, when Laura was in bed, he spilled the whole ridiculous story to Helen and the Major. The Major huffed “Confounded impudence! Where do these lowly tutors get the gall to court wealthy widows?” and Helen blushed so hard her cheeks were pink for an hour. The two of them talked late into the night, laughing so hard their sides hurt, and Pen slept like a log for the first time since his breakup.

Over on the other side of Chatteris, meanwhile, the great theater impresario Mr. Dolphin had wrapped up his business with the Costigan household. He’d signed Miss Fotheringay to a fabulous new London stage contract, and left her a nice lump sum to pay off all their tiny little local debts. Captain Costigan, who’d previously been banned from the chaotic local Buccaneers’ drinking club for not paying his bar tabs, suddenly was the toast of the town—he paid off every last farthing he owed, bought all the neighborhood Creeds children endless gingerbread and apples, and spent through the entire leftover cash in three days flat, forcing him to beg Dolphin for a little extra travel money before they left for London.

The Buccaneers, a crew of local apothecaries, portrait painters, auctioneers, and editors who met every Saturday to get riotously drunk and toast everything under the sun, threw Costigan a farewell dinner at The Grapes inn, eighteen gentlemen sitting down to a massive spread of roast beef and plum pudding. They drank endless bowls of punch, sang drinking songs, and when it was time for the guest of honor to speak, Costigan got to his feet and rambled through a twenty minute speech sloshed full of dramatic flourishes, name dropping dukes he’d definitely never dined with, bragging about his days at the siege of Flushing, and weeping about how he’d never forget the fine, loyal gentlemen of Chatteris. The whole room cheered so loud the windows rattled, and not a single soul there called him out for any of his obvious lies. The first half of our little history ends on that perfectly silly, warm, tipsy note.

Let’s be real: even the guy whose entire university career consisted of failing three exams, hiding from the bursar, and getting chased by campus security for climbing the chapel roof will get all misty-eyed thinking back on his old college chums. Back then, you’d just been cut loose from your mum’s apron strings: no bills, no boss breathing down your neck, no doctor telling you you can’t drink three pints for breakfast, and you were absolutely convinced you were the cleverest, most charming human being to ever walk a university quad. That first sip of freedom? Sparkling, bubbly, so sweet it’ll make your teeth hurt—none of that stale, bitter leftover swill you’re sipping now when you’re 40, complaining your knees hurt every time you climb a flight of stairs. Even old curmudgeons who can only eat a poached egg and a glass of water for dinner love watching kids with good appetites tuck in, right? It’s way more fun to watch a pantomime when you’re not the one falling asleep in the third act, after all.

Not long back, I popped over to visit Oxbridge, the old university where our mate Arthur Pendennis spent a few years misbehaving, and ended up sharing a train carriage with a current Saint Boniface student who’d conned his way into a day pass to London. That lad talked nonstop the entire journey—jokes, loud cackles, wild plans for how he was going to cram 12 different pleasures into his 24 hours off—so much so that the ride felt 20 minutes long, and I was gutted when we pulled into the terminus. He hopped straight into a hansom cab the second we pulled up, grinning like an idiot, ready to hurl himself headfirst into whatever mischief London had to offer. Me? I’d only brought a tiny carpet bag, so I hopped up on the top of the omnibus, squished between a Jewish peddler smoking a cigar that smelled like wet hay and a valet who was doting on a very snooty poodle, and we trundled off at a snail’s pace, zero hurry to get to that smoky, noisy Babylon of London town. Neither of us was champing at the bit to dance at the casino or show off at our club that night. Give that overexcited kid 10 years, and he’ll be right there with us, happy to take the slow bus home.

When Arthur Pendennis first headed off to Oxbridge, there weren’t any trains around yet, so he went by fancy stagecoach, crammed full of stuffy dons, current students, nervous new freshmen, and their very overprotective guardians. One fat old city bloke in grey stockings, sitting inside next to Major Pendennis, was white as a sheet when he heard that the bloke who’d driven the last two stages was Harry Foker, a Saint Boniface legend who was mates with every coachman in the county and could drive a four-in-hand better than most professional drivers. Pen was perched right up on the coach roof, staring at every tree, cottage, and passenger like it was the most fascinating thing he’d ever seen. When those spiky old university towers, tall elms, and shiny river hove into view? His little heart was doing somersaults.

Before they left London, Pen spent a few days crashing at his uncle Major Pendennis’s Bury Street lodgings. The Major decided Pen’s wardrobe was well overdue for a glow-up, and Arthur certainly wasn’t going to argue with new waistcoats and fancy coats. The self-sacrificing uncle spared no expense for the lad, too—though London was dead quiet that time of year: all the rich folks were out of town, the Pall Mall pavements were practically empty, you couldn’t spot a single red-coated club man loitering by the windows. The Major dragged Pen around a couple of these ghostly club buildings, added his name to the candidate list, and Pen was so chuffed he could barely contain himself. When he saw his name written down in the fancy parchment book: “Arthur Pendennis, Esquire, Fairoaks Lodge, [shire], Saint Boniface College, Oxbridge; proposed by Major Pendennis, seconded by Viscount Colchicum”, he felt like he’d already won the lottery. “You’ll get your membership in three years, right when you finish your degree,” the Major told him, and Pen was already mentally redecorating the club’s stucco halls and vast libraries like they were his personal property. The Major snickered to himself watching the lad strut out of the building like he owned the whole street.

One day, Pen and his mate Foker hopped in Foker’s fancy little cab and drove over to their old school Grey Friars, to say hi to their old school friends. The boys crowded around the cab gate, gawking at Foker’s chestnut horse, and his tiny footman Stoopid, who was dressed up in full fancy livery, looking very serious. Just as they were hanging around the playground chatting, the afternoon school bell rang, and the terrifying headmaster walked past carrying his Latin grammar. Foker immediately ducked behind a bush like a kid avoiding detention, but Pen marched right up, blushing, and shook the old dignitary’s hand, laughing at the memory of that very same grammar smacking him over the ears half a dozen times. He was generous, good-natured, and 100% convinced he was the coolest bloke alive.

Then they popped over to the Foker family brewery, a huge pile of brickwork not far from Grey Friars, whose name was gilded on every pub sign within 20 miles. The brewery’s manager gave the pair of them silver flagons of Foker’s famous extra stout, so strong that not just the two lads, but Foker’s horse seemed to get a buzz off it—he zoomed back to the West End so fast he nearly took out three pie stalls and a bunch of old ladies crossing the road, smacked the cab steps off a street corner post, and made poor Stoopid swing around on his little perch behind like he was on a rollercoaster.

The Major was dead chuffed that Pen was mates with Foker the brewery heir, listening to all his silly stories like they were state secrets, buying the two lads a massive dinner at a Covent Garden coffee house and taking them to the play after. But he was over the moon when Mr and Lady Agnes Foker, who were in London at the time, invited the Pendennis pair over for dinner in Grosvenor Street. “Now that we’ve got an invite into Lady Agnes’s house,” the Major told Pen, deadly serious like he was sharing top secret military intelligence, “you can never let that slip. Whenever you’re in London, you make sure you call by there. Go read up Debrett’s on the Rosherville family tree, alright? Drop a little clever, complimentary reference to them sometime—you’ve got that poetic fancy, you can pull it off easy. Old Foker is a good bloke, even if he’s not from posh stock or well-educated, and he’ll make sure you get offered the family special porter after dinner. Don’t you dare turn it down. I hate beer, it disagrees with me something terrible, but I’ll drink it too, just to keep in good graces.” And the old martyr did exactly that, sitting there grinning through old Foker’s 100-year-old joke about Foker’s Entire stout that he told every single dinner guest.

Lady Agnes, who was 100% wrapped up in her precious son Harry, was the friendliest but silliest woman alive, and she fussed over Pen like he was a long-lost nephew, rambling on for an hour straight about how hard Harry was studying, and how she was terrified it’d ruin his poor dear health. Old Foker cackled so loud at that line the wine glasses rattled, and Harry winked very obviously at Pen behind his mum’s back. She went on for ages: Harry’s near-death from measles, the time he almost drowned, that horrible mean headmaster at Grey Friars who she’d never, ever forgive, no matter what. By the time she finished her rant, she declared the two Pendennises the most charming men she’d ever met, and invited them both up to their country house at Christmas, or whenever Harry was home for vacation.

Later that night back at Bury Street, as they were lighting their candles to go to bed, the Major clapped Pen on the shoulder. “That little reference you made to the Rosherville bloke who fought at Agincourt? Perfectly done, even if Lady Agnes had no clue what you were on about. Very impressive for a first timer—though stop blushing like a schoolgirl, won’t you? Promise me, Arthur: if you’ve got the right introductions, it’s just as easy to hang around good company as bad, no extra work at all. Don’t go making rubbish friends at Oxbridge, alright? First steps in life are the most important. Did you write to your mum today? No? Go do it before we leave, and ask Foker for a free postmark—they love that sort of thing. Good night, God bless you.”

Pen wrote a hilarious, rambling letter back home to his mum Helen at Fairoaks, going on about the play, the old school visit, the brewery, the dinner at the Fokers’, and Helen and little Laura read that letter over and over a dozen times, cooing over every line, the way mums and their surrogate daughters do. It was Pen’s very first big step out into the world, and boy, was that path full of potholes. Fellow travelers, hope you’ve got someone to catch you when you trip, that mercy’s there for you when you mess up, and love’s lighting your way the whole time—you’d be completely blind without it.

Soon enough, the coach pulled up to the Trencher, that lovely old inn on Oxbridge’s main street, and Pen’s eyes went wide: he spotted students wandering around in their gowns, chapel bells clanging nonstop from dawn to evensong, old towers peeking over the gabled roofs of the busy little town. They’d already sorted Pen’s enrolment with Dr Portman back home, and Mr Buck, his Saint Boniface tutor. Once the Major had fussed over his cravat to make sure he looked impressive, the pair walked down Main Street past Saint George’s grand gate and belfry, until they reached Saint Boniface’s own ivy-covered old gate. It had a little half-buried dome on top, a statue of Saint Boniface, and carvings of all the old royal benefactors’ coats of arms.

The porter pointed out the wonky old tower where Mr Buck’s rooms were, and they crossed the quad—Pen’s eyes drinking in every detail: the little fountain splashing in the middle of the grass, the tall chapel windows, the great hall with its fancy lantern window, the master’s lodge where the head of the college would soon emerge swishing around in his silk robes. The Major, who’d spent 40 years staring at Pall Mall pavements, didn’t care about any of the pretty old architecture, of course.

Big, fancy Saint George’s is the Oxbridge college everyone knows, with four massive quads and grounds so grand all its students strut around like they’re better than every other person on the planet. Tiny little Saint Boniface is basically a cosy little hermitage next to it, but it’s got an excellent reputation: posh families from half the counties send their lads there, the jobs you get after graduation are amazing, the fellows barely have to do any work, the college boat comes third on the river, the choir is almost as good as Saint George’s, and the Boniface ale is the best in the whole of Oxbridge. The walls of the wainscoted great hall are covered in portraits of old famous alumni: martyred scholars, archbishops, chief justices, dukes, poets, old friends of Dr Johnson, and the college cook is one of the best chefs in the whole university, his son even took top honours at the rival Camford University. The wine in the fellows’ common room is legendary, too.

So Pen, hanging on his uncle’s arm, trotted into Mr Buck’s rooms, where the tutor was waiting for them. Dr Portman had already written to Buck gushing about Pen, saying he was from a super old landed family, clever, and bound to make the college proud, so Buck was as nice as pie to them. He invited the Major to eat dinner in hall with Pen, to see the lad wear his gown for the first time, then asked them both to come back to his rooms for wine after dinner, and even offered Pen the best set of student rooms in the whole college, which had just happened to become free. The pair left Buck’s study chuffed, the Major declaring the tutor the politest man alive—because when a college don decides to be nice to you, they lay on the charm so thick you could spread it on toast, all grand and stately in their fancy robes.

In Buck’s antechamber, they found a tailor waiting with a whole bag of gowns and caps for Pen to try on. Pen was practically bouncing with excitement as the tailor fitted the gown, then plonked his new cap on at a jaunty angle, just like his old Grey Friars favourite teacher Fiddicombe used to wear it. He stared at himself in the big gilt mirror, preening like a peacock, and the Major snickered—he wasn’t mad at how handsome the lad looked.

Then Davis, the college servant, led them across the quad to Pen’s new rooms, which had just been vacated by a bloke called Spicer. They were cosy as anything, with big old wooden beams, tall wainscoting, and little windows set deep in the walls. Spicer was selling all his furniture, so the Major bought most of the basics for Pen, though they both drew the line at his collection of six terrible sporting prints and four posters of half-nude opera dancers.

They went off to hall after that, where Pen sat with all the other new freshmen eating their dinner, and the Major sat up at the high table with the dons and all the other visiting guardians. After dinner they had wine back at Buck’s, then trundled off to chapel, where the Major sat very properly up in the back, watching the Master, the learned Dr Donne, sit on his fancy carved throne under the organ loft, looking like a very pious marble statue. All the new freshmen were on their best behaviour, but Pen was horrified to see that little terror Foker rock up late, with a group of his mates, all giggling and whispering like they were sat in the opera stalls instead of chapel. To be fair, this was back when William IV was king, young lads are far better behaved these days, and Saint Boniface was always a bit of a party college.

Pen couldn’t sleep a wink that night back at the Trencher inn, he was so excited to move into his new rooms. He tossed and turned thinking of a hundred things: his mum back home at Fairoaks, definitely. That old ridiculous infatuation he’d had with the actress Miss Fotheringay, too—he blushed bright red in the dark thinking about how besotted he’d been, how he’d almost ended up with old drunk Costigan as a father-in-law, and laughed out loud at how stupid that was. “Poor old Smirke,” he mumbled to himself, “I’ll write him a letter, cheer the old sod up. He’s not going to die of a broken heart, ha!”

The Major might have heard a dozen of those silly little mutters through the wall that night. Admittedly, it would have been nicer if the lad had spent his first night thinking nicer, more wholesome thoughts, but his old selfish uncle had already gotten him all wrapped up in the ways of the world. Pen was a good lad, generous and frank, but vain, impetuous, a little bit selfish, and very easily distracted by whatever fun was in front of him. That whole six months of ridiculous heartbreak had aged him way more than a few months, and he was already convinced he was far more experienced and clever than all the other 18-year-old freshmen around him.

The next morning, he and the Major went out and bought all the extra bits and bobs he needed for his rooms, since old Spicer’s china was all chipped, his lamps were smashed, and his bookshelves were way too small to fit all the boxes Helen had packed back at Fairoaks. When those boxes arrived a few days later, Pen’s heart softened seeing his mum’s familiar handwriting on the labels, and he carefully unpacked all his old books, the linen Helen had picked out of the family store, and even all the little jam pots that Laura had wrapped up in straw for him.

Pen had a whole new Alma Mater now. Not every student, though, gets on with their new university mum half as easily as he did.

Our boy Pen could not have been more relieved when his chaperone, Major Pendennis, waved him off for the London coach two days after they arrived at Oxbridge. The Major, for his part, was even more over the moon to be done with his three-month long nephew-sitting gig.

Think about it: three whole months of his precious, carefully curated selfish bachelor time, wasted on keeping a teenage boy out of trouble! Has any self-absorbed Major ever made so grand a sacrifice? Show me ten men you know who’d give up their easy chair, their dinner parties, their grouse shoots and gossiping sessions over post-prandial port to chaperone a snotty teen, and I’ll eat my top hat. We’ll all happily lay down our lives for honour, but handing over our daily comforts? That’s a trial almost no modern man can survive. I won’t speak ill of ladies, of course—they’ve been trained from day one to put other people first, God love ’em—but most of us gents will have a pretty sad looking credit line for self-sacrifice on our final heavenly balance sheet. All that to say: I fully side with the Major on this one. He earned his holiday, and he bolted straight from London to Cheltenham, then bounced between the fanciest nearby country houses that still had good shooting and even better dinner guests, no boring nephew required.

Foker and Pen saw him off on the coach, and Foker even made the driver promise to take extra special care of the old gent tucked in the back. The Major was chuffed to bits that Pen already had a friend like Foker to introduce him to the absolute best, most fashionable university set.

Now, we’ve already dragged out one little stretch of Pen’s life for roughly a quarter of the word count most three-volume serial novels reserve, so rest assured we’re not going to document every single boring minute of his university career from here on out. For one thing, most teenage lads’ daily lives are far too messy to print. Be honest, reader—would you want all your dumb college hijinks written up for strangers to gawk at? I thought not. As long as our honour’s still intact, we’re all happy enough to keep the rest buried. The world’s full of imperfect men, after all—go count the spotless saints in your own acquaintance, I’ll wait. I’ve got a huge social circle and I can’t find ten, so don’t pretend you can.

Pen started his first term fairly keen, turning up to classics and math lectures on the regular… until he very quickly realized he hated exact sciences with every fibre of his being. What’s more, a handful of scruffy lads who didn’t even own trouser straps to cover their thick, cheap work boots and holey stockings were absolutely destroying him in problem sets, and his ego could not take that public humiliation. He stopped showing up to math lectures entirely, and wrote to his mum to announce he was dedicating himself exclusively to fancy Greek and Roman poetry from here on out.

Mrs. Pendennis was over the moon. Her darling boy should chase whatever learning made him happy! The only thing she begged was that he didn’t stay up too late studying and give himself a brain fever, like all those poor tragic student stories she’d heard about that died halfway through their degrees. Pen promised her he wouldn’t burn the midnight oil, and shockingly, he actually kept that promise far better than he kept most of his other casual pledges to her.

Pretty soon, Pen realized the classics lectures were even more of a dud than the math ones. The other students were too slow, the tutor Mr. Buck was less sharp than a fifth-former at Pen’s old Grey Friars school, and the man had about as much understanding of the actual poetry in Aeschylus as Pen’s bed-maker Mrs. Binge. Pen could read and understand a whole passage of a play in ten minutes, while the rest of the class stumbled over the lines for an hour, and he got so bored he bailed on those lectures too. Private self-study was the only way to learn properly, he wrote to his mum, and she nodded along happily—she knew about as much Homer as she did algebra, so she had zero doubts her brilliant boy would top every exam he sat.

Pen didn’t come home for Christmas, which disappointed his mum and little Laura to bits, who’d been begging him to help build a giant snow fort like he’d done three winters before. But he’d been invited to Lady Agnes Foker’s estate at Logwood, where they were doing private theatricals and hosting a huge fancy Christmas party full of the exact high-society people the Major had insisted he befriend. He finally turned up home for the last three weeks of the vacation, loaded down with new fancy clothes, and Laura noticed he’d picked up a very grown-up, decisive swagger that hadn’t been there before.

He skipped coming home for Easter too, and when he showed up for the long summer vacation, he had even more fancy gear: bright shooting jackets with ridiculous giant buttons, velvet waistcoats for dinner, embroidered silk cravats, fancy imported linen, and a stunning silver-mounted dressing case full of little rings and trinkets. He’d dumped his late dad’s giant, heavy old fob-watch, the one his mum had given him before college with a whole solemn speech about respecting his father’s memory and using time wisely, calling it “about as useful as a warming pan with a clock face” and shoving it in a drawer full of out-of-fashion primrose gloves and discarded old cravats. Laura thought it was horribly disrespectful, and cried for three days when Pen swapped his old gentle horse Rebecca out for a faster, fancier new model that cost a fortune.

He also turned up with a huge box of fancy imported cigars with ridiculous names like Colorados and Telescopios, and started smoking them everywhere—even the conservatory, where the fumes were doing terrible things to his mum’s rosebushes. He told her he was working on a prize poem, and couldn’t possibly compose without a cigar in hand, quoting Lord Byron’s old lines about how smoking was mandatory for great writers. The soft old soul caved immediately, and one day when she found him “hard at work” on the sofa (actually reading a racy new popular novel, for “light literature research”) she didn’t even disturb him—she went to his bedroom, fetched his cigar case and a box of matches, walked back, stuck the cigar in his mouth, and lit it for him. Pen laughed, kissed her hand, and joked “Dear old mum, if I told you to burn the house down, I do believe you’d do it.” And he was probably right.

On top of his English light novels, he brought back stacks of racy French books from Oxbridge, and the first time Helen flipped through one by Paul de Kock her eyes nearly popped out of her head. Pen insisted this was all critical language study, just as important as reading Moliere or Swift, and Helen sighed and let him keep them—though both her and Pen, the self-appointed moral authority, strictly banned Laura from ever touching the stack. A young lady’s education didn’t require that sort of thing, obviously.

Over that long vacation, Pen drank through every last bottle of his dad’s old carefully laid-in claret, the same collection he’d once bragged didn’t have a single headache in the whole hogshead. Once it was gone, he wrote off to the London wine merchants old Doctor Portman had recommended before he went to college, ordering a huge new shipment of port and sherry. The rector had warned him that Oxbridge local wine sellers charged a fortune for terrible plonk, and told him to stock up from a respectable London firm if he wanted to host wine parties for his mates. The bill that arrived at Fairoaks made poor Helen nearly faint, but Pen laughed it off, said it was totally normal, every gentleman drank claret and champagne these days, and Helen paid up. She and Laura could pinch pennies and eat simple meals while Pen was back at college, after all—why spoil his few precious weeks at home?

Pen’s allowance was absurdly generous, far higher than the allowances of most lads whose families were twice as rich as the Pendennises. Years before he died, Pen’s dad had started putting aside cash for what he called the A.E.F., or Arthur’s Education Fund, and Helen had added to it every year after he passed. Major Pendennis had insisted he give Pen a huge, fat allowance: no stinting a boy, he argued. If Pen entered university living like a gentleman, he’d make all the right friends and pick up all the right gentlemanly habits, and be perfectly prepared to fight his way through the real world later. It was an excellent argument, all the better for the fact the Major didn’t have to pay a single penny of it.

So Pen waltzed around Oxbridge looking like he was the heir to a huge aristocratic estate, with his easy swagger, free-spending ways, good singing voice, and habit of buying rounds for all his mates. He rode well to hounds, turned up to meets in full pink hunting gear, and while no one of his hobbies was outrageously expensive on its own, he did all of them. He collected rare books with fancy gilded bindings, bought fancy Rembrandt etchings and Wilkie prints to decorate his rooms, wore ridiculous amounts of jewellery, and once people swore he even wore rings over his kid gloves. (He still denies this, but we all did stupid stuff when we were 19.) He took perfumed baths, he claimed, only to wash off the stench of the awful working-class lads he’d had the misfortune to sit next to in dining hall.

In his second year, when Miss Fotheringay was the biggest star in London and prints of her were sold on every street corner, Pen hung a giant framed print of her over his desk, and regaled all his mates with the tragic, dramatic story of how madly, passionately he’d once loved her. He showed them the terrible emo love poems he’d written for her, heaved big dramatic sighs and rolled his eyes recalling his broken heart, and the boys ate it all up. Nothing makes you more popular at university than having a reputation for a grand, tragic romantic past. Soon freshmen were pointing him out on the street, whispering that he’d almost killed himself over the actress, that he’d fought a duel with a baronet for her honour. He’d swagger around campus at 2pm surrounded by his crew, tipping his hat to the Proctors like they were rival heads of state, and bragging to his mates about how ugly the tourist ladies gawking at the university were. People were proud to be seen walking with Pendennis of Boniface, like they were strolling down Pall Mall with a duke.

He was the undisputed campus trendsetter. The second he bought a crimson satin cravat, two dozen lads turned up to lectures the next week wearing the exact same one. The local Jewish jeweler sold two full gross of the little fancy pin Pen picked out one afternoon, all to copycat undergrads.

If you sat down and did the math, you’d realize that Pen dabbled in every single expensive hobby that existed at the time—unlike his rich mates, who were all one-note: Lord Paddington only cared about mirrors and fancy dinners, Guttlebury hated riding and didn’t care what he wore, Snaffle never read anything except the Racing Calendar. Pen collected rare books, bought fancy art, rode horses, hosted dinner parties, bought fancy clothes, smoked imported cigars, and threw huge wine parties for his mates. By the end of his second year, his stack of unpaid bills was so tall you could use it as a side table, and Pen hadn’t even bothered to do the math yet. He was far too busy being the coolest man on Oxbridge campus to worry about something as boring as money.

A little before our man Foker hightailed it out of Oxbridge, a new bloke turned up at Boniface College by the name of Horace Bloundell. Word leaked pretty quick he’d gotten the boot from rival university Camford after a very heated falling-out with the tutors and campus authorities, and the undergrad wags immediately dubbed him the heir to the mythical Bloundell-Bloundell estate of Bloundell-Bloundell Hall, Bloundell-Bloundellshire. The only reason Boniface took him on, after half a dozen other colleges had slammed their doors in his face? The Master, Dr. Donne, was a Suffolk local, vaguely related to the Bloundells, and Bloundell had a very cozy family church living coming his way if he just scraped through his degree. He’d previously been in a dragoon regiment, all crimson pantaloons and tall fuzzy shakos, until his third brother (the guy who was supposed to take that church job) conveniently dropped dead. Bloundell promptly traded his spurs for a clerical black coat and starched white neckcloth, and wasn’t about to let a silly little ban from Camford derail his very comfortable life plan.

By Pen’s second year at Oxbridge, Major Pendennis came to visit his nephew for a long weekend, and got introduced to Pen’s whole crew: soft-spoken, nice-as-pie Lord Plinlimmon, gallant open-hearted Magnus Charters, snarky quote-a-minute Harland, reckless Ringwood (nicknamed Rupert in the Union Debating Club for his absolutely unapologetic terrible takes and bold, unbacked arguments), and Barebones Broadbent, the firebrand republican Bristol dissenter whose debate rants could make even the most apathetic audience cheer so hard they spilled their port. Last but far from least was Horace Bloundell-Bloundell, who’d already muscled his way right into the top tier of the university’s cool kids club.

The Major might not have understood half of Harland’s Greek references, or approved of Broadbent’s scuffed thick-soled shoes and perpetually ink-stained hands, but he thought Pen’s friends were a delight—all except for the one guy who strutted around like he owned every inch of the quad, acting like he was the most worldly, well-traveled man within 100 miles of the campus.

Over breakfast the morning after the raucous dinner Pen had hosted for the crew, the Major was in excellent spirits, handing out hot takes left and right. He’d trotted out all his best old club anecdotes the night before—stories that were so stale in London that the waiters at White’s could recite them backwards for tips—but they were brand new to the Oxbridge lads, who’d laughed so loud they’d woken up the skip on the next staircase, and hung on every word like they were gospel. Only Bloundell had rolled his eyes every few minutes, making it very obvious he’d heard every single one of those stories a hundred times before. At one point he even had the gall to correct a tiny detail of the Major’s anecdote, saying “actually old boy, I heard the whole story at the club from two blokes who were there, and that part didn’t happen like that at all.” The lads all stared at him like he’d grown a second head, shocked anyone would dare interrupt the charming old major. Bloundell later went around the common room sneering that “old Pendennis is a total bore, eats like a trencherman at Gaunt House, and is the most fuddy-duddy old fogy I’ve met in years.”

The Major, naturally, took an instant dislike to Bloundell. This sort of thing never fails to be mutual, right? You don’t even need someone to tell you they’ve been calling you a bore; you just smell that Dr. Fell vibe the second they walk in the room, and you know you hate each other before anyone says a word.

“Pen my boy, your dinner went off perfectly,” the Major said, chipping the top off his boiled egg that morning. “You carved the roast beautifully—glad you learned that trick, everyone’s doing carving at the sideboard now but it’s still a handy skill to have later in life. Young Plinlimmon’s a total sweetheart, the spitting image of his mother Lady Aquila Brownbill, who I knew back in the day. Broadbent’s got a great set of lungs and a head full of books, and Foker’s always an absolute delight. But that new mate of yours Bloundell? Total bad news. Not eligible for your friendship in the slightest.”

“Wait, sir, Bloundell-Bloundell?” Pen laughed, confused. “He’s the most popular guy in the whole university! We called an emergency special meeting of the Barmecides drinking society just to vote him in the week he arrived! He’s from super old Suffolk gentry, descended straight from Richard the Lionheart’s Blondel! His family coat of arms has a harp on it! Motto’s O Mong Roy!”

“Fancy coat of arms doesn’t stop a bloke from being a total scammer, my lad,” the Major said, popping a bit of egg in his mouth. “Mark my words, that man is a tiger. I’ll lay you any wager you want he got kicked out of that dragoon regiment (and his old C.O. Lord Martingale is one of the most decent men I’ve ever met) for something so ungentlemanly the whole regiment still won’t talk about it. Look at that stack of cheap rings he wears, the reek of low billiard halls and third-rate gambling clubs oozes off him. He’s got ‘scamp’ written all over his face in big neon letters. Avoid him like he’s got the plague. Also, the dinner was a little fancier than strictly necessary, but I don’t object to you splashing out a little for friends, as long as you don’t make a habit of it and stay within your allowance. The cutlets were excellent, the soufflé so light it practically floated off the plate, though that third bottle of champagne was completely unnecessary.”

Poor Pen! His well-meaning uncle had no idea these fancy dinners were happening every other night, because Pen was dead set on acting like a seasoned gourmand and man of the world, the sort of guy who could rattle off vintage dates and critique a salmi like he’d been born with a napkin tucked under his chin. The college cook absolutely worshipped him, and by the start of his third year at Oxbridge, Pen’s staircase was no longer full of waiters carrying silver platters and popping iced champagne. Instead, it was full of scowling tradesmen, debt collectors, and laundresses lingering by his outer oak door waiting to corner him the second he left his rooms.

Naturally, Pen completely ignored his uncle’s warning about Bloundell. What 20-something boy doesn’t think the guy who swears he’s seen every wild party in Paris and pulled off a dozen rakes’ tricks is the coolest person alive? Half the public school boys at Oxbridge had a tattered copy of *Don Juan* hidden under their mattresses they’d read a dozen times, and they all ate Bloundell’s “I’ve seen the world” schtick right up.

It only took one of these walking bad influences to tank the whole college vibe. The Boniface tutors were tearing their hair out within months of Bloundell’s arrival—suddenly their formerly mild-mannered undergrads were skipping chapel, sneaking out at night, and being rude to the dons. The cool kids over at St. George’s College weren’t fooled though: Broadbent had dubbed him Captain Macheath, and swore he’d end up hanged one day. Cautious old Foker wouldn’t say a bad word about him out loud, but pulled Pen aside to mutter “Look, if you have to play cards with him, make him your partner, don’t play against him. That guy cheats at every game going, he’s got the sleight of hand down pat. And don’t take any weird odds from him, yeah? I swear the man hasn’t had spare cash in his pocket for three months.”

But Pen was too headstrong to listen. He was hell-bent on “seeing life,” quoting Terence and Horace out of context to justify skipping tutorials to party, convinced he’d turn into a fully-fledged legendary roué by the time he turned 22 if he kept up the pace.

One night after a supper party in his rooms, when everyone was getting ready to head home after a low-stakes game of vingt-et-un, Bloundell playfully grabbed an empty green wine glass off the table, pulled a pair of dice out of his waistcoat pocket, and waved the glass around like he’d done it a thousand times before. He started belting the chorus from the hit opera *Robert the Devil*, the rest of the lads joined in, and before anyone knew it they were all huddled around the table rattling dice for a game of hazard, the green glass getting passed from hand to hand until Pen smashed it after throwing six winning mains in a row.

After that night, Pen was addicted. Bloundell even rigged up a special felt-lined dice box so the rattle wouldn’t carry out the window and tip off the sharp-eared tutors. They almost got caught once when tutor Mr. Buck was wandering through the quad and heard someone yell “Two to one on the caster!” through Pen’s open window. When he burst in to catch them, the lads had three open copies of Homer spread out on the table, and Pen dead-faced asked him “Sir, quick, do you happen to know if the River Scamander is still navigable? We’re trying to work it out for our tutorial.” The confused tutor left without a single penalty.

Bloundell had a million other wild made-up stories to entertain Pen, too. He ranted about his wild days in garrison, his trips to the Paris casinos, the night he stumbled back to his rooms blackout drunk after a dinner at the Café de Paris and woke up with 12,000 francs stuffed under his pillow and 149 Napoleons tucked into his boot. Pen’s eyes practically sparkled hearing that—he thought Bloundell was the luckiest, most badass man alive.

When Pen told him all about his old grand passion for the actress Miss Fotheringay, Bloundell laughed his head off, and proceeded to slander her so thoroughly Pen believed every made-up story he spun: that she had no reputation left at all, that twenty different rich men had spent hundreds of pounds on her, that Pen was a total idiot for ever wanting to marry her. Sweet, trusting Pen never once doubted the word of the man who never had a good thing to say about any woman, and was left mortified, convinced his great grand love had all been a stupid fool’s delusion.

One Easter, Pen lied straight to his mother and uncle’s face, saying he was staying back at Oxbridge to cram for his exams. Instead he snuck off to London with Bloundell, who had a running “tick” (read: unpaid tab) at a dodgy Covent Garden hotel. They hung out at Bloundell’s old military club, dined with a bunch of spurred, mustachioed ex-dragoons, drank pale ale for breakfast and hit every theatre and singing den in the city. They very nearly ran straight into Major Pendennis on Pall Mall once, and the closest call of all was at the Museum Theatre, where Pen and Bloundell were in the stalls box, separated from the Major (who was in Lord Steyne’s private box) by nothing but a single thin plank of wood.

Pen watched Miss Fotheringay perform that night, and all the magic was gone. He could hear her thick Irish brogue under the fake stage accent, all her sweeping dramatic gestures were the exact same rehearsed schtick he’d seen a hundred times before, and he cringed thinking that once he’d thought that loud overacting was pure genius. He bailed on going backstage to see her, and went off to watch Taglioni dance and *Don Giovanni* at the Opera instead. They went out for a roaring, raucous supper after, Pen woke up with a splitting headache the next morning, and trundled back to Oxbridge completely skint.

He was soon so deep in debt he could barely keep track of how much he owed. Even the money lender Bloundell introduced him to, the infamous Finch, only charged him a 100% interest rate, which was basically a kindness by London usurer standards. The college cook who adored Pen even offered to send him free dinners for as long as he needed them, no pressure to pay the bill. For all his foolishness, Pen was impossibly likable: he’d leave the raunchiest party mid-rager to go sit with a sick friend, he’d split his last guinea with any mate who was broke, and no one could stay mad at him for long even as he careened straight towards total ruin.

Any halfwit who’s ever flipped through a peerage guide or a random weird heraldry meme page knows that the noble family Helen Pendennis hailed from has a very specific, very dramatic crest: a full-grown mama pelican, chest all pricked open and oozing, feeding a whole nest of squawking, greedy baby pelicans who peck at her like they’re ordering 2 a.m. Uber Eats. Turns out that wacky medieval legend isn’t nearly as made up as you’d think. Us men will never wrap our heads around it, but women absolutely love pouring every last drop of their energy, cash, and peace of mind on the chaos gremlins they love.
