Doesn’t matter if it’s a deadbeat husband, a son who can’t stop racking up bar tabs, or a brother who lost the rent money at the races: they’ll have the fatted calf on the table before you even finish texting that you’re on your way home, no questions asked. If we only had cold, unforgiving justice to look forward to, half of us would be sleeping in a ditch right now, let’s be real.

Towards the end of Pen’s time at Oxbridge University, his stuffy old bachelor uncle the Major had gone full stage mom over the lad. He was absurdly proud of Pen’s high spirits, easy charm, good looks, and posh little aristocratic mannerisms, and loved bragging that Pen ran with all the most promising young lords at the university. This was the same guy who was so notoriously stingy that his club friends made up a whole bunch of running jokes about him never buying a round, but he suddenly started hosting cozy little private dinners for Pen and his noble pals, plying them with top-shelf claret, and showing off all his very best (and some very not appropriate) old club stories that landed way better with his dapper old-guy delivery than they would have from anyone else’s. He schmoozed the young lords’ parents through the boys, popped down to Oxbridge half a dozen times to let the students host parties in his honour (half the time just to mess with him, half the time because he was actually kind of a fun old dude), and even showed up to cheer at Pen’s big Union debate, thumping his cane so loud and whooping so hard he nearly broke the thing, then came away 100% convinced his nephew was the next William Pitt, destined to run the country.

He wrote Pen playful little letters full of London gossip and cheeky advice, bragged about him nonstop at his clubs, and even finagled invites for Pen to the fancy Gaunt House fête, gushing so hard about the boy’s brilliant oratory skills and guaranteed top-tier exam results that he might as well have been a mom carting her 12-year-old to a child beauty pageant. He swore up and down Pen would get taken on for a fancy embassy post under Lord Runnymede, or get hand-picked by some duke to represent one of his pocket boroughs, and he sent every last one of these delusional hot takes straight to Helen, who ate every word up because her Arthur could never do any wrong.

All that pride? Wiped to ash, because Pen had been an idiot who spent all his time carousing instead of studying math. He flunked his exams. Properly plucked. The guy who was supposed to be a rising political star had blown his big shot before it even started, drained his mom’s bank account dry like a total chump, and now the shame hit him twice as hard: he knew he’d screwed over the sweetest, most forgiving person on the planet, and his overinflated ego was absolutely shattered at the thought everyone was laughing at him. He was so convinced every single person on campus — his angry tutor, his bill collectors, his bedmaker, even the freshmen he’d once looked down on — would point and whisper about his failure, he locked himself in his rooms, scribbled a dramatic note to his tutor full of remorse and over-the-top despair begging to have his name struck off the college books, implying death would probably come for him any day now, then snuck out the back of the college down the least used alleyways, rain soaking his ragged old academic gown, until he wandered down to the empty banks of the Cam, usually packed with cheering students at boat races, and kept walking until he was miles outside the city.

That’s when his old acquaintance Mr. Spavin — the guy they’d known back at Baymouth, who’d gotten plucked not once, not twice, but three whole times before finally scraping a degree — came rattling past in his gaudy bright yellow postchaise, puffing on a cigar and yelling at his postboy to go faster. Spavin spotted Pen’s wild, pale face bobbing up the hill, screamed for the driver to stop, leaned halfway out the window, and waved at Pen until he trundled over.

“Whoa, old boy!” Spavin yelled, grabbing his hand. “What’s the big crisis? Don’t mope just ‘cause you got plucked! It’s no big deal once you get used to it — I’ve been plucked three times, and after the first one I barely even cared. You’ll nail it next time, easy.”

Pen stared at him, dumbfounded. This guy who’d failed three times, who could barely read and write properly when he first showed up to uni, had passed, and he, the great would-be statesman, had not? It was almost too much for his ego to handle. Spavin wouldn’t let him wander off alone into the rain, dragged him into his yellow chaise, lent him a spare coat and hat, and dumped him at the Ram Inn in Mudford, 15 miles out of Oxbridge, just in time for Pen to grab a spot on the London mail coach.

Back at Boniface College, the drama got so thick you could spread it on toast. Some walkers found Pen’s beat-up old cap a few feet from a mill stream three miles down the Fenbury road, his name half-visible stitched on the inside, plus his family seal crest engraved on a little charm nearby, and for 24 whole hours everyone was convinced Pen had drowned himself in despair. His tutor was panicking, all the tradesmen he owed money to were seething that their deadbeat customer had supposedly bailed by dying, until a letter postmarked London showed up and everyone exhaled in relief.

Pen rolled into London at 5 a.m., bleary, unshaven, still wearing yesterday’s shirt, 100% convinced every random stranger he passed had seen the exam results and was laughing at him. Even the porter at the Covent Garden inn where he usually stayed, even the maid scrubbing steps outside his uncle’s Bury Street lodgings, even his uncle’s valet Morgan — Pen was sure every single one of them knew he’d flunked.

The Major popped out of his bedroom seconds later, freshly wigged, perfumed to the nines from Delcroix’s fancy shop, and chirped, “Well done, future Senior Wrangler! Double First Class, eh? I’ll be right out!” When he finally got a good look at Pen’s ghastly face, his cheery tone dropped so fast it was comical. “Good God, Pen — what’s the matter?”

Pen mumbled that his name wasn’t on the exam lists. The Major, who’d never heard of failing uni, assumed Pen had been a coward in a duel or something and squawked “Lost your honour? You didn’t show the white feather, did you?” Pen had to hold back a hysterical bitter laugh and explain he’d gotten plucked. The Major had a very weird very specific misunderstanding at first, thinking “plucking” was some barbaric public corporal punishment the college doled out to bad students, and it took Pen a minute of spluttering to clarify no, he’d just ignored his math textbooks for three years and flunked the exam.

Then Pen spilled about the 700 pounds in random stupid debts: tailor bills, horse hire, fancy shirts and gloves, prints, jewellery, stacks of unread books, absurdly expensive college desserts, hair products, hotel tabs, you name it. The Major blew a gasket. He ranted that Pen was going to ruin his poor mother before he turned 25, stormed off to his club without even finishing breakfast, and spent the whole day badgering every one of his old fogey friends at the club to ask what you do with a nephew who flunks uni and owes 700 quid. Every single one of them just shrugged and went “Dude, half the rich kids at Oxbridge owe way more than that. Just pay it.” Slightly comforted that he wasn’t the only relative stuck with a uni idiot, the Major decided to punt the hard part of breaking the news to Helen to Doctor Portman, because old Victorian guys love making other people deliver bad news instead of doing it themselves.

Pen spent the rest of the day skulking around London, hiding from groups of loud Oxbridge boys, wandering past Ackermann’s print shop in the drizzling rain, reading half a tattered novel at a secondhand stall near the Temple, and going to a theatre pit show that night he was so distracted he couldn’t even remember the name of the play the next day.

Two days later, a kind letter arrived from his tutor, telling him no one hated him, begging him not to drop out, and that all he needed was a month of cramming math to nail the resit — along with Pen’s full trunks, all packed, every single one of his unpaid bills neatly stacked on the very top.

On the third day, the letter from home arrived. Pen read it, burst into tears, prayed his heart out with his head buried in his bedclothes, ate a breakfast so huge he could barely move, then trundled off to the Bull and Mouth in Piccadilly to grab his spot on the evening Chatteris coach, finally ready to head home and face his very forgiving mom.

When that overblown, drama-laden letter from Major Pendennis landed on Dr. Portman’s doormat, the old rector grabbed his hat and coat faster than a terrier after a stray sausage. Any decent clergyman who’s got bad news to deliver moves like a dentist who’s just spotted a perfect extraction spot: he’s sorry you’re in pain, he’s sympathetic, but by gum that tooth’s coming out right now, and he’s not going to dally around getting cold feet. It’s always your tooth, of course—if it was his own he’d be hemming and hawing for three weeks minimum.

Portman first read the letter out loud to his wife and daughter, cussing Pen as a “young scapegrace slipping straight down the ladder to the workhouse” so loud the parlormaid could hear him from the pantry. He left the two Portman ladies to spread the juicy gossip across Clavering, a task they executed with the speed and accuracy of two well-trained town criers with a personal grudge against fun, before he stomped over to Fairoaks to break the bad news to Pen’s widowed mother Helen.

Turns out Helen already had the news from Pen himself. And far from fainting dead away or wailing into her handkerchief, she breathed a sigh of relief. She’d had a dark, nameless dread hanging over her for months, and now the worst was out: her darling boy was coming home, repentant, soft-hearted, and all hers again. What more could she want? All of Portman’s perfectly sensible, historically vetted rants about Pen flunking his degree and racking up debts bounced right off her. “What’s the big fuss about that silly old degree anyway?” she said, tilting her head. “What good was it ever going to do him? You and the Major are the ones who insisted he run around with all those rich, wild young people full of temptation, why didn’t you just leave him home with me? And his debts? That’s his father’s money, it’s all his, he’s every right to spend it.” Portman left that conversation so confused he nearly tripped over the garden gate on his way out.

See, for years Pen and his adopted little sister Laura had run around the house tackling each other, stealing cookies, and greeting each other with messy, casual kid kisses the second they were in the same room. When Pen came home from college that winter, though? The scrawny little 12-year-old he’d left behind was now a tall, slim, 16-year-old beauty standing five foot four, who curtsied politely instead of launching herself at him, blushed so hard her cheeks turned the exact shade of the spot he’d used to smooch as a kid, and held out a hand instead of leaning in for a hug.

I’m no fancy beauty critic, and frankly I think decency beats pretty face any day, but let’s lay it out straight: half the catty local ladies called her a lanky maypole and complained her feet were too big just because she wore long dresses, but that “maypole” had roses twined all over its head, and every single unattached swain within three miles of Clavering was tripping over his own boots trying to ask her to dances. She was usually pale with a faint rose flush to her cheeks, but when she got flustered? She blushed so hard that pink stayed there for an hour after the excitement was long gone. She’d had giant, bright eyes since she was a toddler, and the local gossips insisted she was winking and flirting with every person in the room on purpose. Nonsense—she was just born with eyes that shine brighter than a tavern lantern, and eyelashes so long and dark they looked like little silk veils laid over her lids, the poor thing couldn’t help it. Her mouth was a little on the large side, if you asked the prissy Miss Piminy who could barely swallow a breadcrumb, but her smile showed off a perfect set of pearly teeth, and her voice was so soft and sweet it sounded like you were listening to a half-drunken street musician playing a nice tune under your window. She was just a good, modest, sweet young thing, the kind that makes you think England might not be entirely full of idiots after all.

Laura hadn’t been happy with Pen’s college antics for ages. His letters home got shorter, rarer, full of vague excuses about “important engagements” and he kept skipping vacations at Fairoaks to party at rich friends’ fancy country houses. Helen kept making excuses for him, fumbling through lines about “networking” and “the Major’s good advice,” but Laura rolled her eyes. “It’s better he lose a stupid prize than forget he has a mother who loves him more than any duke ever will,” she’d huff. She was properly furious when she heard about that awful Fotheringay actress nonsense, too—Major Pendennis had let it slip on a visit, and the Clavering gossips filled in all the juiciest bits. A Pendennis throwing himself at a hussy of an actress, gallivanting home every other day to kneel at her feet and drink ale with her drunk old dad? He was going to bring that lot back to Fairoaks and make Helen wait on the lot of them? “I’d run away barefoot through snow before I let that happen!” Laura declared, before Helen sighed and said “You’d leave me too, then?” and the two of them dissolved in a sobbing hug that lasted five minutes.

But the second Laura heard Pen was sad, humiliated, and coming home? All that anger vanished faster than a plate of cakes at a church social. He was her old Pen again, the sweet, generous little boy she’d adored as a kid, and she immediately turned on Dr. Portman the second he started ranting about Pen’s “enormous sins.” “Debts? Those are nothing! The Major made him hang around all those fancy rich boys, he had no choice but to keep up! He was sick for the exams! The tutors were jealous of him, they picked their own pets to pass instead of him, it’s obvious!” She grabbed Helen’s hand, glared daggers at Portman, and looked like she was two seconds away from telling the old man to get off their property if he said one more bad word about Pen.

Portman left completely bewildered, muttering about stubborn women the whole walk home. The two of them immediately ordered fires lit in Pen’s old rooms, his bedding hung out to air, and Laura added a postscript to Helen’s gushy love letter to Pen that said *COME HOME AT ONCE, DEAREST PEN* with three thick, dramatic underlines, and signed it “your affectionate sister Laura.”

That night, long after their Bibles were put away and they’d snuck a quick peek at Pen’s empty room on their way to bed, Laura sat up in the dark and announced she had a genius plan. “You’ve never charged me for the last ten years living here, right? That’s at least fifty pounds a year I owe you. That’s five hundred quid sitting in Mr. Rowdy’s bank in Chatteris, and we’re going to go get it tomorrow, pay off every last one of Pen’s debts, and none of us has to worry about a single thing ever again.” Helen’s response was just a lot of incoherent sobbing, hugs, and kisses that nearly knocked over the little pincushion on her dresser that had a lock of Pen’s baby hair sewn into it.

Pen was so determined to be thrifty that January that he refused an inside seat on the London coach, and rode up top with the guard, who remembered Pen slipping him an extra shilling last year and lent him three thick old greatcoats to keep the frost off. His knees were shaking when he got off at the lodge gates, partly from the cold, partly from panic that his mom would be mad at him. Old John the servant didn’t even wear the blue and drab livery no more—he was full-time gardener and stable hand now, and he grinned and blushed as he grabbed Pen’s bag. Before Pen could even make it up the drive, Helen came barrelling around the shrubbery, face glowing so bright you could have lit a candle off it, already forgiving him before he even said a single sorry word.

Laura was waiting in the hall, and for the first time in years, she skipped the formal curtsy, ran right up to him, and gave him a big hug and a kiss right on the cheek. They didn’t make a big dramatic fuss over the “fatted calf” bit, but they cooked all his favorite meals for a week, and nobody mentioned the flunked exam or the debts, not for ages. Pen sold his horse and handed the money over to Helen, who thought that was the most virtuous act any young man had ever pulled off—Laura just sniffed and said it was the absolute least he owed. He gave up wine, cut back on smoking, went to church every Sunday with his mom, and avoided Dr. Portman like the plague, who kept glowering at him from under his big shovel hat every time they passed each other on the road.

After a few weeks of moping around the house, Laura went up to him, blushing so hard her ears went pink, and told him he was being a coward if he didn’t go back to Oxbridge, do the exam again, and get his proper degree. Pen grumbled, but he went, and passed the second exam so easy he barely had to study. He popped up to London to visit his uncle afterwards, and the old snob barely even gave him a single cold finger to shake, and the second time Pen called his valet lied straight to his face that the Major was out of town.

Back to Fairoaks he went, moping harder than ever. He wrote three terrible gloomy tragedies, a whole stack of angsty poetry, made a dozen study plans he abandoned after three days, daydreamed about enlisting in the army, joining the Spanish Legion, running off to sea—anything to get out of the doldrums. Helen kept saying soon they’d go abroad, soon he’d move to London, get a proper career, get away from the boring quiet country house with just two old women. But Helen’s own face was getting paler and more tired every month, and Laura watched her grow more and more languid, and started to feel a quiet, cold worry settle in her stomach that none of them were going to get the happy ending they’d all daydreamed about.

The residents of Fairoaks were dozing their way through the dullest possible humdrum routine, while the great manor perched on the hill across the River Brawl finally shook off the two generations of deep slumber it had been stuck in, and started making enough noise to wake the very rooks nesting in its chimneys.

Right around the time Arthur “Pen” Pendennis was moping over his own tiny personal mishap, so wrapped up in his own suffering he failed to notice anything happening to people less important than himself, a little announcement popped up in the provincial papers that sent the entire county’s gossip network into a tailspin. From Clavering Market to Cackleby Fair, at Chatteris court sessions, on Gooseberry Green where the local squire and vicar would stop their respective carriages mid-road just to chat for 45 minutes, even at Tinkleton Church gate as folks trundled in for Sunday service in their white smocks and scarlet cloaks, everyone was saying the same thing: Clavering Park was finally going to have people living in it again.

Five years prior, the county papers had run a blurb about Francis Clavering, only son of Sir Francis Clavering the baronet, marrying Jemima Augusta, the widowed daughter of Calcutta’s very own Samuel Snell, at the British Legation in Florence. The county rumor mill immediately decreed that Clavering, who’d been flat broke for decades, had landed himself a rich Indian widow. A handful of locals spotted the newlyweds gallivanting about Europe: the Kickleburys ran into them at their rented Florentine palace, throwing fancy parties, though never once daring to come back to England. A year later, young Peregrine of Cackleby stumbled on them staying at Schloss Schinkenstein on the Mummel See. For years, whispers about them would blow back to Clavering’s ancestral home from random gambling dens and bath towns across France, Germany, and Italy, like stray confetti someone had accidentally mailed across the continent.

Last anyone heard, they’d set up shop in Paris, living it up in maximum splendor right after news arrived that Samuel Snell of Calcutta had kicked the bucket, leaving his only daughter a fortune big enough to make every indebted English aristocrat drool.

Old Sir Francis Clavering, meanwhile, didn’t have a single good anecdote attached to his name. The son of an outlaw hiding out in a moldy old chateau near Bruges, he’d tried and failed to make a decent go of it as a dragoon: he gambled away every penny he owned before two years were up, was forced to sell his commission, did a stint in the Fleet prison, then hightailed it to Ostend to move in with his gouty exiled dad. For years after that, you could spot the sad, washed-up would-be prodigal loitering in billiard halls, hanging around watering hole casinos, dancing at boarding house balls, and entering steeplechases on horses that belonged to other people.

It was at a Lausanne boarding house that he pulled off what he called the best lucky strike of his life: marrying the very newly widowed Mrs. Amory, fresh off the boat from Calcutta. His dad died not long after, and the second his wife got to call herself Lady Clavering, old Snell in Calcutta was so overjoyed by the fancy title he doubled her allowance. He popped his own clogs shortly after, leaving her a fortune so big the gossips kept inflating it by ten thousand pounds every single time they retold the story.

Before all that, the posh expat English community abroad had side-eyed Lady Clavering something fierce. Her manners weren’t exactly salon-ready, her origins were notoriously low, and the retired East India crowd in every continental English resort town loved to sneer at her dad, the disreputable old indigo smuggler and shyster lawyer, and her first husband, the ship’s mate who’d captained the vessel she’d taken out to Calcutta to join her father. Nobody in Calcutta high society knew them, they’d never set foot in Government House, and old Sir Jasper Rogers, former Chief Justice of Calcutta, once told his wife he knew a “real doozy of a story” about her first husband that he absolutely refused to spill, no matter how much his wife and daughters begged.

But once Lady Clavering was installed at Paris’s Hotel Bouilli, throwing parties and lighting up the social scene in 183-, every last stuffy moral English lady in the city came crawling to her guest list. The Faubourg St. Germain embraced her, the British ambassador paid her special attention, even the famously rigid, morally priggish Lady Elderbury, Lady Rockminster, and the venerable Countess of Southdown — women renowned for turning away anyone with even a hint of a scandal — all queued up to call on her. Turns out a fortune of ten thousand a year (some gossips swore it was twenty) does wonders for your reputation, no questions asked.

Her generosity was endless, too. Any charity within walking distance of her Paris home got a handout: she gave cash to the Armenian patriarch, a priest from Mount Athos begging for monastery funds, the Baptist missionaries off to convert the cannibals on Quashyboo island, and on the very same day she gave five Napoleons to a French nun supporting persecuted Jesuits, she wrote a check to the Reverend J. Ramshorn, who’d had a divine vision ordering him to convert the Pope. On top of all that, she threw the best dinners, balls, and late-night suppers in the entire city that season.

Somewhere in all that fun, the good-natured lady must have sorted out her husband’s mountain of English debts, because Sir Francis popped back up in London without a single arrest warrant hanging over his head, got his name printed in the Morning Post and the county papers as a resident of Mivart’s Hotel, and one fine afternoon, the elderly housekeeper at long-shuttered Clavering House watched a carriage drawn by four horses roll up the overgrown long drive, and stop right in front of the moss-covered, gloomy front steps.

Three men were riding in the open carriage. On the back seat sat our old acquaintance Mr. Tatham the Chatteris lawyer, while up front were a portly, handsome man dripping with mustache, side-whiskers, fur collars, and gold braid, and next to him a pale, listless fellow who climbed out of the carriage slowly once the other two had hopped out nimbly.

They trundled up the overgrown steps, and a foreign servant with gold earrings and a gold-laced cap yanked the mansion’s giant front bell so hard the clang echoed through every dark empty room. Seconds later, scuffling footsteps sounded on the marble entryway tiles, the great doors creaked open, and out trotted Mrs. Blenkinsop the housekeeper, her assistant Polly, and Smart the gamekeeper, all bowing so low their necks ached.

Smart tugged at the wisp of hay-colored hair on his sunburnt forehead, kicked out a leg like he was warding off a nipping farm dog, and bowed so far his nose almost touched his boots. Mrs. Blenkinsop dropped a deep curtsey, little Polly did three rapid-fire bows on top of her own curtsey, and the old housekeeper choked out tearfully, “Welcome home to Clavering, Sir Francis! It does my old eyes good to see one of the family back at last.”

She was talking straight to the dashing, fur-clad man who had his hat cocked rakishly to one side and was twirling his giant mustache like a royal. He burst out cackling immediately. “Wrong bloke, old lady! I’m not the lord of the manor come back to revisit his ancestral halls. Subjects and vassals — meet your actual master!”

He pointed at the pale, languid man, who sighed and said, “Don’t be an ass, Ned.”

“Right, Mrs. Blenkinsop, it’s me, Sir Francis,” the baronet said, taking the old lady’s trembling hand. “I remember you perfectly, though I expect you’ve forgotten me. How do you do?” He nodded at her in a surprisingly kind, if vacant, way.

Mrs. Blenkinsop swore on her life she’d recognize him anywhere — he was the spitting image of his father Sir Francis, and his grandfather Sir John before him, back to the days of yore.

“Right, yes, thank you, very much obliged,” Sir Francis mumbled, staring blankly around the giant entry hall. “Gloomy old dump, isn’t it, Ned? I only saw this place once before, when my old man had a screaming fight with grandad back in 1823.”

“Gloomy? It’s glorious! It’s Castle of Otranto, it’s The Mysteries of Udolpho, by Jove!” the man called Ned yelped. “Look at that fireplace! You could roast an entire elephant in there. That carved gallery? That’s Inigo Jones work, I’d put five to two on it.”

“The top half is Inigo Jones, the bottom was redone by Dutch architect Vanderputty under George the First, for the fourth baronet Sir Richard,” the housekeeper corrected him primly.

“Oh right,” Sir Francis yawned. “Gad, Ned, you know absolutely everything.”

“I know a few things, Frank,” Ned grinned. “I know that painting over the mantel definitely isn’t a Snyders — three to one it’s a copy. A little coat of varnish, mate, and this whole place will shine brighter than any manor in the shire. That old bloke in the red gown there is Sir Richard, yeah?”

“County sheriff, sat in Parliament under Queen Anne,” the housekeeper said, shocked he knew so much. “The portrait to his right is Theodosia, second baronet’s wife, painted by Lely as Venus, with her son Gregory the third baronet next to her as Cupid with his bow and arrows. The next panel is Sir Rupert, knighted by Charles the First and had all his property seized by Oliver Cromwell.”

“Thanks, no need to go on, Mrs. Blenkinsop,” Sir Francis said. “We’ll wander around ourselves. Frosch, hand me a cigar. You want one, Mr. Tatham?”

Little Mr. Tatham tried the cigar the courier handed him, and immediately started spluttering so hard he nearly choked. “You don’t need to follow us,” Sir Francis told the housekeeper. “Smart, get the horses fed and watered. We won’t be long. Come on, Strong — I know the way, I was here once in 1823 right before grandad croaked.” And so Sir Francis and Captain Edward Strong, for that was the grand friend’s name, wandered off into the empty reception rooms, leaving a confused Mrs. Blenkinsop to scuttle back to her tiny lodgings — the only heated, habitable rooms in the whole miles-wide mansion.

The place was so enormous no normal tenant could ever afford to run it, and the two men trundled through room after room, gaping at their vast, dusty, deserted grandeur. To the right of the entry hall were the saloons and drawing rooms, to the left the oak parlor, the grand dining room, and the library where Pen had snuck in to read books as a kid. A gallery ran around three sides of the hall leading off to dozens of stately, forgotten bedrooms, and the top floor was a warren of tiny drafty garrets built for servants back when the house was first constructed — a sobering reminder that our ancestors were perfectly happy sleeping in gilded canopies while their staff froze and breathed moldy air in attics worse than most modern stables.

While Sir Francis drifted along looking half-asleep and utterly indifferent to owning the giant manor, Captain Strong was buzzing with so much excitement you’d have thought he was the owner, and Sir Francis the random guest. “This place has so much potential, sir, huge potential!” he whooped. “Leave it to me, we’ll make it the pride of the whole shire for peanuts. We can turn the library into a private theater, hang curtains between the columns to divide the space! That grand ballroom? We can fit the entire county in there for a galop. Hang the morning parlor with that tapestry you’ve got stored from the Rue de Grenelle apartment, deck out the oak room with medieval cabinets and armor — armor looks chef’s kiss against black oak — and I saw a perfect Venice glass vase on the Quai Voltaire that’ll fit that high mantelpiece to an inch. The long saloon? White and crimson, obviously. Yellow satin for the main drawing room, pale blue with lace trim for the little one.”

“I remember my old dad caning me in that little blue room,” Sir Francis said glumly. “He always hated me, the old sod.”

“Chintz will do for the lady’s suite of rooms on the south landing — bedroom, sitting room, dressing room, we’ll stick a conservatory out over the balcony. Where d’you want your own rooms?”

“Stick me in the north wing,” Sir Francis yawned. “As far away from Miss Amory’s bloody piano as humanly possible. I can’t stand the noise, the girl screeches from dawn till midnight.”

The captain cackled, and mapped out every last renovation for the rest of their walk. Once they’d finished wandering the house, they popped back to the steward’s room where Mr. Tatham was poring over the estate plans, and Mrs. Blenkinsop had laid out a little spread of snacks for the new lord and master. After that they checked the kitchens and stables, which Sir Francis actually cared about, and Captain Strong was all set to go inspect the gardens — until the baronet said “to hell with the gardens and all that rubbish,” and they all climbed back into the carriage and drove off as casually as they’d arrived. That night the whole of Clavering found out Sir Francis Clavering had visited his ancestral park, and was moving back to the county for good.

When the news hit Chatteris, every last soul in the town lost their minds. High church and low church folk, half-pay captains, gossipy old maids, sporty small squires, farmers, shopkeepers, factory workers — everyone had an opinion. The news made its way to Fairoaks, and Pen and the two ladies living there got a little giddy over it. “Mrs. Pybus says there’s a very pretty girl in the new family, Arthur,” Laura teased, as only women who love teasing boys can. “Miss Amory, Lady Clavering’s daughter from her first marriage. I’m sure you’ll fall head over heels in love with her the second she steps off the carriage.”

Helen huffed. “Don’t talk nonsense, Laura.” Pen laughed right back. “Oh yeah? Then the little four-year-old heir of Sir Francis is all yours, I suppose.”

“Sir Francis the kid is four,” Laura said. “But I’m saving my attention for that handsome officer, Sir Francis’s friend. I saw him in the Clavering pew at church last Sunday, his mustache was absolutely magnificent.”

Pretty soon every single detail of the new household was common knowledge around town. On summer evenings, the drive leading up to the park was full of locals trundling up to gawk at the renovations going on. Van after van of furniture rolled up from Chatteris and London, and Captain Glanders the local retired soldier knew exactly what was in every last one, and personally escorted them up the drive to the manor house.

He and Captain Ned Strong were already thick as thieves. Strong had rented the very same quiet lodgings the meek Mr. Smirke had lived in before, and he was fast on his way to being everyone’s favorite person in town. He was broad-shouldered, jolly, blue-eyed, dark-whiskered, a little plump around the waist in the most charming way possible, and when he sauntered down Clavering High Street with his hat cocked to one side, cane tapping along the pavement, laughing loud enough to be heard down the whole high street, he was as welcome as sunshine after three weeks of rain.

The very first market day, he knew every pretty girl at the stalls by name, joked with every farmer about their sheep and cows, and showed up to the Agricultural Ordinary dinner at the Clavering Arms where he made every last farmer snort beer out their nose laughing at his stories. “That there Ned Strong is a proper top-notch gent,” every last farmer in top boots agreed. He shook hands with a dozen of them as they rode out of the inn yard on their old nags, waving his hat from the inn doorway while he smoked his cigar. By the end of the night, he knew exactly what the innkeeper paid in rent, how many acres he farmed, how much malt he put in his strong beer, and whether he snuck unexcised brandy in from the fishing villages down the coast.

He’d tried living at the big manor house first, but it was so dead quiet he couldn’t stand it. “I’m a creature born for society, mate,” he told Captain Glanders. “Frank’s got no energy, none at all, he’s not built for running this whole estate. But I need company. Old Mrs. Blenkinsop goes to bed at seven sharp and takes her assistant Polly with her. The first two nights I spent there it was just me and the house ghost. I like company, what can I say? Most old soldiers do.”

When Glanders asked him where he’d served, Strong curled his mustache and laughed, said Glanders might as well ask where he hadn’t served. “I started as a Hungarian Uhlan cadet, quit after a fight with my commanding officer when the Greek war of independence broke out, was one of seven men who escaped Missolonghi, got blown up on one of Botzaris’s fireships when I was seventeen. I’ve got my Cross of the Redeemer back at my lodgings, come over tonight for a glass of grog and I’ll show you. I’ve got the White Eagle of Poland too — Skrzynecki gave it to me on the field at Ostrolenka, I was a lieutenant in the fourth regiment and we charged straight through Diebitsch’s entire line without firing a single shot. Got this scar at the king’s side outside Oporto, served in Spain with the king’s troops until my dear friend Zumalacarreguy died, and I realized the war was lost. Now you know Ned Strong, Chevalier Strong to all my mates abroad, the whole story.”

Within a month, every last person in Clavering knew Ned Strong’s whole epic fictionalized backstory. He told Madame Fribsby his landlady, he told the George Inn landlord,

Every house has a skeleton tucked somewhere in its closets, and it’s a great comfort for miserable folks to learn that even their richest, sunniest-seeming neighbours have secret annoyances that make their lives a living drag. You’d never guess that Blanche Amory – the sweet-voiced, poetry-spouting, soft-smiling little “muse” everyone cooed over – was the exact skeleton of Clavering House, the bane of every single person who lived under its roof. It’s just like that tiny little pebble in your shoe that makes a 10-mile hike absolute torture: one stupid little obstacle, no bigger than a thimble, can ruin every single minute of your day. Who on earth would have guessed that a dainty, grinning little fairy like Blanche could turn an entire household against each other?

“Oi, Strong,” Sir Francis Clavering slurred one evening, leaning over the billiards table post-dinner, both men puffing on cigars – the world’s most reliable secret-spilling devices – “I swear to God, I wish your wife would drop dead already.”
“Mate, so do I,” Strong replied, sinking a cannon shot with a grin. “But she won’t. She’s gonna outlive all of us, mark my words. Why d’you want her six feet under, Frank?”
“Because then you could marry Missy,” Sir Francis drawled. “She’s not bad looking, and she’s got ten thousand quid coming to her – that’s a pretty penny for a sad old bloke like you.” He took a long drag and scowled. “And Strong, I swear I hate her more every single day. I can’t stand the little madam. Not one bit.”
“Not for twice that money, I’d marry her,” Strong cackled. “I’ve never met such a tiny demon in all my days.”
“I’d bloody poison her if I thought I could get away with it,” the Baronet said darkly. “No joke.”
“What’s she done this time, then?” his friend asked.
“Nothing new, just the same old rubbish. That girl’s got a superpower for making everyone around her miserable, it’s honestly impressive. Last night she made the governess run off crying straight from the dinner table. Then I passed little Frank’s bedroom, heard the poor kid wailing in the dark, turns out his sister spent half an hour scaring the life out of him with made-up ghost stories about the house. At lunch she gave my wife a right turn, too – and my lady’s a bit daft, but she’s a good sort, I won’t hear a word against her.”
“What’d Missy do to her?” Strong asked.
“Got out one of those silly keepsake books, pointed at a random portrait and said it looked exactly like her precious dead dad,” Sir Francis said, rolling his eyes. “Then she demanded to know where his grave was. Ugh, her dad! Every time Blanche so much as mentions the late Amory, my wife bursts straight into tears, and the little cow does it on purpose just to wind her up. I lost my rag this afternoon, yelled that I was her dad, that’s enough of that rubbish – and then she had a go at me.”
“What’d she say?” Strong snickered.
“Said I wasn’t her dad, that I was too thick to even understand her, that her real dad must’ve been a genius with fine feelings, unlike some bloke who only married her mum for her money.”
“Well, you did marry her for her money, didn’t you?” Strong pointed out.
“Doesn’t make it any less crap to hear it out loud, does it?” Sir Francis huffed. “I’m no literary genius, but I’m not the total idiot she makes me out to be. She always finds a way to make me look like a right prat, you know? Sways the whole house around her with her stupid sentimental airs. I wish she was dead, Ned.”
“Five minutes ago you were wishing my wife was dead,” Strong said, still perfectly good-humoured, and the Baronet, ever the model of unfiltered candour, replied: “Fair. If someone’s ruining my life, I wish they’d just pop off. I wish Blanche was at the bottom of a well, honestly.”

So it’s pretty clear from that very honest chat that our accomplished little friend Blanche had a few… character quirks that made her extremely unpopular with everyone who had to live with her. She fancied herself a misunderstood genius, full of exquisite deep feelings and brilliant literary talent, stuck surrounded by dull relatives who couldn’t possibly appreciate her. Her stepdad’s reading material extended no further than *Bell’s Life* and the Racing Calendar, and her wife Lady Clavering still wrote like a 13 year old schoolgirl who’d never heard of grammar. Since Blanche was completely convinced she was intellectually leagues above everyone she lived with, she made a point of reminding them of their inferiority every five minutes, framing herself as a poor martyred soul and making absolutely sure no one forgot how much she was suffering. If a poetess can’t wail about how unfair her life is, what’s even the point of owning a lyre? Blanche only played hers at the most miserable, weepy tunes, belting out elegies for her dead hopes and dirges for her nipped-in-the-bud teenage romances like she was the main character in the saddest French novel ever written.

Her actual hardships, for the record, were basically nonexistent. All her griefs lived inside her own head – she was perpetually miserable and dissatisfied, so of course she cried all the time. Her “Mes Larmes” bit was a party trick she could pull out on demand, and the more she practiced crying, the easier it got. Sentimentality is just like the dropsy Horace wrote about: the more you indulge it, the worse it gets.

Blanche was gushing dramatic nonsense from a shockingly young age. Lamartine was her favourite poet as soon as she could read, and by 16 she’d devoured every single novel Balzac and George Sand ever wrote. While her real-life family were a bunch of boring oafs, she insisted she had hundreds of soulmates in the “spirit world” – the moody heroines of all those French novels, the high-souled convict Trenmor, the fiery poet Stenio, the random princes from all those romance paperbacks. She’d been in love with two separate fictional princes while she was still in pinafores, and had strong opinions on divorce and women’s rights long before she was old enough to go to a party. She played at romance with these imaginary characters the same way other little girls played at being mums with their dolls – one week her favourite tragic hero was Rodolph, the next week he was tossed under the bed and replaced with a different sad poet.

Novelists, you see, know all the secrets of the female heart even the ladies themselves don’t pick up on, so we can confirm that 11 year old Betsy (her real, original name, don’t tell anyone) fell madly in love with a little Savoyard organ grinder in Paris and was fully convinced he was a kidnapped prince. At 12, she had a huge crush on her old, ugly drawing master, because obviously no amount of old or ugly can stand up to a great tragic romance. At 13, at her Parisian boarding school right next to an all-boys academy, she was pen-pals with two separate teen boys at the same time.

Oh right, that name! She was christened plain old Betsy, but rebranded herself “Blanche” all on her own when she got old enough to decide Betsy was far too common for a poetic genius. Sir Francis’s favourite secret weapon to keep her in line was the threat to yell “BETSY” at the top of his lungs in the middle of a fancy dinner party, a move that would make her turn bright red and back down immediately.

We were talking about dolls earlier, right? Well Blanche treated her real-life human friends exactly the same way: collecting locks of their hair to keep in a little fancy box, declaring they were soulmates for life, then dropping them the second they disappointed her. She once ran into her old boarding school “beloved sister” Leocadie in a Paris grocery shop, working the till for her grocer dad, and was so horrified by the disillusionment she wrote three poems about it. She considered every single one of these tiny inconveniences the well-earned penalties of being a great genius.

While she was off writing sad verses about her tortured soul, she made her mum so nervous she couldn’t even speak without messing up her words – if Lady Clavering accidentally said “sparrowgrass” instead of asparagus, Blanche would calmly correct her in front of guests until the poor woman was stuttering so bad she’d mix up half the other words in the sentence. The only person completely immune to her snark was Captain Strong, who just laughed at everything she said.

Over in the little town of Clavering, no one was more desperate to snoop on the fancy new family at the Park than Madame Fribsby, the local milliner. The very first Sunday the Claverings showed up at church, she ignored Doctor Portman’s entire 40 minute sermon (one of his proudest, oldest ones, too) to catalogue every single piece of clothing the ladies were wearing, from their bonnets down to their little booties. A few days later she’d wangled her way into a chat with Lady Clavering’s head lady’s maid at the Park’s housekeeper’s room, passed over her fancy bilingual business cards boasting of direct Paris fashion shipments and dressmaking for the entire local aristocracy, and was immediately hired.

Head maid Mrs. Bonner was soon a regular at Madame Fribsby’s little shop, spending every free evening there gorging on green tea, hot Sally Lunn cakes, scandal, and cheap novel readings. Blanche’s own lady’s maid Pincott never got to join the fun, though – her “muse” mistress worked her harder than any urchin in a factory. Blanche was forever getting random new urges to completely remake her dresses at 10pm, and since Pincott had trained as a dressmaker in Paris before coming to work for her, she was ripping seams, stitching new frills, and ironing crimps from 5am to 1am every single day. She couldn’t quit, either – her dad was a disabled cripple back home in England who couldn’t work, and she was the only one sending money home to feed her family.

Blanche, ever the gracious “generous” soul, never let Pincott forget it. “I should sack you, you know,” she’d say softly, while Pincott was doing her hair. “Your eyes are all red and puffy, you’re always snivelling about needing a doctor, you’re absolutely useless – but I’m keeping you on for the sake of your poor starving parents. You should be grateful.” Other days she’d sigh and say “Your sad little pale face is giving me a migraine, Pincott. I think I’ll make you wear rouge tomorrow so you don’t look so miserable.” Then she’d keep Pincott standing over her for an hour combing her hair while she read a George Sand novel, and at 1am she’d pat her hand and say “Alright, you can kiss me goodnight. Make sure the pink dress is completely remade by 7am tomorrow, okay?” before rolling over and going to sleep. She truly thought she was a benevolent, kind mistress, too. For sheer quiet soft-voiced cruelty, no screaming slave driver with a whip could ever top a delicate, weepy poetic genius like Blanche.

While Pincott was slaving away to the point she never got a single night off, another member of the Clavering household was a permanent guest at Madame Fribsby’s: the head chef, Monsieur Alcide Mirobolant.

The rustic locals of Clavering had no idea what to make of this very fancy Frenchman. On his days off, he’d wander the streets dressed head to toe in his favourite “Parisian gentleman” fit: a light green frock coat, crimson velvet waistcoat with blue glass buttons, gigantic bright plaid trousers, orange satin necktie, shiny leather-tipped boots, gold embroidered cap, and a fancy gilded cane. He was fully convinced he looked like the most stylish man alive.

He’d stroll down the main street grinning and winking at every woman he passed, peeking over garden railings at ladies through their windows, thinking his smouldering looks would make them drop dead on the spot. Unfortunately for him, Mrs Pybus’s maid Betsy shrieked and hid behind a laurel bush when he winked at her, the Miss Baker family stared at him like he was a madman, and a whole crowd of ragged urchins abandoned their dirt pies to follow him down the road.

At first he thought they were all gathering to admire him, and he strutted even prouder. But then the factory kids got off work, joined the crowd, and started hooting “FRENCHY! FROG EATER!” at him, yelling to get a lock of his long curly ringlets for a souvenir. It finally dawned on him he was being laughed at, not admired.

That’s when Madame Fribsby spotted him, poor red-faced and flustered with a whole jeering mob at his heels. She ran straight across the road, hailed him in her very terrible but very enthusiastic French, and dragged him inside her shop. Then she planted herself on the doorstep, yelled at the whole crowd that they were a bunch of cowards picking on a poor foreign man who couldn’t even speak their language, and everyone scattered, a little sheepish – Madame Fribsby was well-liked in town, and no one wanted to mess with her.

Mirobolant was so grateful to hear his native language spoken, he immediately declared her his guardian angel, the only nice, refined English lady he’d ever met. The next day he sent a basket over to her shop, full of pineapple flavoured cream, a lobster mayonnaise he’d made himself, and a box of Provence preserved fruits, alongside a gallant little note saying her kindness had made a blooming garden in the desert of his sad life. A fast friendship bloomed immediately.

The only fly in the ointment? Mirobolant kept calling her “my respectable Fribsbi! My virtuous Fribsbi!” and insisting he thought of her as his mother. Madame Fribsby, staring up at the old portrait of her long lost Carabineer love hanging over her mantelpiece, sighed deeply – she’d been half hoping this dashing young Frenchman was going to sweep her off her feet, and now she’d been demoted to mum. She told him, a little sadly, that she still felt as romantic as an 18 year old girl. Mirobolant very politely steered the conversation elsewhere, and told her he was actually very deeply in love with someone else entirely.

He didn’t waste any time launching into the full, dramatic backstory of his great grand passion.

Our readers have already heard Sir Francis Clavering’s brutally unvarnished hot take on the wife who dumped her entire fortune on him to drag him home from abroad and install him as a big shot in his native shire, and the man was not even slightly off. Lady Clavering was never going to win any awards for being the sharpest or most erudite lady in the county. She’d notched exactly two years of formal education in a London suburb she swore was called “Ackney” until her dying breath, before being dragged off at 15 to rejoin her wealthy dad in Calcutta.

On that voyage out aboard the *Ramchunder* East Indiaman, under command of grumpy, no-nonsense Captain Bragg, she met her first husband Mr. Amory: third mate, professional troublemaker, and all-around chaos gremlin. We won’t drag you through every boring detail of her early life, but suffice it to say Captain Bragg got so fed up with the mate’s nonsense he locked him in irons, dumped him at the Cape of Good Hope, and delivered his ward safely to her Calcutta dad after a voyage so stormy half the cargo nearly ended up at the bottom of the Indian Ocean. Amory, ever the resilient scammer, worked his way back to Calcutta as a common deckhand, married the rich attorney’s daughter over her dad’s loud protests, and then proceeded to fail at literally every business he touched: indigo planter? Broke. Import agent? Broke. Editor of the *Sunderbund Pilot*? So broke the paper only ran for three issues before creditors seized the printing press. He fought nonstop with his wife and father-in-law the entire time, until one last very illegal bit of forgery meant he had to hightail it to New South Wales faster than you can say “debts owing.”

At that point, the now very miserable Mrs. Amory grabbed her four-year-old daughter Blanche (called Betsy back then) and her loyal maid Mrs. Bonner (the same lady you met last chapter, now Lady Clavering’s head confidante) and hightailed it back to Europe. She moved into a house near grumpy Captain Bragg in Pocklington Street, who was so awkward about the whole Amory mess he acted like she carried the plague, and she quickly decided the whole of London was whispering about her husband’s scandals (they weren’t) and the King, Queen, and entire East India Company board were keeping a file on her (they absolutely weren’t). So she bolted for the continent, and proceeded to have the time of her life.

She gallivanted through every major European city, paid her boarding house bills months in advance, turned every picnic she attended into a feast with three times more champagne and cold ham than anyone else brought, made curries and pillaws for every boarding house guest, gave away every spare play ticket and masked ball pass she could get her hands on, and bailed out every broke, mustachioed aristocrat and stranded dowager whose remittance was late. No one cared that she said “Ackney” instead of Hackney or dropped half her H’s – she was the most popular guest in every boarding house in Brussels, Paris, Milan, Naples, and Rome. It was in Rome that she got the news Amory had finally kicked the bucket, at the exact same time deadbeat Captain Clavering and his equally broke buddy the Chevalier Strong were stuck in the city unable to pay their hotel bill. The good-natured widow married the fancy “ancient baronet heir” without shedding a single tear for her dead scapegrace first husband, and soon found herself mistress of Clavering Park.

Little Blanche tagged along for every single one of her mum’s European adventures, so she learned a whole lot more about life by 14 than most girls her age. She had a governess, a stint at Madame de Caramel’s fancy Paris boarding school in the Champs Elysees, and then the family moved back to England – at which point her whole world turned upside down. Granddad died, little baby Frank was born, and suddenly everyone in the Clavering household acted like the squawking toddler who spilled his dinner on every available surface was the greatest heir the British aristocracy had ever produced. If it hadn’t been for him, Blanche would’ve been the heiress to the whole estate! She didn’t care much about money, but it was still pretty obvious that her stepdad was a dull dimwit who only cared about smoking his cigars, her mum was way less “refined” than the other posh ladies in the county, and little Frank was a spoiled monster who kept ruining her nice dresses. Naturally, none of these three people could possibly understand her deep poetic soul, right? So she went looking for someone to dump all her dramatic unspent affection on.

Unsurprisingly, Lady Clavering and Sir Francis were just as eager to get Blanche married off as she was to find a suitable crush – Sir Francis had even weirdly daydreamed that Mrs. Strong would conveniently drop dead so he could marry Blanche off to Chevalier Strong, but when that didn’t look likely, any nice smart young man with a pulse was welcome to try for her hand. Our own pal Arthur Pendennis, who checked every box of handsome and educated, could’ve sauntered up to the house, proposed, and been greeted with open arms before he even finished his sentence. But Pen was being a total dork about it: he was mortified by his recent failures, his empty bank account, and the fact that his stupid romantic follies had left his mum scrimping at Fairoaks to make ends meet. There was equal parts vanity and guilt holding him back – how could he, the broke loser who couldn’t even make rent, possibly end up with a dazzling creature like Blanche Amory who lived in a mansion with a dozen footmen? So he just moped around, half-heartedly daydreaming about romance but too shy to actually do anything about it, the way so many silly young men do.

Pen’s mum Helen, though? She’d already seen right through Blanche, no effort required. The poor widow had watched enough of her son’s romantic nonsense to spot a frivolous, self-absorbed girl a mile off, and she was convinced Blanche was full of worldliness and selfishness hidden behind pretty verses and sweet lines, with no respect for her parents or anything even remotely sacred. At first Pen and Laura argued with her, Pen laughing off her objections and saying “Pshaw mum, all women are jealous over Laura, that’s all this is!” and Laura being completely starstruck by her new fancy poet friend. But the more Helen watched the two of them traipsing between Fairoaks and Clavering Park every single day, the more her heart sank – her secret dearest wish was that Pen and Laura would end up married, the two kids she loved more than anything in the world, and she was terrified Blanche would snatch Pen right out from under her. One day, she broke down and told him so, tearful, saying she was sure she didn’t have long left to live and all she wanted was to see the two of them settled together before she went.

Pen gave her that big patronising soothing hug he was so good at, and got a little smug jolt of self-satisfaction when he realised: wait a minute, I could have Laura? The sweet, kind, honest girl I’ve known my whole life, who’s always been there? When she walked in from the garden a minute later, cheeks flushed, holding a basket of roses to give to Helen, Pen looked at her and thought, wow, that really is a prize I could just ask for. For a second he felt all giddy and triumphant, the idiot.

But it didn’t take Laura more than a few weeks to realise Helen was 100% right about Blanche. The “Muse” spent all their private time complaining about how no one at home understood her, how embarrassing her mum’s Ackney accent was, how dim Sir Francis was, how her horrible little baby brother had stolen all her mother’s love. But none of that explained the time little Frank spilled a glass of water on her drawing, and she boxed his ears so hard he screamed, while yelling very rude insults at him in both English and French. Or how she ordered his governess around like a personal servant, demanding she run across the house to fetch her books or handkerchief any time she couldn’t be bothered to stand up. Laura, who always thanked every servant and helper who did anything for her, was horrified. The final straw was when Blanche read her this absurdly weepy original poem about how she’d love her baby brother forever, no matter what, how she’d cling to his feet if he ever pushed her away – and then 10 minutes later was the one shoving him and boxing his ears, not the other way around. That was Laura’s first big life lesson: poets don’t always practice what they preach, not unlike that silly boy Pen, who was also prone to grand dramatic speeches he didn’t follow through on. But Pen was a man! That was different! He had a good heart under all his nonsense! Blanche was just… faking all the sentiment.

Laura’s former gushy admiration for her new friend quickly curdled into quiet, amused tolerance, which Blanche hated. No one likes getting found out to be a fraud, especially when they’ve been acting like they’re too good for everyone around them. The situation finally exploded one terrible afternoon, when Blanche was in a truly foul mood: she’d been rude to her mum, yelled at little Frank, been horrid to his governess, and then drove her own poor maid Pincott out of the room in tears for no reason at all. Laura, who’d been watching this all build up, absolutely snapped. She gave Blanche a full on loud, indignant dressing down, telling her it was positively wicked to go around torturing everyone weaker than her just to make herself feel big, then grabbed her bonnet and ran all the way home across the park, leaving Blanche gawping.

When she got back to Fairoaks, she told Helen the whole story, and declared she was done being Blanche’s friend forever. “Oh mamma, you were right!” she said. “She seems so soft and sweet, but she’s all selfishness through and through. Anyone who talks constantly about how full of love they are and treats their servants and family like that can’t possibly have a real heart.” Helen was absolutely over the moon, secretly – that meant Blanche couldn’t possibly marry Pen now, right? He’d see how horrible she was any day now, and turn back to nice, steady Laura.

But Laura didn’t even admit to herself the real first crack in the friendship that day had happened long before Blanche started bullying her maid. The two of them had been sitting in Blanche’s bedroom, and Blanche had very slyly brought up Pen, making snide little comments about his old romance with that actress. When Laura jumped to his defence, sputtering that Pen had acted perfectly honorably the whole time, Blanche decided to mess with her even more. She ran to her fancy mother-of-pearl desk, pulled out a handful of crumpled, slightly green-stained love poems Pen had written her, all about Naiads and rivers and Undine, and showed them to Laura like they were a huge top secret. Laura stared at them for ages, barely able to see the words through the funny blur in her eyes. Blanche acted all coy, insisting she’d never dreamed of writing a reply. Then she sat down at the piano and belted out a few flashy Rossini arias, while Laura sat there in silence, barely listening. They went down to lunch with their arms around each other’s waists ten minutes later, Laura even giving her a sisterly kiss on the cheek, but the damage was done. By the time Blanche started her reign of terror over the rest of the household later that afternoon, the friendship was already well and truly dead.

The falling out between the two girls blew over in no time flat. Laura was the sort who’d apologize and forgive you for stealing her favorite hair ribbon before you even finished denying it, and as for Blanche, her tantrums were never long-lived anyway — this particular tiff hadn’t even ruffled her feathers, truth be told. No vanity gets bruised over being called wicked, after all; if anything, Blanche was flattered that Laura cared enough to get mad. The whole fight, both girls knew, wasn’t really about anything they’d said out loud anyway.
With a sigh, Laura had to admit the giddy, romantic best-friend-forever phase of her friendship with Blanche was dead and gone. Now she could only summon the sort of mild, polite fondness you have for a roommate who keeps stealing your milk.
Blanche, for her part, immediately cranked out a page of heart-rending poetry all about her cruel desertion and broken illusions. Same old story, she wrote: love meeting coldness, loyalty repaid with neglect. Just her luck, a new family with two daughters moved into the neighborhood from London right then, so Blanche quickly adopted one of them as her new eternal soulmate, and poured all her tragic woe over missing the old dead Laura friendship straight into this new girl’s ear. The tall footmen from the Hall barely turned up at Fairoaks with notes anymore, the little pony carriage was almost never sent over to run errands for the ladies there. Whenever Laura popped in to visit, Blanche would pull on her best sad martyr face, and Laura would just laugh at her over-the-top sentimental schtick with zero respect, which only made Blanche pout harder.
If Blanche had new female friends to keep her occupied, the honest historian has to note she also racked up a whole crowd of male admirers who seemed equally charmed by her little routine. Put this artless young thing in a garden path, a drawing room window nook, or a lull between waltzes for ten whole minutes, and she’d be leaning in all soft and earnest, fluttering her big beautiful eyes, spilling fake intimate secrets and making tender little appeals, before saying goodbye and turning the exact same performance on the next poor sap who wandered past.
Back when the Claverings first moved to the Park, there was barely anyone around for Blanche to perform for. Which meant Pen got all the flirty little glances and secret asides all to himself, by the drawing room window or down the garden walk. As mentioned before, the whole town of Clavering had exactly zero eligible young men; the surrounding countryside had nothing but a couple of scruffy curates and country squires whose feet were so big they looked like they were wearing rowing boats for boots. Sir Francis wouldn’t even say hello to the dragoons quartered at Chatteris — turns out that was his old regiment, and he’d left on terrible terms over a whole messy cocktail of a scam horse sale, disputed gambling debts, and a whole host of old embarrassments no one bothered digging into, since we’ve got more than enough current drama to keep track of.
But autumn rolled around, Parliament adjourned, and the London season wrapped up, dumping a horde of county families back to their country estates, filling up the tiny nearby Baymouth watering place, opening the doors of Mr. Bingley’s ramshackle Theatre Royal in Chatteris, and drawing crowds for the assize balls and race meets. Up until that point, the snooty old local county families had all given Clavering Park the cold shoulder: the Fogeys of Drummington, the Squares of Tozely Park, the Welbores of The Barrow, all of them gossiped absolute nonsense about the new neighbours, inventing whole secret criminal backstories for Sir Francis, his mysterious wife, Blanche, and sketchy Captain Strong that would make a tabloid editor blush. Country folks don’t lack imagination, trust me.
Then the bigwigs showed up: the Earl of Trehawk, Lord Lieutenant of the whole county, moved into Eyrie Castle, and the Dowager Countess of Rockminster took a fancy mansion on Baymouth’s Marine Parade. Both great houses immediately paid formal, state calls on the Claverings at the Park, and every other county family’s carriage went trundling right up the avenue after theirs, no one daring to be the last to suck up to the new crowd.
That’s when the fancy French chef Mirobolant finally got to show off all his skills, distracting himself from his sad unrequited love pangs by making ten different complicated sauces for every dinner. The giant footmen at the Park were so busy waiting on guests they never got a chance to loiter drinking small beer with the maids at Fairoaks anymore. Blanche had new best friends to hang out with and fancy parties to go to, no more wandering down to the riverside where Pen fished. He showed up day after day casting his line, but not a single fish bit, and his river nymph “Undine” never showed up.
Strictly off the record, we can mention that little secret hollow tree Pen used to post secret love letters to Miss Fotheringay back in the day? He’d converted it into a tiny private post office. Tucked under a loose stone and a clump of moss, he’d leave flowery little poems and over-romantic letters addressed to the river nymph who haunted the bank, and a couple times he’d gotten a note back: a pressed wild flower, or a tiny couple lines of soft acknowledgement scrawled on pink, rose-scented paper, half jocular half sweet. Of course Blanche walked that riverside all the time, and of course she used that exact same pink scented stationery. But once all the fancy visitors moved into Clavering Park, no one came to retrieve Pen’s letters. His white pages sat untouched under the stone and moss, while the tree’s reflection wobbled in the Brawl river as it rolled past. The letters were hardly racy, the pink notes barely said anything at all — but silly old Pen decided the whole thing was a grand tragic romance. Why didn’t he just walk up and say hello, you dummy? Neither of you were even that serious, you were both just playing at being in love.
But when the game gets cut off mid-round, a man is very likely to throw a dramatic little temper tantrum. When no one came to collect his poems anymore, Pen suddenly decided he was heartbroken, just like he’d been over the actress, and he was dead set on getting a full explanation. One day he turned up at the Hall and there were twenty visitors packed in the drawing room; the next day, Blanche was nowhere to be found, napping to rest up for a ball that night. Pen cursed all balls, cursed his tiny allowance, cursed the fact that he was too low down the local social ladder to ever get invited to them. The third time he visited, Blanche was strolling the garden in state with no less than the Bishop of Chatteris, his wife, and his whole family, who all scowled so hard at Pen when they heard his last name he almost flinched — they absolutely remembered that whole mess where Pen got caught making out with Miss Fotheringay in the Dean’s garden.
“The Bishop says you’re a sad, bad young man,” good-natured Lady Clavering whispered to him. “What’ve you been up to, eh? Nothing to upset that dear sweet ma of yours, I hope? How is your dear ma? Why hasn’t she come to visit us? We’ve been gallivanting round so much we haven’t seen any of the neighbours. Send her my love, and bring Laurar too, come eat dinner with us tomorrow!”
Mrs. Pendennis was too unwell to make the trip, but Laura and Pen turned up for the huge packed party, and Pen only got two whole minutes alone with Blanche.
“You never come down to the river anymore,” he said.
“I can’t,” Blanche shrugged. “The house is absolutely full of people.”
“Undine has left the stream, huh?” Pen said, leaning hard into his whole poetic bit.
“Undine never should have gone there in the first place,” Blanche said. “It was stupid, it was just a silly game. Besides, you’ve got way nicer consolations waiting for you back at home, don’t you?” She gave him one quick pointed look, then dropped her eyes.
If he’d wanted her, that was the exact moment to say so, and she probably would have said yes! But her mention of “consolations at home” made him think of Laura, all soft and loyal, and his mother’s dearest wish that he’d marry her adopted daughter. “Blanche — Miss Amory!” he started, voice all flustered.
“Laura is staring right at us, Mr. Pendennis,” Blanche said, and before he could say another word she’d run off back to the main group, leaving Pen standing there biting his nails, staring at the moonlight over the garden totally confused.
Laura was indeed staring at him. She was pretending to listen to Mr. Pynsent, son of Lord Rockminster and grandson of the Dowager Countess who was holding court in the best armchair, patting Sir Francis on the head like an overexcited golden retriever because she wanted to lock in his political support for the county. Turns out Pynsent and Pen had gone to Oxbridge together, back when Pen was the big popular man on campus, super successful and maybe a little supercilious towards the quieter, lower-ranked Pynsent. They met that day for the first time since graduation, and exchanged that classic half-assed British university guy nod that translates perfectly to “Confound you, what the hell are you doing here?”
“I knew that Pendennis bloke at Oxbridge,” Pynsent said to Laura. “I think he’s sweet on Miss Amory, isn’t he?”
Laura agreed, but said nothing else.
“Is he a huge county landowner? He used to run his mouth at the Union about running for Parliament. Whereabouts are his estates?”
Laura snickered. “His estates are on the other side of the river, right by the park lodge gate. That’s Fairoaks, where I live.”
“Nice, lots of pheasants there?” Pynsent laughed.
“Nine hens, one rooster, a pig, and a very old lazy pointer dog,” Laura said, grinning.
“Pendennis doesn’t do pheasant shooting then?” Pynsent asked.
“You should come visit us and see for yourself,” Laura said, highly amused that Pynsent thought her Pen was some grand big county aristocrat.
“I’d absolutely love to catch up with him again,” Pynsent said, his tone making it very clear he was far more excited to visit her than her chicken-owning cousin.
Blanche sashayed over with her most sparkling smile, begged dear Laura to sing the second part of a duet with her. Laura, being the good-natured soul she was, headed to the piano, where Pynsent stood and listened politely through the whole song, before wandering off to find his travelling companion Wagg as soon as Blanche started her solo number.
“Man, that tall one Laura is such a nice, frank, well-bred girl, Wagg,” Pynsent said. “Ringlets, very red lips — but a total genuine person.”
“What do you think of the actual hostess Blanche?” Wagg asked.
“She’s a scrawny, fake little humbug,” Pynsent said, no filter at all. “She’s forever hunching her shoulders out of her dress and ogling everyone like a flirty French chambermaid.”
“Pynsent, shut up, someone’ll hear you,” Wagg hissed.
“Oh, hey, it’s Pendennis of Boniface!” Pynsent said, waving. “Nice evening, mate, we were just talking about how charming your cousin is.”
“Wait, are you related to my old friend Major Pendennis?” Wagg asked Pen.
“His nephew. Met you at Gaunt House once,” Pen said, pulling on his most posh polite gentleman face, and the three were instant acquaintances.
The next afternoon, Pen trundled back from a totally fishless fishing trip to find Pynsent and Wagg sat in his mother’s drawing room, chatting away happily with Helen and Laura. Wagg was the nosiest man alive, he’d noted every single silly detail on his walk up the drive: old gardener John in his beat up red livery waistcoat, Pen’s white swimming trunks flapping in the breeze hung to dry on the gooseberry bushes, mismatched umbrellas and scuffed ladies’ clogs lined up by the front door. He had the soul of a former butler who’d been promoted to dinner party joker, and thought it was the funniest thing in the world when people weren’t dripping with wealth, as if no one could be respectable unless they had a carriage and shopped at a fancy French milliner. He babbled on for ages dropping the names of thirty different dukes and lords, leaving Helen who’d only been to London once before she had Pen completely bewildered, thinking he was the tackiest man alive.
Pynsent, meanwhile, was chatting happily with Laura, saying he planned to canvass the county for a seat in the next Parliament, and hoped he could count on Pen’s support — he remembered perfectly well what a brilliant orator Pen had been back in their Oxford days. Pen walked in, played the posh big-shot act for all it was worth, dropping names of all their old uni friends and acting like marquises were casual mates he ran into every day at the shops.
Then Betsy the maid, who had zero idea there were fancy visitors, flung the door wide open and trundled in holding their usual six o’clock tea tray: three chipped teacups, a dented old teapot, and a plate piled high with thick, buttered homemade bread. Pen went bright scarlet, completely mortified. Wagg stuffed his tongue in his cheek and winked so hard his eye almost stuck shut. Pynsent, for his part, thought the whole thing was totally normal — people could have tea at six if they wanted, no law against it.
On the walk back to the Park, Wagg cackled so hard he could barely breathe, and said he was going to tell old Major Pendennis the whole story the second he got back to London, about how they caught the snobby pretender Pen red-handed eating cheap thick bread and butter. Wagg told the whole exaggerated story at dinner that night, complete with silly voices, making fun of the beat up old furniture at Fairoaks, the clothes hanging on the gooseberry bushes, the maid trundling in with the tea tray. Blanche, who adored witty funny men, cackled until she cried, calling him the most delightful droll creature alive. But Pynsent was so fed up with Wagg’s cheap mockery he slammed his hand on the table, and announced “I don’t know what sort of low people you hang around with, Wagg, but those two ladies at Fairoaks are the most perfectly well-bred women I’ve ever met in my life — and I do hope, ma’am,” he added, turning straight to the Dowager Countess, “that you’ll do them the honour of paying them a call.”
Sir Francis snickered and whispered to the man next to him that that was the perfect burn, and Lady Clavering tapped Pynsent on the arm with her fan, winked, and called him a real good fellow.
After that little incident, a faint, sharp, almost sad edge crept into the way Laura spoke to Pen. She watched him laze around the house all day, smoking on the lawn, flipping through books he was too listless to actually read, and Helen noticed that little almost-scornful look cross her face sometimes. “What’s happened between you two?” Helen asked Laura, worried. “Has that wicked little Blanche been stirring up trouble?”
“Nothing at all,” Laura said.
“Then why are you looking at him like that?” Helen pressed.
“Because he’s wasting his whole potential,” Laura said softly, a little bitter. “We’re just two boring women, we’re not interesting enough for his big brilliant genius. He never leaves the garden gate, he’s bored of Captain Strong and Captain Glanders even, he’s just sitting here tied to our apron strings wasting away. He’s got all that talent, everyone at Oxford said he was going to go far, and he’s got no job, no plans to make a name for himself out in the world. He’ll never be happy stuck here with us forever.”
Helen’s heart started beating fast. “We have more than enough money if we’re careful. He barely spends a penny, I know he’d be happy here with us.”
But Laura was right, and they both knew it.

Arthur Pendennis had camped himself in a dark window nook draped in calico curtains, working so hard at his gloom and scowl his eyebrows looked like they’d fused together, all to glower at sweet Miss Laura Bell dancing her very first quadrille with partner Mr. Pynsent. That gentleman was every inch the solemn stiff Englishman at formal events, trudging through the dance like he was marching up the aisle to his Sunday church pew, zero flicker of a smile on his face, fully committed to treating a casual party dance like it was a mandatory government audit.

Laura, meanwhile, was positively glowing. The lights, the crowd, the waltzing music all had her giddy as a kid on a fairground ride. She fluffed out her white skirts, bounced through each dance step grinning, her brown ringlets slipping back over her pink, honest shoulders, and half the men in the room were sneaking glances at her the whole time. Lady Fogey, the local social climber who fancied herself the official London fashion expert even when she was stuck in the country, leaned over to Lady Rockminster to ask who the delightful young thing was, drawling that she resembled a super trendy London beauty everyone was fawning over and “would absolutely do” as a party hit. Lady Rockminster was this close to rolling her eyes right out of her head: of course her protégé would do, who did this Fogey woman think she was to pass judgment? She peered at Laura through her lorgnette, approving of the girl’s artless, unforced joy. “Her manners are excellent,” she thought to herself. “Arms a touch rosy, but that’s just from running around fields at her age. Far less annoying than that pert little Miss Amory prancing across from her.”

Blanche Amory was, in fact, Laura’s vis-a-vis for the quadrille, grinning her most dazzling fake smile at her “dearest friend” and bobbing her head every time they passed each other across the floor, acting like she was personally responsible for teaching Laura how to walk, let alone dance. Her shoulders were the whitest in the whole room, and they never stayed still in her dress for a single second: her eyes darted around the crowd nonstop, her tiny little frame practically yelling at every guest, “LOOK AT ME NOT THAT PINK HEALTHY COUNTRY LASS! I taught her everything she knows! This is real Parisian grace! Look at my fancy tiny French dancing shoes, Mr. Pynsent! Look at them, you grumpy Arthur Pendennis hiding behind the curtain, I know you’re dying to ask me to dance!”

Laura never took her eyes off Pen the whole quadrille, though. He didn’t budge from his curtain nook once the first dance ended, not even until good-natured Lady Clavering waved him over to the raised dais where all the fancy dowagers were camped out.
