Pen trundled over bright red and awkward as anything, exactly like every overconfident 20-something who just got called over by their mum in front of a whole room of people. He gave Lady Rockminster a dramatic fancy bow, which she ignored so hard he might as well have been a stray cat wandering into the party, then went to say hello to Blanche’s mum, the widowed Mrs. Amory, who was decked out in so many diamonds, velvet, lace, and feathers she looked like a jewelry shop display that got into a pillow fight.

Laura’s next dance partner was 16-year-old Eton schoolboy Young Fogey, who was still waiting impatiently for his first beard to grow in so he could join a fancy dragoon regiment. He stared at Laura like she’d invented chocolate, and blurted out completely unprompted: “I like you way better than that stuck-up French girl I was just dancing with.” Laura cackled, looking even more good-humored than usual, and mid-laugh she spotted Pen still pouting and strutting around being absurdly pompous. Next up was the waltz, and Young Fogey sighed so hard his whole chest caved in — he couldn’t waltz at all, and immediately swore to book 12 private dance lessons the second he got home for the holidays. Mr. Pynsent grabbed Laura for the waltz, and Pen saw red. A year ago, they’d all shoved the furniture aside at Clavering Park, called the governess down to play piano, and thrown impromptu tiny balls, with Chevalier Strong doing perfect hornpipes, waltzes, even Spanish fandangos if you asked him, no one cared who was twirling who. Now boring, stiff Mr. Pynsent had his arm around Laura’s waist spinning her around the whole room? The absolute audacity!

Blanche, who was a brilliant dancer, got stuck with the worst possible partner: Captain Broadfoot of the local dragoons, who was so determined not to mess up he moved slower than a man wading through molasses, and had zero ear for rhythm at all. The whole room was oohing over Laura’s obvious joy, while Blanche, stuck trundling along behind Broadfoot, realized she was losing the crowd’s attention to her country cousin. She faked a dramatic fatigue attack after two turns, made Broadfoot escort her back over to her mum, where Pen was hanging around chatting. “Why didn’t you ask me to waltz?” she huffed at him. “You left me stuck with that horrible spurs-and-red-coat oaf!” Pen snickered. “Wait, I thought all young ladies thought red coats and spurs were the most fascinating thing alive? My boring black coat couldn’t possibly compete with that fancy uniform.”

“You’re such a cruel, sulky, naughty thing,” Blanche huffed, shrugging her white shoulders dramatically. “Go away, your cousin’s staring right at you over Pynsent’s shoulder.”

“Wanna waltz with me next?” Pen offered.

“Not this one, obviously, I just sent Broadfoot away. I was supposed to dance the next waltz with Mr. Poole, but I’ll ditch him for you,” Blanche grinned.

“Women do forget their promises awfully easy, don’t they?” Pen teased.

“But we always come back later all apologetic and sweet, don’t we?” Blanche winked. “Oh look, here comes Mr. Pynsent, that human fireplace poker, with dear Laura leaning on his arm. Doesn’t she look ridiculous?”

Laura bounded up, holding her hand out to Pen, while Pynsent gave him a stiff little bow, every bit as graceful as the iron poker Blanche had just joked about. “I’m having the best time, Pen!” Laura said, all bright and kind. “The three first dances are over, I’m yours for the next one, right?”

“Ah, sorry,” Pen said, full of petty glee, “I just promised Miss Amory the next waltz.” Blanche nodded at Laura all smug, giving her a tiny fake curtsy.

“Well then you’ll waltz with me the one after that, right Pen?” Laura laughed. “Won’t you?”

“Sorry, I promised Miss Amory that one too.” Pen said. Laura huffed in mock exasperation, gave a curtsy back, and went to go tuck herself under Lady Rockminster’s ample wing.

Pen was absolutely delighted with himself. Two of the prettiest girls at the whole ball were fighting over him! He was such a genius! He leaned against the wall all dandified, elbows propped up, and roasted every single person in the room without mercy: the dragoons squeezed into skin-tight jackets that looked like they’d been painted on, the local county dandies in their absurd ill-fitting suits, the ladies with insane hair that looked like it had entire birds’ nests and bunches of fake grapes tucked in it. “That woman over there has a hairstyle made entirely of almonds and raisins,” Pen cackled. “They could serve her up for dessert straight after the roast.” Blanche was laughing so hard at his jokes she could barely breathe, making extra sure Laura saw how close and merry the two of them were being across the dance floor.

Laura still didn’t get mad at Pen at all. She just figured he was throwing a silly jealous tantrum, which was kind of cute, honestly. So when the next waltz struck, and Pen was still ignoring her, she grabbed Chevalier Strong instead, who was a far better dancer than half the people in the room, and spun off across the floor having the absolute time of her life, not glancing Pen’s way once. Pen, twirling Blanche around like they were both professional opera dancers, was feeling on top of the world… right up until he and Blanche plowed full tilt into Captain Broadfoot, who was waltzing with the giant Miss Roundle, wearing a dress the exact shade of strawberry ice cream. All four of them lost their balance completely. Broadfoot and Miss Roundle toppled flat, Blanche landed safely on a bench against the wall, and Pen ate the dance floor face first in a sprawling mess. The whole room burst out giggling.

Pen was furious. He hated being laughed at more than anything in the entire world. He scrambled up, red as a tomato, sputtering apologies to Blanche, and blaming the whole mess on Broadfoot and that silly lady in the pink dress. Blanche was seething too, declared she was terribly hurt and would never waltz again. Then a random guy with a blue ribbon and a fancy medal pinned to his chest sprinted over, handed her a glass of water. Blanche, in full dramatic mode, gave him her haughtiest, most gracious smile, put her hand on his arm, and asked him to escort her back to her mum. The guy nearly fainted with delight, bowed so deep his forehead almost touched her hand, and preened around the room like he’d just won the lottery.

“BY JOVE!” Pen yelped before he could stop himself. “That’s the cook! Mirobolant!”

Blanche was too flustered to hear the comment, but Mirobolant did. He turned and glowered at Pen over his shoulder, looking like he was two seconds away from stabbing him through the chest with a meat skewer. Chevalier Strong, who knew all the tea about the poor chef’s infatuation with Blanche, rushed over, babbled a few lines of Spanish the Gascon-born Mirobolant understood, and told him he’d get fired instantly if he was caught parading around the ball with a guest on his arm. Mirobolant dropped Blanche’s arm like it was a hot potato, gave a sad deep bow, and backed off. “That’s Mirobolant, the chef,” Strong explained to Blanche as he led her off to get an ice. “He’s so madly in love with you he was this close to running Pen through with a roasting spit just now.” Blanche sighed, “Poor M. Mirobolant! But how was I supposed to know he was a cook? He has a medal! His eyes are beautiful! He looks like a fancy foreign noble!”

Then they heard a huge scuffle and yelling coming from the main ballroom, and Strong sprinted off to see what the chaos was about, leaving Blanche alone in the select refreshment room with Colonel Altamont, the weird guy with the black wig and the medals who worked for the Nawab of Lucknow. He’d been camped there all night drinking champagne, and he stared at Blanche for a full minute with his little red, wine-glowing eyes before he slurred out, “Hey, you’re Betsy Amory, aren’t you? Betsy Amory, by Jove!” Blanche froze, confused, and the noise from the ballroom got so loud we simply have to rush back there to see exactly what caused all the commotion.

It was all-out ungentlemanly chaos by the ballroom window, shoving match and all, as half the party crowded shoulder to shoulder yelling over one another. Below in the street, onlookers pressed up against the glass yelling unhelpful heckles like “Punch ‘im!” and “Where’s the bleedin’ copper?” while a ring of gawkers—with Madame Fribsby front and center, squawking so loud her bonnet askew—circled seething little French chef Alcide Mirobolant on one side, and our very own Arthur Pendennis on the other. The incomparable Chevalier Strong shouldered his way through the crush, shoving past Madame Fribsby who lit up like a Christmas tree at the sight of him and wailed “Save him! Save him!” like he was seconds from being fed to the lions.

The whole mess had kicked off right after Strong slipped away to the refreshment table. Pen, still simmering so hard over his recent waltz disaster that his ears were bright red—where he’d tripped, sent dance partner Blanche Amory flying face-first into the wall, and collapsed on top of a very portly dragoon and even portlier Miss Roundle, making him the laughing stock of the entire three counties—had snuck over to the window to stare out at the calm, sparkling sea and try to talk himself down from throwing a full hissy fit. He was even halfway admitting he’d acted like a peevish idiot all night, when a hand landed firm on his shoulder.

He spun around, and was so shocked he almost choked: the hand belonged to Mirobolant, Sir Francis Clavering’s head chef, his dark ringlets sticking up every which way, eyes blazing like he was ready to duel a man over an undercooked sole meunière. For a Pendennis, descendant of generations of gentry, to be tapped on the shoulder like an old pal by a *cook*? His blood boiled so hot he thought his top hat would catch fire.

“You speak French?” Mirobolant hissed at him in his native tongue.

“What’s that to you, mate?” Pen snapped back in English, stamping his foot.

“Ah, so you understand it at least,” the chef said, giving a dramatic little bow.

“Yes sir,” Pen gritted out. “I understand it perfectly well.”

“Then you will comprehend, Monsieur Pendennis,” Mirobolant rumbled, rolling his R’s so hard they rattled, “when I tell you you are a coward. Monsieur Pendennis—un lâche. Do you hear me?”

Pen lurched forward like he was about to tear the man in two.

“You know what that word means for men of honour?” the chef snarled, hand on his hip, glaring daggers.

“The only consequence is I hurl you straight out this window, you insolent little scoundrel!” Pen roared. He would absolutely have done it, too—he was a strapping young 23-year-old, the chef was barely five foot nothing, the window was two steps away—if two burly army captains hadn’t bodily thrown themselves between them, ladies hadn’t started screaming so loud the fiddles screeched to a halt, the whole rest of the party hadn’t come sprinting over, and Laura hadn’t pushed through the crowd white as a sheet begging someone to tell her what on earth was wrong. That was exactly when Strong wandered back from the champagne table, to find Mirobolant grinding his teeth and yelling oaths in thick Gascon French, and Pen trying and failing to look calm and dignified for the onlookers.

“What on earth happened?” Strong asked the chef in fluent Spanish.

“I am a Chevalier of July!” Mirobolant squawked, slapping his own chest hard enough to make himself cough. “He has insulted my honour!”

“What did he call you?” Strong asked, already fighting a grin.

“He called me—*a cook*!” the little man hissed, like it was the worst slur ever invented.

Strong almost burst out laughing right there. “Come on, mate, come away with me,” he said, herding the chef gently off. “Can’t have a duel in front of all these ladies. I’ll pass your grievance along to Mr. Pendennis. Poor bloke’s had a bit too much of the bubbly, don’t mind him,” he stage-whispered to the surrounding guests, leaving Pen to be swarmed by concerned pals and Laura.

Pen shrugged, still half cross. “The man was just about to offer his arm to a young lady, so I made an offhand comment that he was a cook. Next thing I know he’s calling me a coward and challenging me to a duel. If you blokes hadn’t held me back I would have tossed him right out that window.”

“Bloody right you would!” the local bucks cheered. “Impudent little foreign twat.”

“Wait—wait, I do feel a bit bad if I hurt his feelings, actually,” Pen added, and Laura’s face lit right up, relieved.

“Go shake his hand and apologize before you go home, won’t you Pen?” she said, soft as an angel. “Foreigners are way more touchy about their honour than we are, they have different manners. If you hurt a poor man’s feelings, I know you’d be the first to say sorry. Wouldn’t you, dear Pen?”

Pen took both her hands, looked at her sweet forgiving face, and swore he absolutely would. *She’s so fond of me*, he thought, giddy for half a second. Should I propose to her right now? No, not now. I’ve got to sort this stupid cook business out first.

“Won’t you stay and waltz with me then?” Laura asked, desperate to keep him from storming off. “I’m not scared to waltz with you!”

It was the sweetest possible line, but the worst possible thing she could have said. Pen immediately flashed back to face-planting in full view of the whole ballroom, everyone laughing at him, Laura included.

“I am never dancing again,” he said, dark and dramatic. “Never. Can’t believe you’d even ask me that.”

“Is it because you can’t get Blanche Amory to be your partner?” Laura teased, not knowing how badly she’d just stepped in it.

“Because I don’t want to make a total fool of myself for everyone to laugh at!” Pen snapped back. “For *you* to laugh at me, Laura! I saw you snickering with Pynsent. No man gets to laugh at me, not on my watch.”

“Pen! Pen, don’t be daft!” Laura cried, hurt by his sudden vicious pride. He was already glaring across the room at poor Mr. Pynsent like he wanted to duel him next, too, over absolutely nothing. “Who thinks worse of you for tripping in a waltz? Why are you so quick to assume everyone’s making fun of you?”

Of course, that exact second Mr. Pynsent wandered over to them, all smarmy polite, and said: “Lady Rockminster sent me to ask if I may escort you in to supper, Miss Laura?”

“I—er, I was going to go in with my cousin,” Laura fumbled.

“Nonsense, you’re in perfectly good hands with him,” Pen said, cold as ice. “I’ll be off home, then. Night.”

“Good night, Mr. Pendennis,” Pynsent said, dry as dust (which translated very clearly to: “Go to hell, you insolent jealous little prat I’d love to box around the ears”). Pen just gave him a stiff little bow, ignored Laura’s imploring little looks, and stormed right out of the ballroom.

Pynsent sighed, staring out at the moonlit sea. “Beautiful night out, isn’t it? Way nicer to walk on the beach than be stuck in this hot stuffy room full of people I have to pretend to like. I’ve had to schmooze the attorney’s daughters, the apothecary’s wife, half the village, all night. Some bloke in the refreshment room forced me to drink champagne with him, looked like a sailor, half sloshed, dressed like a circus clown. As a local politician I’ve got to be nice to everyone, but it’s absolute torture—especially when I’d much rather be somewhere way more interesting,” he added, blushing bright pink.

“Sorry,” Laura said, not listening at all, her head full of Pen’s stupid fight. “I was distracted, I’m still worried about that quarrel between my cousin and that French gentleman.”

“Your cousin’s had a pretty unlucky night,” Pynsent said. “He’s managed to annoy about five different people already: the officer he fought with, the lady he tripped over in the waltz, Blanche Amory, the poor chef, and I don’t think he’s all that fond of me right now either.”

“Well, he left me in your care, didn’t he?” Laura said, peeking up at his face then dropping her eyes all coy like a guilty schoolgirl.

“Then I forgive him absolutely everything!” Pynsent said, delighted, and offered her his arm to lead her to the supper room.

Laura didn’t care a fig about the fancy spread (that the local newspaper would gush about for three whole paragraphs the next day, anyway). She was too miserable, replaying the whole night: Pen was quick to anger, petty, selfish, jealous, and rude the second his pride was pricked. How on earth could Helen have spent months dropping hints that Laura should marry him? Would that make him happy, ever?

Half an hour later, when it felt like three hours, a waiter brought her a crumpled pencil note from Pen: “Met Cooky downstairs, he was all ready to duel me. I apologized. I’m glad I did it. Wanted to talk to you tonight, but I’ll save what I have to say till you get home. God bless you. Dance all night with Pynsent, have fun. — Pen.” Laura breathed a huge sigh of relief. The boy still had a good heart under all that silly pride.

Pen was already halfway down the stairs by that point, kicking himself for being such a prat to Laura. He itched to turn right back up and apologize to her, but then he remembered he’d left her alone with that stuck-up Pynsent, and his pride wouldn’t let him go back and grovel in front of the other bloke. He might as well make peace with the chef first.

Strong was waiting for him in the hall, grinning like he’d just pulled the best prank in the world. “Got him locked in the coffee room,” he said. “Got a brace of dueling pistols and a candle on the table for you. Or do you fancy swords out on the beach? Mirobolant’s a dead shot with foils, killed four royal guards in the July barricades all by himself, he says.”

“For Christ’s sake,” Pen groaned. “I can’t duel a *cook*.”

“He’s a Chevalier of July! They salute him in France!” Strong cackled.

“Are you seriously asking me to fight a household servant?” Pen snapped. “I ought to call a policeman on him, not duel him!”

“Thank you very much, I’m not getting dragged into a hair-trigger fight with you two idiots,” Strong said, laughing. “I’ve already calmed him down. Told him you didn’t call him cook to be rude, that it’s against English custom for a household employee to offer his arm to the daughter of the house. Also—between you and me—Madame Fribsby just told me he’s head over heels madly in love with Blanche Amory. That’s why he lost his temper, he was about to ask her to dance.”

Pen burst out laughing so hard his sides hurt. All his rage vanished instantly. He’d been exactly that jealous fool over Blanche all night, too, dying for an excuse to pick a fight with anyone who looked at her. He wandered into the coffee room, held out his hand to Mirobolant, and made a very dramatic, very slightly grammatically incorrect French apology, waxing poetic about how sincerely sorry he was to have hurt the great artist’s feelings, and how he never meant to insult him for a second.

“Bravo! Bravo!” Strong cheered, cackling at his terrible but very earnest French. Mirobolant, of course, immediately forgave him, apologized for calling Pen a coward, shook his hand, and the whole mess was sorted. Pen scribbled that note to Laura immediately, then he and Strong piled into the Butcher Boy gig to drive back to Fairoaks.

On the drive back, Strong complimented him on being such a good sport, and on his fluent French. “Mate, you speak French like Chateaubriand himself, I swear.”

“Oh, yeah, been speaking it since I was a kid,” Pen said, preening, and Strong had the decency to hold his laugh in for five whole minutes before he burst into hysterics so hard he nearly fell out of the carriage. Pen never worked out what was so funny.

By the time they rolled up to Fairoaks at dawn, the Baymouth ball was long over. Madame Fribsby and Mirobolant were trundling home in the Clavering family carriage, Laura was fast asleep in a soft bed at Lady Rockminster’s, and Blanche Amory was scribbling a dramatic sonnet to her “murdered, heroic, persecuted father” in her diary, after confiding to her maid that she’d just had the most romantic adventure of her life, meeting a man who’d known her long-lost dad.

Pen got back to Fairoaks and hadn’t said a word of the big proposal he’d been planning to Laura, but he told himself it didn’t matter—she’d be home the day after tomorrow, and he was dead certain Laura would say yes. His mom Helen had spent months gushing about it, after all, Laura was half in love with him already, he just knew it.

When Helen greeted him, all anxious to hear if he’d popped the question, Pen laughed it off, told her there was no way he could propose in the middle of all that chaos, that there was loads of time for sentiment once Laura got home. He spent the next two days preening in the mirror, packing his books and portmanteaus, sorting his papers, cleaning his gun, making all his plans to move to London and make his fortune. He was 23, after all, high time he got serious. He planned to take chambers in an Inn of Court, knock out his half-finished tragedy, comedy and novel, sell them for a fortune, make it big at the bar, marry Laura, and live happily ever after, no need to drain his poor mom’s purse. He wandered around the Fairoaks garden smoking his pipe, telling his old dog Ponto all about his grand plans, and Ponto wagged his tail and nuzzled his hand like he agreed every single word.

Laura rolled home late on the second day, and of course, Pynsent was the one who drove her over. Pen’s face immediately went thunder-dark, much to Laura’s distress, but Helen was none the wiser, desperate to get the two of them alone together so Pen could propose that very night. She faked being tired and announced she was heading up to bed, but Laura blurted out that she was exhausted too, and beat her to it, leaving Pen stuck waiting another whole day to pop the question. He huffed and pouted about being kept waiting like some random suitor in antechambers, then slept like a log all night.

He woke up at dawn to find his mom leaning over his bed, nudging him awake. “Pen, honey, get up! It’s the most beautiful sunny morning ever. I couldn’t sleep a wink after sunrise, Laura’s been out in the garden for an hour already, tending her roses. Go find her!”

Pen laughed, and kissed his mom’s cheek. He knew exactly what she was up to, her little matchmaking scheme, and he thought it was adorable. Half an hour later, he was shaved, dressed in his finest coat, and strolling down the garden path to find Laura, feeling weirdly serious, like he was about to perform a huge sacrifice. *I’m doing this to make mom happy*, he thought. Laura’s sweet, she lent me that money to go to university, I owe it to her. Two of the best women in the world want me to marry her, how bad can it be?

Laura was in her gardening uniform: big floppy straw hat with a bright blue ribbon, a linen pinafore belted tight at the waist, heavy gardening gauntlets on her hands, looking very much like a tiny, sweet soldier out to war with the rose thorns. She spotted him coming, and grinned that exact same grin she’d had the night he face-planted in the waltz, and Pen’s mood soured instantly.

“Sorry for the glove,” she said, pressing his hand gently through the thick leather. “We’re not still cross, are we, Pen?”

“Why are you laughing at me?” Pen said, scowling. “You laughed at me the other night at the ball, made me look a total fool in front of everyone.”

“Darling Arthur, I didn’t mean a bit of harm,” she said, laughing. “You and Miss Roundle looked so daft all tangled up on the floor together, I couldn’t help it! You popped right back up in two seconds, it was Miss Roundle who looked the most miserable, sitting there in her red crape dress all bewildered.”

“Ugh, confound Miss Roundle,” Pen groaned.

“I’m sure she was very confounded,” Laura teased, and did a silly little impression of Miss Roundle’s lost face, before stopping, feeling bad. “We shouldn’t laugh at her, but you can’t blame us for teasing you just a little bit over such a silly little accident, can you?”

“Not you, Laura,” Pen said, bitter. “Of all people, you shouldn’t laugh at me.”

“Oh, really? Are you too grand for a silly joke?” Laura asked, arching an eyebrow.

“No, I’m the most pathetic guy alive,” Pen sighed. “Haven’t you made fun of me enough?”

“I have never made fun of you!” Laura cried, shocked. “I thought a clever man like you wouldn’t mind a little joke from his sister, almost sister. If I hurt your feelings, I’m so sorry, Pen, truly.”

“Your kindness makes me feel worse than the laughter,” Pen said, glum. “You’re always better than me. You lent me that money, remember? I hate feeling like I owe you, and then you laugh at me too,

C’mon, reader, tear yourself away from the Clavering village gossip, Fairoaks’ soggy garden paths, and the nice boring humdrum of west country life, and hop the “Alacrity” stagecoach alongside Arthur Pendennis: he’s off to London for good, ready to wrestle the world into submission and make his fortune. As the wheels spin him further and further away from his home’s familiar creaky gates under the dark night sky, Pen’s brain buzzes with a hundred half-baked life plans, all full of prudence, hard work, and obviously imminent fame and success. He’s dead certain he’s sharper than half the guys who’ve zoomed past him in the life race so far: that first disastrous romantic misadventure left him with a healthy dose of remorse and a lot of time to think, but didn’t kill his nerve, or — let’s be honest — his very high opinion of himself.

A hundred giddy fancies and bright hopes keep him wide awake. He feels a decade older than the callow kid who trundled down this same road to and from Oxbridge only 12 months prior. His thoughts drift soft and tender to his mum, who knelt to bless him goodbye at the gate, who loves and trusts him completely even after all his stupid past follies. He stares up at the stars through the coach window and prays he’s got the grit to work, to stick it out, to stay honest, dodge dumb temptation, and be worthy of that woman who loves him so unreservedly. He’d bet good money she’s awake right now too, sending up far purer, better prayers for his safety to the same God. That mother’s love is his lucky charm, the one thing he’s counting on to keep him on the straight and narrow.
And Laura? He’d love to say he carried her affection with him, but she turned him down flat, and he knows he doesn’t deserve it. He squirms a little with shame admitting her heart and character are far better than his own — and yet, deep down, he’s low-key relieved to be free. “I’m not good enough for someone that perfect,” he tells himself, shrinking back from her spotless goodness the way a guy who snuck off to get drunk every Sunday for a year avoids the church he used to go to as a kid: he’s not mad at it, just knows he’s got no right to walk back in that pure space yet.

With all that rattling around his head, Pen doesn’t doze off until the sharp nipping October dawn creeps over the horizon, and wakes up all refreshed when the coach pulls into that old familiar breakfast stop, the same one he’s eaten a dozen merry meals at on his way to and from school and college since he was a little kid. By the time they pull out, the sun’s blazing bright, the coach is flying, the guard’s horn is blaring, and the milestones are zipping by so fast they’re almost a blur. Pen smokes, jokes with the guard, his fellow passengers, and every random person hanging around the roadside stops, and the whole road gets busier, louder, more exciting by the minute. The last team of dappled greys swap in at the next stop, and the Alacrity rolls straight into London.
What young lad from the country hasn’t felt that electric little zing when he first lays eyes on the sprawling, roaring big city? Hundreds of other carriages full of thousands of strangers are all hustling towards the same chaos. “This is my spot!” Pen thinks, practically bouncing on the coach roof. “This is where my fight starts. I’ve got to win, or face plant trying. I’ve been a lazy dawdling boy long enough. I want to prove I can be a proper man.” He leans over the edge of the roof, gawking down at the endless streets, with that same feral giddy excitement a new 19-year-old private gets the night before his first battle.

On the ride in, Pen struck up a chat with a cheery fellow passenger wrapped in a shabby old cloak, who would not stop gossiping about all the famous literary blokes he swore were his closest personal pals. Turns out he was Doolan, a London newspaper reporter, heading home after covering a big professional wrestling match out west. This guy apparently knew every single well-known writer of the era, chatting about Tom Campbell, Tom Hood, Sydney Smith and all the rest like they drank pints with him every Tuesday. When they passed Brompton, he points out Mr. Hurtle, the legendary vicious book reviewer, ambling along with his umbrella. Pen cranes so far over the coach railing to stare at the great man he nearly topples right off. “He went to Boniface College, same as me!” Pen crows. “Faith, he did, and I know him like the back of my hand,” Doolan replies. Pen is over the moon just to have gotten a glimpse of the reviewer whose work he’s obsessed with. At this point in his life, he still believes every author, reviewer, and newspaper editor is a magical genius, even that hack Wagg whose books he doesn’t even think are that good — he still secretly reveres him as a successful writing legend. When he mentions he ran into Wagg out in the countryside, Doolan drops the bombshell that Wagg makes three hundred pounds *per volume* for his novels. Pen’s brain immediately does the maths — wait, that means I could make five grand a year? No problem!

The very first person Pen recognises the second the coach pulls up at the Gloster Coffee House is his old pal Harry Foker, prancing down Arlington Street behind an absurdly enormous cab horse, wearing crisp white kid gloves and holding white reins, his chin now sporting a very proud tuft of new facial hair. A tiny knock-kneed cabby boy in skin-tight leather breeches swings off the back of Foker’s custom ride. Foker spots the dusty old Alacrity he used to ride as a kid, and blinks. “Pen! My boy!” he yells, waving his whip, and Pen whoops back, thrilled to see his weird old mate’s friendly face. Mr. Doolan is suddenly extremely impressed that Pen has a friend who owns such a fancy private cab, and Pen is bouncing off the walls with excitement that he’s actually, finally free in London. He invites Doolan to eat dinner with him that night at the Covent Garden Coffee House, where he’s booked a room, hops in a random hack cab, and rattles off there in the highest of spirits. He’s so glad to see the bustling overworked waiters and bowing landlord he remembers from years prior, he asks after the landlady, misses the old boots man, and would have happily shaken every single employee’s hand if he could. He’s got a hundred whole pounds tucked in his pocket! He pulls on his very best suit, eats a modest dinner with one single pint of sherry (he’s determined to be extremely economical, thank you very much), and trots off to the theater right next door.

The bright lights, blaring music, massive crowd and giddy vibe completely charm him, the way those things always charm young lads fresh out of college and the country who haven’t seen a proper big city show yet. He cackles at every bad joke, cheers loud enough to make his throat sore at every song, to the complete bewilderment of all the grumpy old regulars in the boxes who haven’t felt a single spark of excitement at the theater in 20 years, who are delighted to see someone young and dumb and happy to be there. After the first act ends, he struts up and down the theater lobbies like he owns the place, like he’s at the fanciest royal reception in town. Every single tired grown-up who’s lived in London for 10 years must remember that exact silly first delusion, and wish they could get that giddy naivety back, right?
And sure enough, there’s Harry Foker again, the dedicated professional party boy, hanging around with Grandy Tiptoff of the Household Brigade, the brother of Lord Tiptoff, and Lord Colchicum, Tiptoff’s ancient uncle — a very old peer who’s been partying nonstop ever since the first French Revolution kicked off. Foker spots Pen, barrels over to drag him straight into his private box, where a lady with the longest ringlets and fairest shoulders you’ve ever seen is sitting. That’s Miss Blenkinsop, the famous star of high comedy, and dozing in the back of the box in a very obvious ill-fitting wig is old Blenkinsop, her dad. The theater papers call him “the veteran Blenkinsop,” “the reliable Blenkinsop,” “the nation’s favourite old trouper Blenkinsop” — he spends his whole career playing the grumpy, stuffy “heavy father” roles in every drama, and acts exactly the same grumpy stuffy heavy father in real life, too.

Meanwhile back home in Fairoaks, Mrs. Pendennis has just gone to bed, wondering if her dearest Arthur is fast asleep after his long journey. Laura’s still awake too, wide-eyed. 24 hours prior, Pen was rumbling over quiet west country commons dotted with glowing cottage windows and under dark starlit woods, swearing he’d reform, resist all temptation, and keep his heart tied to home. Right now, the farce on stage is going down a storm, and Mrs. Leary, decked out in a hussar jacket and braided pantaloons, is enchanting every single person in the audience with her cheeky grin, great figure, and catchy ballads.

Pen, new in town, would have loved to sit and listen to her sing, but the rest of the people in the box couldn’t care less about Mrs. Leary or her songs, chattering nonstop the whole time. Tiptoff knows exactly where she got her stage tights from. Colchicum saw her perform back in 1814. Miss Blenkinsop declares her singing is completely off key, which shocks Pen — who thinks Mrs. Leary is as beautiful as an angel and sings better than a nightingale. When Hoppus walks on stage as Sir Harcourt Featherby, the young hero of the farce, the guys in the box all complain he’s way too old for the part, and Tiptoff threatens to throw Miss Blenkinsop’s bouquet at his head.
“Absolutely not!” Miss Blenkinsop squawks. “Lord Colchicum gave that to me!”
Pen perks up, blushes, and bows. He says he owes Lord Colchicum a favour — he proposed Pen for membership at the Megatherium Club, at the request of Pen’s uncle Major Pendennis.
“What, you old Wigsby’s nephew, are you?” the old peer says. “Pardon the familiarity, but we all call your uncle Wigsby behind his back. We voted you in last week, didn’t we? Last Wednesday. Your uncle didn’t even show up.”
Pen is over the moon, gushes a whole fancy speech thanking the old lord, who just stares at him through his double opera glass. Pen can’t believe he’s officially a member of the fancy posh club.
“Stop gawking at that other box, you naughty thing!” Miss Blenkinsop yells at Tiptoff.
“Mirabel’s one hell of a fine looking woman, isn’t she?” Tiptoff says. “Though Mirabel was a total fool to marry her.”
“Silly old softie,” the old peer agrees.
“Mirabel?” Pen echoes, confused.
Harry Foker snorts laughing. “Oh we’ve definitely heard of her before, haven’t we, Pen?”
It was Pen’s first ever great love. Miss Emily Fotheringay. A year prior, she’d walked down the aisle with Sir Charles Mirabel, Knight Grand Cross of the Bath, former envoy to the Court of Pumpernickel, who’d had a very important role in all the peace negotiations before the Congress of Swammerdam, and signed the Peace of Pultusk on behalf of the British crown.
“Emily was always as thick as an owl,” Miss Blenkinsop sniffs.
“Eh, eh — not that stupid,” the old peer rumbles.
“Oh for shame!” the actress cries, having no idea what he just said.
Pen stares out at her across the theater, and sees his first love again — and suddenly he can’t for the life of him work out what he ever saw in her in the first place.

That’s how, on his very first night in London, Pen gets himself introduced to a fancy gentleman’s club, a famous leading lady, a grumpy veteran stage dad, and a whole crew of jolly party lads both young and old. Lord Colchicum is ancient, balding, and wobbly on his feet, but he still parties harder than blokes half his age, and brags he can drink more claret than any 20-something in his social circle. He hangs around with young “men about town” all the time, throws endless huge dinners at Richmond and Greenwich, is a massive patron of every kind of stage performance from opera to ballet, and invites every type of performer — English, Italian, French, you name it — to his banquets. His villa on the Thames hosts sumptuous parties for hordes of posh young guys, who are all very happy to hang out with all the greenroom actors and actresses (mostly the actresses, because Lord Colchicum swears they’re way more fun and witty than the blokes).

The next day, Pen trots over to the club and pays his membership entrance fee, which eats exactly a third of his 100-pound travel fund immediately. He wanders around the fancy building, eats lunch there grinning like an idiot, flops into an armchair in the library and tries to read every magazine on the shelf, wondering if all the other members are staring at him and thinking how weird it is that everyone is allowed to keep their hats on indoors. He sits down at a desk and writes a long letter to his mum in Fairoaks on the fancy club letterhead, gushing about how amazing this place will be to relax in after a long day of work.
Then he heads over to his uncle Major Pendennis’s lodgings on Bury Street, a little nervous, just like his mum begged him to, and is very relieved to find the Major hasn’t come back to London yet. The apartment is completely empty, covered in dust sheets, bills and unopened letters piled up on the mantelpiece waiting for the owner to return. The landlady tells him the Major’s off in Badnbadn on the continent, vacationing with the Marquess of Steyne. Pen leaves his card on the mantelshelf with all the rest, still marked “Fairoaks” under his name.

When the Major does get back to London right as November’s thick yellow fog rolls in, planning to spend Christmas at a country friend’s house after he’s suffered through a few weeks of London fog, he finds another card of Arthur’s, this one engraved with “Lamb Court, Temple,” plus a note from Pen and his mum saying he’s moved to London, joined the Upper Temple law school, and is studying hard to become a barrister.
Lamb Court, Temple? Where the hell is that? The Major vaguely remembers some posh society ladies talking once about dining with Mr. Ayliffe, the famous barrister who “moves in high society” and lived there, and he swears Lamb Court is probably a part of the old debtors’ prison near the King’s Bench. He sends his valet Morgan off to find out exactly where Lamb Court is, and report back on what kind of digs Pen has landed himself in. Morgan is the kind of discreet guy who could track down a runaway in hiding three counties over, so he finds Pen’s apartment in 10 minutes flat.
“What sort of place is it, Morgan?” the Major asks from behind his bed curtains the next morning, as his valet helps him dress in the thick, murky yellow London fog.
“Bit of a quiet, run-down little spot, sir,” Morgan says. “All lawyers live there, paint their names on their doors. Mr. Arthur’s three flights up, sir. A Mr. Warrington lives there too.”
“Warrington from the Suffolk Warringtons! Good family, that,” the Major thinks. “Loads of well-connected younger sons go into the law for a living. Comfy rooms, I suppose?”
“Only saw the outside of the door, sir, with their two names painted on it, and a scrap of paper stuck up saying ‘Back at 6.’ No sign of a servant anywhere, sir.”
“At least he’s being economical,” the Major says.
“Extremely, sir. Three flights up, sir. Nasty black moldy staircase the likes of which I’ve never seen. Can’t imagine how a gentleman would choose to live there.”
“And since when are you the expert on where gentlemen should and shouldn’t live, Morgan?” the Major huffs, very dignified. “Arthur’s studying for the bar, you know.” He closes the conversation, and gets dressed in the thick yellow fog.
“Boys will be boys,” the soft-hearted uncle thinks to himself. “He wrote me a damn good letter. Colchicum says he met him at dinner, thinks he’s a proper gentlemanly kid. His mum’s one of the nicest souls alive. If he’s sown all his wild oats and decides to stick to his work, he could turn out very well. Can you believe old fool Charley Mirabel married that girl Pen had a crush on, the Fotheringay? And Pen’s being polite, waiting for me to reach out before he shows up at my place, instead of waltzing in uninvited. He put it very nicely, very manly. I was furious with him after all his silly Oxbridge escapades, made it very clear when he was here before — but gad, I’ll go visit him, damn it all.”

Once Morgan confirms he can get to the Temple easily via city omnibus, the Major heads over one morning after breakfast at his own club (not the new-fangled Megatherium that Pen just joined — the Major is far too smart to have his nephew hanging around the same club he frequents, after all). He hops on the omnibus, tells the conductor to drop him right at the Upper Temple gate.
It’s about noon when he steps through the Temple’s dingy old entrance. A friendly porter in a white apron shows him through a bunch of dark alleys and sad little archways, every court more dreary than the last, until he finally finds Lamb Court. If it was dark and foggy over in Pall Mall, it’s pitch black in Lamb Court. Candles are already burning in half the rooms: in special pleader Mr. Hodgeman’s pupil room, where six law students scribble out legal documents under the flicker of tallow candles; in

Universities, ancient public schools, and the Inns of Court are stubbornly stuck in the 1600s, bless their stuffy old hearts, clinging to all the weird customs our great-great-great grandpas dreamt up that every sensible person dumped decades ago. You know what’s far better set up for clean, comfortable living than a venerated centuries-old college or a learned Inn of Court? A properly run workhouse, that’s what. Over in the Temple, grown men happily pay the price of an entire nice suburban villa just for two tiny rooms: a sitting space, and a dingy little closet they call a bedroom to crash in. A random mechanic in Spitalfields gets unlimited running water right out of a tap any time he wants, but the high-and-mighty barristers? They make their laundresses haul water up three flights of stairs in dented jugs, like running water’s some unholy new fad for peasants.

Plenty of grumpy rich gents out there love sneering at the “unwashed masses” like their own ancestors weren’t literally nicknamed the Great Unwashed back in the day. The Temple in particular? You’d have had to fight tooth and nail to practice that virtue everyone says comes right after godliness (that’s cleanliness, in case you missed the hint). Take Old Grump, of the Norfolk Circuit, who’s lived under Warrington and Pen’s chambers for 30 whole years. He gets woken up at 7 a.m. every morning by the roar of their shower bath, and half the time the water leaks straight through his ceiling onto his law papers. He’s been cussing showers every single day as a ridiculous new-fangled dandy fad, and yells at their laundress every time she drips water on the staircase he has to walk down. The man is pushing 60, and he’s never once set foot in a shower in his whole life, convinced all his ancestors did just fine going their whole lives without washing too. All those fancy knights and lords with their coats of arms painted on the Upper Temple hall walls? Not a single one of them thought to install a public bathhouse for their lawyer pals, did they? The local Temple history nerd never wrote a word about it. Sure there’s Pump Court and Fountain Court, but no one’s ever seen a senior judge splashing around in the fountain. Shocking, how many old barristers could’ve used a good soak.

For all their weird grime and backwards ways, though, those Inns with their Lamb and Flag and Winged Horse emblems are weirdly charming: that rough, no-rules bachelor comfort you still think about fondly years later. Law students might be too busy cramming torts to get poetic, but any book lover wandering past those old chambers gets starry eyed thinking about the greats who lived there: Eldon scribbling case notes, Coke obsessing over Littleton, Fielding pounding out newspaper articles at 2 a.m. with ink all over his cuffs while his printer’s kid naps on the landing, Dr. Johnson tramping through the fog to visit Goldsmith in Brick Court, even Sir Roger de Coverley strolling the Temple Gardens gawking at ladies in their huge hoop skirts. If some mischievous Temple Asmodeus could sneak us a full day’s play-by-play of one of those four-story dingy court houses where Pen and Warrington live, you’d end up with one very chaotic, very funny book.

Take the ground floor: there’s a hotshot parliamentary barrister who makes tens of thousands a year, drives out to Belgravia for fancy dinners every night. Not long ago, though, he was a starving unknown stuck in a garret, scribbling anonymous penny-a-line literature just to eat, waiting years for a single client, dodging angry debt collectors, convinced he was ruined forever before he hit that lucky jackpot on the Bar’s weird lottery wheel. There are hundreds of better lawyers than him who make less than his old clerk, who a year ago couldn’t even get shopkeepers to extend him credit for boot blacking for his boss’s unpaid shoes.

First floor? There’s that ancient famous scholar Mr. Doomsday, who’s lived in the same chambers for 50 years, his shelves crammed full of classics and legal books, all alone, hoarding knowledge and money like a tiny book dragon. He trundles home from his club after dinner every night, to his empty chambers, no wife, no kids, no mates, nothing but his books. When he kicks the bucket, the Inn will put up a fancy little plaque honoring him, and his ungrateful heirs will toss half his library straight into the fire. Sounds like a great old age plan, right?

We can’t hang around his door too long, though. Right above him lives our old mate Grump, who’s holed up with three other equally ancient barristers for their regular weekly rubber of whist, after polishing off three full bottles of port between them. You can spot all four of them fast asleep in the pews at Temple Church every single Sunday, no attorney has bothered them for 20 years, they’ve all got little nest eggs enough to never work a day again.

On the other side of that third landing, where Pen and Warrington live, is Mr. Paley, the super-achiever college fellow who got all the top honours back in his university days. He reads and scribbles case notes until 2 a.m. every night, wakes up at 7 a.m. sharp, works at the pleader’s offices straight through until an hour before dinner, then comes home to cram right up until dawn the next day. Half the time he’s just blowing out his lamp to head to work when Pen and Warrington are stumbling home from some late-night pub adventure. Everyone praises Paley as the hardest working bloke alive, everyone says Warrington’s wasting his whole life lazing about. But who’s actually using his time better? Paley never has a spare minute to think, never makes a friend he doesn’t think will advance his career, never stops to look at a pretty sunset, read a poem, listen to a nice song. His whole world ends at the edge of his reading lamp. Art, love, nature? He’s locked all of it out, just so he can memorize thousands of pages of tedious legal jargon to make a living. He still shivers when he runs into Warrington on the stairs, like Warrington’s a hellbound sinner who’s throwing his whole life away.

That very pale, pinched, ambition-crazed look on Paley’s face is half the reason Pen never had the energy to grind his way to becoming a judge. He was having way too much fun living the easy Temple bachelor life. His uncle Major Pendennis was writing gushing congratulatory letters back to Pen’s widowed mom at Fairoaks, swearing up and down that Pen had sown all his wild oats, was a changed hardworking law student now. The truth was, Pen had dropped all the stuffy fancy-gentleman airs he’d picked up around his aristocratic college mates, and the rough, cheap fun of London bachelor life was brand new and thrilling to him. He didn’t even care anymore that he couldn’t afford fancy horses to ride in Rotten Row, he was just happy strolling through the park looking at everyone else’s. His days were perfect: a few hours of law reading in the morning, a walk in the park, a row on the Thames, a hike up to Hampstead, a cheap hearty tavern dinner, some nights out with the boys having innocent fun (Pen thought women were so great he refused to hang around any he didn’t think was proper and good), quiet nights at home with Warrington, a couple of pipes, a bottle of cheap gin that their laundress Mrs. Flanagan always insisted on taste-testing first to make sure it wasn’t swill. No wonder he never wanted to go back to boring stuffy high society parties. The Major would’ve had a full-on heart attack if he found out how little actual law studying Pen was doing.

The only law-related thing Pen was actually perfectly diligent about was showing up to eat his required dinners in Hall, like every law student has to. The Upper Temple Hall is a delightfully weird time capsule, the kind of place you sit down to dinner and feel like you’re eating a 17th century meal. The judges sit up on the raised high table under all those portraits of old kings and dead chief justices, the working barristers have their own messes, and the students sit at their own tables, a mixed bunch of everyone: 60-year-old grey-haired attorneys working to qualify as barristers, dandies who just want the “barrister” title for clout, sunburnt colonial students who’ve come over from the Caribbean to qualify before they go home, a whole herd of Irish lads hanging around Middle Temple Lane before they go back to Ireland. There are the nerds who talk nothing but law the whole dinner, the rowing boys who only chat about sculling matches and the Opera, the political nerds who yell at each other about debating club meetings. Pen made friends with every single group except the law nerds, whose whole conversation was gibberish to him.

The Temple charges you practically nothing for a full dinner: soup, roast meat, tarts, a glass of port or sherry. You sit in messes of four, each quartet gets a whole roast joint, an apple pie, a full bottle of wine. The veteran students have all kinds of silly little schemes to score better food than the basic roast, what they call “dodges.” One of those veteran food schemers was a bloke named Lowton, who grabbed Pen’s gown on his very first night. “Hang on a minute, mate,” Lowton said, eyes glued to the benchers’ high table, “there’s only three old judges up there facing 12 silver covered dishes. They’re never going to eat all that. If we hang back a minute, we might get their leftover turtle soup!”

Lowton was the undisputed captain of every mess he joined, always fighting to get the prime cut of meat, hoarding the gravy, making sure he was the one to grab the 13th glass from the bottle so he got an extra pour. His greatest dream in life never cost more than 18 pence, bless his heart. He immediately latched onto Pen, who looked like a proper well-bred gent way too posh to hang around the other scruffy students. “It’s boiled beef day today, innit, sir?” he said to Pen, starting the conversation. Pen, who had no idea what day it was, laughed and said it was his very first term. Lowton immediately gave him a full guided tour of the room, pointing out every notable: the bald bencher Boosey who’s probably eating turtle soup, old Grump who’s been eating dinner here 40 years, the fancy rich lads at the opposite table — the Bishop of Ealing’s son, Lord Cinqbar’s little brother, who go to all the fancy society parties that get written up in the Morning Post. “You think those blokes are gonna eat this dinner?” Lowton snickered. “Their carriages are waiting right outside Essex Street this second. They’re gonna sit here for ten minutes, then bounce off to a fancy banquet. If I get put in their mess? I get the entire bottle of port all to myself, free!”

Then he pointed out the Irish contingent: the flashily dressed Molloy Maloney who brags nonstop about his uncle the Major-General Sir Hector O’Dowd, who showed up to Hall once in silver striped trousers the day he got presented at court, the fiery radical who gives rants at the debating club that make everyone’s blood run cold, and Jack Finucane the newspaper reporter. Pen was already delighted with the chaotic Lowton, when a ragged-looking barrister in a faded old gown and a shirt that was clean but definitely had frayed cuffs trundled over, couldn’t find a seat anywhere else, and plopped down right next to Pen. He glanced at Pen’s name scribbled on the mess slip, and grinned. “Hey, we’re both Boniface College boys, aren’t we? I’m Warrington.”

Pen’s face lit right up. He’d heard stories about the legendary Warrington, the college hero who’d thrashed the abusive bargee Bill Simes back at Oxford. They immediately fell to chatting about old college pranks, Pen even stopped pretending he hated port wine when Warrington poured himself a big glass, much to Lowton’s disappointment. After dinner, Pen said he was planning to go dress up and hear Grisi sing in *Norma* at the opera. “Nah, that’s boring,” Warrington said. “Come back to my place in Lamb Court, smoke a quick pipe with me, we can gush about old college times for hours.”

They left, Lowton staring after them wistfully, reverent that Pen was mates with a baronet’s son. That night Pen and Warrington became best friends for life. Pen liked hanging around the cheap pubs with Warrington way more than he ever liked stuffy fancy parties, and within a few weeks he’d given up his expensive St. James’s lodgings, moved straight in with Warrington at Lamb Court, and their two staff — Pidgeon the errand boy and Mrs. Flanagan the laundress — now split their work looking after the two of them.

Arthur Pendennis was absolutely buzzing with the thrill of “experiencing real life” his first few months loose in London, bouncing between every weird dive and hole-in-the-wall the city had to offer like a golden retriever who’d just escaped the garden gate. He loved to imagine he was mixing with every strata of society, gawking at coal heavers in their taprooms, bare-knuckle boxers in their pub parlours, and suburban day-trippers messing about on the Thames. He’d have gladly clinked ale mugs with a famous pickpocket or sat down for a pint with a crew of burglars if the chance ever popped up, he was that determined to collect “authentic” characters.

His deadpan, unflappable flatmate Warrington was his perfect tour guide, it turned out. Arthur could barely keep a straight face watching Warrington lean in all earnest to chat boxing stats with the Tutbury Pet at the Champion’s Arms, or swap shift gossip with the coal heavers camped out at the Fox-under-the-Hill. Warrington knew every pub within 10 miles of central London, was on first name terms with every landlord and landlady, and got free run of the bar just as easily as he got a seat at their fancy club back in the Temple. He swore up and down he liked pub company better than his own stuffy upper-class crowd. “At fancy society do’s everyone’s identical,” he’d grumble. “Same suit, same boring small talk, same overpriced claret. One dandy at a club is a carbon copy of the next, one debutante at a ball is indistinguishable from the rest. Here? Everyone’s got actual personality. I’d rather drink gin and water than claret any day, sit on a wooden bench on a sanded Carnaby Market floor than a fancy sofa on a chalked carpet in Mayfair. I’ll say it loud: I prefer hanging out with unapologetic snobs to boring posers.” This guy was a full-on social republican, and never once thought he was better than the working lads he drank with — though he definitely secretly enjoyed how deferential they all were to him.

Pen followed him everywhere, grinning from ear to ear, full of his own silly 20-something pomposity. He fancied himself a prince in disguise, slumming it among the loyal subjects of his father’s non-existent vast kingdom. The pub crew all thought he was a swell high chap, a nice generous young toff, and his natural air of easy, imperious good humour sold the bit perfectly — even though all he was actually heir to was tuppence halfpenny, barely two generations removed from a shopkeeper selling medicine pots. Pen’s whole “oh so gracious condescension” schtick at this point in his life was equal parts ridiculous and charming, the kind of silly innocent conceit that only fades once you hit 30 and realise no one’s actually impressed that you went to university.

Their routine was reliably great: they’d pretend to study law for half the morning, then get distracted reading political pamphlets, novels, and the newspaper until dinnertime rolled around, then charge out onto the town with the kind of energy only a 24-year-old with zero hangovers and no real responsibilities can pull off. Those are the golden days, aren’t they? When every part of you feels sharp and healthy, the whole world feels brand new, and you can drink half a dozen pints and still get up the next day without feeling like you got run over by a cart. The old tunes you hum when you’re grey are the ones you learned back then, the lads you got drunk with then are the only ones who still make you feel 20 again — even if now the pleasure gardens are run down, the old garlands are tattered, half the lights have gone out, and the only thing that greets you when you wake up at dawn is a headache instead of the sunrise over the Thames.

Pen’s mum Helen and his guardian Laura would have had a collective heart attack if they’d seen the pair of them rattling over the Temple flagstones at 5am, still half tipsy, after one of their wild nights out. But the nights weren’t nearly as wicked as they could have been: Warrington was a self-declared woman-hater, and Pen was too much of a silly romantic to mess around with any lady who wasn’t a literal goddess in his head. He could barely speak to a barmaid without being unfailingly polite, and cringed away from any coarse joke like he’d been burned. Guys only treat women like royalty when they’re kneeling, after all — the second they stand up, they leave.

That’s exactly what an old friend of Pen’s told him one night, over a frothing pint. It was none other than good old Mr. Bows, formerly of the Chatteris Theatre, now working as the resident piano man at the Back Kitchen, a tiny, raucous club tucked away in the back of the Fielding’s Head pub in Covent Garden.

Half of Pen’s casual London mates were regulars there. The Fielding’s Head had been a famous boozer ever since Henry Fielding himself was the local Bow Street magistrate back in the 1700s, and they still pointed out his old supposedly original chair that the night’s host sat in. That host was almost always landlord Cutts, unless he was laid up with gout. He was a big jolly guy with a booming baritone who sang nonstop during their weekly harmonic meetings, all hits from the British Brandy-and-Water school of ballads: *The Good Old English Gentleman*, *Dear Tom This Brown Jug*, all the songs about patriotism, good beer, and dying happy with your mates around you. He could work the whole room up crying over a song about a dying old soldier, then before the applause had even died down, he was shoving a menu at you going “C’mon lads, place your orders! John, get that gentleman his sausages and mash, Mr Green’s got his champagne cup over there!”

“And a glass of punch for me too, John, and make sure the water’s boiling!” a familiar voice would yell across the room, making Pen jump and blush the first time he heard it. It was the one and only Captain Costigan, now based full time in London, and one of the Back Kitchen’s most beloved regulars.

The Captain’s chaotic stories drew dozens of young lads to the pub to hang around him. He was a local celebrity almost overnight, ever since his daughter Emily married the super rich Sir Charles Mirabel. He’d corner every stranger drinking next to him to ramble about “me daughther”: her fancy carriages, how madly Sir Charles adored her, how he had full permission to withdraw a hundred pounds from his son-in-law’s bank whenever he wanted. Then he’d casually add that he planned to “draw the money next Saturday, I give ye me word of honour, lads, right at Coutts the second I show the cheque” — and immediately follow that up by begging the guy next to him for half a crown “to tide me over till then, I’ll pay ye back the second the cheque clears.”

Sir Charles, for the record, wasn’t nearly as fond of his father-in-law as Costigan claimed — though on nights he was extra sloshed, Costigan would sob to everyone around him about his ungrateful daughter and her stingy rich husband. They were decent enough to him, though, settling a small regular pension on him that the Captain cashed before the week was even half over. On pay day, he’d storm straight into the Back Kitchen waving the banknote, yelling at the top of his lungs for the landlord to change it before he even sat down. “That note’s good for the Bank of England, Cutts me boy!” he’d roar. “Bows, get yourself a drink, no skimping tonight! A glass of punch’ll make you play all the more spirited!” He was lavishly generous with his money when he had it, and only ever buttoned his breeches pocket the second the last penny was gone, or a creditor wandered through the door.

It was on one of these pay day highs that Pen spotted him, swaggering over at the singers’ table handing out free brandy and water to every mate that walked in. Warrington, who was already mates with the pub’s bass singer, steered Pen straight over that way.

Pen’s face lit up. He’d just run into Emily Mirabel at a fancy party the night before, the first time he’d seen her in years, back when she was the absolute centre of his whole stupid teenage world. He ran over with his hand outstretched, ready to greet the old man like a long lost relative. The Captain, whose grip had gone wobbly from decades of lifting pints of punch, shook his hand hard, stared right at Pen’s face, and slurred: “Good heavens, is that you? Me dear boy, me dear old friend — wait. I know yer face. Bedad, I can’t for the life of me remember yer name.” Five years of nonstop punch had addled his memory, and Arthur had changed a lot since they last met, so the guy had a pretty good excuse.

Pen laughed, a little bit mortified, but not offended. “It’s me, Captain! Arthur Pendennis, from Chatteris!”

The sound of his name snapped Costigan’s foggy brain right back into focus, and he exploded in a volley of over-the-top greetings. Pen was his dearest gallant young friend, he’d kept him close in his heart all these years, how was his — wait, his father? No, his mother, his guardian the Major? “I can tell by yer fancy getup ye’ve come into yer inheritance, haven’t ye? And bedad ye’ll spend it like a proper gentleman, I’ll bet. No? Not yet? Well look, if ye need a few quid, I’ve got a couple of guineas right here, me dear boy, you’ll never want for anything as long as old Jack Costigan’s around! John! Get this gentleman a glass of punch, on me! And yer friend there, I’ve seen him before, sir! Allow me to introduce meself, have a glass of punch on the house!”

Pen made a mental note that he definitely did not envy Sir Charles Mirabel his father-in-law. “Where’s me old friend Mr Bows, Captain?” he asked. “Have you seen him lately?”

“Sure he’s around,” Costigan said, jingling his money and whistling his famous party tune *The Little Doodeen*. “Me dear boy — wait I forgot yer name again, but it’s Costigan, Jack Costigan! Drink as many punch tumblers in my name as ye want, I’m not ashamed of it.” And he wandered off, waffling to the next guy at the bar.

“Pay day for the old General,” the bass singer Hodgen whispered to Warrington. “He’s three sheets to the wind. He already butchered that Little Doodeen number ten minutes ago right before I sang King Death. Hey, you heard my new hit *The Body Snatcher*? Wrote it special, first performed at St Bartholomew’s the other week. You two want a copy? John, hand over one of the song sheets — there’s a portrait of me on the front in costume, sitting on a coffin with a spade, everyone says it’s a perfect likeness.”

“Thanks,” Warrington said dryly. “Heard it nine times already. Know it by heart.”

Just then the piano started up, and Pen looked over to see that exact Mr Bows he’d been asking about, sitting in front of a beat-up old piano that sounded hoarse and wobbly from decades of late nights. Bows had spotted Pen the second he walked in, and noticed how warmly he’d greeted the sloshed Captain. He struck up an old familiar tune, the one that used to play in the Chatteris Theatre right before Emily’s big entrance in *The Stranger*. Pen’s chest got all tight — he could remember exactly how his heart used to pound hearing that exact music, waiting for her to walk on stage. No one else in the raucous pub noticed the tune, though, not over the clatter of cutlery, people yelling for poached eggs and kidneys, and waiters running back and forth.

Pen walked over at the end of the song and shook the old man’s hand. “You haven’t forgotten the old tune, have you Mr Pendennis?” Bows said, grinning. “I knew you’d remember. That was the first fancy theatre tune you ever heard, wasn’t it? You were just a little kid back then. The Captain’s proper wasted tonight, pay day madness, I’m gonna have to drag him home later. We still share a flat, you know — still running the old partnership, even if Miss Em — Lady Mirabel — left the firm. You remember those old days, huh? She was a right beauty, wasn’t she? Your very good health, sir.” He took a long sip of the porter sitting next to his piano.

Pen popped back to visit them both again and again after that, rebuilding his old friendship with the old musician and the chaotic Captain. The Back Kitchen always had a rotating cast of the most random people you could imagine: country farmers in from the villages on business, young apprentices sneaking out after they locked their shop shutters, scruffy loud medical students yelling along to the songs, posh university boys with that weird unplaceable Alma Mater smug smirk, handsome guards officers, St James’s Street bucks, even Irish and English senators, and a couple of actual members of the House of Lords.

Hodgen’s *The Body Snatcher* was the biggest hit in the whole city, and everyone was turning up just to see him perform. He’d pull back the little curtain, sit on a prop coffin with a gin flask, a spade, and a candle stuck in a human skull, and belt out the song in a deep rumbly voice that made everyone’s skin prickle, before slamming his spade on the table and letting out a demon cackle so loud all the glasses on every table rattled. Even landlord Cutts himself admitted he couldn’t compete, so he’d sneak off to the bar or his private rooms before the song started. After Hodgen finished, most of the crowd cleared out, leaving only the hardest partiers still there to listen to Costigan mumble his *Little Doodeen* for the two or three drunks left who still cared.

One night, well after midnight, two familiar regulars wandered in almost at the same time. “Mr Hoolan and Mr Doolan,” Warrington whispered to Pen. Pen recognised Doolan right away — that was the guy from the Alacrity coach, who’d turned down Pen’s dinner invitation because he said he could never make Friday dinners for work reasons.

Doolan’s newspaper *The Dawn* was splayed on the table, stained with porter, right next to Hoolan’s rival paper *The Day*. *The Dawn* was rabidly Liberal, *The Day* ultra-Conservative. Half of London’s newspapers back then were staffed by Irish lads, who’d show up to work for whatever side paid them best, then meet up at the pub after to drink pints like best mates no matter what they wrote that day.

“Kidneys John, and a glass of stout!” Hoolan yelled. “How are you Morgan? How’s Mrs Doolan?”

“Grand, thanks Mick,” Doolan said. “She’s used to it by now. I might pop over to Kilburn on Sunday, we’ll have a glass of punch over at yours.”

“Don’t bring your kid Patsy, our Georgy’s got the measles,” Hoolan said, and then the pair dived straight into shop talk: foreign mail schedules, who the Paris correspondent was, how much the rival morning paper was wasting on couriers, who had the biggest circulation.

Warrington laughed, picked up *The Dawn*, and pointed at a scathing leading article that started off by calling their rival *The Day* a gang of assassins who hired thugs to ruin innocent people’s reputations. “How’d you lot like that article at your place, Mick?” he asked. “Your Captain Shandon wrote that in two hours, sitting in the pub while the errand boy waited outside.”

“Our governor says no one cares about newspaper catfights anymore,” Hoolan said. “Told Dr Boyne not to even bother writing a reply. The Doc was gutted, he says writing mean insults is the easiest work you can do, no need to do any research at all.”

“Eloquence is dead these days,” Doolan sighed.

“Sure is,” Hoolan agreed. “Remember back when the Doc wrote for *The Phoenix* and him and Condy Roony wrote page long insults at each other every single day?”

“Didn’t they duel twice too?” Doolan laughed. “Roony winged the Doc the second time.”

“They’re talking about Dr Boyne and Captain Shandon,” Warrington explained to Pen. “The two top writers for the rival papers, who are actually best mates in real life. They’ll write 10,000 words a day calling each other traitors, then go out drinking together after work.”

“Your servant Mr Warrington, Mr Pendennis!” Doolan said, bowing very formally. “I’ve thought about that lovely night we spent on top of the Alacrity coach a hundred times, and told my wife all about it. I’ve even seen your friend Mr Foker in here a few times! Back when we met I was working on the *Tom and Jerry Weekly*, but now I’m sub-editor of *The Dawn* — one of the best written newspapers in the whole empire,” he added, with a polite little nod to Warrington, his tone suddenly all formal and posh, nothing like the rough casual brogue he used with his fellow Irishman.

Pen had been bumbling through his tiny, cheap, delightfully cozy little streak of revels for months now, completely oblivious that a giant metaphorical guillotine was hanging over his last crumbs of cash, waiting to chop all his frolics short at any second. His funds were down to the dregs. His club subscription had already eaten a full third of his remaining money; he’d splurged on cute little knickknacks to make his dingy rented bedsit feel fancy, and now all that was left in his pocketbook was a single crumpled five-pound note, with absolutely zero plan to replace it. The poor fool had been raised like a coddled royal toddler, never once having to fend for himself or wonder where next week’s grocery money came from.

Warrington had no clue his best mate was basically one pint of porter away from being fully skint. Pen was an only kid, after all, with a widowed mom in the country and a dandy old uncle who dined with lords every other night, so for all Warrington knew the guy had a secret fortune stashed under his mattress. He carried a gold chain shiny enough for a duke, owned a dressing case fit for a peer, and while he’d happily chow down on a plate of cheap cookshop beef and a pint of porter with zero complaints, he couldn’t be arsed with the tiny penny-pinching bits of real life. He’d never tip a waiter twopence if his life depended on it, would hail a cab the second a single raindrop hit his shoulder, and overpaid every driver by sixpence out of sheer laziness. He scoffed at people who rewashed their gloves to make them last, and couldn’t say no to any beggar with a half-sad story or a pair of big piteous urchin eyes. If he’d been born to ten thousand a year he couldn’t have been more free-handed, and half the time he suspected he was doing it just to get a little extra praise from the waiters and cabbies. None of us are actually as in touch with our own dumb motives as we like to pretend, after all.

Warrington had no idea Pen’s finances were circling the drain, and Pen had been too embarrassed to spill the tea. His friend knew Pen had been a wild, overspending idiot at Oxbridge like every other college lad, but he had no clue just how much cash the kid had blown, or how little his mom actually had to spare.

The truth finally tumbled out one evening, when Pen was glumly prodding that last five-pound note across the pub tray next to Warrington’s tankard of ale.

“It’s the last rose of summer,” Pen moaned. “All its blooming mates are long gone, and this last one’s about to shed its leaves for good.” And then he spilled the whole sad saga: his mom’s tiny income, all his stupid college follies, how Laura had bailed him out last time he was broke. Warrington just puffed his pipe the whole time, listening intently, and knocked the ash out when Pen finished talking.

“Being skint’ll do you a world of good, mate,” he said. “There’s no better tonic for an honest bloke than owing people a few quid. It’s like a workout for your morals! Suddenly you’re staring down your problems head on like you’re about to jump a fence on horseback, and it forces you to dig up whatever pluck you’ve got hiding under that soft coddled skin. You’ll learn real quick how much stupid crap you don’t actually need. No more new patent boots, no overpriced cologne, no cabs every time it drizzles. You’ve been spoiled rotten by women your whole life, Pen. Any healthy guy with half a brain who can’t earn a crust in this world deserves to throw himself off Waterloo Bridge, or steal a leg of mutton and get transported out of the country. I’ve spoken. Pass the ale.”

“Easy for you to say!” Pen huffed. “Beef and bread don’t grow on trees, you know, you have to pay for ’em with work or cash. And who the hell’s gonna hire me? What work do I even know how to do?”

Warrington cackled so hard he spilled a little ale down his shirt. “We could stick an ad in the Times for a tutor job at some stuffy boarding school! *Gentleman, B.A. of St Boniface’s, who spectacularly flunked his degree, offers lessons in classics, math, and rudimentary French. Can also cut pupils’ hair and play second piano for the headmaster’s daughters.*”

“Go to hell,” Pen grumbled.

“People make a living out of way dumber stuff!” Warrington went on. “Your mate Bloundell-Bloundell’s a professional card cheat who wanders the continent fleeces rich young idiots. My old school pal Bob O’Toole drives the Ballynafad mail coach now. I knew a doctor’s son once who got so broke he grew a silly mustache, moved to a small town, introduced himself as Professor Spineto, chiropodist to the Emperor of All the Russias, and cured the local newspaper editor’s corn to build up a client base. He lived like a respectable man for three years until he made up with his dad and inherited all his medical supplies.”

“Corns, card tricks, driving a mail coach? None of that’s for me!” Pen said, kicking the leg of the table.

“Then there’s writing,” Warrington shrugged. “Before you told me you were broke I fully assumed you were a rich aristocrat. Your whole fancy vibe makes everyone think you’ve got a fortune stashed away. And for the record? I’ve been writing for cash for years. I don’t tell anyone, because I’m a stubborn idiot who doesn’t want people saying George Warrington scribbles articles for bread. But I write legal reviews, and columns for a friend’s newspaper, *The Dawn*.” He dragged Pen to their club later that week, showed him the file of the paper, and pointed out his sharp, sarcastic, brilliantly written pieces that Pen immediately loved.

Pen shook his head, starry-eyed. “I could never write that. I don’t know jack about politics or history. All I’ve got is a little smattering of poetry.”

“Nah, your stuff’s better,” Warrington said, clapping him on the shoulder. “Those little verses and scraps of yours I’ve read? You’ve got a real natural poetic gift, not the boring plodding stuff I churn out. You’re a poet, you dork.”

Pen got so happy he almost cried. “Wait—you loved *Ariadne in Naxos*, right? I wrote that when I was 18! And the college prize poem?”

Warrington burst out laughing so loud the whole pub turned to look. “That garbage? *Ariadne in Naxos* is the mushiest, most overwrought slop I’ve ever read. Your prize poem is so pompous and dull it’s a miracle they didn’t give you the medal for how boring it was. You’re not gonna out-write Milton, you silly little wren, but you *can* churn out a pretty magazine article and a half-decent set of verses. That’s a perfect living for you.”

Pen jumped up, stamped his foot. “I’ll show you I’m way better than you think!” Warrington just snickered and blew 24 rapid puffs out of his pipe as a reply.

Opportunity knocked faster than Pen expected. Big-shot publisher Mr. Bacon (formerly Bacon and Bungay) of Paternoster Row put out a fancy gilt-edged book every spring called the *Spring Annual*, edited by the posh Lady Violet Lebas, full of verses from the fanciest poets and rich young lords of the day. Plates for the illustrations were engraved months in advance, so the poets had to write to fit the pictures, not the other way around. One day Warrington popped into Bacon’s office to see his manager Hack, and found him panicking over a half-finished page: Percy Popjoy had turned in the absolute worst set of verses ever written by a nobleman, for a new illustration called *The Church Porch*. It was so bad Lady Violet refused to put her name next to it, and rival publisher Bungay was putting out a competing annual that was eating up all the hype. They’d already spent 60 pounds on the plate, they couldn’t scrap it.

“I know a guy who can write you decent verses,” Warrington said, tucking the plate in his pocket. “I’ll drop them off tomorrow, you pay him properly, yeah?”

He went straight home, shoved the plate at Pen, and said “Go earn your dinner, idiot.” Pen stared at the picture: a pretty young girl hurrying into a church with her prayer book, a boy hidden in a niche watching her with all the pining in the world. He sat up all night cranking out verses, and handed them to Warrington at two in the morning. They were perfect. Pen dug up another old ballad from his notebook, and the two of them marched down to Paternoster Row. Pen was too nervous to go inside the shop, so he paced up and down the street staring at all the wild book displays: ancient black letter tomes next to penny true crime rags next to dissenting preacher pamphlets next to cheap Catholic saint prints next to anti-Catholic sermons. Every opinion under the sun had its little shop under the bells of St Paul’s.

Inside, Warrington pulled off the greatest bluff of his life. He perched on the edge of the table, left his hat on (something Bacon, who loved being treated rudely by upper class guys, ate right up), and told the publisher that Arthur Pendennis was a rich aristocrat, cousin to Lord Pontypool, who dined at Gaunt House every single week. He threatened to take Pen’s verses straight to Bungay if Bacon tried to pay him with a free copy of the book. Bacon caved instantly, wrote Pen a handsome check no questions asked.
