When Warrington walked out grinning and handed him the check, Pen screamed so loud passersby stared. He immediately wanted to treat Warrington to a fancy dinner at Richmond, buy his mom and Laura fancy gifts, and yelled that he’d never have to sponge off his family ever again. “I’m gonna make my own way! I’m gonna be famous!”

“Sure, you are,” Warrington laughed, clapping him on the back. “Soon you’ll have a mansion in Belgrave Square and a castle in the air, no doubt. But for what it’s worth? Bread you earn yourself always tastes better than free bread your mom gives you.” They headed back to the club, cracked open a giant magnum of claret, and toasted the *Spring Annual*.

It never rains but it pours, as the saying goes. A few days later, a tiny ragged printer’s devil banged on their chambers door, handed Pen a letter, mumbled “From Captain Shandon, sir,” and then immediately curled up on the bench in the passage and fell fast asleep, like he’d done a hundred times before. The letter read:

MY DEAR SIR,—Bungay will be here to-day, about the Pall Mall Gazette. You would be the very man to help us with a genuine West-end article,—you understand—dashing, trenchant, and damned aristocratic. Lady Hipshaw will write; but she’s not much you know, and we’ve two lords; but the less they do the better. We must have you. We’ll give you your own terms, and we’ll make a hit with the Gazette.

Shall B. come and see you, or can you look in upon me here?—Ever yours,

Our very much imprisoned Captain Shandon pounded out his prospectus in such bold, brassy, loud-mouthed prose you’d half-expect it to leap off the page and punch a radical agitator on the nose. The time had come, he declared, for every right-thinking English gentleman to band together and defend their glorious order from every threat under the sun: foreign revolutions, home-grown radical louts, lying mill-owners, and the dumb mobs those greedy cotton lords had gulled into voting against their own betters. “The ancient monarchy is being insulted by a ferocious republican rabble!” he boomed, waving a half-torn sheet of paper like a battle standard, the hole in his left slipper flapping as he hopped about on the rickety table. “The Church is deserted by grudge-bearing dissenters and gnawed at by sneaky infidels! All the fine old institutions that made our country great, and made ‘English Gentleman’ the proudest title on the planet? They’re left defenceless, getting jeered at by lot who don’t respect a single sacred thing, don’t know a scrap of history older than last week’s street brawl, and will smash any law they’re strong enough to get away with, the second their gang leaders yell “loot!” The reason Saint Louis’s French monarchy collapsed? Their kings didn’t trust their gentlemen! The reason England beat the greatest enemy any nation ever faced? Our people still believed in their gentlemen! It was gentlemen leading our armies that chased the French eagles all the way from the Douro to the Garonne! It was a gentleman who broke the line at Trafalgar, and swept the plain of Waterloo!”

Publisher Bungay bobbed his head like a fairground bobblehead at the Waterloo line, winking so hard his eye almost stuck shut. Warrington burst out laughing so hard his half-pint ale sloshed over the rim onto his trouser leg.
“See how our venerable old mate Bungay takes it?” Shandon snickered, peering up from his papers. “That’s the true test. I’ve dragged the Duke of Wellington and Waterloo out a hundred different times, and that old boy never once lets me down.”

Shandon went on to confess, with performative candour, that up till now English gentlemen had been far too confident in their own status to bother fighting their corner in the press, leaving all their political interests in the hands of hireling hacks and paid proctors, the same way they hired stewards to run their estates and lawyers to sort their legal messes. That era was over, he declared. Gentlemen had to be their own champions now! Their enemies were bold, numerous, and fully ready to fight dirty. “No more letting Grub Street print fake government gazettes!” he yelled, turning to nudge Bungay in the ribs. “That’s a dig at that hack Bacon’s lot, by the way.”

Bungay whacked his walking stick so hard on the floor little Mary Shandon dropped her rag doll. “Sic ‘im Capting!” he whooped, giddy as a schoolboy. “Rattle his bones so hard he’ll be begging for mercy! For a slashing article, sir, no one on God’s green earth can touch the Capting. Not a single soul.”

The prospectus rambled on, explaining that a group of very important gentlemen, whose names for very obvious top-secret reasons could not be shared with the general public (at which point Warrington snickered so loud he had to stuff a handkerchief in his mouth) had decided to launch a brand new journal. “These men are proud of their order, and determined to defend it!” Shandon cawed, flourishing his paper with a big grin. “They’re loyal to their king by birth and by heartfelt conviction! They love their Church, where their kids will worship, and where their forefathers spilled blood to build! They love their country, and want to keep it the greatest, freest nation on the planet — we’ll print “GENTLEMEN OF ENGLAND” in huge capital letters, Bungay my lad, don’t you worry! And just like the gentlemen who appended their names to the document that secured our liberties at Runnymede —”

“Wot’s that?” Bungay interrupted, squinting.
“One of my ancestors sealed it with his sword hilt,” Pen put on his most grave, posh voice, deadpan.
“It’s the Habeas Corpus Act, Bungay,” Warrington said.
Bungay nodded slowly. “Right, sounds legal enough. Carry on, Capting.”
“— at Runnymede, we’re ready to defend that same liberty today with sword and pen, same as our ancestors, and rally round England’s old laws and freedoms!”

“Bravo!” Warrington yelled. Little Mary stared up at all the shouting grown-ups, confused, and Mrs Shandon sat sewing quietly, looking at her husband like he was the cleverest man who’d ever lived. “C’mere little one,” Warrington said, reaching out with his big rough paw to pat her curls, but she shrieked and scurried away, clambering straight up into Pen’s lap to play with his fancy gold watch chain. Pen puffed up with pride so much his collar almost stood up on its own — he was soft and sappy under that stuffy, pompous exterior, and he’d never admit how delighted he was the kid liked him better.

Shandon snickered at Warrington. “You were cackling about those “obvious reasons” I mentioned for hiding the backers’ names, you heathen unbeliever? I’ll show you. “We cannot publish the names of the parties behind this venture, for very obvious reasons. We count influential friends in both Houses of Parliament, and secured allies in every diplomatic circle across Europe. Our sources are so top-secret they could never be made public — no other London or European paper could ever hope to get their hands on intel half as hot. But this much we can say: the very first breaking news on English and continental politics will only ever be found in the pages of the *Pall Mall Gazette*. The Squire, the Bishop, the banker, the country parson — they’ll all be our readers, because our writers are one of them. We speak straight to the upper classes we were born into! The field preacher has his own paper, the radical free-thinker has his own paper — why should English gentlemen be the only group with no voice in the press?”

Shandon went on, very modestly, to gush about the paper’s literary and society pages, which would all be written by famous, well-respected gentlemen, big shots from the universities, regulars at all the fancy clubs, who actually knew the society they wrote about. He subtly pointed out to advertisers that there was no better place to flog their fancy goods than the *Pall Mall Gazette*, and yelled very eloquently at the nobility, baronetage, clergy, barristers, fine ladies, and every English home and hearth to rally round the good old cause. Bungay had nodded off for a full ten minutes at that point, and woke up with a snort just as Shandon finished, banged his stick on the floor and declared it was all perfect, no notes.

Once the reading was done, the group chatted about the paper’s political and literary plans, and Bungay sat there bobbing his head like he understood every single word, even though the only two points he cared about were: 1) Shandon could write the best most brutal, slashing takedown articles in all of England, and 2) he wanted rival publisher Bacon absolutely destroyed. If Shandon had handed him a copy of the Church Catechism and told him it was a slashing takedown of Bacon, Bungay would have cheered and paid him double. He stuffed the manuscript in his coat pocket grinning, paid Shandon for the work, and called little Mary over to give her a penny before he trundled off.

With the work out of the way, Shandon launched into a whole bunch of stories about fancy society bigwigs, acting like he dined with dukes every other night. He barely knew a single person in high society, but he’d seen a few of them once at a party, and he milked that for all it was worth, dropping names and telling silly anecdotes about private dinners and gossip he’d definitely not made up on the spot. Pen was highly amused, watching the shabby man in a tattered dressing gown ramble about earls and duchesses like they were his drinking buddies. Mrs Shandon ate every single story up, hook line and sinker — she didn’t want to join high society herself, but she was fully convinced Charles was born to shine there, and still kept the one dinner invitation he’d gotten from an Earl ten years prior tucked in her workbox.

Bungay had had enough of the fancy name-dropping after five minutes, and got up to leave, so Warrington and Pen went with him. Pen wanted to stay longer, he was so charmed and touched by the weird, sweet messy family, so he asked if he could come visit again. Shandon gave a rueful grin, said he was always at home, of course he’d be delighted to see him.
“I’ll see you to the prison gate, lads,” Shandon said, grabbing his hat, ignoring Mrs Shandon’s quiet little “Charles, don’t —” protest. Off he shuffled in his holey slippers, leading them through the drafty, mildew-smelling prison corridors, his fingers already fiddling with his waistcoat pocket where Bungay’s five-pound note was tucked. Pen was so relieved to step out onto the flagstones of Farringdon Street and breathe fresh air he almost hugged a random lamppost.

Back in their cell, Mrs Shandon sat by the window sewing, watching Shandon hotfoot it towards the prison tavern, two of his drinking buddies right on his heels. She’d saved a nice bit of roast beef and a bowl of salad on the windowsill, planning to have a nice family dinner with him and little Mary that night. But that was obviously never going to happen now. He’d drink in the tavern till closing time, then go play cards in some other prisoner’s room, come back late with glazed eyes, reeling a little, and she’d have to nurse him to bed. What sorts of pain don’t we heap on the women who love us, after all?

She sighed, went to the cupboard, and made a pot of tea instead. Oh, that trusty tea pot! It has heard more secret sobs than every priest and best friend combined, ever since that kind little plant made its way over to England. It’s sat by a thousand sick beds, given cool sips to a thousand fevered lips, heard every heartbreak from Melissa pining over her secret lover, to poor Polly crying over the letter from the boy who left her, to little Mary bringing a cup up to her widowed mum. That night, it sat on the rickety table with Mrs Shandon and little Mary, while Shandon was off drinking, and that was all the comfort they needed.

In trundled Jack Finucane, Shandon’s loyal Irish sidekick, who smelled faintly of pickled herring and always had a peppermint sweet stuffed in his pocket for little Mary. He thought Shandon was the greatest genius to ever walk the face of the earth, and he ran all of Shandon’s silly little errands: dodging duns, haggling with publishers, arranging for people to buy Shandon’s IOUs, running every single tiny favour an embarrassingly broke Irish gentleman could ever need. Every broke Irishman like Shandon has one of these loyal sidekicks, who is also completely broke, of course, and they’ve got their own ragged little crew of followers tagging along behind them.

“Bet you a guinea that five pound note won’t last him till sundown,” Bungay said, as he trundled down the street with Pen and Warrington. And he was exactly right. When Mrs Shandon emptied Shandon’s pockets later that night, all she found was two shillings and a handful of halfpence. He’d given a pound to a broke friend stuck in the prison, sent a leg of mutton and beer to a mate in the poor wing of the prison, paid off his long overdue tavern tab, had a big dinner with two drinking buddies, and lost all the rest to them at cards. He was exactly as broke when he stumbled back to the cell that night as he’d been when he woke up that morning.

The three of them chatted as they walked, and Warrington sang Pen’s praises to Bungay, same as he’d done to rival publisher Bacon — Pen was a brilliant fellow, great genius, mates with half the aristocracy, related to no end of lords. Bungay practically salivated, said he’d love to work with Mr Pendennis, and invited both of them over for a roast mutton dinner at his house soon to meet all his other literary writer chums. They parted with lots of polite back-slapping.

Pen was still musing that night, back at their rooms, over the whole wild day. “It’s so unfair,” he said. “A man as talented, funny, and clever as Shandon? Spends half his life stuck in a debtor’s gaol, dancing to the tune of a oafish bookseller like Bungay.”
“I’m a bookseller’s hanger-on, you’re about to start your career as a hack writer,” Warrington snickered. “We’re all hacks on some road or other. I’d far rather be me than our neighbour Paley in the next set of chambers, who enjoys life about as much as a mole enjoys digging through mud. People waste far too much unnecessary pity on “bookseller’s drudges”.”
“All that solitary pipe smoking and ale has turned you into a cynic,” Pen laughed. “You’re Diogenes hanging out by a beer barrel. No one can tell me a genius like Shandon deserves to be bossed around by that vulgar slave-driver Bungay, who fattens himself on the profits of Shandon’s brain, who couldn’t even write a decent sentence, let alone polish Shandon’s boots.”
“So you’re already girding up to fight the publishers, and take the side of us poor writers? Bravo Pen!” Warrington cackled. “Who do you think threw Shandon in prison, eh? Was it Bungay draining that five pound note we saw today, or Shandon himself, running to the tavern the second the money touched his hands?”
“Misfortune drives a man to bad company,” Pen protested. “It’s easy to yell “shame!” at a poor guy who’s stuck in prison, with nothing but drink and forgetting his troubles to look forward to. We have to cut geniuses some slack — the same wild, passionate temperament that makes their writing brilliant is the exact same thing that makes them go a little off the rails.”
“Rubbish about all these so-called geniuses!” Warrington huffed. He was a very strict moralist on this sort of thing, even if he didn’t always live up to his own rules. “There’s thousands of clever blokes who could write verses, pen articles, and judge books just as well as any so-called famous author. The talk of professional writers isn’t one bit brighter or funnier than the talk of any other group of educated people. If a lawyer, or a soldier, or a parson spends more than he earns and won’t pay his bills, he gets tossed in gaol. Why should authors get a pass? If a writer gets drunk, he still gets a hangover the next morning. If he orders a new coat from the tailor, he still has to pay for it.”
“I’d give him enough money to buy as many new coats as he wants,” Pen grinned. “I want our profession to be well-dressed, for one thing. I’m sick of that awful middleman publisher standing between Genius and the public, robbing more than half their earnings and their fame.”
“I’m a plain old hack labourer,” Warrington said. “You, my boy, are a tiny bit of a poet, so you think you’re allowed to be all flighty. What do you want, exactly? A gang of capitalists forced to buy every manuscript any random hack shoves in their face? Every idiot who wrote an epic poem about turnips, every driveller who can barely spell, who churns out a terrible novel — they all get a bag of sovereigns for their garbage paper? Who gets to decide what’s good or bad, worth selling or not? Can the buyer still say no? Back when Samuel Johnson sat behind a screen at St John’s Gate, eating his dinner alone because he was too ragged and poor to sit with the fancy literary bigwigs at Cave’s dinner table, the publisher wasn’t doing anything wrong. You can’t force a publisher to recognise a genius in some ragged, hungry kid who shows up at his door. Rags aren’t proof of genius, but money is a very real thing, and the one who puts up the cash gets to call the shots. If I make a new novelty for the book trade, I can do whatever I want with it, but I can’t force Mr Murray to buy my travel book any more than I can force a horse dealer to give me a hundred guineas for my horse. I might think my Pegasus is the finest animal on earth, but the dealer gets to decide if he’s actually worth buying, or if he’s just a nag no one wants.”
“You’re drowning in metaphors, Warrington,” Pen laughed. “You’re very much the prosaic man you say you are. But that poor Shandon, and his sweet gentle wife? They touched my heart something fierce. I like him far more than I like lots of far “better” men I’ve met.”
“Same here,” Warrington agreed. “We can give him all the sympathy he’s owed for his weaknesses, even if a more proud man would hate that pity. Look at him: he’s in prison, but he’s not miserable. His misery and his little comforts balance each other out, that’s how the world works.”
“His genius sings even behind prison bars,” Pen said.
“Sure,” Warrington said, a little bitter. “Shandon fits in that cage just fine. He could be providing for his wife and kid, but instead he’s drinking with Tom and Jack. He could pay the poor tailor Snip the twenty quid he owes him so Snip doesn’t get tossed in gaol, but Tom and Jack drag him off to the tavern instead. While he’s drinking, his tradesman goes to prison, and his family goes hungry. Let’s all wring our hands about poor oppressed genius, and conspire against the evil publishing tyrants!”
“Hold on, are you ordering another brandy and water?” Pen snickered. They’d been having this whole philosophical chat at the Black Kitchen

CHAPTER XXXIV. Which Hangs Around Fleet Street, And Ends With A Very Unexpected Sort Of Prison Break
Mrs. Shandon, who barely ever dabbled in business matters, had nagged her husband Captain Shandon into making one non-negotiable demand for the upcoming *Pall Mall Gazette*: that John Finucane, Esquire, of the Upper Temple, get the sub-editor gig. And the spirited publisher Bungay, utterly charmed by the Irishman’s relentless chaotic energy, handed the post right over.

Finucane more than earned it. He was so devoted to the Shandon clan that back in the old days when bailiffs were hunting the Captain for every debt under the sun, Shandon would hide out in Fin’s tiny chambers so often the sheriff’s officers eventually started leaving their overnight tea mugs on his staircase, not even bothering to loiter outside Shandon’s own front door anymore. Poor Mrs. Shandon would traipse over constantly to beg for help springing her beloved husband, and Fin fed her and little Mary a dozen free meals in those poky rooms, leaning over the balustrade after she left to make sure no Temple rogue bothered her—secretly half-hoping some lout would try to accost her, just so he could play the dashing hero and break the rascal’s nose. So when the sub-editor job was locked in, making him her husband’s right-hand man on the paper, Mrs. Shandon was over the moon.

Fin would have happily sat chattering with the Shandons till the prison curfew, he’d even sat through little Mary’s bedtime prayers dozens of times, adding an enthusiastic Amen (devout Catholic though he was) whenever she asked God to bless her papa. But he had a very important dinner scheduled with Mr. Bungay to hash out the paper’s details, so he headed off at six sharp, showing up at Fleet Prison bright and early the next day decked out in his very best cheap, blindingly colourful new clothes, a brand new pair of two-shilling gloves on his hands, and his full week’s salary (four pounds two shillings) still almost entirely untouched.

The night before, he’d “cut his mutton” as Bungay called it, at Dick’s Coffee House with Bungay and his literary right-hand man Trotter, blowing them away with his master plan for the Gazette. He knew exactly who to hire to cover markets, horse races and boxing matches, church news, and all the silly fashionable gossip about fancy parties—he was, as Shandon always joked, one of the best sub-editors in all of London, who knew every press hack’s weekly wage and a hundred clever little tricks to save a new publisher every penny he could. His rapid-fire scribbled calculations on the coffee house napkin left slow-on-the-uptake Bungay so dazzled he told Trotter later, “That Irish bloke? Clever as a barrel full of monkeys.”

With Bungay thoroughly impressed, Fin made his big pitch: get Shandon out of prison. He knew down to the penny exactly how much the Captain owed to the Fleet prison authorities, and argued Shandon would write twice as fast if he wasn’t stuck in a cell—though Bungay pushed back hard on that one. “Nah, when the Captain’s locked up? You know exactly where to find him. Let him walk free, and you’ll never track the man down, he’s off gallivanting at some tavern three counties away before you can blink.” But Fin painted such a vivid, sad picture of Mrs. Shandon pining away and little Mary getting sick in the grim prison air that Bungay caved, and promised if Mrs. Shandon came to see him the next morning, he’d sort it out. By the time the second round of brandy and water was done, Bungay wouldn’t even let Fin pay the bill, waving the waiter over to settle the tab himself.

So Fin showed up at the Fleet Saturday morning with his full pay packet still in his pocket, winked so broadly at Mrs. Shandon she instantly knew good news was afoot. He offered to take her and little Mary out for some fresh air, and the kid screamed with joy—Fin always had a newspaper free pass to some puppet show or toy shop tucked in his coat pocket, and never showed up without a sweet for her. “You sure you want to leave your dad?” Mrs Shandon said. “He’s got a terrible headache this morning, I convinced him to stay in bed.” Shandon waved them off cheerfully, flipping open his copy of Burton’s Anatomy for the Greek and Latin quotes he loved dropping in his articles, “Go on, the pair of you. Jack, keep ’em safe. Hand me that book and leave me to my writing, you lot.”

So Fin offered Mrs. Shandon his arm, and Mary skipped all the way out of the prison gates, heading the short walk to Paternoster Row and Bungay’s shop. They got there just as Mrs. Bungay was waddling in the private entrance, clutching a paper parcel of biscuits and a red-bound book of her butcher’s accounts. She was dressed like a walking fireworks display: a shot-silk dress flaming red and purple, a bright yellow shawl, red flowers sticking out of her bonnet, and a light blue parasol so vivid it hurt your eyes a little. Faded black watered silk or no, Mrs. Shandon still looked every inch a lady, and the two women curtsied awkwardly at each other, like two birds sizing each other up on a fence.

“Hope you’re well, ma’am?” Mrs. Bungay rumbled.
“Lovely day out, isn’t it?” Mrs. Shandon stammered, explaining she’d come to speak to her husband’s employer on business.
“Before you go to the counting house, why don’t you leave the little girl with me?” Mrs. Bungay boomed, holding one fat finger out towards Mary like she was offering a cat a treat. The kid hid her face in her mom’s skirt and wailed that she wasn’t going anywhere, until Mrs. Bungay opened her parcel to show off fancy buttery biscuits, the kind her husband loved after a glass of port. Little Mary followed her inside, the lot of them heading up to the drawing room, and soon enough the kid was chattering away happily, munching biscuits.

Downstairs in the counting house, Fin got the shock of his life: Bungay was a total hardass. He’d gotten a full hour’s lecture from his wife the night before when he got home, she’d ranted at him till his ears burned: Shandon was a useless no-good layabout, no amount of money would ever fix him, the Pall Mall Gazette was going to be a total money pit just like the rival paper run by her brother “over the way”, leave the Captain locked up, it was the only place he ever got any work done. Fin pleaded, begged, promised they’d hit 10,000 copies a week, but Bungay wouldn’t budge an inch.

What Fin couldn’t pull off with a hundred clever arguments upstairs in the drawing room, Mrs. Shandon and little Mary did without even trying. Mrs. Shandon, all soft-spoken sweetness, told Mrs. Bungay her whole sad story: how they’d had to send the other two kids off to boarding school because the prison was no place to raise them, how the Captain was such a kind, brilliant man wasted locked in a cell. The tough old Lady Macbeth act melted right off Mrs. Bungay’s face, and she declared she was going down to sort her husband out immediately—everyone in the Bungay household knew that when the missus said “speak”, Mr. Bungay said “yes ma’am” instantly.

She told Fin to head up to the drawing room, then turned to Bungay and laid down the law. “That Captain Shandon’s an Irishman, and I’ve always said I can’t stand the lot of ’em. But his wife’s a proper lady, a clergyman’s daughter, just like my own mum’s side of the family from the West Country. And that little girl? She’s the spitting image of our poor little Bessy, the baby we lost eighteen years ago when we were just tiny booksellers in a country town.”

Bungay’s eyes went soft, he wiped a tear off his wife’s cheek, and caved instantly. “Alright, alright! Fin says 65 quid will get him out the door, we’ll advance that, and deduct it slow from his wages. When that little mite said ‘why won’t you take Par out of prizn?’ I almost cried, Flora, honest I did.”

The couple trundled upstairs to make the offer official, and Fin went absolutely feral with joy. “Bedad Bungay, you’re an absolute legend!” he roared in his thickest brogue, shaking the publisher’s hand so hard it ached, tossing little Mary three feet in the air yelling they’d make the *Pall Mall Gazette* the most famous paper in London. Mrs. Bungay insisted they all pile into her one-horse carriage for the ride over to the creditors, little Mary squeezed tight between the two ladies, Fin perched happily on the back seat, as they drove past the rival Bacon & Co shop windows with Mrs. Bungay staring daggers at the competition.

Left alone in his counting house, Bungay shook his head, muttering, “It won’t make the Captain work any better, that’s for sure, but women? They remember every single tiny sad little thing from 18 years ago, and you can’t argue with that.”

And we’re happy to report that Mrs. Shandon handled the creditors like a total pro—she’d talked them down from impossible bills with nothing but tears and kind words a hundred times before, so offering them ten shillings in the pound to settle the debt got them all to agree instantly. That coming Sunday would be the last one Captain Shandon spent in the Fleet Prison, for a good long while at least.

On the appointed day, our two heroes Pen and Warrington turned up at Mr. Bungay’s Paternoster Row doorstep—*not* the scuffed public entrance where a never-ending parade of dewy-eyed, nervous scribblers loitered clutching crumpled virgin manuscripts, praying Sultan Bungay would toss them a few shillings for their unwritten masterpieces. No, this was the fancy private side door, from which Mrs. Bungay would glide out to her chaise, fluffing her satin skirts on the cushions and sending unmistakeable death glares straight across the street at Mrs. Bacon’s drawing room windows. Because at that point, Emma Bacon had no chaise at all.

Whenever Mrs. Bacon was particularly seething at her sister-in-law’s new carriage, she’d fling her sash all the way up, plant herself behind the glass with her four cherubic little kids lined up beside her, and practically hold up a giant neon sign that read “*Look at these four darlings, Flora Bungay!* You’d give a whole coach and four to have a brood of your own instead of sitting alone in that pretty chariot, childless and seething.” Those four little Bacon kids were her sharpest weapons, and she was not afraid to fire them straight across the cobblestones.

Just as Pen and Warrington trotted up, a grand old carriage and a rickety cab pulled up directly opposite, at the Bacons’ door. Portly old Dr. Slocum descended from the carriage like a boulder rolling downhill—his whole ponderous vibe, from his creaky boots to his booming lecture voice, made all the booksellers in the Row pause and listen, just out of habit. Two impossibly bright white waistcoats then tumbled out of the cab.

Warrington snickered. “See? Bacon’s throwing his own dinner party too. That’s Slocum, who wrote *Memoirs of the Poisoners*. You’d never spot our friend Hoolan under that fancy waistcoat, would you? And there’s Doolan, one of Bungay’s lads, making his way over.” Sure enough, Hoolan and Doolan had ridden over from the Strand in the same cab, bickering the whole trip over who got stuck paying the shilling fare. Doolan was clad in sober black, but his hands were engulfed in a pair of enormous brand-new white gloves he kept staring at like they were a pair of priceless pet swans.

Inside Bungay’s hallway, the porter was squeezed into a too-small evening coat, and a small army of men wearing gloves nearly as big as Doolan’s (but of fancy Berlin web) were lined up to snatch hats and coats and yell guests’ names up the stairs as loud as they could. A few folks had already arrived, and the only lady in the drawing room was Mrs. Bungay, decked out in scarlet satin and a turban that looked like it had been decorated for a royal parade. She curtsied to every new arrival, but it was obvious her mind was elsewhere: she was fully obsessed with spying on the Bacons’ dinner party. The second she’d greeted the last guest, she zoomed back to the window nook to gawk at every single one of Emma Bacon’s guests pulling up in their carriages. The sight of Dr. Slocum’s giant, horse-drawn vehicle made her physically wince—so far, every single guest who’d knocked on *her* door had showed up in a shabby hack cab.

All the guests were literary types, most of whom Pen had never met. There was Mr. Bole, the actual hardworking editor of Bungay’s magazine, whose entire job was done for the glory of the nominal chief, Mr. Wagg. There was Mr. Trotter, who’d burst onto the scene a decade prior as a tragic suicidal poet, and had since mellowed out to spend all his days reading slush piles in Bungay’s back office. Then there was Captain Sumph, a washed-up old dandy who claimed he was besties with Lord Byron and vaguely related to half the peerage, who had never once opened his mouth without mentioning some long-dead Regency poet. “I remember poor Shelley at school, getting sent up for good for a poem I wrote every single line of, by Jove!” he’d announce, or “I was right there with Byron at Missolonghi, offering to bet Gamba fifty pounds that the wind would turn…” and everyone would nod along like he was telling the truth. Mrs. Bungay ate every single one of his aristocratic anecdotes up, and he was almost as big a celebrity in her eyes as the great Mr. Wagg himself. If he’d turned up in his own carriage? Bungay would have bought every book the man ever scribbled, sight unseen.

Mr. Bungay bounced around his guests doing the honours, brimming with over-the-top cordiality. “How do, sir? Fine day, innit? Glad to see you, sir! Flora love, let me introduce Mr. Warrington to you. Mr. Warrington, Mrs. Bungay; Mr. Pendennis, Mrs. Bungay. Hope you lads brought huge appetites, eh? You, Doolan, I *know* you have, you’ve always had a deuce of a hollow leg.”

“Lor, Bungay!” Mrs. Bungay huffed, pretending to be scandalized.

“Faith, a man’d have to be a proper fussy eater not to polish off a good dinner in this house,” Doolan simpered, winking and stroking his lean cheeks with his giant fluffy gloves, trying to flatter Mrs. Bungay so hard she nearly rolled her eyes out of her head. She later told her friends privately that she couldn’t stand that simpering little man, no matter how hard he tried.

As they chattered, Mrs. Bungay’s eyes went wide: a magnificent dapple-grey cab horse came trotting fast down the Row, with a pair of tiny white-gloved hands holding the reins, a pale face sporting a very dramatic chin tuft peeking out the window, and a tiny little groom bobbing over the top of the cab. “The Honourable Percy Popjoy is *perfectly* on time!” she yelped, and zoomed to the front door to wait for him.

“That’s Percy Popjoy,” Pen said, leaning out the window to see the man himself clamber down the cab steps in boots so lacquered you could see your face in them. It was none other than the eldest son of Lord Falconet, who was Bungay’s very posh star author.

“He was my fag at Eton,” Warrington said, snorting. “I definitely should have whupped him a few more times.” Pen had also run into him at the Oxbridge Union debates, where he’d absolutely wiped the floor with poor Percy, who soon stumbled into the drawing room, hat under his arm, face round and dimpled and grinning with that particular dumb good humor that comes from being born into a title.

The porter yelled “THE HONOURABLE PERCY POPJOY” at the top of his lungs, and Percy jumped so hard he nearly dropped his hat. “Why’d that bloke go and take my hat, Bungay?” he complained. “Can’t bow to Mrs. Bungay without it, mate. You look absolutely stunning today, Mrs B, by the way. Haven’t seen your carriage in the Park lately—where’ve you been? I missed you, honest I did.”

“P’raps you’re just teasing,” Mrs. Bungay simpered, already half in love.

“Me? I never make jokes—hullo! Pendennis? Warrington? How the devil did you two end up here?” he said, spinning around on his shiny lacquered heels, forgetting all about Mrs. Bungay for a second.

“Those two know him?” Mrs. Bungay hissed at Bungay, eyes wide.

“Top fellers, I tell you,” Bungay whispered back. “The young one’s chums with half the nobility.” The pair of them immediately started beaming and bowing even harder, just as the next two great celebrities were announced: Mr. Wenham and Mr. Wagg.

Wenham drifted in with his usual smug little smile, staring down at the tips of his own shiny little boots like they were the most interesting thing in the room, barely bothering to make eye contact with anyone. Wagg, meanwhile, burst in with a laugh loud enough to rattle the teacups, his enormous bright white waistcoat glowing, his red chubby face shining with excitement at the thought of jokes and a full dinner. Nothing bad could get Wagg down for long—no debt, no bad review, no domestic squabble, could ruin his mood when he knew a good dinner was waiting. And if he spotted a lord? He’d fire off a pun before he even said hello.

Wenham crept over to Mrs. Bungay, whispering soft little compliments and peeking at her from under his eyebrows, while Wagg told her she looked ravishing, then bounded straight over to Percy Popjoy, called him “Pop”, and immediately launched into a raunchy funny story seasoned with just the right amount of coarse salt. He was over the moon to see Pen too, slapping him on the back so hard Pen nearly spilled his drink, yelling about how they’d last met down in Baymouth, asking after all their fancy posh friends at Clavering Park, and making sure everyone in the room knew he dined at country manors and was chums with baronets and duchesses. Wenham also shook Pen’s hand, and Mrs. Bungay took careful notes, already mentally jacking up the fee for any article Pen might write for her husband’s magazine later.

Pen had read and even admired some of the work of the last guest, Miss Bunion, the poet who wrote *Passion-Flowers*. He’d expected her to match her own self-description: a shy shrinking violet, a timid little fawn hiding in the oak woods, delicate and soft. So he was more than a little surprised to see a bony, six-foot woman in crumpled satin come creaking into the room with a step heavy enough to match a grenadier’s march. Wagg spotted a piece of straw stuck to the hem of her dress and was about to leap up and pick it up, but Miss Bunion just planted one big boot on the straw to separate it from her skirt, picked it up herself, and told Mrs. Bungay she was sorry she was late, but the omnibus was very slow, and wasn’t it lovely that you could ride all the way from Brompton for sixpence? No one laughed—she said it so plainly, no trace of embarrassment at all, like being poor was no more shameful than having a bit of dust on your coat.

“Is that the Passion-Flowers poet?” Pen whispered to Wenham. “Her portrait in the book makes her look like a sweet young thing.”

“Passion flowers go to seed same as every other flower,” Wenham murmured, smirking. “That portrait was painted nigh on twelve years ago, I’d wager.”

“Well, I like her for not being ashamed of it,” Pen said.
“Oh, I quite agree,” Wenham said, who would have starved in a garret before he ever turned up to a formal dinner on an omnibus. “But does she really have to wave that straw around like a trophy? My dear Miss Bunion, how do you do? I was in a duchess’s drawing room this very morning, and everyone was raving about your new volume. Those lines you wrote about Lady Fanny Fantail’s christening? Brought the Duchess herself to tears. I told her I’d likely meet you today, and she begged me to pass on her thanks for such a masterpiece.”

That story, delivered with such a smug, soft smile, completely one-upped Wagg’s whole name-dropping routine, and Wenham spent the next ten minutes reeling off endless anecdotes about dukes and lords, trying to drag Percy Popjoy into the conversation. But Percy couldn’t care less—he’d snuck back to the window nook with Mrs. Bungay, gawking at the Bacons’ guests pulling up across the street, and Mrs. Bungay was glowing, because she was 100% sure Emma Bacon could see her with the Honourable Percy Popjoy hanging out in her drawing room.

By the time St Paul’s bells tolled half past the dinner hour, the last two guests finally turned up, and Pen’s face lit up: it was Captain and Mrs Shandon. After greeting the Bungays and nodding awkwardly at the rest of the room, Pen and Warrington bounded over to shake Mrs Shandon’s hand warmly, and she got a little teary, remembering where they’d last met just a few days prior. Shandon was all spruced up for the occasion, in a red velvet waistcoat and a starched frill with his wife’s very best brooch pinned right to the front. But poor Mrs Shandon was trembling like a leaf near the formidable Mrs Bungay in her scarlet satin and bird-of-paradise feather, and only relaxed a little when Mrs Bungay boomed out asking after her “dear little gurl.”

“Nice-looking woman,” Percy Popjoy whispered to Warrington. “Introduce me to Captain Shandon, won’t you? Everyone says he’s dead clever, and dammit I adore intellect, by Jove I do!” It was true: Heaven hadn’t blessed Percy with much of a brain of his own, but it had given him an overabundant urge to hang around people who did. “And introduce me to Miss Bunion too! She’s clever, right? She looks a bit odd, but who cares. Dammit I’m a literary man myself, I want to meet all the clever folks.”

Just as the two of them finished getting acquainted, the dining room doors were flung open, and everyone trooped in to sit down. Pen ended up wedged between Miss Bunion on one side and Mr. Wagg on the other—Wagg had made a dramatic show of fleeing the empty seat next to the poetess, leaving Pen high and dry.

Miss Bunion barely talked the whole dinner, but Pen noticed she had a fantastic appetite, and never turned down a glass of wine the butler offered her. After staring Pen over for a minute, in his fancy dandy getup with all his gold chains and fancy shirt studs, she pegged him immediately as a stuck-up Mayfair prig, and decided it was far more sensible to focus on her food than make small talk with him. She told him as much later, straight out: “I thought you were one of those silly little dandies, looking as solemn as a tiny undertaker, and since I couldn’t stand the odious bloke on the other side of me, I decided to eat my dinner and keep my mouth shut.”

“And you did both perfectly, my dear Miss Bunion,” Pen laughed.

“Well, I did, but next time I’m going to talk your ear off. You’re not half as stupid or prissy as you look.”
“Ah, Miss Bunion, I’m already counting the days till that next dinner,” Pen said, grinning. But back to the feast at Paternoster Row.

The dinner was unbelievably over-the-top, what Wagg whispered to Pen as “florid Gothic style, all gaudy garnish and no actual flavour.” The waiters, all wearing creaky shoes and formal black coats, zipped back and forth behind the guests chattering quietly to each other, and Doolan accidentally yelled “WAITHER!” at one of them before blushing so hard his ears turned bright red. Mrs. Bungay’s tiny footboy was completely lost in the crowd of giant waiters, half of whom Pen realized were moonlighting undertakers from Amen Corner across the road, the kind who worked both funerals and fancy dinner parties.

“See that bloke over there with the huge bow window waistcoat?” Wagg murmured. “He buries people for a living. Cold meat for funerals, hot meat for dinners—simple. And mark my words, wherever there’s a fake undertaker butler, there’s fake wine. This sherry’s absolute rubbish. Hey Bungay, old boy, where’d you get this *delicious* brown sherry? Best I’ve tasted all year!”

“Glad you like it, Wagg! Glass with you!” Bungay boomed. “Got it from Alderman Benning’s store, paid a fortune for the stuff, I’ll tell you that. Mr Pendennis, join us! Your health, lads!”
“Old rogue bought it from the pub down the street,” Wagg snickered to Pen a second later. “This stuff’s so strong you need two blokes to carry a single bottle. I wish I had a case of my old friend Lord Steyne’s wine here—your uncle and I drank dozens of bottles of the stuff together. He sends his chef and his butler around with ice buckets full of champagne to all his friends’ dinner parties, you know. I remember once at poor Rawdon Crawley’s—Sir Pitt’s brother, the governor of Coventry Island—Steyne’s whole kitchen staff showed up at the door before the guests even arrived.”

“This dinner’s absolutely perfect,” Percy Popjoy gushed. “You must have a proper cordon bleu French chef in your kitchen, Mrs Bungay!”
“Oh yes, your lordship,” Mrs Bungay said, having not the faintest clue what a cordon bleu was, and assuming it was some kind of fancy new jack chain for the stove.
“I mean a French cook,” Percy clarified politely.
“Oh yes, your lordship, of course we do!”
“Is your chef actually claiming he’s French, Mrs B?” Wagg yelled across the table.
“Well I’m sure I wouldn’t know,” she said, blushing.
“Because if he is, he’s *quizzin’ yer*!” Wagg cackled, but no one got the pun, and the poor joke fell completely flat, leaving him a little deflated. “The whole dinner’s from Griggs, the caterer in St Paul’s Churchyard, same as Bacon’s,” he whispered to Pen. “Bungay pays half a crown a head more than Bacon to make his fancier, Bacon pays half a crown more than Bungay to make *his* better. They’d poison each other’s ices if they could sneak into the other’s kitchen. This dish here? *Brimborion à la Sévigné*? Absolutely delicious, Mrs B!” he added, helping himself to whatever weird creamy mess the undertaker waiter was holding.

Mrs Bungay nodded, flustered, having never heard of that dish name before, and instantly decided she hated Wagg with every fibre of her being. She would have fired him from running Bungay’s magazine

The new paper blew up almost instantly. Rumour had it a powerful political party was bankrolling the whole operation, and half the top names in Westminster were secretly writing for its columns. Were the rumours true? We’re not at liberty to call them outright lies, but we can spill the full, far funnier tea: that celebrated foreign policy essay everyone swore was penned by a well-known Foreign Office cabinet minister? It was actually Captain Shandon, scribbling on a wobbly pub table at the Bear and Staff near Whitehall Stairs, where the printer’s boy had tracked him down while he was crashing with his chaotic literary mate Mr. Bludyer. And that lauded series of finance papers everyone credited to a senior Commons statesman? Entirely the work of George Warrington of the Upper Temple, dashed out between cigars and lazy breakfasts.

It wasn’t all nonsense, of course. Percy Popjoy, whose dad Lord Falconet was a big shot in that same ruling party, could be spotted traipsing up to Warrington’s chambers three times a week, and the paper did run scoops no other outlet could get their hands on. Popjoy even had a regular byline of “P.P.” for his loud, overblown poems that had zero going for them besides volume, and his mediocre new novel got such absurdly over-the-top glowing reviews in the *Gazette* that readers were joking the protagonist could walk on water by the third chapter.

Pen never touched the political side of the operation, but he was the paper’s most giddy, hyperactive literary contributor. The *Gazette* offices were tucked in Catherine Street off the Strand, and Pen would bounce over several times a week with crumpled manuscripts stuffed in his coat pocket, buzzing with that new-car-smell thrill of a fresh literary career, where seeing your own name in print still feels like a magic trick and you’re fully convinced every line you write is sending ripples through London high society.

That’s where sub-editor Jack Finucane held court, running his little gossip empire with paste pot and a pair of scissors. No aristocrat’s dinner party, no old dowager’s funeral, no obscure country house fête slipped past his eagle eye. He dug up gossip from the tiniest provincial rags, remote Irish local papers, forgotten Scottish parish newsletters, all to pad his society column with endless accounts of the upper crust’s doings. It was equal parts ridiculous and heartwarming to watch Jack, tattered trousers and stained shirt sleeves, eating a cheap plate of takeaway meat and a pint of porter for lunch, describe a duchess’s glittering masked ball like he’d been seated right next to her at the banquet. Jack had barely ever stepped foot in a nice house, let alone a West End club, but his writing made him sound like he was on first-name terms with every ambassador in town. His bosses wisely kept him mostly on clipping duty rather than letting him write original copy, since half his society anecdotes were so full of errors they’d get the paper sued by every aristocrat in the country.

Pen poured all his energy into his reviews. He’d picked up a whole load of random reading in his youth, had a sharp eye for the funny bits, and his pieces went over a treat with both his bosses and the public. He was dead proud of the money he was earning off his own brain. Naturally, the whole of Fairoaks was a regular subscriber, with Pen’s mom and aunt poring over every issue like it was scripture. Clavering Park got it too, where that literary-minded young lady Helen Pendennis thought Pen’s writing was the cleverest thing she’d ever read, and old Dr. Portman, who got the paper once the widow had memorised all her son’s articles, gave it his full seal of approval, saying the lad had spirit and wrote like a proper gentleman even if he wasn’t a stuffy classical scholar.

The real fun kicked off when Major Pendennis wandered into his club, the Regent, to find Wenham, Lord Falconet, and half a dozen other posh, well-connected gents cackling over a recent issue of the *Gazette*. They were obsessing over Pen’s latest review, a sharp but perfectly polite takedown of the Countess of Muffborough’s new travel book about Spain and Italy, a volume so full of mangled French and butchered English no one could tell which language she was writing half the time. Pen had torn through her mistakes with such playful mock gravity that the whole thing was side-splitting, not a single rude word in sight, but the countess was now the laughing stock of every drawing room in Mayfair. Wenham was grinning so hard his bilious little face was crumpled up—Lady Muffborough had snubbed him and not invited him to any of her parties the year before. Lord Falconet was snorting with laughter too: he and Lord Muffborough had been petty rivals since they were at Eton together. Even the legendary Lord Steyne, who the Major showed the review to later, cackled and swore the Muffboroughs would be squirming under the burn like a harpooned whale.

Suddenly Major Pendennis, who’d been pretending his nephew’s little newspaper gig was far too low-brow for a dignified retired army man, was bragging to everyone in earshot, saying he’d always known the “young rascal had real talent”. He wrote a gushy letter to Pen’s mom at Fairoaks listing every great lord’s praise of her son, then wrote to Pen himself inviting him out for a chop, and saying he’d scored an invite for Pen to dinner at Gaunt House—Lord Steyne loved anyone who could make him laugh, no matter if they were a lord, a hack, or a total oddball.

Pen flung the letter across the table to Warrington, fully expecting his mate to lose his mind over the big news. Warrington just glanced at it, puffed on his cigar, and shrugged like it was no big deal.

That’s the thing about 24-year-old critics: they have zero imposter syndrome at all. Pen would’ve happily reviewed Macaulay’s entire *History of England* or Herschel’s giant astronomy tome after skimming it over one post-work cigar, acting like he was the older, wiser patron of both authors. A couple of months later, when he’d re-read those same reviews, he’d stare at the pages astonished at how learned and authoritative he sounded, and he couldn’t even remember which reference books he’d pulled all the obscure dates and names from. Luckily Warrington roasted him nonstop to keep his ego from ballooning so big it popped—if it were up to Shandon, he’d feed Pen’s delusions all day long, since he loved the kid’s snappy, flashy writing far more than Warrington’s slower, heavier, more thoughtful work.

For all his unearned confidence, though, Pen was a scrupulously honest critic—far too honest for their publisher Mr. Bungay’s liking, in fact. Bungay stormed into the office one morning frothing mad, because Pen had written a glowing, 5-star review of a book from their direct rival, Bacon’s publishing firm. Shandon pulled Pen aside to ask what on earth he was thinking.

“C’mon, kid,” Shandon said, “you think I sunk money into this whole journal just to give free publicity to the bloke who steals half my customers?”

Pen blinked at him, completely bewildered. “You think I should lie and say a perfectly good book is garbage just because the other guy printed it? Ruat coelum, let the sky fall, I’m not going to lie about what I read.”

Shandon laughed. “And what about our original prospectus? That thing was full of overblown nonsense, half the promises we made never happened. Did you lose sleep over that?”

“Nah,” Pen said, grinning. “Me and Warrington talked about that. That’s just a circus poster, making the giant on the bill look a foot taller than the actual giant inside the tent. No harm done. We’re just the fiddlers playing the tunes, we don’t have to own the whole show. But I’m not gonna lie about a book. I’ll hype all our authors as much as you want, I’ll roast a bad book from the other guys as hard as you like, fair and square—but I’m not gonna punch an opponent below the belt. I’d rather starve than write something unfair just to make us a few extra pennies.”

Shandon shook his head, amused. “Alright, kid, from now on we’ll get Bludyer to do all the hatchet jobs on Bacon’s books. Your soft little novice conscience is a luxury, you know. I had one once, too. But that bloom wears off after you’ve been a hack in this game long enough.”

Pen left the conversation feeling very proud of his integrity, vowing he’d never let the cynical world of journalism wear down his commitment to telling the truth. The universe, of course, decided to serve him a perfect little slice of humble pie immediately.

The very next issue of the *Gazette* ran a brutal, no-mercy hatchet job tearing apart the new *Spring Annual* poetry collection, by their savage star critic Mr. Bludyer, a man famous for ripping books to shreds for his own amusement. The most thoroughly eviscerated poem in the whole collection? Pen’s own secret submission, which he’d hidden under a fake pen name. He’d refused to review the book himself, so Shandon had handed it straight to Bludyer, who’d smashed every verse into tiny pieces like a bull rampaging through a flower bed. When he was done tearing the whole volume apart, he sold the tattered, marked-up copy to a used book stall, and spent every penny he got for it on a pint of brandy. Warrington was rolling on the floor cackling so hard he could barely breathe, while Pen stood there red-faced, staring at his own words being roasted to a pulp in the very newspaper he worked for.

Let us fast forward a few months of young Arthur Pendennis’s life, skipping over all the days that were thrilling for him but mind-numbingly tedious for anyone else reading his memoirs. Last we saw him, he’d officially signed on as a professional writer — a “literary hack,” as his mate George Warrington affectionately labels both himself and Pen. Let’s be real: the life of any hack, whether you’re churning out legal briefs, parish sermons, regimental reports, or merchant ledgers, is one boring routine after another, not worth detailing at length. One day’s grind looks almost identical to the last. A scribe who has no other marketable skill but his wits? He’s got to put Pegasus himself to work for rent, bills, and the next plate of mutton, no exceptions. Those fancy poetic aerial flights? Gone. Now Pegasus only soars like Mr. Green’s famous hot air balloon: only when the ticket money’s all collected, and only on pre-advertised dates. Most days, the poor winged beast is harnessed to a cobblestone street, trotting along pulling a Hansom cab, sides heaving, knees wobbly, and more often than not getting whipped by his driver for going too slow.

That said, we’re not wasting too much pity on Pegasus here. No reason a poetic horse should get a free pass on chores, sickness, or old age any more than the rest of God’s creatures. If he gets the whip, half the time he earned it, and Warrington and I will both fight any soft-hearted poetic soul who insists that “genius” and professional writers should be exempt from the boring, grindy, tax-paying, bread-winning drudgery the rest of us schmucks have to muddle through.

So there Pen was, chugging along at the newly launched *Pall Mall Gazette*, where his editors had pegged him as a flippant, sharp, entertaining critic, and he dutifully cranked out copy every week — flippant, sure, but honest, and as well-done as he could manage. Sure, the 70-year-old historian who’d spent 25 years slaving over a 12-volume tome on the Visigoths might’ve been a touch miffed that Pen knocked out his entire review of it in two days of half-distracted reading over tea at the British Museum. And the poet who spent three years polishing sublime sonnets might’ve been furious that Pen dismissed his entire magnum opus in 20 lines of cheeky sass, acting like he was a high court judge and the poet a shivering little nobody suing for crumbs of fame. The theatre folks complained about him too, and odds are he was way too hard on their new plays. But no real harm was done! Back in Pen’s day, there were barely any great historians, great poets, or great actors to pick fights with anyway. The few that got a light whipping found it did ‘em more good than harm — and for what it’s worth, Pen never pretended he was smarter or better than the people he was reviewing, he just had a good sense of humor and fair play, and zero overblown respect for his own writing. Plus he had Warrington at his elbow, ready to tear a strip off him faster than he’d tear into any unlucky author if Pen ever got too big for his breeches.

Between these critical labors, the occasional leading article he could write in good conscience, bits sold to monthly magazines, and the very hush-hush gig as London correspondent for the Chatteris Champion (no one ever gets Pen to admit that last one, but everyone agrees the letters from the metropolis in that paper were shockingly sharp), he was pulling in almost 400 pounds a year. By his second Christmas in London, he turned up at Fairoaks and handed his mom a full hundred pounds as a partial payback for the debt he owed to Laura. You can guess what that was like: Helen Pendennis read every single word he ever published, thought he was the greatest thinker and most elegant writer the world had ever seen, considered that hundred-pound payback an act of literal angelic virtue, fussed nonstop that he was ruining his health pulling all-nighters over manuscripts, and glowed every time he told her about the famous authors and fancy society people he’d met. Any of you who’ve seen the way mothers worship their sons, or how country women hang on every letter their darlings send back from London, know exactly the vibe. If John passes his law exam, Tom gets invited to a fancy ball, George dines with a famous politician? The whole village hears about it for months! Pen only stayed two weeks that winter, short as a sugar plum, but it was the best fortnight of Helen’s whole life. The Claverings were out of town, Laura was off visiting the ancient Lady Rockminster, Doctor Portman and the rest of the local gentry called on him and treated him like visiting royalty. The holidays flew by far too fast, and soon Pen was back to the busy London grind, and Helen was alone again, sending Pen’s hundred pounds straight on to Laura. No one’s quite sure why Laura conveniently chose that exact window to go visiting, and no one’s sure if Pen was more annoyed or secretly relieved she wasn’t around.

By this point, thanks to his own sharp wits and his uncle’s introductions, Pen was well-connected across London, in both literary and high society circles. The fact everyone assumed he was a posh gentleman with a nice private income who wrote for fun, not rent, was the greatest possible advantage a young scribbler could ask for. Bacon, Bungay & Co. practically fought each other to publish his articles; Mr. Wenham invited him to dinner; Mr. Wagg greeted him like an old friend, and all the publishing crew gushed about running into him at fancy Mayfair parties. The ton loved him too: he was handsome, well-spoken, never bothered anyone about their money problems, and had a reputation for being clever. Soon he was bouncing from one dinner invitation to the next, getting to know everyone from the grubby publishers of Paternoster Row to the fancy lords of Pimlico, just as comfortable at a tavern supper with his fellow hacks as he was at a dukes’ formal dining table.

Full of energy and curiosity, Pen fit in everywhere he went. One morning he’d eat breakfast at Mr. Plover’s house, surrounded by a peer, a bishop, a star parliamentary orator, two blue-stocking socialites, a wildly popular preacher, the author of the latest runaway bestseller, and the newest “exotic lion” fresh off a boat from Egypt or the American frontier. An hour later he’d be back in the grubby back room of the newspaper office, pens, ink, and soggy wet proof sheets waiting for him. Sub-editor Finucane would stop by with the latest gossip from the Row; Captain Shandon would wander in, nod at Pen, and scribble his leading article at the far end of the table, his daily mandatory pint of sherry set beside him that the office boy brought out silently the second he spotted the captain. Before long, you’d hear the booming roar of critic Mr. Bludyer in the front hall, where that brutal fellow would grab stacks of brand new books off the publisher’s counter despite poor Mr. Midge’s timid protests, sell ‘em off to the secondhand bookstall for drinking money, eat and drink all the proceeds in a tavern booth, then stumble back to the office to write a scathing “smash job” review of the exact novel he’d just sold for beer money. In the evenings Pen would stroll over to his club, pick up Warrington for a walk to get some fresh air, work up an appetite for dinner, then go make his bow at one of the many fancy houses that had opened their doors to him. Or there was the opera, or a night at the Eagle Tavern, or a Mayfair ball, or a quiet evening smoking cigars and chatting with Warrington about books, or a great new silly song down at the Back Kitchen. Back then Pen got to see every corner of London, and it never occurred him how much fun he was having — not until decades later, when balls bored him, farces didn’t make him laugh, tavern jokes left him cold, and even the most talented ballet dancer in the world couldn’t make him get up out of his armchair after dinner. Those days are long gone now, anyway. Bludyer won’t ever bully a new author or cheat a tavern landlord out of his tab again. Shandon, the clever, broke, witty fool, is long in his grave. We buried honest Doolan just the other week, he’ll never flatter anyone or chug a whisky noggin ever again.

The London season was in full, glorious swing, and the society papers were stuffed full of reports of grand banquets, routs, and balls keeping the ton entertained. The queen was holding levees and drawing rooms at St. James’s; the bow windows of the Mayfair clubs were crammed full of red-faced, respectable old guys reading their newspapers; thousands of carriages trundled along the Serpentine; whole squadrons of dandy horsemen trotted over Rotten Row. Everyone was in town — which of course meant Major Arthur Pendennis, who counted as somebody in that crowd, was not about to miss it.

One morning, the good major sat by his fireside, a fancy bandana tied around his head, his scrawny frame wrapped in a brilliant Turkish dressing gown, feet gently steaming in a foot bath, sipping his early tea and poring over his *Morning Post*. He couldn’t face the day without his two hour long toilet routine, that first cup of tea, and his beloved newspaper. Almost no one on earth besides his valet Morgan knew just how old and wobbly the major was getting, and how many tiny little comforts he needed to make it through the day.

People love to snicker at old ladies’ elaborate beauty routines — their paint, perfume, ringlets, all the little tricks they use to patch up the damage time has done. But the ladies know full well that old bachelors are just as vain, and their toilet routines are just as ridiculous as any old beauty’s. How does old Blushington keep that permanent little rose tint on his cheeks? Where does old Blondel get the stuff that dyes his silver hair a bright, youthful gold? Have you ever seen Lord Hotspur get off his horse when he thinks no one’s watching? You’d never guess that dashing young nobleman trotting along in Rotten Row is so wobbly on his feet once he’s out of the stirrups he can barely totter up his own front steps. Have you ever imagined 60-something “Dick Lacy” in his natural state, no stiff stays holding him upright? These old bucks are just as much fun to observe as the eldest vain old Venus of Belgravia. The old reprobate who hasn’t said a real prayer in 50 years, who’s given up hard liquor but still sits up with the lads telling dirty stories over toast and water, who can no longer chase pretty girls but still talks about it like he’s 22 — if a parson dragged him up to the front of his church one Sunday and preached a whole sermon about him, the old rascal might be shocked to find out he could actually be used for something good for once. But we’re wandering off our topic, the good major, whose feet Morgan pulls out of the foot bath now, dries them daintily, and starts dressing him: tight waistband, wig, starched cravat, spotless boots and gloves, the whole nine yards.

These long toilet hours were when Morgan and his master had their confidential chats. The major hated hanging around his empty lodgings by himself, and once Morgan was done getting him dressed and handing off his letters, he had most of the rest of the day to himself. That free time the polite, sharp valet spent hanging out with his peers: the valets and butlers of half the nobility of London. “Morgan Pendennis,” as the other gentlemen’s gentlemen called him, was a regular, welcome guest at the servants’ halls of some of the fanciest houses in town, a member of two exclusive servants’ clubs, and he knew every single bit of town gossip. The footmen and valets of the ton know all their masters’ secrets, the exact same way our parlour maids chat back in the kitchen about our stinginess, our money problems, our petty little spats with our spouses. If I leave this manuscript open on my desk, Betty the maid will definitely read it, and the whole staff will be gossiping about it over tea tonight, then she’ll bring me my breakfast tomorrow with a perfectly innocent face that could fool any judge. Nothing is secret in any house, great or small. No duke is a hero to his valet, and those valets get together and chat about their masters: who’s cheap and hoards all their money, who’s in hock to moneylenders signing bills of exchange, who’s having an affair with whose wife, which father refuses to marry his daughter off to the guy the family wants even if he’s got a title — every single bit of that gossip gets passed around, and anyone who’s anyone in society knows it. Everyone says Major Pendennis knows every secret in town, scandalous and discreet all at once — but most of that information he got straight from Morgan, who went out foraging for tea and gossip at the servants’ halls. After all, what better way to learn how high society works than to start on the ground floor — the kitchen?

So they chatted away as Morgan shaved the major. The day before there had been a drawing room at St. James’s, and the major spotted that Lady Clavering had been presented by Lady Rockminster, and Miss Amory by her mother Lady Clavering, and the paper went on for paragraphs describing their gowns in all sorts of weird jargon that would confuse any 22nd century historian who found the copy. The names jogged the major’s memory of the country. “How long have the Claverings been in London?” he asked. “Morgan, have you run into any of their staff?”

“Sir Francis fired his foreign valet, sir,” Morgan replied. “Hired a friend of mine as his personal man, actually — recommended him myself. You remember Towler, sir? Tall red-headed bloke, dyes his hair, was groom of the chambers for Lord Levant until his lordship’s whole household fell apart. Bit of a step down for Towler, sir, but poor blokes can’t be fussy,” he added, sounding genuinely sympathetic.

“Devilish hard on Towler, by gad!” the major cackled, highly amused. “And not great for Lord Levant, he he!”

“Knew that mess was coming ages ago, sir — told you four Michaelmases back, when her ladyship pawned all the diamonds. Towler was the one who stuffed ‘em all into two cabs and took ‘em to Dobree’s pawn shop, and most of the family silver went the same way. You don’t remember seeing the Levant crest on that silver at the big dinner the Marquis of Steyne threw at Blackwall, with Lord Levant sitting right across from the table staring at it? Beg pardon, sir, almost nicked your chin with the razor there.”

Morgan kept talking as he carefully ran the strop over his razor. “They’ve rented a big house in Grosvenor Place, and they’re putting on a huge show, sir. Her ladyship’s planning three big parties, plus a dinner every single week — her fortune can’t possibly keep up with that.”

“Gad, she had a fantastic cook back when I was staying at Fairoaks,” the major said, not feeling even a little bit bad about the widow Amory’s dwindling fortune.

“Name was Mirobolant, sir, and he’s left their service,” Morgan said — and this time the major was genuinely sympathetic, saying he was devilishly sorry to hear the good cook was gone.

“Whole enormous row about that Mirobolant bloke, sir!” Morgan continued. “At that ball in Baymouth? The cheeky sod challenged Mr. Arthur to a duel, and Mr. Pen was two seconds away from knocking him flat and throwing him out the window — serve him right, too — but Chevalier Strong stepped in and stopped the fight. Those French cooks have got more pride and arrogance than actual lords half the time.”

“I heard a little about that fight,” the major said. “But they didn’t fire him over that?”

“No sir, Mr. Pen was real generous about it, let the whole thing blow over. He got sacked over Miss Amory, though. Those French lads all think every single woman is in love with ‘em — he climbed right up the big grapevine outside her window trying to get in, and they caught him mid-climb. Mr. Strong ran out, got the garden pump, and hosed the whole lot of him down, absolute chaos.”

“Confound his impudence! You don’t mean to say Miss Amory encouraged him?” the major yelped, catching a very odd little smirk on Morgan’s face.

Morgan immediately smoothed his face back to perfectly neutral. “No idea about that sort of thing, sir. Servants never hear that kind of gossip. Odds are there was nothing to it, families get slandered left and right. Mirobolant left bag and baggage, saucepans, his whole kitchen, even his piano — the bloke had a piano, wrote French poetry for fun. He took a lodging down in Clavering, hung around the estate forever, people said the milliner Madam Fribsy was sneaking letters between him and Miss Amory, though I don’t believe a word of it. The whole story that he tried to kill himself with charcoal fumes? All a fake between him and Fribsy, and the gamekeeper almost shot him skulking around the park one night.”

Later that very day, the Major planted himself in the big bow window of Bays’s Club on St. James’s Street, at that 4pm hour where half a dozen other respectable old fogies are lined up there exactly the same way, people-watching. Back in the Prince Regent’s day those old gents in that window were the flashiest dandies in the whole empire, even if Bays’s is now a club mostly full of men over 50. The major was staring out when he spotted his nephew Arthur walking down the street, out for a stroll with his mate Mr. Popjoy.

“Look!” Popjoy said to Pen as they passed. “Have you ever walked past Bays’s at four o’clock and not seen that whole collection of old fogies lined up in the window? It’s a human museum. They ought to cast all of ‘em in wax and stick ‘em in Madame Tussaud’s.”

“Stick ‘em in the Chamber of Horrors all to themselves,” Pen cackled.

“Chamber of Horrors! Gad, that’s perfect!”

For all that valet Morgan fancied himself the most well-informed man in London about everyone else’s bank balances, half of Mayfair knew more about Lady Clavering’s alleged vast fortune than he did. The gossip mill was running so hot by the time the Claverings rolled into polite society that folks were swearing her wealth was basically infinite: indigo factories spitting out profit day and night, opium clippers cutting through the ocean with holds full of cash, London banks overflowing with rupees, piles of stolen native prince jewels, and stacks of interest payments from Indian royals who’d borrowed fortunes from her dad three generations back. Her banker’s account number had so many zeros appended to it that every listener’s jaw dropped to match, and everyone whispered that there was even a special envoy from the Nawab of Lucknow — Colonel Altamont, that wild adventurer who’d supposedly converted to Islam and survived a dozen near-death scrapes — over in London trying to sell Lady Clavering (aka the Begum Clavering, as society had already dubbed her) the Nawab’s legendary 200-carat nose ring diamond, “The Light of the Dewan.”

Historians of the British constitution love to brag that any low-born bloke with enough merit can waltz right into the House of Lords and sit next to a Cavendish or a Stanley, and it’s equally true that London high society, for all its haughty sniffing and gatekeeping, will drop every last one of its barriers the second someone shows up with enough cash. That’s not snobbery, that’s good old British honesty: our fancy aristocrats aren’t the stuck-up prigs the unwashed masses think they are! No, if you’re rolling in money, they’ll shake your hand, eat your truffle suppers, dance at your balls, marry your daughters, or hand over their own perfect heiress children to your sons, just as affably as any common tradesman would.

Chevalier Strong, who’d already overseen the kitting out of Clavering’s country estate, was only too happy to lend his exquisite, extremely self-serving taste to the London upholsterers decking out their Grosvenor Place townhouse. He fussed over hanging and rehanging every painting, tested every sofa for ideal napping placement, schmoozed wine merchants and caterers for the new household… and also used the opportunity to kit out his own cozy Shepherd’s Inn chambers and stock his tiny cellar with perfectly drinkable claret. His friends complimented his very neat new digs, and the select few who stopped by for a lamb cutlet with Strong now got to wash it down with something far better than the swill he’d been serving six months prior. The Chevalier was, as he put it, “in clover,” waited on by an old comrade from the Spanish Legion he’d last seen half-dead in the breach of a fort, and then stumbled across sweeping a crossing in Tottenham Court Road. This ex-legionary also looked after Strong’s current lodger, who was none other than that famous Nawab of Lucknow favourite, the valiant Colonel Altamont.

Ned Strong was the least curious (or, more accurately, the most discreet) man alive, and he didn’t poke around at the weird, clearly blackmail-adjacent dynamic that had popped up between Sir Francis Clavering and Altamont ever since their first meeting at Baymouth. It was obvious the Colonel held some very juicy dirt on the weak-willed Baronet, and Strong, who already knew Clavering’s youth had been extremely wild and his Indian army career far less than glorious, assumed Altamont had caught Clavering out in some forgery or dodgy scheme back in Calcutta that he could ruin him for.

One day after a very heated meeting, Altamont crumpled up a note Clavering had sent via Strong, snarling that a hundred quid was nowhere near enough. “Tell Clavering I need a thousand, or by Jove I spill every last secret and blow his whole life to smithereens,” he’d roared. “Give me the money by next Friday noon, and I’ll ship off abroad and leave him alone for a full year. If it’s not here? I’m running a gossip paragraph in the local paper Saturday, and the whole world will know everything by the end of the week.”

Strong delivered the message, and the very next Friday at noon he was at Altamont’s Baymouth hotel with the full thousand. Altamont behaved like the perfect gentleman, settled his bill, announced he was off on a foreign tour, and Strong even saw him set sail at Dover. “Must be a dodgy forged bill he’s got Clavering over a barrel for,” Strong thought.

But less than a year later, Altamont was back, grumbling that a completely unheard-of run of fourteen reds in a row at Baden-Baden roulette had cleaned him out. No man could survive that streak, he insisted, no matter how lucky he was. He’d been forced to draft a bill on Clavering to pay for his passage home, and Clavering, who was already hemorrhaging cash on election expenses, the country house, and the new London townhouse, was absolutely furious, cursing that he wished the Colonel could rot in a German debtor’s prison for the rest of his days so he’d never have to see his face again.

He couldn’t ask his wife for the money, though. Lady Clavering was more than generous with household spending, but she’d inherited her father Snell’s sharp business brain alongside his massive fortune, and only gave her husband a very handsome, but firmly capped, gentleman’s allowance. She’d occasionally spot him extra cash or pay off a gambling debt, but she always demanded a full itemized account for every penny, so Clavering couldn’t possibly tell her about the thousands he was shovelling to Altamont.

Strong’s new unofficial job was sourcing all that hidden cash for his patron. Back in his Shepherd’s Inn chambers, a parade of money lenders popped in to meet Clavering, passing over bank notes and stamped promissory notes back and forth like they were handing out tea biscuits. When a man gets used to running up debts and trading 12-month IOU’s for ready cash from the time he’s a teen, no windfall of fortune ever actually fixes the habit. Strong, who didn’t own a penny to his own name, ended up signing as the drawer on almost every single one of Clavering’s bills, haggling with the holders when they came due, negotiating instalments, and drawing up new bills to extend the deadlines. Hundreds of these gallant London souls wander around town cheerfully, eating well and sleeping soundly, even with a bailiff lingering around the corner and a whole noose of debt wrapped around their necks, just like the garrison at Comorn who put on plays and danced balls even as the enemy was at the gates and a gallows waited for them if they lost. Old soldier Ned Strong bore that little inconvenience like a total champ.

But before we go poking around the rest of Shepherd’s Inn’s colourful residents, we’ve left Lady Clavering and her guests loitering on the Grosvenor Place doorstep far too long.

First they piled into the grand dining room, which was kitted out in heavy middle-aged oak for no reason Lady Clavering could name. “Unless it’s ‘cause me and Clavering are actually middle-aged,” she laughed, and offered them the leftovers of the luncheon she and Blanche had just finished. Everyone had assumed Blanche, the little Sylphide, only ate six grains of rice a day like Amina, the Ghoul’s friend in the Arabian Nights — but out of sight of the servants, she’d dug into three very substantial mutton cutlets, a bit of fashionable hypocrisy that every other debutante in London was also very familiar with. Pen and his uncle politely declined the leftovers, and admired the room with all the correct, required compliments, calling it “exquisitely chaste,” which was the mandatory buzzword for any fancy rich person’s decor. There were clunky 17th century Dutch high-backed chairs, a 16th century carved buffet, a sideboard made out of stolen choir stalls from a Low Countries church, a giant brass cathedral lamp hanging over the round oak table, fake old family portraits bought off Wardour Street, secondhand French tapestries, and full sets of cardboard armour and battleaxes that wouldn’t have hurt a toddler. Nothing, the whole party agreed, could possibly be more chaste.
