The library behind it was equally solemn: matching sets of books no one would ever read, huge overstuffed easy chairs, and severe bronze statues, guarded by double doors so Sir Francis could smoke his cigars, read *Bell’s Life in London*, and fall asleep after dinner in perfect peace when he wasn’t off playing billiards at his club or punting at the Saint James’s gambling houses.

But none of that could hold a candle to the drawing rooms. The carpets were so thick and fluffy your foot made no more noise on them than your shadow, dotted with roses and tulips printed as big as warming pans. There were high chairs, low chairs, bandy-legged chairs, chairs so spindly it was a wonder anything heavier than a fairy could sit on them, marquetterie tables heaped with cheap fancy trinkets, china from every country on earth, gilt daggers, Books of Beauty, Turkish slippers, and open boxes of Parisian bonbons. Dresden shepherds and shepherdesses sat on every end table alongside porcelain blue poodles and squawking chickens, there were Boucher nudes and Greuze shepherdesses (all extremely chaste, of course), layers of muslin and brocade curtains, gilded birdcages full of lovebirds, and two cockatoos screaming at each other so loud you could hear them down the block. One clock played silly little tunes on the console, another boomed the hours like Great Tom of Oxford over the mantel. It was perfect, the peak of modern high society taste — the decadent Romans of the late Empire, or the fancy courtesans of Louis XV, couldn’t have done better. Everyone who stepped through the doors agreed Lady Clavering’s reception rooms were far more chaste and elegant than even Lady Harley Quin’s, or the railroad millionaire’s wife Mrs. Hodge-Podgson’s.

Poor Lady Clavering herself didn’t care two figs about any of it. “All I know is the whole thing cost a fortune, Major,” she told her guest, “and I’d strongly advise you not to sit on one of those flimsy gilt chairs. I fell straight through one the second night we threw a dinner party. Why didn’t you lot come visit us sooner? We’d have invited you.”

“You’d have loved seeing Mamma crash through a chair, wouldn’t you Mr. Pendennis?” Blanche snarked, the little Sylphide annoyed that Pen was getting on so well with her mother.

“I’d have been very happy to offer Lady Clavering my arm to catch her, if she’d needed it,” Pen replied, blushing a little as he bowed.

“ Quel preux Chevalier!” Blanche huffed, tossing her tiny head.

“I’ve got a lot of sympathy for people who fall over, remember,” Pen grinned. “I made a total fool of myself falling once not long ago.”

“And you ran straight home to Laura so she could console you,” Blanche shot back. Pen winced. He hated that his humiliating rejection by Laura was now common gossip, so he immediately pivoted to gushing about how stunning all the furniture was, to change the subject.

“No, don’t praise me,” honest Lady Clavering waved him off. “The upholsterer and Captain Strong did it all while we were up at the country park. Lady Rockminster came round the other day and said the salons are very nice, so that must be good.”

“Lady Rockminster is my cousin Laura’s patron,” Pen said.

“The *Dowager* Lady Rockminster,” Major Pendennis corrected, his voice dropping to a reverent hush like he was in church the second the most famous name in all of fashionable society was mentioned. “If you have her approval, Lady Clavering, you cannot possibly go wrong. She’s the absolute centre of taste and fashion, everyone knows that. These rooms are magnificent.” He looked around the drawing rooms with the sort of awed reverence he usually reserved for royal coronations.

“Yes, she’s took us up,” Lady Clavering said.

“*Taken* us up, Mamma,” Blanche yelped, sharp as a tack.

“Taken us up, then,” Lady Clavering laughed. “It’s very kind of her, but it feels a bit odd at first, being taken up like a parcel. She wants to arrange all our balls and write all our dinner party invites for us, but I told her no way. I want to invite my own old friends, not have her sit at the head of my own table like it’s her house. You two must come over on the 14th, Arthur and Major — it’s not one of the huge fancy dinners, Blanche,” she added, noticing her daughter was biting her lips and frowning.

The Major smiled and bowed, saying he far preferred small quiet gatherings to stuffy big dinners, he’d had more than enough of those in his decades of social climbing.

“I always think a dinner tastes best the second day,” Lady Clavering added helpfully, trying to fix her phrasing — and making it way, way worse. Blanche clasped her hands in despair, and groaned “O mamma, vous etes incorrigible!” The Major swore he loved small snug dinners more than anything in the world, even as he mentally noted he could easily dump the Claverings if a better invitation popped up before the 14th. Pen, meanwhile, was far too new to the dinner-party circuit to turn down the chance at a fancy feast in a fancy house, and accepted immediately.

Once they were walking down the street, the Major turned to Pen. “What was that little spat you two were having with Miss Amory? I thought you two were getting on famously not long ago.”

“‘Used to’ is a very vague word for where a woman’s concerned,” Pen said, striking a dandified pose. “Was and is are two completely different things, especially when it comes to ladies’ hearts.”

“Egad, they change faster than the weather,” the Major laughed. “When we were stationed at the Cape, there was a lady who swore she’d poison herself over me — and three months later she ran off with some other man, leaving her husband behind. Don’t get tangled up with that Miss Amory, Arthur. She’s forward, affected, and not nearly as well-bred as she pretends, and she’s only got ten thousand pounds to her name. That’s nothing! I could barely pay her milliner’s bills with the interest on that sum.”

“Bit of a connoisseur of millinery, are we, Uncle?” Pen teased.

“I was, sir, I was!” the old man winked, giving a very over-the-hill flirtatious little bow to a fancy carriage rolling past them into Hyde Park. “That’s Lady Catherine Martingale’s carriage — her daughters are very pretty, but their mother was a thousand times more beautiful back in the day. Listen to me, Arthur, with your good looks and your prospects, you could make an excellent marriage someday. Don’t go telling anyone back at Fairoaks this, but a reputation for being a little bit wicked, a dangerous homme dangereux, does no harm at all with the ladies. They hate milksops, they love a bit of rogue in a young man. But when it comes to actual marriage? That’s a different matter entirely. Marry a woman with money. I’ve told you before, it’s just as easy to marry a rich girl as a poor one, and infinitely more comfortable to sit down to a fancy well-cooked dinner with three courses of entrees than a cold leg of mutton with a wife who can’t afford anything better.”

Pen quoted the old hackneyed ballad back at him: “It has to be a cottage with a double coach-house, a cottage of gentility, sir,” and the Major, who had no idea what poem he was referencing, carried on with his very cynical life advice, delighted his nephew was such a quick study. Grumpy Warrington was still back at their lodgings complaining that Pen was turning into such a insufferable little puppy no one would be able to stand him soon — but the truth was, Warrington loved seeing his young friend bright, healthy, and full of hope, the way a grown man long past pantomimes still gets a kick out of watching a kid gape at clowns and harlequins. Pen’s old sullen moods had vanished now that fortune was finally smiling on him, and he was blooming like a rose in summer sun.

# CHAPTER XXXIX: In Which Colonel Altamont Crashes The Dinner Party, Makes A Spectacle Of Himself, Then Bails Before Anyone Can Even Offer Him A Proper Slice Of Pineapple
The pre-arranged dinner at Sir Francis Clavering’s found Major Pendennis turning up only because every far more impressive boring snob engagement he’d bet on fell through, and Arthur Pen tagging along because he couldn’t think of a single better way to waste the evening. The drawing room was nearly empty when they trundled in: only the Claverings, plus their old mate Captain Strong. Pen was chuffed to see him, but the Major pulled a face so sour it could’ve curdled the nearby lemonade, dead annoyed at the thought of dining next to what he rudely called “Clavering’s blasted glorified house steward.”

His mood perked up considerably when Welbore Welbore, Clavering’s stuffy country neighbor and fellow MP, wandered in. Though perfectly dull, and so quiet at dinner he contributed less conversation than the footman looming behind his chair, Welbore was an ancient-family country gentleman with seven thousand a year, and the Major always relaxed instantly around people that rich and unthreatening. The rest of the party trickled in after that: the sharp old Dowager Lady Rockminster, who had very specific scheming reasons to stay in the Claverings’ good graces, plus Lady Agnes Foker and her doofus son Harry, our old acquaintance. Mr. Pynsent bailed last minute for parliamentary duties – duties the other two MPs at the party were actively ignoring so hard they’d already eaten half the bread rolls.

Last to show was Blanche Amory, decked out in a devastating white silk gown that showed off her pearly shoulders so well they could’ve been on a fancy pastry platter. Harry Foker leaned over to Pen, who was staring at her with obvious heart eyes, and stage whispered that she was a total stunner. Blanche was on her absolute best “simple country ingénue” behavior that night, greeting Pen like they’d been separated at birth, gushing about dear Fairoaks, asking after Laura and his mom, and carrying on about how she was dying to get back to the quiet countryside. Harry Foker, who usually clammed up so hard around ladies he could barely order a drink, suddenly found himself chattering away like a magpie, even before the dinner bell rang.

He was dead gutted he didn’t get to escort Blanche down the sweeping carpeted stairs, but as an earl’s grandson he got stuck with Mrs. Welbore Welbore instead – turns out that stuffy rank thing has real downsides. But he whooped internally when he got seated right next to Blanche at the dinner table, convinced he’d pulled off the greatest dining table heist in history. Spoiler: it was Blanche who’d moved the place cards, duh. She had Pen on one side and Harry on the other, and both boys were tripping over themselves to be charming.

Harry’s mom, from across the table, stared at her son like he’d grown a second head he was being that lively. He rambled about Taglioni dancing in *The Sylphide*, begged the footman for more volaille soup, pondered out loud where all the chicken legs go when they serve that soup, and hummed the ballet’s little tune off-key. Pen purred that Blanche was basically a sylph herself. Blanche gushed that Harry had the most wonderful tenor voice she’d ever heard, that she sang a little too, and they absolutely should do a duet. Pen remembered Blanche had said the exact same line to him a couple years back, and snickered to himself wondering how many other guys she’d tricked into singing duets with her, but obviously he didn’t say that out loud – instead he oozed that her voice was still his absolute favorite in the whole world, way better than Laura’s annoying contralto that was always going hoarse anyway, the little bitter gremlin.

Harry loudly declared he liked English music best, and could the footman pass him the saddle of mutton please? Blanche instantly agreed that English ballads were the only real music worth listening to. Pen begged her to sing one after dinner, and Blanche turned to Harry, giving him the full force of her big flirty eyes, and said she’d sing an English song for him if he promised to come right upstairs after the men finished their wine. Harry promised he’d be up fast, said he didn’t care about fancy expensive wine anyway, he was just here for the dinner, and then rambled on about how the hell did they get cherries inside jelly? That was the kind of question no one had ever asked at a fancy London dinner before, but Blanche listened to every single word like he was reciting poetry. When the ladies left, she dropped her gloves by Harry’s chair and her handkerchief by Pen’s, so both of them had a little souvenir to moon over. Her last bright, dazzling glance went straight to Harry, who sighed so loud his wine glass rattled, then chugged a full bumper of claret.

This dinner was way earlier than the usual 9pm London season fancy banquets, so it was still full daylight when the ladies trundled upstairs to the drawing room. The windows were propped open to let in the summer air, little urchins on the sidewalk jumped up at the area railings to peek in and gawk at the spread of pineapples and decanters, dandies trotted their horses past on the way to their fancy parties, the gas man lit the street lamps, and soon the chandelier inside the dining room was glowing bright, while outside the sky was soft blue, with a couple of stars just starting to pop on. The footman Jeames was leaning against the portico pillar staring at the sky, when he noticed a very flashily dressed bloke hanging off the railings, covered in chains and jewelry, wearing shiny boots and a slipping black wig, staring right into the dining room and cackling every time Strong laughed at a joke.

The guy was clearly three sheets to the wind, and when Strong laughed extra loud, he slapped his thigh and winked at Jeames like they were old mates. Jeames hated the look of him, so he softly called over the passing Policeman X, pointed, and didn’t say a single other word. Policeman X told the guy to move along, but he just kept cackling and staring at Strong. When X tapped his shoulder, the bloke squared up like he was ready to fight, slurring that he knew everyone inside, that was Sir Francis Clavering the MP, that was Strong, that was the kid who caused a fight at the Baymouth ball. He yelled “Strong! Strong!” so loud everyone inside heard him. “Bloody Altamont!” Sir Francis yelped, jumping out of his chair like he’d sat on a pin. Strong groaned, got up, and ran outside to drag him away, calling for a cab.

But Altamont was furious the copper had tried to move him on, and he fought his way past Strong right through the front door, slurring that he was going in the dining room to get a bottle of the good yellow seal wine that cost 100 rupees a dozen. Sir Francis, panicking that the very boring, very wealthy Mr. Welbore would think he was a total weirdo, caved and said fine, come on in. Colonel Altamont – the “ambassador” for the Nawaub of Lucknow everyone had met once at that messy Baymouth ball, if you don’t remember – stumbled into the dining room, plopped down in the first empty chair, and immediately started guzzling wine.

Strong, never one to lose his cool, deadpanned that the man had lived for ages in an Indian native court, where they all had very eccentric habits. Major Pendennis stared, wondering what on earth sort of low-rent dinner he’d accidentally been invited to. Harry Foker perked up, recognized him as the guy who sang that terrible Malay song at the Back Kitchen pub, and offered him some pineapple. Altamont snorted and said the Lucknow Nawaub fed pineapples to his pigs. Then he looked over at Pen, cackled and said he remembered him from the Baymouth ball, trying to fight that French guy. Then he locked eyes with Major Pendennis, and the second their gazes met, he went stone cold sober for two seconds, his wig slipped sideways off his head, he yelped “Captain Beak! Captain Beak!” and bolted right out the door, Strong hot on his heels. He was there for all of 15 minutes tops.

Sir Francis Clavering went white as a sheet, staring at the Major like he’d just seen a ghost. The Major squinted, pretended to think for a minute, then went “Oh! Wait a second, that’s that ex-Horse Artillery deserter who got a job with the Lucknow court! I recognize his face perfectly.” Clavering exhaled so hard he almost collapsed with relief. The cab Strong had called pulled up, they shoved Altamont inside, and the rest of the guys trundled upstairs to the ladies. Harry Foker told Pen it was the weirdest, rummest event he’d ever witnessed in his entire life, which Pen agreed was a very well thought out observation.

Blanche sang her heart out on the piano, even busted out a little tune she’d set to Pen’s own old poetry, so Pen was completely wrapped around her finger the whole time, turning pages for her like a loyal little lapdog. Harry sighed so loud the curtains fluttered, saying he wished he could write poetry like Pen, he’d make Blanche fall for him in two seconds flat. No one mentioned the drunk chaos downstairs to the ladies; Pen almost spilled the beans but caught sight of Clavering’s terrified face and kept his mouth shut. The evening was pretty dull after that: Welbore fell asleep in his chair, the Major didn’t tell any of his usual scandalous little stories, just sat quiet watching Blanche sing.

When it was time to leave, the Major gushed to Blanche that she was a perfect nightingale, the best singer in all of London, and that every fancy drawing room in the city would be begging to invite her. Pen was shocked, because his uncle had been calling Blanche a useless little sylph five minutes earlier that very morning. On the walk home, Harry stuck with the two of them, smoking a giant cigar, chattering nonstop about Blanche Amory the whole way back to Pen’s rooms in the Temple, gushing about her singing and how wonderful she was, and barely even noticed when the Major ducked into his own house and scurried inside, clearly very deep in some scheming, snobbish thought. The Major called Pen back for a second, whispered at him to make sure he went and called at Grosvenor Place the next day, they’d been extremely civil to him, very civil indeed. Pen promised, all confused, and then Harry dragged him off to ramble more about his new crush for the rest of the walk.

Since that giddy, fateful night in Grosvenor Place, Mr. Harry Foker’s heart had been in such a dither you’d never believe the self-styled great philosopher of the fast set could endure it. Just think: not long ago, he was the one doling out perfectly cynical advice to young Pen, boasting that a life of well-funded self-indulgence would harden him right up, until he cared for nobody on God’s green earth other than Mr. Harry Foker himself. He should have grown more jaded with every claret toast and winning billiards break, right? But as it turns out, no man escapes the universal mishap of falling head over heels at least once — not Achilles, not Ajax, not Lord Nelson, not even old Adam himself — and Harry’s number was up. The all-conquering Love had grabbed him good.

That night, after dumping Pendennis at his crummy Lamb Court staircase landing, Harry trundled over to his usual hangout the Back Kitchen, but the gin twist tasted like dishwater and the devilled turkey had all the appeal of cardboard. His mates’ jokes bounced right off him, and when the famous Mr. Hodgen debuted his brand new comic masterpiece *The Cat in the Cupboard*, the follow-up to his viral hit *The Body Snatcher*, Harry sat there grunting “Bravo, Hodgen!” on pure autopilot, not catching a single lyric. He stumbled home dead tired an hour later, flopped onto his downy feather bed, but his brain was running a fever, and the very second he blinked awake the next morning, Blanche Amory’s face was right there behind his eyelids, grey eyes sparkling, grinning like: “I’m your princess now, dummy. No more silly pugilism bets for you.”

Suddenly every single one of his old favorite hobbies felt stale and disgusting. Harry had never been much for hanging around “modest women” of his own class — their boring decorum couldn’t compete with the lively, fun chatter of the theatre girls he was used to. His own mother, for all her kindness, couldn’t tell a joke to save her life, and his cousins, the Earl of Rosherville’s daughters, bored him witless. One was a geologist who ranted about trilobites for hours on end, one was a rabid horsewoman who smoked cigars and only talked steeplechases, one was ultra Low Church who was convinced her sister (the very High Church girl who’d turned her closet into a tiny personal altar and fasted every single Friday) was basically a secret heretic. Harry swore he’d rather spend three days on a prison treadmill than visit their family seat at Drummington. His cousin Erith, Rosherville’s heir, called Harry vulgar and ill-mannered; Harry for his part called Erith a prig so dry he could put the entire House of Commons to sleep mid-philanthropic speech. Even the old Earl himself still hadn’t recovered from that night he condescended to play billiards with Harry, and the lad poked him in the ribs with his cue and said, “Well old cock, I’ve seen plenty of bad shots in my day, but that’s the worst one I ever laid eyes on.” The Earl finished the game with forced angelic temper, nearly had a stroke that night, and hid in his private rooms until Harry left for Oxbridge. He’d never mention the incident, never invite Harry to play billiards again, and could barely force a “how do you do” when they met, but he dared not kick Harry out entirely — Harry’s dad the brewer held stacks of the Earl’s IOUs from very private business deals.

The Earl did have a fifth daughter, though: Lady Ann Milton. Since they were both in pinafores and scuffed school boots, the whole family had agreed Ann would marry Harry when he came of age. Ann never thought to question it any more than Esther would argue with King Ahasuerus. Harry’s dad had sat him down once, gruff and to the point: “Marry Ann, she’s got the best blood in England, and I’ll keep you living comfortably. If you say no, you get 200 quid a year while I’m alive, see how you like scraping by.” Harry, who knew his dad never made empty threats, just shrugged and said, “Sure, sir, if she’s fine with it, I’m game. She’s not bad looking. No rush though, right? I’d like to get my fling out first.” His dad laughed and said “Fling away, no one’s stopping you.” Harry had a tiny, boring little portrait of Ann tucked on his sitting room shelf, but it looked absolutely ridiculous next to the camp French prints, signed portraits of opera dancers, and racing art he’d collected. Ann, for her part, was the most unexciting figure in society: everyone knew she was reserved for Harry, no other man even bothered to flirt with her.

Bright and early the morning after Lady Clavering’s dinner, Harry woke up humming the little tune Blanche had sung the night before, moping “ofttimes I hover, ofttimes I hover” under his breath while propped up against his crimson silk bed coverlet. His room was plastered with the art he’d spent years collecting: French prints of Turkish romance trysts, opera nymphs, signed portraits of actress Miss Calverley in her famous tight pageboy tights, Miss Rougemont posing as Venus. His nightstand was cluttered with all his usual weekend plans: a rambling note from Miss Calverley who called him “Hokey-pokey Fokey” in atrocious spelling, reminding him he’d promised a dinner at the Elephant and Castle in Richmond; a private box ticket for Miss Rougemont’s upcoming benefit; a stack of invites to a prizefight night where Conkey Sam, Dick the Nailor, and the Worcestershire Nobber were set to put on a show. And tucked right there, almost forgotten, was the tiny sprig of verbena Blanche had given him at the party.

Suddenly all of that garbage was meaningless. Who cared if the Worcestershire Nobber won his fight? What was the point of 37-year-old Calverley cooing silly nicknames at him? The thought of that Richmond dinner filled him with utter disgust.

His mum Lady Agnes wandered in, frowning at his pale, glum face. “Harry! I know you’ve been off playing billiards at that wicked Spratt’s again! Those late nights with the billiard cues will kill you, mark my words!” Harry moped “It’s not the billiards, ma’am.” “Then it’s that terrible Back Kitchen!” she huffed. “I’ve half a mind to write the landlady and tell her to put barely any wine in your negus, and make sure you wrap a shawl around you before you climb in your brougham!” Harry sighed. “You can write her, she’s a perfectly nice woman, but it’s not the Back Kitchen either.” Lady Agnes was the softest, most indulgent mum alive, never said no to Harry, and he never hid his shenanigans from her — she’d gush to all her friends that Spratt’s was de rigueur, just like the gambling parties at Brookes’s in Mr. Fox’s day, and the Back Kitchen was full of important authors her own papa had always wanted to invite to Drummington, before Dr. Johnson died.

Harry decided to butter her up to get what he wanted. “That dinner at the Claverings yesterday? Unbeatable. Their clear soup’s better than ours, Moufflet our cook dumps way too much tarragon in everything. Their maraschino jelly? Stunning. Their house? Loads of fancy plate, no expense spared at all. The Claverings are great fun, we should invite them over as soon as possible.” Lady Agnes made a noncommittal “hmm” but agreed, and marked a date in her diary two whole weeks away — a stretch of time that felt like a thousand years to lovestruck Harry. When he glanced at the written invitation, he saw she’d left Blanche out entirely. “You forgot Miss Amory, Lady Clavering’s daughter!” he protested. “Oh that little creature!” Lady Agnes said. “No Harry, she seemed awfully forward, only sang right at you and Mr. Pendennis all night.” Harry lied so badly you could practically see the sweat bead on his forehead. “Pendennis is dead madly in love with her, that’s all! She sings wonderful. C’mon add her name in.” Lady Agnes sighed and scribbled Blanche on the card, and Harry hugged her so tight he nearly crushed her bonnet.

Back in his own room, he flopped on the ottoman in his fancy embroidered smoking jacket, puffing his pipe and daydreaming a hundred dumb schemes to run into Blanche. His polyglot valet Anatole, who spoke every language equally badly, read all his mail, and knew every address of his seedy mates, noticed Harry was impossibly fussy that morning. He tried on a dozen pairs of trousers, complained every stripe was ugly, every boot was over-varnished, every shirt pattern too loud. Finally, blushing bright tomato red, he mumbled at Anatole: “When you got hired, you said you could do hair styling, right?” Anatole said yes, confused. “Go get a pair of curling tongs,” Harry mumbled, “and make my hair a little curly, yeah?” Anatole hunted high and low for tongs, first asking Harry Senior’s butler whose master was bald as an egg, finally borrowing Lady Agnes’s hair tongs, and curled Harry’s hair until it was poofy and tight as a prize poodle’s coat. After he primped for another hour, Anatole whispered, “Shall I order the drag to Miss Calverley’s at six o’clock, sir?” Harry almost snarled. “Tell her I can’t — no, hang it, Poyntz and Rougemont and all the rest are coming, I have to show up. Fine, pick me up at Pelham Corner at six.”

Instead of heading straight to the livery stables, Harry ordered his own private carriage, and made a very silly detour past Blanche’s house in Grosvenor Place, just to gawk up at her pink window curtains for ten whole seconds like that counted as a romantic encounter, before he drove off to Pen’s tiny crummy Lamb Court chambers. Wait a minute! Pen had been living in London for two whole years, and Harry had visited his chambers less than half a dozen times total! Why was he in such a tearing hurry to see him now?

Oh, you girls know exactly why! When that silly love fever hits you, suddenly your crush’s most boring mate is the most interesting person on the planet, just cause he stood next to her! He knows what her favorite song is! He’s near the rose, even if he ain’t the rose himself. Poor Harry had completely forgotten his quiet, boring fiancee Lady Ann back at Drummington — if she could have seen him right then, primped up with his poodle curls and scent all over his coat, she’d have fainted dead away.

Poor Harry Foker would later swear that the Richmond dinner he threw was the single most wasteful thing he’d ever dropped a guineas on, worse than that time he bet on a tortoise race at a country fair. Sitting glumly at the head of the table, staring at melting ices and cigar ash sprinkled over half-eaten pineapple, he had a full-on epiphany: when the hell did I ever think these people were fun? He could see Rougemont’s crow’s feet peeking out under her makeup, which was slathered on so thick you could scrape a chunk off with a dinner knife like a pantomime clown’s greasepaint. Calverley’s overdone street slang was so cringey it made his teeth hurt, and old Lord Colchicum? The doddering peer had rolled up in his brougham, coronet gleaming on the door, and wedged himself tight between French acrobat Mademoiselle Coralie and her mum like he’d won a raffle for the spot. “I’m not stuck up,” Foker thought to himself, stirring his melted ice with a fork, “but an English lord getting all cozy with a Franconi’s horse rider? Nah mate, that won’t do.”

“Twopence halfpenny for your thoughts, Fokey!” Rougemont purred, popping her cigar out of her bright vermilion lips.
“Does Foker ever have thoughts?” drawled Poyntz, snickering. “Got a fair capitalist over this end of the table ready to pay good money for whatever’s bouncing around that empty little head of yours, old chap.”
Calverley leaned over to her neighbor, sneering: “Ugh, what’s Poyntz even on about? He’s such a drawling, sneering little creep.”
“Mon dieu, what a droll tiny man that little Fokare is,” Coralie trilled in her thick Gascon accent, dark eyes glinting. “He doesn’t look a day over twenty.”
The ancient, purple-faced Lord Colchicum sighed, leaning into his giant goblet of claret. “I wish I was his age.”
“Bah, youth? I couldn’t care less,” Coralie’s mum Madame Brack huffed, pinching a huge helping of snuff straight out of Colchicum’s fancy gold snuff box. “I only like grown men, like milor here. Coralie, you only like grown men too, don’t you, my little pet?”
My lord grinned. “You flatter me, madam.”
“Shut up mum, you’re being an idiot,” Coralie shrugged, which made Colchicum snort and pocket his snuff box fast before Madame Brack’s grubby fingers could dive in for a second helping.

We don’t need to drag you through the rest of that raucous, unedifying dinner chat – rest assured not every ballet girl is as daft as Calverley, same as not every peer is as sad and sloshed as Viscount Colchicum. We’ve all met old rich blokes who’d rather hang around rowdy teens than their equally stuffy peers, passing down all their “precious” life lessons and bad habits, and plenty of supposedly respectable gents who don’t mind a wild party as long as no one sees them doing it. Our old mate Major Pendennis, for one, would never say no to a fun little gathering as long as it was kept hush-hush and folks like Lord Steyne or Colchicum were on the guest list. “Let the lads have their fun,” he’d tell Pen over and over, puffing his cigar. “I’m no prude, I’m an old man of the world, and I know young lads will be young lads. Though I reckon the fancy ones get to keep sowing their wild oats till they hit seventy, no harm done.”

That night Foker drove his guests back to Brompton in his drag, so gloomy and quiet he didn’t even crack one of his usual dumb jokes. When the girls invited him in for a post-dinner drink, he turned them down so sadly they assumed he’d had a falling out with his dad. He ignored Calverley yelling from her balcony like a modern day Jezebel begging him to plan another party, sent the carriage home with a groom, and wandered off by himself, hands in his pockets, staring up at the moon. He traipsed all the way to Grosvenor Place to gawk up at the windows he thought belonged to Blanche Amory, sighing and moaning so dramatically a passing copper reported a weird little bloke in a fancy suit was loitering around Sir Francis Clavering’s house, perving through the windows.

That was the start of Foker’s full-on, chaos-fueled Blanche hunt across all of London. If Lady Clavering had a box at the French play (which Foker barely understood a word of), he’d be front and center in the stalls. He paid his valet Anatole to befriend Sir Francis Clavering’s footman and sneak a peek at their social calendar, and suddenly he started showing up at every boring fancy party he’d previously called a total waste of time. He badgered his mum Lady Agnes to beg for party cards, lying straight to her face that he was “seeing the world to better himself”, and went to the French plays supposedly to “perfect his accent” – when Anatole knew full well his master was sneaking off every morning to a secret dance academy in Brewer Street, practicing waltzes like a schoolboy hiding a candy stash.

Major Pendennis never missed church on a Sunday – he thought it was a gentleman’s social duty to be seen there, same as stopping by the club for a brandy after lunch. One Sunday he dragged Pen along with him, having already spotted the posters for a charity sermon at his local parish that would have forced him to drop a few quid into the collection plate. “We’ll cut across the park to St. Benedict’s,” he told Pen, “then we’ll pop over to the Claverings’ and invite ourselves for lunch. Lady Clavering loves having people over, she’s the most hospitable woman in London.”
“I met them at dinner at Lady Agnes Foker’s last week,” Pen laughed. “She’s lovely, but I think you were right about Blanche Amory – that girl is the biggest flirt in the whole of London. She spent the whole dinner chatting Harry Foker’s ear off even though I escorted her in.”
“Pfft, everyone knows Harry’s engaged to his cousin Lady Ann Milton – that was a master move by Lady Rosherville, by the way,” the Major grinned. “Old Foker’s health is terrible, he had a fit last year at your place, Pen. Harry’s going to pull in at least fourteen thousand a year off the brewery alone, plus all that Norfolk property. I’m not proud, Pen, I like a good birth same as anyone, but a brewery that spits out fourteen grand a year? That’s the sort of man you stick to when you’re making your way in the world, mark my words.”
“Harry’s already my mate,” Pen said. “He pops by my chambers all the time, invites me to dinner. All he ever talks about is Blanche Amory, he’s properly smitten.”
“His family will never let that happen,” the Major huffed. “These rich families arrange marriages like state business. Lady Agnes was forced to marry old Foker by her dad even though she was mad for her cousin who died at Albuera – the bloke even saved her from drowning in a lake once! But she did what she was told. Old Foker’s a parvenu, he’s obsessed with climbing the social ladder, Harry will do exactly what his dad says. I’ve seen a hundred of these silly young love affairs. They kick and scream and throw a fit, but they eventually listen to reason.”
“I was half in love with Blanche once, years ago,” Pen admitted, laughing. “But my mum didn’t like her, and it fizzled out.”
“Blanche would not be a bad catch, laddie,” the Major said, side-eyeing him. “I’ve got it on good authority she’s got way more than the ten thousand quid everyone thinks. Clever girl, pretty, sharp as a tack. I wouldn’t be surprised if she’s got thirty, forty, even fifty grand tucked away.”
Pen laughed. “You know everyone’s secret business, don’t you?”
“Nah, I don’t tell all the things I know,” the Major winked. “Though if you end up marrying her, I won’t be a bit surprised. Oh look, here’s St. Benedict’s – everyone says Mr. Oriel the preacher is absolutely brilliant.”

The bells were tolling, carriages full of fancy bonnets and feathered hats were pulling up, and the two of them trundled into the church. Pen felt a little weird that they’d spent the whole walk gossiping about people’s finances and arranged marriages right up to the church door, but the Major didn’t notice a thing – his wig was perfectly combed, his neckcloth tied so tight he could barely turn his head, and he spent the whole service pretending to read his prayer book while he side-eyed every single other person in the pews. Halfway through the service Pen spotted Harry Foker tucked away in the back near the servants’ seats, staring so hard at the front pews his prayer book was upside down. Following his gaze, Pen saw exactly what he was gawking at: two bonnets, one yellow, one pink, belonging to Lady Clavering and Blanche Amory. Looks like Foker wasn’t the only one who’d snuck their romantic drama right into the church pews.

When the service let out, Foker was the first one out the door, loitering by the steps too nervous to leave until Lady Clavering’s barouche pulled up. The two ladies stepped out to find Pen, the Major, and Foker all clustered in the sun, Foker sucking on the crook of his walking stick like a lost toddler. The ever-generous Lady Clavering invited all three straight back to their house for lunch, no questions asked.
Blanche was extra sweet to Pen, grinning: “O do come, I’m dying to talk to you about *Walter Lorraine*! The review in the *Pall Mall Gazette* this morning was so lovely, I cried reading bits of it!”
Foker blinked at them, confused. “Walter Lorraine? That little bloke died of measles when we were at Grey Friars, I remember his mum coming to pick him up.”
Pen cackled, hooking his arm through Foker’s. “I wrote a novel, you dope. I forgot you don’t read the Sunday papers.”
“Nah, I read Bell’s Life every week, mate,” Foker said, referring to the horse racing paper.

After lunch Blanche gushed over Pen’s book nonstop, cooing about the poetry bits and the fancy duke characters, making eyes at Pen so hard even the Major noticed. The old man, who’d read a grand total of six pages of Pen’s novel, nodded along pretending to be deeply moved. When Blanche found out the fancy duke had originally just been a regular baron before the publisher made Pen bump everyone’s rank to sell more copies, she laughed and called Pen a wicked little satirist, all the while batting her long lashes at him like she was still the shy little country girl he’d known years ago.

Foker meanwhile sat there quietly gnawing on his cane, wishing he was a clever literary bloke like Pen instead of the guy who read horse racing news all day. The Major, who’d slipped Blanche the tea that Foker was already engaged to his cousin earlier at lunch, fully expected her to start ignoring Harry immediately – but nope, she got even nicer to him, gushing over his mom, his park pony, the ruby-eyed monkey head buttons on his waistcoat until Foker was blushing so hard his ears turned pink.
Then she turned to Pen, grinning: “Poor Mr. Foker looks so sad! We heard all about that wild party he threw at Richmond the other day, you naughty thing! I’d love to go to Richmond, or Greenwich, or anywhere fun for a dinner party. I’ve never even met an actress, I adore talented people!”

That’s when the Major jumped in, gallant as anything, much to Pen’s total surprise. “Well why don’t we three bachelors take you two ladies out for a fancy dinner at Greenwich? Lady Clavering is always having us over to eat, it’s high time we returned the favor! I’m old, I’ll volunteer the two young lads – Pen’s got a pocket full of money from his book, and Harry’s got more than enough to cover the bill too. Harry, how’s your lovely cousin Lady Ann doing? Lady Clavering, be my guest for the night, and Blanche can be Arthur’s escort, if she’ll have him.”

Blanche shrieked with delight, Lady Clavering happily agreed, and they booked a fancy dinner at the Greenwich hotel right away. The day was a total success: Foker got hours of alone time chatting to Blanche on the hotel balcony, and Pen and the Major turned up in a borrowed brougham – the Major proudly declaring he was an old soldier who’d learned to travel in style, conveniently letting the two young men split the entire dinner bill between them. On the ride back home he spent the whole journey teasing Pen about Blanche being obviously mad about him, and winking again that she was secretly way richer than anyone expected. Pen just shook his head, wondering how on earth his uncle kept track of every single person’s hidden fortune in London.

You may recall that after Pen crashed out of Oxbridge and moped around his hometown for months, chasing two very unimpressed ladies and generally wallowing in teen angst, he spent most of that time scribbling a very dramatic, very gloomy full-length novel. The thing reeked of unfiltered Byronic brooding, Werther-level suicidal despair, and Mephistopheles-adjacent edgy snark, because Pen had just picked up German and was copying his favourite poets as diligently as every overzealous clever 20-year-old with a notebook and a grudge against the universe tends to do.

He’d dripped actual tears on half the pages back then, smudging ink all over his dramatic little lines about heartbreak and cosmic unfairness. A few years on, if he picked up those old beloved poetry books he’d annotated to death, he couldn’t even summon that same over-the-top emotion — the fountain of teenage soppiness had dried up completely, like a waterhole in the desert that only leaves camel hoof prints and dead grass as proof it ever existed.

He made that very silly desert simile one morning to Warrington, who was slouched over his pipe, while Pen gesticulated so hard he slammed his decades-old manuscript down on the table hard enough to rattle the tea set and make the thin blue skim milk in the jug do a little impromptu jig. He’d fished the thing out the night before from a long-forgotten chest full of junk: ratty old shooting jackets, his crumpled Oxbridge exam scribble books, the surplice he’d worn for the mandatory chapel services, his dented graduation cap and gown, and all sorts of other embarrassing mementos of youth. He’d read half the novel in bed before falling asleep, tired out from a London party, and the first half was so boring it had actually lulled him to sleep.

“Good grief,” Pen laughed bitterly, prodding the stack of pages, “I can’t believe I wrote this just a couple years ago. I was fully convinced I’d be in love with that little minx Miss Amory forever back then! I used to hide my stupid little poems for her in a hollow tree, and dedicate them *Amori* — get it, a pun on her name? Fancy scholarship, right?”

“Clever little wordplay,” Warrington puffed around his pipe, then snatched the manuscript right out from under Pen’s nose with the fire tongs he was using to tuck a new coal into his bowl. “Let me read a bit of this rubbish.”

“‘False as thou art beautiful! Heartless as thou art fair! Mockery of Passion!’ Walter Lorraine shouts at Leonora,” Warrington boomed, barely letting Pen lunge for the book, “‘what evil spirit sent thee to torture me so? O Leonora —’”

“STOP,” Pen yelped, making a wild grab for the pages that did not work. “Don’t read that out loud! That bit’s about my other old flame, the one who’s Lady Mirabel now. I saw her last night at Lady Whiston’s party, she invited me over to her place, said we old friends should catch up more. Funny how she never spoke to me for two whole years until she saw Wenham chatting to me, and that fancy French literary guy Dubois, who was wearing so many medals he looked like he could pass for a French Marshal, all of a sudden I’m good enough for her guest list. The Claverings are going to be there too — won’t it be a riot to meet both my old crushes at the same dinner table?”

“Two flames? More like two piles of completely burnt-out ash,” Warrington snickered. “Are both of those beauties in this book of yours?”

“Sort of,” Pen admitted. “Leonora, the one who marries the duke? That’s the Fotheringay. The duke is based on my old Oxford mate Magnus Charters, a little bit, and Miss Amory is Neaera. Man, I thought of the Fotheringay the whole walk home last night in the moonlight, all those old dumb memories felt like they happened yesterday. So I dug this out, and — don’t laugh — it’s actually got some good bits! If Bungay won’t print it, I’m sure Bacon will.”

“Classic poets,” Warrington said, rolling his eyes. “Fall in love, get dumped or do the dumping, suffer a little, scream that no one has ever suffered this much in human history, then write it all down and hawk it for cash. All poets are humbugs, every last one of ’em. The second you start selling your feelings like groceries, you’re a humbug. If a poet gets a stomach ache from eating too fancy a dinner, they wail *Ai! Ai!* louder than Prometheus chained to his rock.”

“Poets just feel things sharper than other people, that’s what makes them poets!” Pen huffed, indignant. “You get all fired up writing your newspaper editorials when you catch a politician lying, right? Paley will ramble for an hour straight about a boring legal technicality he finds interesting. Why do you get to use your special brain skill for cash, but a poet can’t do the exact same with his? It’s not like money makes you spot a bad argument, it’s just the thing you trade the skill for. A painter paints a face on canvas to sell, why can’t I put my feelings on paper and do the same? If I ever think I’m good enough for an epic poem, I’ll write it. If I’m only good enough for silly jokes and stories, I’ll do that too.”

“Not bad for a speech, kid,” Warrington said, “but that still doesn’t stop all poets from being humbugs.”

“What — Homer, Aeschylus, Shakespeare? All of them?”

“Those legends are on a whole different level from you little amateurs,” Warrington scoffed. “There are great men, and then there’s the rest of us chumps.”

“Hey, Shakespeare wrote plays for cash just the same as you and me,” Pen shot back, which earned him a withering look from Warrington before the older man went back to flipping through the manuscript.

It was very obvious the whole book was 90% Pen’s own messy personal drama, the exact same heartbreaks and silly mistakes we already saw back in the first half of his life, so we won’t bore you by dumping too many excerpts from it. Pen hadn’t hidden it in his storage chest all that time out of some fancy Horatian desire to let it “ripen” like a fine wine, either — he’d just forgotten where else to put the thing, and didn’t want to look at his own teenage cringe. Anyone who hides a manuscript away for ten years hoping it’ll get better is in for a rude awakening when they dig it back up and find it’s turned as flavourless as cheap white wine that went bad.

To his credit, Pen never thought he’d written a masterpiece. When he re-read it now, he was very aware of all its flaws, and pretty humble about the bits that worked. It wasn’t great, but it was just as good as all the other trashy fashionable novels flying off circulating library shelves that season, and he knew he could write English just as well as those popular authors. Even Warrington, the grumpiest critic alive who’d gotten through two full pipes of tobacco while reading it, had to admit it wasn’t all garbage.

“I can’t read any more of this balderdash right now,” he said, yawning so wide his jaw ached, “but there’s some good stuff here, Pen. It’s got that fresh, green, unselfconscious spark that vanishes the second you start shaving. You can’t get that rosy, unforced sincerity back later, no matter how hard you try. You’ll end up primping your whiskers with curling irons and macassar oil and all that nonsense, looking all fancy and polished, but the springtime of your writing days is the best part.”

“What the hell do my whiskers have to do with anything?” Pen spluttered, very offended, because he did in fact spend an absurd amount of time curling, oiling, perfuming and fussing over his facial hair like it was a prize houseplant. “Do you think we can sell this thing? Or should we burn it in a little dramatic auto-da-fe?”

“Burning it would be fun just to punish you for being such a little humbug,” Warrington grinned, “but I’m not dumb. You love this thing way too much to actually hurt it.”

“Watch this!” Pen yelled, and tossed the whole stack of pages straight onto the coals in the hearth. But the fire had burned out ages ago after boiling their breakfast kettle, and Pen had snuck a quick glance to confirm it was totally cold before he threw the manuscript over. Warrington fished it right back out with the tongs, cackling so hard tears ran down his face.

“Pen, you are the clumsiest humbug I’ve ever met,” he wheezed. “Fine, we won’t burn the lad. We’ll take him to the publishers, sell him for cold hard cash, enough to buy beef, beer, tobacco, new shirts, the whole works. It’s a silly little book, but it’ll sell. My advice is, next time you go home on holiday, tuck Walter Lorraine in your bag, touch up the old cringey bits a tiny bit, add some comedy, some jokes, some light satire, and we’ll hawk it to Bacon or Bungay for a solid hundred quid. It’s not a masterpiece, but it’ll do.”

Pen lit up like a christmas tree — praise from cynical Warrington was higher praise than any literary award. “You silly little fool, I think it’s actually uncommonly clever,” Warrington said, softening up, and tapped Pen on the cheek playfully with the manuscript. Pen blushed so bright red his old teenage flush was back, and squeezed Warrington’s hand so tight he almost crushed his fingers, before he ran off to his bedroom to spend the whole rest of the day re-editing the book, adding bits, cutting bits, shaping it into the story you’ve almost certainly read as a respectable novel fan already.

While Pen was hard at work, Warrington pulled all the strings he could, hyping up Walter Lorraine to the two professional readers who worked for both Bungay and Bacon, telling them Pen was a total society insider who got invited to all the fanciest houses in London. He even roped silly friendly Percy Popjoy into talking to Mrs Bungay, telling her the book was going to be the biggest smash of the season, full of wit, pathos, satire, everything the public went crazy for. Bungay, who didn’t know a good novel from a Hebrew textbook, didn’t care what was in the book at all, but he trusted his wife’s opinion, and immediately invited Pen and Warrington over for dinner to negotiate.

Bacon, the rival publisher, got wind of this, panicked, and decided he wanted to outbid Bungay immediately. He sent his top man Hack to snoop around, and the next thing you know, Bacon himself is huffing and puffing his way up three flights of rickety stairs to their apartment in Lamb Court.

To call their digs un-fancy would be an understatement. The ragged carpet had gotten way more ragged over the two years they’d lived there, the whole place smelled permanently of tobacco smoke, Bacon tripped over the laundress’s buckets left in the hallway, Warrington’s shooting jacket had elbows more tattered than an old rag, and the second Bacon sat down in the chair they offered him, the whole thing collapsed under his weight. Warrington cackled so hard he dropped his pipe ash in his tea, teased Bacon that if he liked their “elegant” furniture, he could buy the whole lot for his wife’s drawing room. Bacon, who knew Warrington was a huge practical joker, later told his staff “I can never tell if that guy is messing with me or being serious.”

Bacon was this close to deciding they were both total impostors, until he glanced down at the breakfast table and saw that morning’s post: a fancy embossed invitation for Pen from the Marchioness of Steyne, another invite to a fancy dance at a duchess’s house, all super high society people. Warrington saw him staring. “Ah, Pen’s the most laid-back posh guy you’ll ever meet,” he said, deadpan. “He dines with all the lords and ladies in London, but he’s just as happy eating a mutton chop with us common blokes. Refreshing, right? He could afford a mansion in Mayfair if he wanted, but he likes slumming it here with me in our rickety old rooms.”

“Bet he doesn’t pay much rent here,” Bacon thought to himself, already salivating at the idea of a bestseller by a real live society author.

Then he got right down to business: begged to see the manuscript, offered a huge advance, said his publishing house was the most generous in London. Pen put on a very cool unbothered act, said he was already negotiating with Bungay and couldn’t promise anything, which made Bacon throw out such absurdly generous offers Pen was convinced he’d just stumbled into the land of El Dorado and made his entire fortune in one morning.

I’m not going to tell you exactly how much he got for the first edition, okay? I don’t want every 19-year-old kid with a notebook to quit their perfectly boring stable job and try to write novels for a living. Half of all novels never get published, most of the ones that do flop, Pen got absurdly lucky, don’t risk your rent money on it. That said, the sum was big enough that Pen immediately started daydreaming about opening a bank account, buying a horse and cab, moving to a fancy first-floor apartment in Lamb Court, or even relocating all the way to the fancy west end.

Uncle Major Pendennis nearly had a heart attack when Pen told him the news. He could not for the life of him figure out who on earth read these stupid novels — he only ever read Paul de Kock, the one author that made him laugh, hadn’t touched a novel in 30 years. “Gad, Pen’s the luckiest lad alive,” he thought. “He could knock one of these out in a month, that’s 12 a year, make a huge fortune in 5 years. He needs to move to a proper fancy apartment, buy a brougham, start living like a gentleman.”

Pen laughed and told Warrington about his uncle’s very dumb plan, but he had way better sense than that. He wrote a nice, sincere letter straight to Laura, enclosing almost every penny he owed her, paying off almost his entire debt. When the widow Pendennis and Dr Portman read that honest, grateful letter, where Pen thanked heaven for giving him such good friends who supported him when he was broke and miserable, old Dr Portman got so teary his voice cracked, his glasses fogged up, and before he knew what he was doing he was holding Mrs Pendennis’s hand, then gave her a big soft kiss on the cheek. Helen burst out crying happy tears all over his shoulder, and the doctor, bright red as a tomato, mumbled a little line from his favourite old poet about how he’d made “the widow’s heart to sing for joy”.

If you’ve ever had the misfortune to wander the grubby, sun-starved alleys tucked between Lincoln’s Inn Fields and the Temple, you’ve almost certainly dodged your way past Shepherd’s Inn—an ancient quadrangle so hidden behind smoky chimneys and black gables that the sun doesn’t even bother to drop by, probably because it owes half the vendors tuppence. The narrow passages leading to it are lined with vendors selling soggy shellfish, rock-hard peppermint drops, cheap theatrical prints for bored teen clerks, and dingy secondhand beds that look like they’ve hosted nothing but bad indigestion and terrible nightmares. The doors are strung with so many bells it sounds like a cat walked through a Christmas decoration factory every time someone pushes one open, and dirty kids mill around the doorsteps so thick you can barely see the pavement under them, which is already slathered in a permanent layer of mud that never dries, no matter how hard it tries.

Ballad singers bellow off-key anti-Whig, anti-bishop, anti-royal-relative satire down every alley, Punch’s puppet show draws a crowd of urchins fighting over halfpennies, housewives scream at their loitering brats, and husbands stumble out of the gin shop yelling about how they definitely didn’t spend the rent money. Step through one rickety arch, though, and suddenly you’re in the quiet, rickety old quadrangle of Shepherd’s Inn proper, with a sad little patch of mangy grass in the middle, a statue of its founder Shepherd fenced off so the local urchins can’t beat it to bits, and tall old chambers that used to belong to lawyers, back before all the slightly dodgier crowd moved in.

These days, you won’t find any fancy barristers hanging their shingles here. The ground floor has the offices for the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum Copper Mines (which are, for the record, still 100% underwater), the Patent Inventions Registry, and the Union of Genius and Capital Company, which no one’s ever actually seen do any business. The only name that even makes it to the official Law List is Mr. Somerset Campion, who loans money to the aristocracy out of his fancy Mayfair office, and shows up at his Shepherd’s Inn front once a week in a cab with harness covered in heraldic crests that no one’s ever heard of, his groom’s breeches so blindingly white they cast a little patch of fake sunlight on the otherwise gloomy courtyard.

Wandering the courtyard watching that fancy cab every afternoon is none other than our old friend Captain Jack Costigan, shuffling around in ratty carpet slippers and a dressing gown that’s seen at least three wars, his old hat cocked over one eye. He hangs out at the porter’s lodge half the day, petting Mrs. Bolton the porteress’s kids and gushing about “theatres and me own darlin’ Leedy Mirabel” to anyone who’ll listen. Mrs. Bolton used to dance at Sadler’s Wells back in the day, as the 13th of Mr. Serle’s 40 dancing pupils, so she humours his stories.

Costigan shares the third floor of No.4 with his pal Mr. Bows, the piano teacher—their door still has the name of the last tenant, Charley Podmore the tenor, on it, which is a running theme in Shepherd’s Inn, where half the doors have names no one living has ever met. Bows gives singing lessons to the porteress’s pretty daughter Fanny, who dreams of being on the stage, and in exchange she makes their beds and cooks their breakfast. Costigan? Fanny thinks he’s the fanciest gentleman who ever lived, all his ridiculous Irish tall tales 100% true, except for the tiny flaw that he’s permanently half-cut on whisky punch. Bows, for all his grumpiness, is secretly as fond of Fanny as if she was his own kid—old softie can’t stand not loving someone, bless him.

Costigan’s stories are so grand you’d think his daughter Lady Mirabel and her rich husband Sir Charles show up to his third floor hovel every afternoon with the entire House of Lords in tow, but the truth is their last formal visit was months ago, the couple’s visiting card still stuck in his mantelpiece mirror. At first, when Mirabel married Sir Charles, she gave her da a small pension, and invited him over for dinner every now and then. For the first couple of trips, Cos even managed to stay sober, prattling on about the Dublin Castle levees he swears he attended, begging for “jus’ a little more champeane, if ye plaze” and being so absurdly polite to the footmen waiting on table that the poor lads could barely keep a straight face.

But then caution went out the window. He showed up to Sir Charles’s dinner one night absolutely sloshed, slurring anti-royal songs and trying to kiss the butler, and they had to stuff him in a carriage and send him home, locking their front door behind him. After that, Cos hung around the Back Kitchen pub for days, crying to his mates that he was exactly like King Lear, betrayed by his ungrateful daughter, forced to drown his sorrows in punch because no one loved him.

He got even worse when his money ran out, cooking up the most absurd lies to shake money out of Mirabel. Once he sent a frantic note saying a bailiff was dragging him off to jail unless she sent three pound five shillings at once, which she did, only to later find out he’d spent the whole lot at the pub. Another time he claimed he’d crashed through a plate glass window in the Strand and owed the shop owner a fortune, the cash ending up right back at the Roscius Head tavern. The third time, he said he was on his deathbed, too sick to call a doctor, so Mirabel drove straight over to Shepherd’s Inn, ran up the three flights of rickety stairs, and found him standing there in his shirt sleeves, holding a gridiron, waiting for Mrs. Bolton to bring home mutton chops for his lunch.

Poor old Sir Charles, who’s a delicate old fellow who hates fuss more than anything, was soon driven half mad by his father-in-law. He’d spot Costigan loitering outside his club, his silly hat cocked at that ridiculous angle, and dive headfirst out of his brougham like a spy running from a pursuer, just to avoid having to stop and talk to him. He cried to Mirabel that he’d have a fatal fit if he saw Costigan one more time, and threatened to move to the countryside or flee to France entirely. Mirabel finally marched over to Shepherd’s Inn, gave her da a strict talking to threatening to cut his pension off entirely if he ever harassed her husband again, and snapped at Bows for not keeping a better eye on the old drunk, leaving the kind old piano teacher with nothing but a dressing down for all his years of looking out for Cos.

Next door, at No.3, on the second floor, live two more of our old mates: Colonel Altamont, self-appointed Agent to the Nawaub of Lucknow, and Captain Edward Strong, the Chevalier himself. Their door doesn’t have a name plate at all—Strong doesn’t want every random debtor he owes tuppence to dropping by uninvited, and Altamont’s “confidential mission” to sell the Lucknow crown jewels to private London capitalists is so hush-hush he doesn’t bother telling the East India Company where he is.

Since Sir Francis Clavering rolled into town, Strong’s chambers are surprisingly cozy. He’s a genius cook, that Strong—whipping up French fricassees, Spanish stews, perfect omelettes, and making such a racket of singing and glass-clinking at his dinner parties that poor Costigan next door would sit in his own miserable rooms, green with envy, listening to the fun through the wall. His guests were the most chaotic, delightful bunch of schemers you ever met, every single one of them carrying a foolproof get-rich-quick scheme that would definitely make them millionaires in 10 months flat: there was Jack Holt who’d fought in Don Carlos’s army and swore he could smuggle tobacco into London for 30 thousand a year if someone just lent him 15 hundred to bribe one last stubborn excise officer. Tom Diver, ex-Mexican navy, said he knew exactly where a sunken treasure ship was sitting with 380 thousand dollars and 180 thousand pounds in gold bars, and all he needed was 18 hundred quid for a diving bell and four men, and he’d come back an MP and buy back his family estate. Keightley, the manager of the still-underwater copper mine, ran a side business selling Smyrna sponges and had a quicksilver scheme he swore would make his fortune too. Filby had been everything from a dragoon corporal to a fairground actor to a missionary converting the Irish, and now owned a plot of land somewhere no one could locate that he swore made him heir to a vast fortune. Even Sir Francis Clavering hung around the lot, even though he was pretty quiet, since everyone loved having a real baronet in their crew.

The problem was Altamont. Strong had dragged him home from a terrible fleabag hotel where he’d turned up almost completely penniless, despite being supposedly entrusted with a hoard of royal jewels, and now the man wouldn’t leave. Strong was sick of him. “That bloke’s no gentleman,” he grumbled to Clavering. “Dresses like a freed slave on a bank holiday. Took him to the theatre the other night, and he started yelling at the actor playing the villain so loud the box office staff almost threw us out. Then when the brigand in the afterpiece died, he sobbed like a toddler, screaming that it was a bloody disgrace, and started a fight with a bloke who laughed at him! I had to drag him out before he got us both arrested. Who even is he, Frank? Spill the beans. You two didn’t rob a church together, did you? Spit it out.”

All Clavering said was “Hang him! I wish he was dead,” and his face went so pale and grim that Strong didn’t press him any further—though he made a mental note to dig into whatever dark secret tied the fancy baronet to this wild, loud-mouthed fake Indian agent, no matter what.

The morning after that chaotic dinner at Grosvenor Place (where uninvited guest Colonel Altamont made quite the unforgettable entrance) found the Colonel himself stumbling blearily out of his garret room in Shepherd’s Inn, to find his roommate Captain Strong already fully settled in his favorite armchair, cigar in one hand, newspaper in the other. This was a man who could turn any temporary lodgings into a five-star campsite, you see—long before Altamont had even blinked awake, Strong had already polished off a massive breakfast of fried eggs and crispy broiled rashers that their man Grady had cooked to absolute perfection.

He was the sort who hated silence more than he hated even the most annoying company, and while he didn’t particularly care for his fellow lodger (he wouldn’t have shed a single tear if Altamont had met with the very “unfortunate accident” Sir Francis Clavering was fervently daydreaming about), he kept things perfectly friendly between them. He’d even tucked Altamont into bed like a fussy nurse the night before, swiped his candle away to avoid burning the house down, and when he found the nightcap bottle of spirits he’d been saving empty, he’d just shrugged, drank a glass of water, smoked his last pipe, and conked out like a baby. Nothing ruffled this guy: whether he was marching into battle the next morning or getting tossed in the Fleet prison, he always had perfect digestion, rosy cheeks, and a snore so loud it could rattle the rafters, waking up the next day ravenous and ready for whatever chaos came his way.

Altamont’s very first move was to roar for Grady to bring him a full pint of pale ale, which he chugged straight out of the pewter tankard in one go, wiped his mouth on his ratty dressing gown (Strong had long since noticed that his dyed jet-black whiskers were a completely different color than the fuzzy grayish-brown beard growing on his chin, not to mention the tuft of fair hair sticking out under his obviously fake black wig, but he was far too polite to mention any of it), and sighed so loud the windows rattled. “Nothing touches this beer when your insides are roasting,” he announced. “Drank a dozen of Bass a day back in Calcutta, me, and—”
“And Lucknow, I’d bet,” Strong snickered. “Grabbed this beer just for you, knew you’d be gasping for it after last night.”
That sent Altamont off on a ramble about the mess he’d made the evening before. “I can’t help it, mate,” he banged his big fist against his forehead. “The second liquor hits my system I turn into a total lunatic, shouldn’t be left alone within a mile of a spirit bottle. Once I start pouring, I can’t stop till the whole thing’s empty, and after that? Lord knows what garbage comes out of my mouth. I was supposed to be so sober the parson would have asked me for tips! Grady only gave me two tumblers of spirits at home, I was gonna spend the whole night at the Black and Red gambling den totally teetotal. But no—some idiot left that damn sample bottle of Hollands out on the table, right? Then Grady ran off to run errands, left the kettle boiling for my tea, and I couldn’t resist. Washed the whole bottle down, and then I swear I had even more swill later at that thieving little dive.”
“Wait, you were already wasted before you showed up at Clavering’s dinner?” Strong blinked. “Starting the party awfully early, aren’t you?”
“Drunk and done with it by nine o’clock, what a talent, eh?” Altamont cackled. “Yep, waltzed right over there like a total berk, found Blackland, young Moss, and a whole crew of their lowlife mates tucking into dinner. If we’d stuck to roulette I definitely would have cleaned them out—can’t lose at that game, me, never! But they were too sneaky, the lot of ’em. That rascal Blackland dragged out a set of dice, we started playing hazard right on the dining table, and I lost every last penny you’d given me that morning. That’s what made me so hot under the collar! Next thing I know I’m tramping over to Clavering’s place begging for more cash, and I don’t remember a single other thing till I woke up at 7 a.m. to that old guy Bows from number 4 murdering a piano tune next door.”
Strong lit a fresh cigar, puffed thoughtfully. “Curious how you always manage to squeeze money out of Clavering, Colonel. Dude’s famous for not paying anyone back, even people he actually likes.”
Altamont burst out cackling. “Ha! He owes me, that’s why.”
“Pfft, owing people money has never once made Frank Clavering want to pay them,” Strong pointed out. “Half the people in London are chasing him for cash.”
“Aw, he just loves me to bits, like a long-lost brother,” Altamont sneered, grinning. “You know that, Captain. No? He don’t? Alright then, ask no questions, I’ll tell no lies—stick that in your pipe and smoke it, old boy.”
“Gonna give up the brandy, though,” he added after a dramatic pause. “Haven’t got a choice, it’s gonna ruin me one day.”
“Makes you say some real wild stuff,” Strong said, staring him dead in the eye. “You remember all the nonsense you spouted at Clavering’s dinner table last night?”
“Say what?” Altamont jolted upright, panicking. “Did I spill the beans? Dammit Strong, did I blow my whole secret?”
“Ask no questions, tell no lies, remember?” Strong teased. He’d heard every weird little line Altamont had slurred last night, and seen him bolt the second he spotted Major Pendennis (who he’d mistaken for some old cop named Beak), but he wasn’t about to jog that particular memory. “Nah, you didn’t blab anything coherent. You were so far gone no one could make head nor tail of a single word you said.”
Altamont let out a huge, relieved sigh. That went down a treat, he thought. He was in a very talkative mood now, and kept rambling. “Yeah, I got my flaws, no denying. Three things I can never resist: a bottle of good brandy, a box of dice, and a pretty woman. No bloke with any spark in him ever could, far as I know. Every country I’ve ever visited, all three of those have landed me in hot water at some point.”
“Go on then, spill,” Strong said, amused.
“Started when I was 15, ran away from home, worked as a cabin boy on an Indiaman,” Altamont said. “Been chasing trouble ever since. You ever hear about that thousand quid I was carrying in Paris? Got swept right away by one woman—black eyes, neck full of jewels, draped in ermine like an actual duchess, I swear. I was playing it real careful at first, staying at Meurice’s hotel, calling myself Colonel Altamont, never gambled except at the public tables, and I won more than I lost. Then I met this fancy bloke called Bloundell-Bloundell, white kid gloves, little tuft of hair on his chin, total poser. He dragged me off to dinners, took me to soirees at the Countess de Foljambe’s place—blimey, that woman! Could sit down at the piano and sing so sweet you’d think she was singing your soul right out of your body. She invited me over every Tuesday, I bought her opera boxes, took her out for fancy dinners, I was having the time of my life. Then one night after supper, we had stacks of champagne, then the brandy started flowing, she mixed me tumbler after tumbler of punch, next thing I know I’m playing cards till I pass out. Woke up the next day with a headache so bad I thought my skull was splitting, and there’s the Honourable Deuceace—earl’s son, super fancy—hanging out in my sitting room, saying I lent him 30 louis the night before. Handed me the cash no questions asked, what a gentleman!
“Ten minutes later Bloundell and the Marquis de la Tour de Force turn up. They’re all grinning, waving bits of paper in my face. Turns out I’d apparently signed IOUs to the Marquis for 340 louis, and Bloundell for 210 more! I was raging, told them I wasn’t paying a farthing, they couldn’t fool me with their fake bits of paper. Then they started yelling at me about ‘affairs of honour’, saying they’d duel me if I didn’t pay. Just as we were about to come to blows, a mate of mine walked in, the one I’d met in Boulogne, who’d warned me about those crooks before. And what does he do? Sides with them! Says I was the one insisting on huge stakes, the Countess even begged me to stop playing. Told me the Countess was upstairs crying her eyes out, hysterical, scared the Marquis would shoot me dead. Said she was so in love with me she’d gotten on her knees and begged him to save my life!
“Course I ran right over to her place the second I got dressed. She shrieked when she saw me, almost fainted, called me Ferdinand! I swear that’s what she called me. Cried so hard her eyes were all puffed up, said she couldn’t stand to see me lose all that money in her house, begged me to take all her diamonds and pay the debts. I told her I’d never touch a penny of her stuff, and I wasn’t paying those con artists a single cent. Then she went all cold on me, said her dad was a Grand Army general, real men of honour pay their gambling debts, no exceptions. She was gonna march out the door and never speak to me again.
“What was I supposed to do? I didn’t wanna upset my sweet Caroline! So I paid every last penny, 560 gold Napoleons, no questions asked. I still can’t tell if they rooked me or not,” he mused, looking genuinely confused. “Sometimes I think they did, but then that Caroline was so crazy about me! She’d never let anyone trick me, I’m sure of it. Women don’t lie about that sort of thing, do they?”
Before he could spill any more of his chaotic life story, there was a loud bang on the front door. Their servant Grady opened it, and who should storm in but Sir Francis Clavering himself, looking so purple in the face he was one step away from bursting a blood vessel.
“The big boss is here!” Strong yelped, completely shocked.
“What the hell is he doing here?” Altamont growled, scowling under his bushy brows. “Can’t be anything good.”
He was right. Clavering never turned up at Shepherd’s Inn unless he was chasing or being chased for money, and today he was spitting nails. “You!” he screamed at Altamont. “You’ve pulled your stupid old tricks again, you absolute scoundrel!”
“Which tricks, exactly?” Altamont said, unphased, grinning.
“You got wasted at the Rouge et Noir gambling den last night, didn’t you?” Clavering yelled. “Used my name to draw a 15-pound bill on me, lost every penny of it at the dice table! And then you had the audacity to march uninvited into my fancy dinner party in Grosvenor Place, drunk off your arse, yelling for more booze like you owned my house! What the hell are all my fancy posh friends gonna think of me, letting a bloke like you wander in off the street?”
“Fair, that’s a bit awkward,” Altamont said, still totally calm. “I apologize, on my honour! 15 quid is nothing to a big shot MP baronet like you, is it? I was so wasted I completely forgot I even did it, that’s all.”
“15 pounds isn’t the point!” Clavering shrieked, swearing so loud the neighbors probably banged on the ceiling. “I will not have you dragging my good name through the mud, you hear me? If you ever so much as dare show your face near my house again, or mention my name in any gambling den ever again, or even speak to me on the street before I speak to you first, I swear I’ll disown you completely, I won’t give you another single shilling!”
“Don’t get your knickers in a twist, Governor,” Altamont grumbled, finally looking a bit annoyed. “You keep taunting me like that, and I’ll go make a scene at your next dinner party just to spite you. I know I shouldn’t have turned up last night, but I was absolutely plastered! That’s the sort of mistake gentlemanly blokes forgive each other for, right?”
“YOU a gentleman?” Clavering spluttered, purple with rage. “How dare a lowlife like you even call yourself that?”

Ever since those very pleasant free feasts at Grosvenor Place and Greenwich we watched Major Pendennis mooch off earlier, the old boy’s apparent fondness for the Clavering family ballooned like a spoiled dessert at their fancy dinner table. He popped by for calls so often the butler nearly mistook him for a permanent boarder, and his little attentions to Lady Clavering were so unrelenting you’d have thought he was running for a seat on her charity committee (spoiler: he was angling for free tickets to every posh party he could sneak into). Being a veteran London socialite who’d weaseled his way into half the fancy drawing-rooms in town, he informed her that of course a lady of her distinction simply *had* to be at the next big ball at Gaunt House, and wouldn’t she and her lovely daughter Blanche care to join him for the famous Watteau quadrille breakfast at Viscount Marrowfat’s Fulham estate? Every single person who mattered would be there—even a royal or two!—and Blanche in a frilly 18th century shepherdess costume would outshine every other girl there by a mile. The obsequious old gent even volunteered to chaperone them to every amusement under the sun, and offered to run any tiny little errand Sir Francis Clavering could possibly dream up.

The problem was, nobody in London seemed all that eager to hang around Sir Francis. The guy got blackballed from two separate clubs in a row, for heaven’s sake! In Parliament, he only ever chatted to the most disreputable backbenchers the place had to offer, with a real gift for seeking out the worst company imaginable as naturally as other people gravitate towards their own relatives. We won’t name *all* his dodgy MP mates, that would be rude, but a handful are too funny not to mention: there was Captain Raff, Epsom’s very own rep, who vanished for a “Levant diplomatic mission” the second he lost every penny he owned at the Goodwood races; there was Hustingson, the super “patriotic” Islington MP who hasn’t yelled about government corruption once since they gave him the cushy governor job of Coventry Island; there was Bob Freeny, from the famous Booterstown Freenys, who’s such a crack shot we’re not daring to say a bad word about him. The party whip Mr. Hotspur, whose job it was to round up every last one of these losers for votes, hated Sir Francis most of all, even though the man was the heir of an ancient family that had sat for their own family borough since the Norman conquest. “If that fool’s needed for a division,” Hotspur would snarl, “ten to one he’s holed up in a gambling hell. He got his education in the Fleet prison, and mark my words, he’ll finish his days on the hulks, blowing the Begum’s entire fortune at thimble-rig.” If even the stuck-up high-born Hotspur was willing to be civil to Clavering for work reasons, why on earth wouldn’t Major Pendennis have his own perfectly logical reasons for sticking around the guy’s house?

“The man’s got a cellar of claret worth drooling over and a cook that could make a saint steal a slice of roast beef,” the Major told his nephew Pen. “He’s quiet as a post most of the time, so he never gets in your way, and he barely ever opens his mouth. If he wants to lose all his money to card-sharps, what’s that to me? Don’t go snooping into every man’s business, Pen, my boy. Every last guy alive has a little locked cupboard in his house he’d die before letting you peek into. Why go digging when the rest of the place is wide open, and it’s a damned good house at that? And if the master of the house isn’t exactly a prize, the women are perfect. The Begum’s not the fanciest speaker alive, but she’s the kindest woman you’ll ever meet, and sharp as a tack too. Little Blanche? You know what I think of her, you rascal. I’m half convinced she’s sweet on you, you could have her for the asking if you bothered.
