Though you’re getting so big for your boots these days I suppose you’ll only settle for a duke’s daughter, eh? Go on, try and bag one, I dare you.”

Pen, who was still a little tipsy off the heady rush of being a minor literary celebrity around town, had to admit his uncle’s nonstop hints about Blanche had made him wonder if the little old flirtation they’d had by the sleepy Brawl river back in their country days might not be ready to pick up again. But he swore blind he was not getting married any time soon, copying his uncle’s worldly tone to sneer a little at the whole institution.

“You’re perfectly happy rattling around on your own, sir, and so am I,” he said. “If I had a wife trailing after me I’d lose my spot in every fun party in town. I’m not about to retire to a damp country cottage with some boring Mrs. Pendennis, or drag a wife into a tiny London lodging where a single overworked servant waits on you hand and foot. My silly little love illusions are all dead now. You cured me of that first schoolgirl crush of mine, remember? She would have married a total fool, and I’d have turned into a grumpy, resentful husband by Christmas. We young folks these days live fast! I’m only five and twenty and I feel as old as those stuffy old fogies loitering in the bay window at Bays’s club—sorry, didn’t mean to call you a fogey, sir! I just mean I’m completely blasé about all that love nonsense. I couldn’t work up a spark for Miss Amory now any more than I could fall in adoration with Lady Mirabel all over again. Though I do feel a little bad for old Mirabel, his whole obsession with his wife is the only halfway respectable thing he’s ever done in his life.”

“Sir Charles Mirabel was a *theater* man from way back,” the Major huffed, thoroughly offended that his nephew was being flippant about someone of Mirabel’s rank. “He acted at Carlton House when he was a page to the Prince, for heaven’s sake, of course he’s dramatic. And he’s rich enough to marry whoever he likes! Lady Mirabel is received everywhere—everywhere, mark you, the Duchess of Connaught has her over, Lady Rockminster invites her to every dinner. It doesn’t become cheeky young boys to run down people of that station. And those old fellows at Bays’s you’re calling fogies? They’re some of the finest gentlemen in England, you youngsters would do well to learn a little manners and modesty from them instead of strutting around like you invented the wheel.” For the next ten minutes he was fully convinced Pen was turning into an unbearably conceited little upstart who was getting far more praise than he deserved.

Pen thought his old uncle’s huff was hilarious. He never got tired of studying his weird little quirks, and found his worldly old mentor endlessly entertaining. “Oh, I’ve been hanging around grown-ups for fifteen years now, sir,” he said smoothly, smoothing over the old man’s ruffled feathers, “if you think *we* are disrespectful, you should meet the actual kids of today. That young protégé of yours, Master Clavering, came round to my rooms for breakfast the other day, just like you told me to invite him. We did the whole tourist London thing, dined at the club, went to the play. He said our club’s wine was worse than Ellis’s stuff in Richmond, smoked Warrington’s cheap cavendish tobacco right after breakfast, and when I handed him a sovereign as a little goodbye gift, he said he had pockets full of the things but he’d take it just to prove he wasn’t stuck up.”

“Did he now? You invited young Clavering?!” the Major was instantly grinning again, all anger forgotten. “Fine boy, a little wild but he’s got spunk! His parents absolutely adore that sort of attention, you couldn’t do anything better for our friends over at Grosvenor Place. You took him to the play and tipped him? That was excellent, sir, excellent!” And off he went, completely convinced the next generation wasn’t half bad, and that he’d still be able to turn Arthur Pendennis into a proper success story yet.

Young Frank Clavering, meanwhile, had grown big and strong enough that none of his family could control him any more. He didn’t obey his parents, his governess, he ruled the roost, basically. He ignored his dad as much as humanly possible, he screamed and threw full-on punch-ups with his mamma whenever she tried to stop him eating too many tarts or buying a new toy, and he kicked his governess’s shins so hard when she tried to make him do Latin grammar that she surrendered on the spot and never dared yell at him again. He tried the same trick on his half-sister Blanche once or twice, but she boxed his ears so hard his ears rang for a week, and after that he never dared mess with her, or the housemaid, or anyone else in the house who didn’t put up with his nonsense.

Eventually, when the family moved to London, Sir Francis announced the little beggar needed to be sent off to school. So off young Frank went to the Reverend Otto Rose’s fancy academy at Twickenham, the kind of place full of rich little noblemen’s sons being prepped for Eton or Harrow. We’re not going to follow him through his entire school career, because the path to learning was absurdly easy for those little rich brats back then. They got to “approach the temple of learning in a carriage and four,” so to speak, stop for snacks and naps whenever they wanted. Even at that tiny age they wore polished patent boots, the smallest size of lemon-coloured kid gloves Privat the glover ever manufactured, and cambric handkerchiefs. They dressed for dinner every night, had soft shawl dressing gowns, fires in their bedrooms, occasional rides on horses and in carriages, and special oil to make their hair shiny. The Reverend Rose had banned all corporal punishment entirely, swearing moral discipline was all you needed to raise boys right. The lads learned “advanced skills” at an alarming rate, too—like how to sneak their dads’ cigars and smoke them in the stables, and how to drink neat champagne, all before they were old enough to walk through the doors of a public school. Ten year old Frank Clavering could knock back a glass of bubbly as easily as any whiskered dragoon cornet half his dad’s age.

When he came home for holidays, Major Pendennis lavished the exact same over-the-top politeness on him as he did on the rest of the family, even though the little brat nicknamed the old man “Wigsby” behind his back, mimicked his little elaborate bows and smarmy smiles for Blanche and Lady Clavering, and drew rude caricatures exaggerating the size of the Major’s nose, the lopsided angle of his wig, and the ridiculous bow tie he wore. The Major was determined to make Pen spoil the little terror too, badgering Arthur to invite him round to his Temple chambers, treat him to a fancy club dinner, drag him to Madame Tussaud’s, the Tower, the theater, and slip him a little tip at the end of the day. Good-natured Pen agreed to do it one day: had Frank over for breakfast, where the kid sneered at his rickety old furniture, chipped crockery, and Warrington’s threadbare dressing gown, puffed away at a tiny cheap pipe, and told dramatic stories about a huge schoolyard fight between two lads called Tuffy and Long Biggings that entertained Pen and Warrington no end.

Just as the Major predicted, Lady Clavering was so grateful for Arthur’s kindness to her precious boy she could have kissed him. Frank didn’t even notice, he took all the free treats and cash as his birthright, and he probably had more sovereigns stuffed in his pockets than poor cash-strapped Pen did anyway.

Major Pendennis, with his sharp old man’s eyes and decades of experience snooping into other people’s business, had made it his mission to find out every last detail of the Clavering family finances, no matter how nosy it looked. As a former country neighbour who’d known their families for ages, he slowly but surely pried every last secret out of Lady Clavering. Turns out Blanche only got a tiny little slice of her mom’s fortune—almost all of the huge pile of cash the Begum had brought back from India was earmarked to go straight to young Frank. Sir Francis didn’t care about anyone or anything but gambling, Lady Clavering was dotingly obsessed with her little boy, and Blanche absolutely loathed her little half-brother. That was the full, simple picture. “Ah, my dear madam,” the Major would say, patting Frank’s head like he was the sweetest child ever born, “this boy could easily end up wearing a baron’s coronet at the next coronation, if only we all play our cards right and Sir Francis stops messing up.”

Lady Clavering heaved a huge, sad sigh. “Oh Major, I’m afraid that man plays far *too many* cards already, if you catch my meaning.” The Major pretended he already knew all about Sir Francis’s terrible gambling habit, sighed right back in pity, and said so many nice, sympathetic things that Lady Clavering decided he was the only person in the whole world she could confide in. She spilled her whole messy life story to him, tears of shame rolling down her face. She’d been sent back to India at 14 after two years of English school, full of silly romance novels, had married the awful first husband Amory who’d told her endless swashbuckling adventure stories. She’d never even met a man outside of her dad’s clerks and native servants before that, and she’d married him thinking he was a hero. He turned out to be a drunk, a gambler, and a terrible husband, made her life a complete hell. Her own dad wasn’t much better, a cruel miser who’d left her all his money but had never been kind to a soul in his life. “If Amory had died in prison before that stupid trial, not after, I’d never have been stuck with this horrid last name, and I probably never would have married Sir Francis either,” she blubbered. “Everyone thinks I’m so happy, with my diamonds and my carriages and all these fancy parties, but I’m not! My husband’s never around, my daughter Blanche sneers at me for not being posh enough and mispronouncing words like Apollo. I never had a chance to get a proper education! I was raised around native servants till I was twelve! If I’d had a good husband I could’ve been a good, happy woman, I swear.” She wiped her eyes quick, said she had to tidy up before Lady Rockminster arrived for their park drive, and ten minutes later she was the loud, cheerful, funniest lady in the room, not a trace of tears anywhere.

“Begad, she’s not half bad,” the Major thought to himself later, sipping a glass of that good Clavering claret. “Not exactly refined, sure, but she’s got a heart, and three full shares of India Stock to her name, all that money earmarked for that little brat Frank? Wouldn’t it be lovely if a good chunk of that cash found its way to Blanche instead, and even better, if one of those India Stock stars ended up in Arthur Pendennis’s name?”

He kept plotting away, using his old age and status as the family’s trusted friend to corner Blanche alone for a nice little chat one day. He popped in at luncheon so often no one even bothered to stop bickering in front of him half the time—Lady Clavering, loud and hot-tempered, fought with her sharp-witted daughter Blanche constantly, and Blanche’s mean little barbs almost always left her mom storming off red-faced and defeated. “I’m an old retired fellow,” the Major would say, “I’ve got nothing to do all day but mind everyone else’s business. I’ve fixed fights between husbands and wives, fathers and sons, moms and daughters for decades. You two are both wonderful people, I’m going to get you to make up, no fuss.”

One afternoon he walked in right as Lady Clavering stormed out of the drawing room, furious, muttering about that little wicked brat of hers as she ran up the stairs to her bedroom. He found Blanche pouting on the sofa, and immediately launched into his flattery routine. “Ah, my dear Miss Amory, I see what’s happened. You and your mom have had one of your little disagreements. Moms and daughters fight everywhere! I fixed a fight last week between Lady Clapperton and her daughter Lady Claudia, and Lady Lear and her eldest haven’t spoken for fourteen years! They’re both wonderful people, they just can’t stand to be in the same house as each other. The only solution for a brilliant, refined creature like you is your own establishment—you could run a better salon than any woman in London, you’d be the queen of your own circle.”

“I’m certainly not happy in *this* house,” Blanche sighed, flicking her fancy lacy sleeve. “My mother’s stupidity could try the patience of a saint.”

“Exactly! You two were never meant to live together!” the Major agreed. “Your mom made a huge mistake raising you to be the brilliant, intellectual woman you are, then sticking you in a house full of people who can’t even spell ‘Watteau’ half the time. You’re not meant to follow anyone—you’re meant to lead. You’re ambitious, you deserve to shine. You’ll never do that stuck here. I hope one day soon I get to see you as the mistress of your own perfect little home.”

Blanche shrugged her lily-white shoulders, looking perfectly dramatic. “Where’s the prince? Where’s the palace, Major Pendennis?” she said. “I’m ready! But there’s no romance left in the world, no real sincere affection anywhere.”

“Not a drop, sadly,” the Major said, putting on the most sad, sentimental face he could fake.

“Not that I know anything about it, of course,” Blanche said, casting her eyes all demurely down, “I’ve only ever read about real love in novels.”

“Of course you haven’t, dear girl! Novels are all lies, everyone knows that, there’s no romance left at all. Begad, I wish I was a young man like my nephew Arthur.”

“All the men we meet at every ball are the same,” Blanche kept moping. “Dumb dancing guardsmen, penniless treasury clerks, total boobies! If

We’ve lost track of good old noble Henry Foker for a few pages, so let’s catch up: true to his brand of stubborn, no-restraint constancy, he’s been fully occupied chasing the great all-consuming love that’s taken over every spare brain cell he’s got.

I’d beg all my young readers currently wrapped up in crush-mode to stop and do the math on how much time this little hobby eats up. Turns out pining after someone is easily the most expensive, unproductive pastime a human being can waste their life on. What *don’t* you sacrifice for it, you poor silly over-romantic lot? First off, you lose hours of precious sleep, tossing and turning replaying that one 2-second interaction you had with your crush, then stumble down to brunch at noon when the rest of the family’s long done with breakfast and off to their day. Then when you finally drag yourself to work or class, you can’t focus for a second—all your wits are glued to the thought of that person. When you’re finally done slacking through your tasks, you ditch all your friends and family just to loiter near their house, stare up at their bedroom window, or creep by their carriage in the park to get a 10-second glimpse. At night, home is boring as dirt; your mom’s chit chat makes you want to zone out, and even her best home cooking goes untouched. The whole rest of the banquet of life tastes like cardboard, except for that one single little side dish you’re fixated on. Work, family, your old hobbies, all the stuff you used to care about? Suddenly intolerable. You’re only happy when you’re chasing your flame.

That’s the kind of brain rot common to over-indulged young men, anyway—and that’s exactly where H. Foker was at. The guy was raised to give in to every silly urge he had, so of course he threw himself headfirst into this one with his usual self-absorbed gusto. No need for worldly folks to side-eye him for turning into love’s little doormat, either, just because he once gave his buddy Arthur Pendennis loads of very sensible love advice. Who among us hasn’t handed out perfect, airtight advice to our friends? Who hasn’t stood on a soapbox preaching, then turned around and done the exact dumb thing we warned them against?

Sure, you madam reading this are probably a perfect little saint, never had a wrong thought in your frigid, spotless little life. And you, sir? You’re so level-headed no silly crush could ever throw off your work rhythm at the office or your stock trades. You’re too tough to need anyone’s sympathy, right? Fine, we won’t give you any. We’ll save all our pity for the rest of us regular schmucks, the ones who stumble, mess up, get back up, and trudge along with everyone else. You never trip, so why would you need a hand? Your perfect serenity never gets clouded by passion, tempted by sin, or gnawed at by guilt. Compassion would just be rude to a perfect angel like you—but also, hanging out with you is insufferable. You’re so high up on the pedestal of your own virtue you’re basically all alone, and none of us regular mortals can bother to climb up just to chat. Bye Felicia. We’re sticking to the messy normal folks in this story, none of whom are perfect—okay, maybe there’s one little sweet character who’s almost perfect, but she’s so humble she thinks she’s a giant sinner, so she doesn’t count.

That near-perfect girl’s not in London right now, and she’s definitely not the one Foker’s losing his mind over. But who needs to be an angel to get worshipped like one? The human race has wild, diverse tastes, after all. No one is too ugly, too old, too dumb, too pompous, too much of a total idiot to get adored. I’ve read stories of two elderly convicts, a pickpocket and his wife, both ugly, uneducated, argumentative drunks, whose love was every bit as grand as Antony and Cleopatra’s. Everyone knows the story of the tiny Polish dwarf Count Borulawski who had the most beautiful baroness in Dresden’s court head over heels for him. And just the other day, teen Cornet Tozer ran off with Mrs. Battersby, who was old enough to be his mom. No poverty, no shame, no crime is bad enough that someone won’t cling to it, hug it tighter than they’d ever hug virtue, out of that ridiculous, stubborn, wonderful human capacity for stupid faithful love.

So there was Henry Foker, pining after his crush, cursing every force keeping him away from her. When Lord Gravesend and his family high-tailed it back to their country estate (leaving their proxy with the stuffy old Lord Bagwig), Harry hung around London way longer than he had any business staying. His fiancée, his cousin Lady Ann, couldn’t have cared less that he was lingering—she didn’t miss him for a second. Wherever Miss Blanche Amory went, this smitten fool followed her around. The whole ton knew he was engaged to his cousin, though, so he had to hide his little passion under wraps. It was so pent up inside him it’s a wonder he didn’t blow up one day, screaming his secret love to the whole street and then collapse from the relief.

One beautiful June evening, Gaunt House threw the ball of the century. The next day’s newspapers printed nearly two full columns of all the fancy nobles and rich gals lucky enough to score an invite. Sir Francis and Lady Clavering and Miss Blanche Amory were there—Major Pendennis, our relentless social climber, had wrangled them tickets. So were our two lads Arthur Pendennis and Harry Foker. Both of them showed up ready to dance as many sets with Blanche as humanly possible. The good Major took it upon himself to chaperone Lady Clavering, and immediately herded her straight to the best part of the whole mansion: the refreshment room. There, surrounded by old master paintings, giant solid gold platters, towers of flowers, and thousands of wax candles, an all-night supper was spread that cost more than most people’s annual rent. We don’t need to name exactly how many jellies, creams, peach tarts, grapes, pâtés, cups of tea, and glasses of champagne Lady Clavering polished off. No one knows how much the Major suffered trailing after her, flagging down every butler and maid to bring her more snacks, with the patience of a saint. He never complained, never let the agony show on his face, just kept carrying plate after plate over to the Begum.

Mr. Wagg tried to keep count of all the dishes Lady Clavering ate, but after a few glasses of champagne his math skills went totally out the window, and at the end of the night he told Lady Steyne’s doctor Honeyman to make sure he checked on the Begum the next day to confirm she hadn’t exploded from overeating.

Sir Francis Clavering wandered in, skulked around the fancy rooms for a bit, decided all this pomp and fancy company was not his vibe, tossed back a couple glasses of wine at the buffet, and bailed straight for Jermyn Street, where his buddies Loder, Punter, little Moss Abramns, and Captain Skewball were waiting for him around the green gambling table. The rattle of dice and their friendly banter soon got his usual weak little grin back on his face.

At one point, Mr. Pynsent came over to claim Blanche for a dance he’d asked her for earlier. But he and Arthur had already exchanged nasty glares across the dance floor, so Arthur jumped up first, claimed Blanche was his partner for that exact set. Pynsent bit his lip, glowered even harder, bowed super sarcastically, and backed off, grumbling that he’d give up his spot. Some men are just permanently in your way no matter what. Pynsent and Pen both thought the other was the worst person alive, and acted accordingly.
“Ugh, that stuck-up little provincial idiot,” Pynsent thought. “He wrote a cheap two-penny novel and now his head’s so far up his own arse a good kick would knock that conceit right out of him.”
“That man is the most insufferable idiot on the planet,” Pen said out loud to Blanche, “His soul lives in Downing Street, his neckcloth is made of office foolscap, his hair is dyed with sand, his legs are rulers, his insides are all tape and sealing wax. He was a prig in his cradle, and hasn’t laughed a single time in his life except three times at the exact same terrible joke his boss told. I like that man about as much as I like cold boiled veal.”
Blanche, of course, gasped and called him wicked, mechant, perfectly horrible, and teased him about what mean things he’d say about her when her back was turned.
“Say? I’d say you’ve got the prettiest figure and the tiniest waist in the whole world, Blanche—Miss Amory, sorry, my bad. C’mon, another turn. This waltz music could make a fat alderman get up and dance.”
“Aw, are you not tripping over your own feet mid-waltz anymore?” Blanche teased, looking up at him all arch and cheeky.
“People trip and get back up again in life, you know,” Pen said. “I used to call you Blanche back in the old days, it’s still the prettiest name I know. And yeah, I’ve had lots of practice since then.”
“Loads of different partners, I bet,” Blanche said, with a fake little dramatic sigh and a shrug. And she was right—Pen had gotten a whole lot of waltz practice in the years since they’d known each other, so he was actually pretty good now.

If Pen was chattery and cheeky, Harry Foker, normally the most talkative, friendly guy alive, turned totally silent and mopey the second he was dancing with Blanche. To hold her tiny waist felt like pure heaven, to spin around the room with her was delirium—but what on earth could he possibly say that was good enough for her? What bit of conversation could he bring that was worthy of a witty, beautiful queen like Blanche? She did all the talking, when she was dancing with him. She asked him how his cute little pony was, looked at him with such soft, regretful kindness, sighed so delicately when he offered to let her ride it, and turned him down. “I never have anyone to ride with in London,” she said. “Mamma’s too nervous, and she thinks her figure looks terrible on a horse. Sir Francis never rides with me. He loves me exactly the way a stepfather loves a stepdaughter. Oh, wouldn’t it be wonderful to have a real father, Mr. Foker?”
“Uh, yeah, it’s pretty great,” Harry said, who’d had a very calm, unremarkable relationship with his own dad. Blanche immediately dropped her sad sentimental act, her grey eyes twinkling so hard they both burst out laughing. Harry relaxed completely, and started chattering away nonstop, that sweet silly Foker banter, full of phrases you’d never find in a dictionary, rambling stories about his horses, his life, and all the other people waltzing past them at the ball, who he gossiped about with zero filter and lots of goofy humor.

When the conversation started lulling and Harry’s bashfulness crept back in, Blanche knew exactly how to get him talking again. She asked him about his family estate Logwood, if it was pretty, if he was a hunter, if he thought women should hunt too (she was all ready to say she adored hunting). But Foker said he hated sporty ladies, and pointed out Lady Bullfinch walking past, who he’d once seen out on a hunt smoking a cigar, calling her a total horse godmother. Blanche immediately said she thought hunting was disgusting, that the thought of killing a cute little fox made her shiver, and Foker laughed so hard he started waltzing with more energy and grace than ever.

At the very end of that last waltz, Blanche asked him about his family’s big house Drummington, if it was nice, said she’d heard all his cousins were super accomplished, that she’d met Lord Erith, and asked which of his cousins was his favorite. “Oh it’s Lady Ann, isn’t it?” she teased. “I can tell by the way you’re blushing.” Then she said she was tired of dancing, it was way too late, she had to go find her mamma. Before Harry could say a single word, she slipped right out of his arm, grabbed Pen’s instead (he was strutting across the dance floor at that exact moment), and yelled “Mamma! Take me to mamma right now, dear Mr. Pendennis!” She was already halfway across the room before Harry could process what just happened, left standing there stunned like she’d shot an arrow straight through his chest.

Lord Steyne, decked out in all his state regalia with the garter and sash, bald head and bright shining eyes, and that little ring of red sideburns circling his face, looked incredibly grand on a big occasion like this. He totally bowled over Lady Clavering when he came over to introduce himself, at Major Pendennis’s humble little request. He handed her a glass of wine with his own regal hand, said he’d heard all about her charming daughter, and asked to be introduced to her. Right on cue, Arthur walked over with Blanche on his arm.

The lord bowed super deep, and Blanche curtsied so low you’d think she was going to touch the floor. He shook Pen’s hand, said he’d read his novel, that it was delightfully wicked and clever, and asked Blanche if she’d read it. Pen cringed and blushed so hard his ears turned pink—because Blanche, with little black ringlets and a few details changed, was very obviously the heroine Neaera in Pen’s *Walter Lorraine*.

Blanche had read it. The look in her eyes said she adored every second of it. Once that little bit of performance was done, the Marquis of Steyne bowed again to both Lady Clavering and Blanche, and wandered off to say hi to the rest of his guests. The second his back was turned, both mom and daughter were gushing about how amazing and noble the Marquis was. “He said you two make a very nice couple,” Major Pendennis whispered to Lady Clavering. Did he really? She was over the moon. All the flattery and fancy food and being noticed by a marquis had put her in the best mood ever. She laughed, winked, nodded knowingly at Pen, tapped his arm with her fan, tapped Blanche, tapped the Major—her joy was boundless, and she showed it in the loudest, most unapologetic way possible.

When the party trundled down the giant staircase of Gaunt House, the sun was already up, pale and bright over the black bare trees in the square, the sky streaked soft pink. Half the people who’d been at the ball looked absolutely ghastly in the morning light—especially that poor devoted Major. He’d spent all night running after Lady Clavering, feeding her every snack she wanted and every compliment she loved, and he was a wreck. The circles under his eyes were deep brown, his puffy orbs looked exactly like the plover eggs Lady Clavering had eaten half a dozen of the night before, the wrinkles in his old face were deep furrows, and little silvery stubble glinted on his chin, next to his dyed whiskers which had gone totally lank and lost their curl.

There he stood, suffering in total silence, no complaints, for hours past when he should have been in bed. He knew he looked terrible (he could see all the other partygoers his age looked just as rough, after all). His rheumatism was twinging in his back and knees, his feet were burning inside his fancy tight patent boots, and he’d even forced himself to eat a little bit of the supper even though he knew it gave him indigestion, just to keep Lady Clavering happy. If the gods admire people who suffer through hardship bravely, then the god of social climbing Major Pendennis worshipped must have been looking down at him totally approving. Martyrs exist for every religion, after all.

The two Pendennises (young Pen and old Major) waited with the Clavering women until their carriage pulled up. The Major’s martyrdom was over, because the nice Begum insisted on dropping him off at his place in Bury Street on her way home. He climbed into the back seat after a couple of wimpy little bows and his last polite thank you speech, determined to keep up his perfectly proper persona right up until he collapsed in his bed. The Begum waved her chubby little hand goodbye to Arthur and Foker, and Blanche smiled sleepily at the two boys out the carriage window, wondering if she looked pale and pretty under her pink hood, and if that was just the ball mirrors playing tricks or actual tiredness making her look wan.

Arthur probably noticed that Blanche’s complexion looked a little yellow, but he wasn’t fooled by any mirror tricks. Our young worldling had very sharp eyes, and he saw Blanche’s face almost exactly as it was, no rose-colored glasses. But poor smitten Foker? Blanche’s face glowed so bright to him he couldn’t see a single flaw in it, same as he couldn’t find a flaw in the sun that was just rising over the rooftops.

Moralists will tut-tut that Pen picked up some very bad late-night London habits, often getting into bed at the exact same time that early-rising country folk were pouring their first cup of morning tea. People get used to any schedule, after all. Newspaper editors, Covent Garden market sellers, night cabbies, late-night ball attendees—they’re all wide awake at 3 or 4 a.m. when normal people are snoring their heads off. We mentioned last chapter Pen was in a very giddy, loose mood these days, more than happy to smoke a cigar and say whatever was on his mind.

So Foker and Pen walked away from Gaunt House, smoking, Pen chattering nonstop and Foker looking like he was dying to say something important. Pen got extra snarky and fancy whenever he spent time around rich noble folks, copying their little airs and graces, and his overactive imagination made him fully believe he was a Very Important Person right now. He ranted and made fun of everyone he’d seen at the ball: Lady John Turnbull’s terrible French she insisted on dropping into every conversation, no matter how much everyone hated it, Mrs. Slack Roper’s ridiculous costume and fake jewelry, the old dandies and the young dandies, he roasted everyone in sight.
“You’re going way too hard on everyone, Pen,” Foker said. “You’ve made

That all-nighter rager at Gaunt House nearly turned Major Pendennis into a heap of gouty old bones on the drawing room rug. The second he could hobble without groaning loud enough to rattle the chandeliers, he tottered off to Buxton, determined to soak his aches out of his ancient carcass in the local healing springs. Parliament dissolved, Sir Francis Clavering and his entire clan fled town, and all the little schemes we’ve been yammering about over the last few chapters ground to a complete halt over the lull of lazy summer weeks. London was all but empty, a ghost town of fancy addresses and abandoned club armchairs.

The social season was well and truly dead and buried. Pen’s lawyer neighbours had bolted for the country circuit, all his flashy fashionable mates had booked their cross-Channel ferries, or legged it up to the Scottish moors chasing grouse and a spot of non-toxic air. You couldn’t spot a single dandy lingering by the club bow windows, or loitering on Pall Mall’s empty pavement. The red-coated foot guards had vanished from outside Buckingham Palace, the St James’s tradesmen were all off on their own holidays, the tailors had sprouted ridiculous mustaches and run off to cruise the Rhine, and the bootmakers were loitering at Ems or Baden-Baden, turning bright red when they spotted their wealthy customers punting at the gambling tables right next to them, too embarrassed to say hello and ask for their overdue bills. The St James’s clergymen were preaching to half-empty pews with not a single notable sinner in the congregation, the Kensington Gardens band had packed up their brass horns and silver trumpets, and only two or three rickety old carriages were creeping along the banks of the Serpentine. Poor Clarence Bulbul, trapped in town by his oh-so-arduous Treasury clerk duties, took his afternoon ride in Rotten Row and sighed so hard his horse almost stopped, swearing the park was as vast and empty as the Arabian desert and he was the only Bedouin dumb enough to be wandering through its dusty, lonely wastes. Warrington stuffed an entire carpet-bag full of Cavendish tobacco and trundled off to his brother’s house in Norfolk, as he always did for the holidays. Pen got left alone in his chambers for a stretch, since this self-proclaimed man of the world couldn’t just up and leave town whenever he pleased: he was stuck running operations for his newspaper, *The Pall Mall Gazette*, while chief editor Captain Shandon was off lolling in Boulogne-sur-Mer with his family, claiming the salty sea air was a health necessity and not just a convenient excuse to never write a single leading article unless someone threatened to dock his pay.

Pen had spent years bragging to anyone who’d listen that he was completely blasé, burnt out on life, and far too sophisticated to get excited about anything. The truth was he was a strapping, healthy young buck with an appetite so ferocious he could polish off even the worst tavern mutton chop with utter glee, and a craving for company that proved he was the farthest thing from a misanthrope alive. If he couldn’t get a fancy dinner at a gentleman’s club, he’d happily chow down on a terrible meal at the nearest public house. If he couldn’t hang out with witty nobles or pretty society girls, he’d pal around with whoever wandered by, perfectly content larking up the Thames on a Greenwich steamboat, trundling out to Hampstead with his newspaper colleague Finucane, or popping over the river to the scruffy summer theatres or Vauxhall Royal Gardens. He was such good mates with the famous comic singer Simpson that he’d shake hands with the top equestrian performers between acts, snickering at their silly gags and softening up when he spotted the rest of the crowd: the roistering lads hell-bent on having a good time no matter the cost, the honest working parents with their kids screaming and clapping at the acrobats, even the ragged street girls cackling loud and rough, bringing their youth and their secrets out to dance and drink till dawn so they could forget their troubles for a few hours. Pen bragged all the time about this little quirk of his, saying anthropology was his favourite hobby, the same way other men obsessed over art or botany. He’d gape with equal delight at a wrinkled old dowager preening in a ballroom, a blushing young aristocrat making her debut, a hulking guardsman flirting with a servant girl in the park, or a tiny toddler feeding ducks while his nurse gossiped. A man with a mostly clean heart can enjoy that kind of people-watching forever, after all, even if it’s a little secret, a little sad, because he’s just far enough removed from the mess to care without getting dragged into it.

He’d ramble about that nonsense at Warrington all the time, all fire and bravado. “I was so madly in love when I was a kid I burned that whole flame right out for good. If I ever get married it’ll be a cold, sensible business deal, some well-bred, good-tempered pretty girl with a little bit of money to cushion our carriage through the rest of life. Romance is dead for me. I spent every last penny of it years ago. I’m old before my time, and I’m proud of it.”

“Piffle!” Warrington would growl around his pipe. “You thought you were going bald last month, bragged about it like it was some fancy medal, and the second the hairdresser told you to use bear’s grease you dove for the pot. You smell like a barber’s shop every time you walk in the door now.”

“You’re just Diogenes living in a tobacco-stinking tub,” Pen huffed, blushing bright pink. “Violets smell better than stale shag, you grizzly old cynic.” For a man who claimed he didn’t care what the world thought of him, Pen spent an absurd amount of time primping, wearing boots so tight his feet went numb halfway through the day, and polishing his boots until they were so shiny you could see your reflection in them.

It was in that dead, boring stretch of autumn, on a sparkly Friday night, that Pen finished writing a brilliant leading article at the newspaper office—so good even Shandon in a good mood would have nodded and stolen all the credit for it, if he’d bothered to show up to work. He read over the damp proof sheet and patted himself on the back, then decided he deserved a treat: he’d cross the Thames and go watch the fireworks at Vauxhall. He stuffed his press pass that read *Editor of the Pall Mall Gazette + 1 Friend* in his pocket, paid his penny to cross Waterloo Bridge, and set off for the gardens. The walk was lovely, stars twinkling overhead, none of the Vauxhall rockets and Roman candles up yet to outshine them.

Everyone who’s ever been to Vauxhall knows you have to trudge through a dark, miserable little entry tunnel first, hiding all the glittering lamps and fun from passersby. The walls of the tunnel have two little illuminated windows, where two men sit at desks ready to take your cash, or wave you through if you’ve got an admission ticket. Pen got to the window ready to show his pass, only to find a man and two ladies already arguing with the ticket taker ahead of him.

The man had his hat perched lopsided on his head and a shabby short cloak he was carrying off like he was the most elegant dandy alive, yelling in a brogue Pen recognised instantly.

“Begorrah, sir, if ye doubt my honour, will ye do me the favour of stepping out of that little box there and—”
“Law, Capting!” the older lady yelped, tugging at his sleeve.
“Stop bothering me,” the ticket man sighed.
“—and go find Mr. Hodgen himself, who’s inside the gardens, and tell him I sent you! I’ve no intention of fighting ye, sir, not in front of ladies anyway. But go ask him, will ye? I had an admission order from him, he’s me dearest friend, he’s singing the *Body Snatcher* for the crowd tonight! I’ve seen Vauxhall a hundred times, I don’t care if I don’t get in, but one of these ladies has never been here in her life. You wouldn’t be so cruel as to take advantage of me misfortune losing the ticket, and rob the poor girl of her fun, would ye?”

“Sorry, Captain, I can’t leave my post to run errands for you,” the ticket man grumbled. The Captain swore a fine colourful Irish oath, the older lady groaned “Law, how provoking!” and the younger girl looked up at the Captain and said “Never mind, sir, I don’t want to go at all. Come on, mamma.” No sooner were the words out of her mouth than she burst into tears, the poor little thing.

“Me poor child!” the Captain wailed at the ticket taker. “Can ye see that and still turn us away?”

Arthur wandered up right then, recognised Costigan immediately, and called out “Don’t you know me, Captain? Pendennis!” He tipped his hat and bowed to the two ladies, and Costigan threw his arms around him like he was his long-lost brother, yammering on about their terrible unlucky situation. He’d had an order from Hodgen for two admissions, he’d thought it would cover three, he’d lost the scrap of paper on the walk over, and now the poor young girl was heartbroken.

Pen was a soft touch for anyone’s misfortunes, and he couldn’t very well turn his back here. He’d seen the girl’s sweet innocent face, the little tremor of her lip before she started crying. Even if it had been his last guinea, he’d have spent it to make her happy. She hid her teary eyes behind her handkerchief the second she spotted a strange gentleman looking at her. Pen stood there, all handsome, kind, blushing a little, hat in hand, the perfect courteous gentleman, and asked who they were. The older lady looked familiar, he realised—he’d seen her around the block before.

“If I can help you out here, Captain Costigan, please let me,” he said. “No need for any more fuss. I’ve got a ticket that admits two people—ma’am, would you do me the honour of letting me walk you all in?” He didn’t want to make a big show of flaunting his money like some grand aristocrat, so he just handed his purse to Costigan and pulled out his ticket, offering his arm to the older lady first.

“Fanny, take the gentleman’s arm,” the older lady said. “You’re far too kind, sir. I’ve seen you walking in and out of our gate a hundred times, you going up to Captain Strong’s rooms at No. 3.”

Fanny curtsied, and slipped her hand under Pen’s arm. She wore a shabby little glove, but her hand was small and pretty, warm against his sleeve. She was barely more than a girl, not quite a full grown woman yet, the tears already drying on her cheeks, her face flushing bright pink with gratitude and delight as she looked up at Pen. Pen rested his free hand gently over hers on his arm. “Fanny’s a lovely name,” he said. “You know who I am, then?”

“We run the porter’s lodge at Shepherd’s Inn, sir,” Fanny said, curtsying again. “I’ve never been to Vauxhall before, papa wouldn’t let me come, and—oh law! Look at all the lights!” She gasped and shrank back, eyes wide as saucers, as the full glittering wonder of Vauxhall lit up in front of her: millions of twinkling lamps, more beautiful than any fairy tale or pantomime she’d ever seen at the theatre. Pen was tickled pink by how giddy she was, pressing her hand a little closer to his side. The self-proclaimed burnt-out blasé young man thought to himself, he’d give half his worldly goods to feel that kind of innocent, uncomplicated joy again.

“Your purse, Pendennis me dear boy,” Costigan’s voice boomed behind them. “You can count it, it’s all there! Ye know ye can trust old Jack Costigan. You’re a saint, Pen, I’ve known ye since ye were a nipper, Mrs Bolton, this gentleman owns Fairoaks Castle, I’ve drunk many a cask of claret up there with the finest gentry in the whole county! Mr Pendennis, ye’ve saved us all tonight! Mr Simpson, your most obedient servant, sir!” Pen was being all polite and grand to the ladies, but he had nothing on Costigan, who was bowing so deep to every stranger he passed you’d think he was meeting royalty, yelling “Bravo!” at every singer and acrobat within earshot.

Costigan, being a descendant of a long line of proud Irish chieftains and county magistrates, was far too dignified to walk arm in arm with a lady who sometimes swept his rooms and cooked his mutton chops, after all. He trundled along behind Pen and Fanny with Mrs Bolton, pointing out all the fancy buildings on the walk over and rambling about all the famous noblemen he claimed to have dined with across Europe. No one expected him to be seen giving an arm to a part-time charwoman in front of 20 thousand glittering Vauxhall lamps, least of all Mrs Bolton herself.

Pen had no such silly hangups. Fanny didn’t make his bed or sweep his chambers, so he was perfectly happy to keep his pretty little companion hanging off his arm. Fanny’s cheeks got pinker and pinker, her eyes sparkling, as she leaned on Pen’s arm, thinking he was far handsomer and grander than any other gentleman in the whole gardens. There was every sort of person there that night: rakish young surgeons, fast clerks from the City, the odd dandy from the guards regiments, and old Lord Colchicum lumbering around leaning on the arm of Mademoiselle Caracoline, the famous trick rider, who was yelling loud French swears that made every nearby prude turn red. Colchicum followed her around, and little Tom Tufthunt followed Colchicum around, perfectly happy to act as the old viscount’s errand boy, carve the fowl at supper, hold his brandy flask, and do all the little menial jobs a rich old roué needs done. When their group passed Pen and Fanny, Colchicum only gave Pen a lazy little leer, but Tom Tufthunt wagged his head and called out “How are you, old boy?” looking like he knew every single secret in the world.

“That’s the famous rider from Astley’s, I’ve seen her perform there,” Fanny said, staring after Mademoiselle Caracoline. “Who’s that old man hanging off her arm?”

“That’s Viscount Colchicum, Miss Fanny,” Pen said, all lordly and protective, not minding a bit that Fanny was hanging off his arm and the old roué had spotted them together. Fanny was very pretty, he noticed: dark bright eyes, teeth like tiny little pearls, her lips almost as red as the rider’s vermilion-painted ones, and her laugh was a thousand times sweeter and more innocent than the older woman’s rough cackle. It was only recently, while dusting the mantelpiece over at Bows and Costigan’s rooms, that Fanny had stared in the little mirror and realised she was pretty. A year before she’d been a gawky clumsy girl, all the other girls at her day school had called her a dowdy nobody, and she’d grown up blooming unnoticed in the dark little porter’s lodge of Shepherd’s Inn, a tiny flower no one had bothered to look at.

They wandered the gardens together, and even though half of London had fled the city, there were still two million people lingering around, plenty of whom recognised Arthur Pendennis. One of them was Henry Foker, wandering around alone, pale, hands stuffed in his pockets, giving Pen a rueful nod when they passed. Poor Harry was trying every distraction he could think of to forget Blanche, but no matter where he wandered in the dark walkways, no matter what pretty light display he stared at, he was still thinking of her. He’d paid the fortune teller to read his palm, and got furious when she told him he was in love with a dark-haired lady who’d make him happy—he wanted names, specifics, guarantees. He didn’t hear a single one of the famous Mr Momus’s comic songs at the concert, his face was so glum.

Fanny noticed his woebegone face and asked Pen what was wrong with the poor odd little man. “I think he’s been crossed in love,” Pen said, grinning down at her. “Isn’t that enough to make any man miserable, Fanny?” Fanny sighed, her dark eyes soft with pity for the lovesick stranger, though Harry never noticed her looking at him.

Then a loud voice boomed out “How dy do, Mr Pendennis!” It was Sam Huxter, the medical student from Clavering, dressed in a huge white coat, red neckcloth, dingy shirt collar, a gaudy cheap brass pin stuck in it, a ridiculous waistcoat with fanciful glass buttons, and trousers so loud they practically screamed “Look how tawdry and cheap I am!” He had one hand stuck in his pocket holding a walking stick, the other looped through the arm of a girl in a pink satin dress. “Forget me, I dare say? Huxter, from Clavering! Remember that row we had at Wapshot’s? Great famous author now, aren’t you? Saw your name in the Morning Post. Too much of a swell to come hang out with your old mates, right? Why don’t you come round Charterhouse Lane tomorrow night? We’ve got a crowd of lads from Bartholomew’s, and some devilish good gin punch. Here’s my card!” He pulled the card out of his case by biting the top off, and handed it to Pen.

“Very kind of you, I’m sure,” Pen said in his snootiest nobleman tone, “but I’m afraid I have an out of town engagement tomorrow night.” He slipped the card into his waistcoat pocket, wondering how this oaf had the audacity to hand him a visiting card. Neither of them seemed to remember that Huxter’s dad was a surgeon-apothecary back in Clavering, and Pen’s own late dad had been a surgeon-apothecary in Bath, once upon a time.

“No worries, old man!” Huxter yelled, already half drunk. “Pop round any time you’re passing, I’m home every Saturday, and we always have a full cheese cupboard. Ta ta—there’s the firework bell! Come on, Mary!”

Costigan never so much as banged on Pen’s door to rouse him from his slumbers, no irate note from Mr. Huxter slid under his door to spoil his morning, and when Pen finally blinked awake, he felt perkier than your average burnt-out, blasé London man ever does at the crack of day.

City blokes jolt awake at dawn with visions of consols and the stock exchange already dancing through their heads before their nightcap’s even slid fully off their foreheads. Lawyers rouse themselves staring down the day’s impossible caseload, already panicking that the solicitor pestering them for papers will turn up at their door before breakfast. Who among us doesn’t wake up to a niggling anxiety instantly tugging us back to the day’s chores? Still, that overnight rest is a superpower, isn’t it? Providence definitely knew what it was doing giving us sleep to face another round of nonsense.

Pen’s daily work, though, was exactly the kind that stopped feeling exciting about ten minutes in. The thrill of seeing your own name in print wears thin after three or four appearances in a magazine, after all. Even the fanciest thoroughbred Pegasus turns into a grumpy hack horse the second you harnessed him to a daily word count—he won’t budge an inch without a snack and a sharp nudge of the whip, no matter how pretty his feathers used to be. Pen wrote his columns for the *Pall Mall Gazette* perfectly well (for a raised salary, thank you very much) but zero enthusiasm, turning out good prose on his good days and total drivel on his bad, a sharp, bright literary hack who could churn out copy faster than most men could drink their morning coffee.

Society didn’t do much for him either. For all his big talk, he was still too young to hang with the old crew of distinguished men who’d already had all the good scandals, too out of practice to charm women without obsessing over how cool he looked doing it, too old to carouse all night with the lads still wasting their paychecks at music halls, and too lazy to keep up with stuffy businessmen. He was perfectly happy being alone most days, but complained about it so much you’d think he was being held prisoner in a boring drawing room.

The second he woke up post-Vauxhall, that little Fanny girl popped straight into his head. “What a nice, uncomplicated little thing she is,” he thought. “Way more genuine than all those fancy society misses faking artless little giggles—looking at you, Blanche, who acts like she’s never held a bottle of champagne until no one’s watching. Who’d have guessed such a sweet little rose would bloom in a porter’s lodge, stuck in that grubby old flower pot of Shepherd’s Inn? Old Bows is teaching her to sing? If her singing voice is half as nice as her speaking voice, it’s a treat. I almost made her call me ‘sir’ the other night, what a total prat move. No tragic Faust and Margaret nonsense for me, thanks. That old Bows is a diamond in the rough, isn’t he? Wore those same raggedy old coats for years, had my back back when I was making a fool of myself over the Fotheringay, he’s got a million sorrowful little stories tucked away. I ought to make a point of hanging out with more different sorts of people, I’m sick to death of stuffy gentry parties. And Costigan! What a walking, talking caricature of an Irish captain, begad I’m shoving him straight into my next novel.”

It was Saturday, too—publication day for the Gazette, which meant Pen had zero deadlines, zero editors pestering him for copy, nothing to do all afternoon. So he decided he’d head over to Shepherd’s Inn, obviously, to pay old Bows a visit. That was totally the whole point of the trip. Nothing else.

Funny enough, if Pen had been the sneakiest heartless rake in all of London, he couldn’t have picked a better way to win poor little Fanny over than the moves he pulled at Vauxhall the night before. His dandyish, kindly protectiveness, his good humor, the fact that he didn’t take advantage of her flustered little crush—both his good points and his silly flaws had her completely smitten. If we’d peeked into her tiny cupboard bed that night, the one she shared with her two little sisters, we’d have found her tossing and turning so much she kept kicking the two poor kids awake, replaying every single second of the night: his posh accent, his sparkly shirt studs, the way he poured her that glass of champagne like it was the most natural thing in the world. Fanny had devoured every crummy three-decker romance novel Miss Minifer lent out from the back of her millinery and brandy-ball shop, and Pen? He was every single dashing aristocrat hero rolled into one. She was already mentally lording it over Emma Baker, who wouldn’t stop bragging that she was engaged to a cheesemonger who wore a white apron all shift, and Betsy Rodgers, whose boyfriend the attorney’s clerk carried a beat-up leather bag everywhere like it was a royal scepter.

At two o’clock that afternoon, right after the Bolton family finished their dinner (Fanny’s dad, part-time inn porter and full-time undertaker’s assistant, was off driving the late Countess of Estrich’s hearse out to the countryside), a fancy gent in a crisp white hat and white trousers strolled under the inn arch and stopped at the porter’s wicket. Fanny didn’t even pretend to be surprised. She knew he’d come. She screamed for her mum, shoved her two little sisters out the way, and wrenched the wicket open, blushing so bright red she looked like she’d eaten a whole jar of pickled beetroot. Pen took off his hat all polite, asked how they’d slept after the chaos of the night before, said he couldn’t walk past the lodge without popping in to check on his little dance partner.

Mrs Bolton was side-eyeing him at first, like “what’s this fancy dandy doing hanging around my porter’s lodge?” but Pen’s charm was unstoppable. There wasn’t a single empty seat free—every chair had a dish cover or a sewing box perched on it—so he plonked himself down on a tiny wooden child’s chair built for a 5-year-old, and all the kids burst out cackling, Fanny loudest of all. The great, fancy Mr Pendennis, slumming it on a tot’s seat! She’d probably keep that chair on her mantelpiece for the next 40 years to fawn over, the silly thing.

Pen chatted a mile a minute, asking about the undertaker business, how many mutes showed up for the countess’s funeral, who lived in the inn, said he’d run into that Mr Campion with the fancy racehorse and cab at a dinner party once, joked he was thinking of buying shares in that Polwheedle and Tredyddlum mining scheme, maybe even move into Shepherd’s Inn full time instead of the boring stuffy Temple. Then he brought up Captain Strong and that weird Colonel Altamont, the bloke who wore so much jewellery he jingled when he walked, a wig so obvious you could spot it across a room, and dyed black whiskers so far gone they shimmered a funny blue-purple in the light. Mrs Bolton was so won over by this point she blabbed every secret: Sir Francis Clavering showed up two or three times a week to Strong’s rooms, her little boy ran out to buy him bill-stamps all the time, oops she wasn’t supposed to say that but he was clearly a gentleman, wasn’t he? Fanny nodded so hard her head nearly fell off, agreeing that of course he was the nicest, most proper gentleman that ever lived.

Once Pen had fished all the gossip on Clavering he’d been secretly after for his own reasons, he remembered his perks as a press man, and offered them free theatre tickets. The two women lost their minds. Fanny clapped so hard her hands turned pink. Their husband/father spent half his time getting drunk and grumpy about the undertaker work, they never got to go out anywhere. The only slight hiccup was when little Betsy-Jane piped up that she wanted to go too, Fanny snapped “don’t be a nuisance” and Mrs Bolton herded the two little tots off to play on the gravel around that dorky old statue of Shepherd the inn’s namesake.

Just as Pen was making both of them cackle at his silly jokes, an old man trundled under the inn arch, spotted Pen perched half on the wicket chatting up the two women, and pulled a face so rueful he looked like he’d just bit into a whole lemon. “Hey Bows!” Pen yelled, waving all cheery. “Perfect timing! I was just about to come visit you, asking these two where your rooms are!”

Old Bows trundled in, shook Pen’s hand, his face still as grumpy as a cat that just found out the whole house was out of milk. “Oh? You were coming to see me, were you?” he muttered.

Somebody in that little lodge room heaved a very quiet, very annoyed sigh. That somebody was Fanny, thinking, “Ugh, drat that old man, couldn’t he have waited half an hour to show up?”

Our man Pen spotted Mr. Bows loitering in the porter’s lodge doorway and yelped “How d’ye do, Mr. Bows!” so loud and cheery you’d think he hadn’t been caught red-handed sneaking off to Vauxhall the night before to gallivant with the lodge keeper’s pretty daughter. He slapped on a dashing, too-offhand grin to shake the old man’s hand, but that didn’t stop a bright cherry-red blush from creeping up his ears — which Fanny immediately mirrored, her cheeks flushing so fast you’d swear someone held a match to a ripe strawberry.
The air in the room immediately curdled into the kind of guilty, thick silence you get after you knock over your aunt’s best china and everyone’s pretending they didn’t see who did it. Pen scrambled to fix it, making such a racket knocking over a stack of dog-eared song sheets and a half-full mug of spruce beer that you’d think a whole team of street porters had stumbled through. The noise vanished quick enough, but the gloom only deepened, like you lit a single match in a damp coal cellar and all you got for your trouble was extra smoke.
Pen tried to do a funny impression of Captain Costigan yelling at the Vauxhall ticket-taker the night before, and it was terrible. No mortal man alive could replicate Costigan’s slurred, whiskey-warmed brogue anyway, and this attempt was so far off base Mrs. Bolton couldn’t tell if he was mocking the ticket-taker or the Captain. Fanny forced a giggle that squeaked like a rusted door hinge. Old Bows scowled so hard his eyebrows furrowed deep enough to grow turnips in, looking exactly as glum as he did mid-orchestra gig when some drunk in the pit yells out for a song he’s already played three times. Pen’s joke died a miserable, flickering death, his voice dwindling to a squeak before cutting out entirely. The whole room went so quiet you could count the clink of the passing inn porter’s boot heels on the flagstones under the archway, every single one.
“You said you were paying me a visit, sir,” Bows said, his voice flat as a week-old pancake. “Would you do me the honour of walking up to my chambers? They’re a bit high up, but —”
“Oh, I live in a garret myself!” Pen cut in, huffing. “Shepherd’s Inn is twice as cheery as my grim little Lamb Court digs!”
“Knew you had third-floor rooms,” Bows said, still deadpan. “Was just going to say — no offence intended — the air three flights up is far wholesomer for gentlemen than the air hanging round a porter’s lodge, isn’t it?”
Pen blew up like an offended hot air balloon, already spoiling for a fight the way only guilty men are. “Sir! Am I not allowed to choose my own company without —?”
“You were so kind as to say you’d come pay my humble hovel a call,” Bows said, his sad old voice not shifting an inch. “Shall I lead the way? Mr. Pendennis and I are old friends, Mrs. Bolton — knew each other when he was still in knee breeches. Crossed paths before he was even old enough to buy a penny lollipop.”
The old man pointed to the door with a trembling finger, hat in his other hand, striking a pose so dramatic it could’ve been pulled straight from the third-rate melodrama he’d played a banished duke in 40 years prior. He wasn’t faking it, though, Pen knew that — even if he wanted to roll his eyes at Bows’s over-the-top theatrical airs.
“Fine, fine, you win, let’s go,” Pen said, marching over to say goodbye. “Good day, Mrs Bolton! Goodbye, Miss Fanny — I’ll always remember our night at Vauxhall. Don’t you worry, those theatre tickets are good as yours already.” He squeezed her hand so hard he nearly crushed her fingers, and she squeezed back just as tight, and then he was out the door.
“What a nice young gentleman, isn’t he?” Mrs Bolton cooed, craning round the curtain to watch him trudge up the inn courtyard with Bows.
“D’you really think so, ma?” Fanny mumbled, staring at the floor.
“Reminds me of that city gentleman I knew back at Sadler’s Wells,” Mrs Bolton babbled, wiping mugs and tucking them into the corner cupboard. “Used to drive up in a little tilbury with a white top hat, looked just like him — only the fellow’s whiskers were black, not that pretty red shade Mr. P. has. He was head over heels for Emly Budd, the dancer who played Columbine that one summer, ran off and married her in a secret ceremony at St. Bartholomew’s, and now she keeps her own carriage! Saw her name in the paper last month, patroness of the Washywomen’s Asylum Ball, fancy as anything. And you know Lady Mirabel — Captain Costigan’s daughter — she was professional once too, and look how far she got!”
Fanny barely heard half of her ma’s chattering. Costigan, it turned out, had forgotten all the old details of Pen’s modest income he’d learned back at Chatteris years prior, and had rambled to Mrs Bolton the night before about Fairoaks Park’s endless acres, Pen’s wealthy Major uncle, and all the land and money Arthur supposedly had stacked up in bank accounts. By the time they finished folding the dinner cloth together, Mrs Bolton was already daydreaming of Fanny riding in her own private carriage, no more porter’s lodge chores, no more mopping the step at 6 a.m. She tapped Fanny under the chin and kissed her, and Fanny blushed so hard her whole face looked dipped in strawberry jam — this time not from embarrassment, but from pure, giddy hope.
Mrs Bolton popped on her bonnet and grabbed her market basket, saying she had errands to run, and told Fanny to mind the lodge. The second her ma was out the door, Fanny glued herself to the window that faced Bows’s front step, not tearing her eyes away for a second. Her two little sisters Betsy-Jane and Ameliar-Ann were making up nonsense stories about fire and brimstone from a very boring religious tract they held upside down in the corner, and Fanny didn’t hear a single word they said.
At last she gave a little jolt, her face lighting up: Pen was coming out! But her smile died instantly. Bows was right on his heels, trailing him like a grumpy little shadow. Pen tipped his hat and gave one tiny nod in through the window, but he didn’t stop, he didn’t hang around, he trundled right under the archway and out of sight.
Four minutes later (or four hours, it felt like to Fanny) Bows trundled back alone. “Where’s your ma, dear?” he asked.
“Search me!” Fanny huffed, tossing her head, her needle stabbing at her embroidery like she was poking every last one of Bows’s annoying interfering habits. “I don’t follow her round the street every time she steps out, do I?”
“Am I my mother’s keeper, eh?” Bows said, that familiar sad, dry edge to his voice. “C’mere you two, Betsy-Jane and Ameliar-Ann — got a gingerbread cake for whoever can read their letters best, and another one for the runner-up.” The little girls scampered off cackling with their cakes after their A-B-C test, leaving Fanny and Bows alone in the lodge. Bows sat where he could see the courtyard entrance, but the only person who walked through the door was Mrs Bolton, coming home from market with a basket of turnips and mutton, no sign of Pen anywhere.
Up in their shared third-floor rooms, the visit between Pen and Bows was far from fun — mostly because Captain Costigan was camped out in the middle of the parlour the whole time, having only just stumbled out of his bedroom ten minutes before Pen arrived. The man reeked so strongly of whiskey-scented cologne you’d think he’d dunked his whole head in a still, and his hands shook so violently it was a miracle he hadn’t sliced his own throat shaving that morning.
While Costigan’s half of the room was a disaster zone of crumpled playbills and empty liquor bottles, Bows’s side was neat as a pin: his threadbare wardrobe hung behind a curtain, his books and sheet music stacked perfectly on his shelves, and a tattered old lithograph of Emily Fotheringay in her role as Mrs Haller hung over his bed, her messy, girlish signature scrawled in the corner. Bows had taught Emily to sing and write once, and when she married the rich baronet and left him behind, he’d poured all that quiet old affection into teaching Fanny instead — the equivalent of a sailor clinging to a raft after a shipwreck, or a man who loses his whole dog and adopts three very spoiled tabby cats. He was heartily unimpressed to see Pen back, sniffing around his new little protégée.
Costigan, for his part, was completely oblivious that he was crashing a tense private talk, gabbing Pen’s ear off for an hour about how he was invited to join Sir Charles and Lady Mirabel at Baden-Baden, how the Grand Duke would roll out the red carpet for him, how he’d been a guest of honour at Dublin Castle back in the viceroy’s heyday. For a minute Pen got all soft and nostalgic, remembering the days back in Chatteris when he was a starstruck teen who’d eaten up every one of Costigan’s wild stories, and he shook both their hands warmly when he left, completely forgetting Fanny Bolton existed for ten whole minutes.
Then he spotted Bows hobbling after him down the stairs, determined to follow him all the way out of Shepherd’s Inn to make sure he didn’t sneak back to the lodge. Pen huffed at first, annoyed that the old fellow was acting like his chaperone, and then burst out laughing on the Strand — a guilty, embarrassed little laugh that made passersby glance at him like he’d just stolen a pork pie from the corner bakery. He blushed so hard he had to turn his face away.
He tried every trick in the book to shake that weird, fuzzy guilty feeling: he panted all the way up the steep stairs to the top of the Panorama, but Care huffed right up behind him, no matter how fast he climbed. He went to his club, wrote a snarky, witty long letter home to his mother and Laura, and didn’t breathe a single word about Vauxhall or Fanny, knowing they’d tease him for being a hopeless fool. He tried to read a novel, ducked away from the unbearably dull Jawkins who wanted to rant about sheep farming for three whole hours, and wandered home past a comic theatre plastered with bright red signs screaming NONSTOP ROARS! STUNNING FARCE! GOOD OLD ENGLISH FUN! He ducked into the pit to watch, but the usual gags — the pretty actress in men’s tights, the funny old actor dressed up as a cackling crone — all felt flat and dull, not a single laugh coming out. He remembered how he’d thought that same show was the funniest thing on the whole planet his first week in London, and it felt like that had been a hundred years ago, not a handful of months.

Fashion bailed on pretty old Temple Gardens so long ago, even the toughest, most weed-resistant rose that Shakespeare’s York and Lancaster plucked for their silly civil war badges would take one whiff of the London smog and refuse to grow a single bud, as every stuffy old Handbook of London will gleefully confirm. Most of the lawyers and clerks milling about the surrounding buildings don’t know or care a fig if roses ever bloomed there, they only use the old gate as a shortcut to their chambers. The solicitors’ runners don’t tuck posies under their arms while they sprint to counsel’s offices, and the barristers who take constitutional walks are far too busy panicking over railway merger paperwork to spare a single thought for the Wars of the Roses. The only folks who get starry-eyed over the gardens are dusty antiquarians and literary nerds, who like to daydream about Sir Roger de Coverley waddling down the path, the Spectator squinting like he’s got a bad case of hayfever, or Oliver Goldsmith camped out in the summerhouse toggling between three equally urgent thoughts: finishing his next *Citizen of the World* essay, stressing that his new suit from tailor Mr. Filby won’t have lumpy shoulders, and avoiding the dunning letter his publisher Mr. Newbery sent that says he’s three quid behind on rent.

Next thing you know, Dr. Johnson comes trundling along the gravel in a snuff-stained suit and a wig that looks like a sparrow’s nest, his Scottish valet trailing three sheets to the wind on port they drank at the Mitre Tavern, yelling that Goldsmith has to come over for tea because Miss Williams already burnt the muffins twice. Those old literary ghosts feel more real than half the living people hanging around Temple, and Arthur Pendennis, being a big old romantic literary nerd who couldn’t care less about the boring legal work everyone else in the quarter does, was definitely indulging in exactly those daft daydreams on the Sunday evening right after the chaos of the last chapter.

Sunday at the Temple is dead quiet, especially during vacation. All the fancy top-tier lawyers are off at dinner parties in Belgravia swilling claret so good it makes their toes tingle, the charming young barristers are tagging along to those parties to flirt with the judge’s accomplished daughters, and the poor sods who didn’t get an invite are camped out at the local pub splitting a sad overcooked roast joint and a half-pint of bitter, telling circuit jokes that no one else in the common room finds even slightly funny. The only people left in chambers are sick Mr. Cockle whose laundress is making him gruel that tastes like wallpaper paste, Mr. Toodle on the second floor who murders *Rule Britannia* on his flute at 9pm sharp every Sunday, and law student Tiger whose windows billow cigar smoke, with a stack of half-eaten chippy plates from Dick’s or the Cock stacked by his door. That’s it, no one else is around.

Pen, who’d dined alone at a tavern down the road, decided to pop into the gardens for a bit, enjoy the fresh air, and stare at the Thames which was surprisingly not murky that evening. After wandering for ten minutes he plopped down in one of the summerhouses flanking the main path, and got all sappy: the sky was cloudless, the chimneys on the south bank weren’t smoking at all, the church bells were ringing for evening service, and he found himself thinking of his mom, back home, walking arm-in-arm with him on their lawn terrace at sunset. He shook himself out of the mope, thumped his boot on the bench and announced out loud “Nah, that’s enough moping. I’m going home.”

He stepped straight out of the summerhouse and nearly flattened two tiny toddlers, who barely reached his knee, trotting along the gravel with their long blue shadows stretching east. One yelped “Oh!” and the other cackled like a tiny gremlin and yelled “Missa Pendennis!” It was none other than his new little friends, Ameliar-Ann and Betsy-Jane. Pen blushed bright red, grabbed the one he’d nearly tripped over, tossed her in the air and kissed her, which made Ameliar-Ann shriek like she was being kidnapped by a circus bear.

That shriek immediately summoned two ladies all dolled up for their Sunday walk: Mrs. Bolton wrapped in a fancy scarlet cashmere shawl, wearing her best black silk dress, and Fanny Bolton in a sweet sprigged muslin dress, yellow scarf, and a frilly parasol, looking every inch the proper lady. Fanny didn’t say a single word, but her eyes shone brighter than the blazing gas lamps on Paper Buildings. “Lor sir!” Mrs. Bolton gasped. “How ever did we all end up here? Ain’t that the most unplanned, extraordinary coincidence ever, Fanny?”

And of course it was a coincidence! You’ve all had those, right? When you’re strolling the country house shrubbery with your friend and the rich young heir you’ve been gushing over just happens to be there? When you’re loitering on Brighton pier listening to the band and the two fancy captains you fancied just clink past you? Pen had only mentioned in passing the day before that Temple Gardens was the nicest spot for a Sunday stroll, and the place was full of perfectly respectable people! Nothing suspicious at all, 100% artless good fun.

Pen was extra fancy that day, dressed in white duck trousers, a crisp white hat, a bright multi-colored neckcloth, gold chains dangling off his waistcoat, and fancy shirt studs, looking like he thought he was at least a minor royal prince. Fanny’s heart eyes were so obvious you could spot them from the other side of the river. The two little girls had played “be Mr. Pendennis” all night the day before, Ameliar-Ann shoving her chubby hands through the armholes of her pinafore to copy Pen’s habit of adjusting his waistcoat, and Fanny had laughed so hard she cried. Fanny looked pale and exhausted, and when Pen asked why, she blushed and mumbled “I was up all night.” “I told her to blow that candle out and go to sleep!” her mom interjected. “But she wouldn’t listen, not for love nor money.”

“Reading what, that was so interesting?” Pen asked, amused.
“Oh it’s the most beautiful book ever written!” Fanny sighed. “Walter Lorraine! I hate that stupid Neaera so much, and I love Leonora, and Walter, oh he’s the sweetest man who ever lived!”

Turns out Fanny had spent all night working it out that Pen was the author: she’d cozied up to grumpy old Captain Costigan, helped him mend his shirts that had holes big enough to stick your whole head through, mixed him his perfect whisky tumbler, got him to sing a wobbly old Irish ballad, then popped the question about what book Arthur had written before he could launch into a second encore. She ran straight to every library she could find, but they were all out of copies or wouldn’t lend one without a shilling. She ran home sobbing, her mom handed over the shilling, and she’d sprinted back to the library terrified someone would check the last copy out before she got there.

Pen was so flattered his chest felt like it was going to burst. “Oh you sweet little thing!” he babbled. “I’ll get you a copy of my own, wait no—no, I can’t, I really have to go, good night, thank you Fanny, god bless you!” He squeezed her hand once, nodded at her mom, and bolted out of the garden, muttering to himself all the way to the gate: “Dear little thing, she’s worth all the stuck-up fancy people I hang around. I need to get home to my mom. I won’t stay, I won’t let this go wrong, I swear—”

He was running so fast he barrelled straight into a tiny old man: it was Mr. Bows, the fiddler. “Your very humble servant, sir,” Bows said, doing the most sarcastic little bow you’ve ever seen, tipping his ratty old hat. “Off somewhere very urgent?”

“Good evening!” Pen snapped, grumpy. “Quit following me around spying, I’m in a huge hurry.”
“Spying nothing,” Bows said, frowning. “Where are the Boltons? They said they were going to Temple Church, they weren’t there. You haven’t hidden them in your chambers, have you? That’d be a right disaster.”

“For crying out loud!” Pen yelled. “Come up to my rooms and search the whole place if you don’t believe me! The court’s right here, the door’s unlocked, come on in.”

Bows followed him up three flights of creaky stairs. Pen’s chambers were as scruffy as ever: Warrington’s beat-up old bookcase full of tattered books, Pen’s writing desk stacked with half-finished essays, not a single Fanny, toddler, or Bolton in sight. “You wanna check under the beds?” Pen said, bitter. “Dig through the coal cellar? Did I hide the kids in the coal scuttle for fun?”

“Your word’s good enough for me,” Bows said, his voice soft and sad. “You say they’re not here, and I know they’re not. I just hope they never are here, ever.”

Pen got huffy immediately. “Who do you think you are, picking my friends for me? I’ve always been kind to you, I don’t deserve to be followed around like I’m a common thief.”

Bows sighed, all the anger draining out of him. He was a lonely old man, he said, all his life he’d wanted to write plays and music and be somebody, but no one ever gave him a chance. No one cared about the deformed old fiddler, playing tunes in alehouses for pennies. Fanny was the only thing he had, he’d taught her everything, and he didn’t want some fancy young dandy to break her heart, toy with her for fun, then toss her aside when he got bored.

Pen softened up immediately. “You’ve got me all wrong,” he said, gentle. “I swear I never meant to hurt her. I was literally running away just now, I didn’t even realize she had such a big crush on me until tonight. I was thinking about my mom back home, all the kind, good women I grew up around, and I was hurrying home to avoid doing anything stupid that I’d regret forever.”

Bows stared at him for a second, then his face relaxed. He apologized for being so hostile, walked over and shook Pen’s hand, the two of them making up like the old friends they’d once been. “I believe you,” Bows said, as he trundled slowly down the stairs. “I’ll pray you keep that promise, young man.”

“Amen to that,” Pen called after him, closing the door behind the old fiddler.

Way back near the start of this chaotic little story, we rambled about Clavering — that drowsy market town a stone’s throw from Pen’s childhood home Fairoaks — and its extremely unimpressive roster of local personalities. The place’s tea-party society was so deathly dull you could use a stack of their calling cards as tinder, so we never bothered droning on about it for long.

Readers will remember Mr. Samuel Huxter, the over-caffeinated medical student we bumped into at Vauxhall a few chapters back. Sam was Clavering’s self-appointed “London sophisticate” every time he came home for vacation, holding court at every dinner table to share the very select gossip of Bartholomew’s Hospital and the seedy Covent Garden music halls he snuck off to, acting like he’d just had high tea with the Prime Minister instead of eating pickled eggs off a pub table at 2 a.m.

You’ll also recall Mr. Hobnell, the young man Pen thrashed mid-Fotheringay drama? Back when he was a grammar school pupil in Clavering, he was a permanent fixture at Mrs. Huxter’s (Sam’s mum) tea table, snuck into the surgery to raid the tamarind sweet jars, and doused his handkerchief in rosewater so heavily he made the apothecary’s cat sneeze for three hours. Before long he fell hard for Sam’s sister Sophy, married her the second his old man kicked the bucket, and carted her off to The Warren, his family’s farm a few miles outside town.
