The Hobnells had been humble yeomen farmers for generations, but the late patriarch tore down their drafty old cottage, built a blindingly whitewashed new mansion with stables big enough for a racehorse and a piano nobody in the family knew how to play, bought a pack of hounds that couldn’t track a lost rabbit, and unilaterally declared himself “Squire Hobnell” no county vote required. Sam bragged about his brother-in-law’s fancy estate, his “elite” hounds, and his never-ending supply of home-brewed cider to all his wide-eyed med school mates back in London, and no one can blame him a bit. Every single year, right when Sophy was up to her elbows in nappies and couldn’t possibly leave the nursery, Hobnell trundled up to London for a lark, booked a room at the Tavistock, and he and Sam went gallivanting around the town. They hit Ascot, fell asleep mid-theatre performance, got thrown out of Vauxhall for singing too loud, and sampled every tavern within reeling distance of Covent Garden. As Hobnell himself put it, when he was in London he liked to “do as London does and go it a bit.” He’d come home bearing a bonnet two sizes too small for Sophy, then hang up his London party boots for 11 whole months of hunting and haymaking.

It was a very mutually beneficial arrangement between Sam and the Hobnells: Sam sent them all the hottest London nonsense gossip he could dig up, and in return they shipped him wicker baskets stuffed full of hares, partridges, and clouted cream so thick you could stand a spoon up in it. To the Hobnell household, Sam was a walking delight: he sang rude songs, pulled pranks on the vicar, kept the whole manor cackling for days, and even fished a stray fishbone out of their eldest toddler’s throat once — a feat they still bring up at every family dinner five years later. He was the undisputed favorite.

As rotten luck would have it, Pen ran right into Sam Huxter a mere three days after their messy Vauxhall run-in. Pen had been obeying his dumb, self-inflicted vow not to go see little Fanny, trying to scrub her from his brain by any means necessary. He slaved away in his tiny London rooms (accomplishing almost nothing), and in his role as critic for the *Pall Mall Gazette*, wrote so vicious a takedown of a newly published poem and a turgid romance that both authors probably cried into their porridge. Having metaphorically murdered two writers for the greater good, he dragged himself off to eat alone at the Polyanthus Club, a place so vast and empty the echo of his own chewing made him even more glum. He trundled off to a theatre after that to cheer up, where the whole audience was rolling in the aisles at a silly farce — but Pen’s face was so doleful the lead comic almost forgot his entire monologue just staring at him. The whole show blurred past him like a weird fever dream.

He decided to mosey over to the Back Kitchen, his old regular spot with Warrington, since he wasn’t the slightest bit sleepy. He’d already tramped 20 miles over Hampstead Common the day before trying to walk off his mood, and still hadn’t slept a wink. At least Bows, the quiet old piano man who ran the place, would be there. The pub was packed with people screaming off-color comic songs loud enough to rattle the windows, but Pen could only see Bows. He liked to brag his chest was an extinct volcano — so it was very annoying to find himself burning with this stupid, all-consuming crush after just two days of avoiding the object of it. He was chugging whisky and water one after another, lost in his own moping thoughts, when in walked Sam Huxter and a crew of his med school mates, fresh from the theatre.

Huxter immediately whispered to his friends, and Pen’s jaw tightened. Huxter weaseled his way through the crowd, plopped down right across from Pen, nodded like nothing had ever happened between them, and shoved a very grubby hand out to shake. Pen shook it. He even felt a little guilty for laying into the guy so hard at Vauxhall. Huxter, for his part, was so perfectly pleased with himself it never crossed his mind he could ever be rude to anyone, and he’d written off their Vauxhall scuffle as a silly bit of harmless “chaff” that wasn’t worth remembering.

Huxter called for four stouts for his crew, cast around for the most fun possible topic to bring up with old Pen, and landed straight on the single most hurtful thing he could possibly say.

“Jolly night at Vauxhall, wasn’t it?” he winked, extremely proud of himself. “Devilishly drunk that night, I was — dined with some blokes at Greenwich. That pretty little bit of muslin hanging off your arm — who was she, then?”

That was the last straw. “Did I ask you for any details about my personal life, Mr. Huxter?” Pen said, ice cold.

“Whoa, no offence, mate, sorry — hang on, you’re cutting up pretty savage,” Huxter spluttered, completely bewildered.

“Do you remember what we agreed on the other night?” Pen snapped, his temper rising. “You were too tipsy to remember, I suppose — you were extremely rude, and I very politely asked you to not treat me as an acquaintance from that point onward, and skip the public small talk. Do try to remember that for the future, won’t you? That new song’s starting, so I’ll leave you to enjoy it.”

He grabbed his hat, bowed to the completely stunned Huxter, and walked right out the door. Huxter’s friends burst out laughing so loud the pub host had to yell for silence, announcing “Quiet, gents! Show some respect for the Body Snatcher!” just as that notoriously gory ballad started up. Pen was weirdly proud he hadn’t fully lost his temper — but he also kind of wished Huxter had taken a swing at him, just so he’d have an excuse to punch something. He trundled home, still miserable, and slept just as badly as he had the night before.

A few days later, Sam Huxter sat down and wrote a very dramatic letter back to the Hobnells, and Arthur Pendennis was the star villain of the whole thing. Sam painted Pen as a full-blown reprobate Don Juan, a stuck-up humbug who was deliberately ruining the life of a poor innocent working-class girl he’d spotted with at Vauxhall. He claimed he’d found out exactly who the girl was from an ex-army bloke at his London club, and they all ought to warn the poor thing’s family to save her. At the bottom of the letter, he remembered to thank them for the last batch of rabbits they’d sent, and very unsubtly hinted that another consignment of that famous clouted cream would make him their grateful servant forever.

As luck would have it, the Hobnells were throwing a christening for their new baby girl the very day the letter arrived. They named the little mite Myra Lucretia, after her two godmothers: Clavering’s Miss Portman and Mrs. Pybus. Naturally, Mr. Hobnell shared Sam’s gossipy letter with his wife, who spilled every single lurid detail to the two godmothers right after the ceremony. By the end of the afternoon, the story had spread through Clavering faster than a cold at a village bake sale. Mrs. Pybus chattered about it to the Portmans, the Simcoes, and Mrs. Glanders — who immediately ordered all her teenage daughters out of the drawing room so they wouldn’t hear the sordid details. Madame Fribsby, the local dressmaker, stared at her framed portrait of her long-lost dragoon lover and sighed that all men are lying deceivers, quoting lines from *Marmion* dramatically while wondering where all those cheating lovers get to end up. Mrs. Pybus didn’t have words strong enough to call Pen a villain, ranting that this is exactly what happens when a boy gets uppity, refuses to drink tea with his perfectly respectable neighbours, and gallivants off to the den of sin that is London. The local Captain of Dragoons whistled and cracked a rude joke about the story over dinner, which earned him a sharp “You brute!” from his wife, who booted the girls out of the room for the second time that day. Mr. Simcoe, the local busybody magistrate, pretended to be calm and impartial — but he was secretly absolutely chuffed, because he’d hated Pen for years for no discernible reason, and this confirmed every dumb opinion he’d ever held. The story even got its own full Sunday evening sermon at the village chapel-of-ease, where the parson ranted for an hour about the evils of London, and the terrible sin of reading or writing novels, to a huge, very enthusiastic congregation. No one stopped to ask if the story was even true. Everyone just assumed Pen was a monster, and they were all racing to be the first to throw a stone at him.

The next day, poor Helen Pendennis ran all the way to Dr. Portman’s house, almost fainting with shock. She’d received an anonymous hate letter, sent by some “good Christian” who thought it was their duty to stab a kind, harmless old mother in the back, full of Bible verses about the fate of sinners and extremely lurid, specific details of Pen’s supposed crime. She was so distraught the letter slipped out of her hand, and Laura read the whole thing. Laura’s face burned, she trembled all over — but not with grief, with rage. “Cowards,” she spat. “It isn’t true. Not a word of it, mother.”

But Helen snapped, half out of her mind with terror. “It IS true, and it’s your fault, Laura!” she screamed. “You turned him down when he asked you to marry him! You broke my heart and refused him, so you’re the one who drove him straight into this other woman’s arms! Don’t talk to me, don’t argue. Martha, get me my bonnet and shawl. You’re not coming with me, Laura. Go away, cruel girl — this shame is all your fault.” She stormed out the door before anyone could stop her.

Dr. Portman glanced at the letter, immediately recognized Sam Huxter’s scrawly handwriting, and did his best to console Helen, pointing out that anonymous letters are always written by cowards and liars, that Pen deserved a chance to defend himself before everyone called him a villain, that a boy raised by such a kind mother could never do something so terrible. Helen saw right through his attempts to be reassuring. “You think he did it,” she wept. “I know you do. But if it *is* true? Then that girl will be my daughter. She’ll be welcome here. Pen will leave that awful London den, come home to me, and we’ll all forgive him. Let’s go get him right now — let’s leave today —”

She didn’t finish the sentence. She fainted dead away, and the doctor’s family carried her to a bed, called the local surgeon, and she lay unconscious all night, refusing to let Laura anywhere near her. Portman, now fully convinced Pen was innocent if only to calm the distraught mother, sat down to write an urgent letter to London, warning Pen about the wild rumours tearing through his hometown, and begging him to come home immediately, to fix this mess before it ruined his whole life.

And poor Laura? Left standing there, shunned by the woman she loved like a mother, the whole village calling the man she’d adored her whole life a monster, her heart ached so bad she could barely breathe. It felt like she’d just lost every bit of love that mattered to her in the whole world.

Doctor Portman posted his earnest, panicky letter off to London, then spent the rest of the afternoon patting Mrs. Pendennis’s hand and lying through his teeth that he was dead sure Pen’s so-called “wickedness” was just a silly, harmless misunderstanding. Helen was all set to hop a carriage for London the second she could stand, determined to march up and yell some sense into her wayward son personally, but the local apothecary nixed that plan entirely. He forbade her from traveling even the short distance to Fairoaks for the whole first day, and it wasn’t until the next morning that she was settled back on her own sofa, with quiet, faithful Laura hovering at her side to nurse her through the worst of her anxiety.

As luck would have it, Pen never even cracked that homily Portman wrote for him for weeks. Day after day, the widow waited for her son’s reply to the charges stacked against him, and every 24 hours that passed without a word made her fever and distress worse. Laura bore the brunt of the whole mess: she watched the woman she loved like a mother waste away with worry, dealt with the cold, sad distance Helen put between them in her grief, and never once complained. That girl was a pro at quiet duty, powered by the kind of gentle, unshowy prayers she said only in her own locked bedroom, far from any prying eyes. Those kinds of quiet, kind virtues don’t belong on public display any more than a delicate rose belongs bloomed right in the middle of a raucous ballroom. We’ll just say this much: a good woman is the loveliest bloom heaven ever grew, all soft grace, sweet fragrance, and quiet beauty. It’s a crying, unfair shame that grief, sickness, or cruel fate can mow those lovely things down in their prime, but apparently Heaven only puts its favorite souls through the wringer to polish them up that little bit more.

So that urgent letter from Portman sat ignored in Pen’s Lamb Court letterbox, dutifully dropped off by the postman, carried in by his laundress Mrs. Flanagan along with his rest of his post (and we all remember that same dear lady had previously been caught mid-swig of Pen’s hidden gin stash, so no one’s shocked his mail sorting was a total disaster).

All the readers who’ve followed Pen’s nonsense this far have probably already figured out his biggest, craftiest enemy, the rascal that’s been landing him in trouble since page one. Surprise! It’s Pen himself. Half of us have that exact same no-good scoundrel living rent-free in our own heads: a rogue who leads us into fights, idleness, bad company, and every kind of silly mess. Pen had spent his whole life coddling, coaxing, and spoiling that internal rascal, so naturally the guy turned into a spoiled, insolent servant who threw a full-on raging tantrum the second Pen tried to make him do anything unpleasant. People like Laura, who’ve spent years practicing giving up their own fun for others? That kind of self-denial is no sweat for them. But Pen? The boy had never said no to himself a single time in his life, so when he decided he was going to stop seeing Fanny to be responsible? It went about as well as telling a toddler they can’t have any more lollipops.

He tried everything to chase her out of his head! Worked 12 hour days, walked and rode 10+ miles at a stretch, drank more punch than a sailor on shore leave, smoked so many cigars his whole chambers reeked of tobacco for a week. None of it worked. Little Fanny’s face was stuck in his overheated brain no matter what he did. A week later, he was flat on his back in bed with a raging fever. If you’ve never been deathly sick all alone in tiny London bachelor chambers? Count yourself very lucky. Some kind soul ought to hire a cartoonist like Cruikshank or Leech to draw a whole series illustrating the horrors of bachelor living to scare every single unattached man straight down the aisle to get married immediately. The cold, smokeless bachelor breakfast, the dinner shirt you pull on only to find it has zero buttons, the empty room you walk into after a cozy country Christmas where the only thing waiting for you is a stack of Christmas bills spread out on your desk? That’s all bad enough when you’re healthy. But being sick in those tiny rooms, stuck tossing and turning through long sleepless nights, no one to hand you a sip of water when you’re parched, no one to fluff up your lumpy, crumpled hot pillow? That’s a special circle of hell no one should wish on their worst enemy.

That’s exactly the mess Pen landed in. He went to bed feeling off one night, woke up way worse the next day. The only visitor he got besides Mrs. Flanagan was the printer’s devil from the *Pall Mall Gazette*, who Pen dragged himself out of his fever fog to appease by writing half his usual quota of copy. Shandon was off partying peacefully in Boulogne, Warrington was out of town, so the paper’s political and editorial pages looked so empty you could drive a horse and cart right through them. Good-natured Irish sub-editor Finucane ran up to check on Pen, saw he was delirious, and decided to save the day. The next few issues of the paper were full of wildly effusive praise for Ireland, rants about how the Royal Academy was stupidly petty for refusing to make painter Rafferty an RA, rumors that Irish MP Rooney was about to be appointed governor of Barataria, and random unprompted odes to the Giant’s Causeway. Shandon got the forwarded paper in France, cackled, and tossed it to his wife Mary saying “Look love, Jack’s at it again, our readers are getting a very heavy dose of Irish patriotism this week.” Finucane even offered to skip his whole weekend holiday to nurse Pen, but Pen insisted he’d do far better toughing out his fever alone.

That Friday night, after the paper went to print, Finucane was chowing down at the Back Kitchen and mentioned Pen was sick to Captain Costigan. The Captain remembered the fact two days later, and trundled over to Lamb Court on Sunday afternoon to check on his young friend.

He found Mrs. Flanagan sobbing in the sitting room, half sloshed on brandy she’d chugged to cope with how terrible Pen looked. Pen had snapped at her to stop hovering over his bed a few times, which only made her cry harder and reach for the anodyne bottle even more. The Captain gave her a very firm talking-to about her intemperance before heading in to see Pen.

Pen was so feverish he lit up the second he heard Costigan’s familiar voice. He made the old man sit down, tossing and turning in his lumpy unmade bed, chattering about the Back Kitchen, Vauxhall trips they’d take soon, and asking over and over how little Fanny was doing.

Well, how was Fanny? She’d wandered home heartbroken the previous Sunday night, after watching Pen light his lamp in his chambers while he spoke to Bows. Bows trundled past her mom’s lodge a little later, looking as glum as a rain-soaked bank holiday. Fanny tossed and turned all night, disturbing the little kids she shared a room with over and over. She couldn’t sneak a reading session of her new Walter Lorraine book because her dad was home and wouldn’t let anyone light a candle, so she kept patting it under her pillow in the dark to make sure it was still there. She was up at dawn, and even though she was furious at Bows for what he’d said to Pen, she trundled to his room at her usual time to confront him.

“I saw Mr. Pendennis last night, Fanny,” Bows said gently.
“ I knew you did,” Fanny snapped, glaring daggers at the melancholy old musician.
“ I’ve cared about you ever since we moved here, you were a tiny little kid back then, you used to like me right up until you met this gentleman a few days ago,” Bows said.
“Oh I bet you’re about to start saying horrible things about him,” Fanny huffed. “Go on, it’ll make me like you less, which is impressive.”
“ I won’t say a bad word about him at all,” Bows told her. “He’s a very good, honest young man.”
Fanny clenched her tiny fist, paced up and down the room, cheeks flaming. “If you say one single mean thing about him I will never speak a word to you again ever! You want to trash talk him but you’re too scared, aren’t you?”
“I knew him years ago,” Bows went on, “back when he was almost as young as you are, and he was head over heels in love with Captain Costigan’s daughter, who’s Lady Mirabel now. He wanted to marry her, but they were totally wrong for each other, no money, totally different stations, it would’ve made them both miserable. Mr. Pendennis has his whole career to build, he has to marry a lady from his own world. No honest girl would want to ruin his prospects, make him fight with his family, drag him into poverty just to feed her own silly little crush.”

Fanny’s fiery anger melted straight into sobbing dismay. “What are you even talking about? No one ever said a word about marriage! We met at Vauxhall because the Captain took me and Ma there, he rescued us when we got separated, he was just being kind! We walked in the Temple Gardens once, that’s all! I wish I was dead, I wish I was buried right now, I never should’ve laid eyes on him!”
Bows told her Pen had said the exact same thing, explained that Pen could never possibly marry her, and that she owed it to her own good reputation to try to forget him. Fanny agreed to stay away from him, even though she was still head over heels in love, and ran straight home to tell her mom everything. She rambled about how their story was just like the Lady of Lyons, why was everyone allowed to cheer for Pauline staying faithful to poor working Claude, but a girl like her couldn’t even get to be a servant to Pen’s mom out in the country, just so she could catch a tiny glimpse of him once in a while?

Her mom stroked her hair and told her to stop thinking about him, then immediately turned around and assured her he’d come crawling back soon, just like Emily Budd’s old beau did, the one Emily’s dad the cellist chased off before they ended up happily married. Then surly Mr. Bolton, the undertaker’s assistant, trundled in for his supper, and Fanny wiped her tears in two seconds flat, sat demurely in the corner with her needlework, and the poor man never suspected a single thing was wrong.

Everyone around Fanny was accidentally making her crush worse! Her mom encouraged it, Bows’s whole “Pen is too noble to ruin your life” speech just made him sound even more like a dreamy tragic poet hero, with his pretty scented auburn hair and grand gentleman manners. Every respectable grown-up would tell you Pen was 100% right to nip that romance in the bud: marrying a poorly educated working class girl would wreck his whole career, right? Everyone says no one ever dies of a broken heart, you just get over it, move on, pick someone more suitable. But maybe there’s something to be said for that dumb, all-consuming love that makes you willing to throw all your sensible worldly plans out the window for someone? We’ll leave that debate up to the armchair moral philosophers to fight over later.

Pen, being the snob he was at this point in his life, had zero intention of ever marrying a penniless working girl anyway. He thought he was doing the noble, honest thing by crushing his little crush for both their sakes. But Fanny sat and waited for him day after day, week after week, sure he’d come visit. That’s when Costigan mentioned to her that poor Pen was stuck in his chambers deathly ill with a fever.

That same night, Pen’s uncle the Major rolled back into town from Buxton, all cured of his aches, and sent his valet Morgan over to Lamb Court to invite Pen to breakfast, before they both headed off to Lord Steyne’s country estate to shoot partridges. Morgan came back white as a sheet. “Sir, Mr. Arthur’s in bed with a raging fever, he’s very bad, he hasn’t even called a doctor yet!” The Major panicked. He couldn’t skip the trip to Lord Steyne’s, that’d be social career suicide! Also, he never had the measles, what if Pen’s fever was measles? That’s deadly for grown men! He demanded to know if anyone was looking after Pen, and Morgan said yeah, someone was trying to nurse him, but he still hadn’t gotten any proper medical care.

The second Fanny laid eyes on the two ladies at the door, the younger one’s face set like a block of carved marble and the elder’s eyes wild with a look of someone who’s just found a rogue mouse eating her prized jam tarts, she knew exactly who she was looking at: Pen’s mother. The widow’s haggard, harried eyes were the spitting image of Arthur’s, the same glassy, fever-bright look he’d had thrashing around in his sickbed, yelling nonsensical college ditties at the ceiling. Fanny blinked back tears, darting a desperate pleading look past them to the Major hovering behind their skirts. Old Pendennis peeked out from under lowered lids like a mischievous old sparrow sizing up a stray crumb, not about to interfere with the maternal freight train barrelling through the hallway.

“I— I wrote you yesterday, ma’am,” Fanny stammered, shaking so hard the tray of lemonade in her hands rattled, pale as the stone-faced Laura peering over Mrs. Pendennis’s shoulder.

“Did you, madam?” Mrs. Pendennis’s voice was sharp as a pin. “You may stand down from nursing my son now. I am his mother, you understand.”

“Yes, ma’am, only— wait a second!” Fanny yelped, skittering to block her path for half a second. “I have to warn you first, he’s—”

The widow, who’d marched in looking ready for a showdown, gasped so loud she nearly choked, stumbling back a step. A full-throated shriek of delirious laughter boomed out of Pen’s open bedroom door, followed by a yell, then a slurred, off-key rendition of the worst drinking song he’d learned at Camford, him thumping the wainscot so hard the laundress’s teacup rattled in the kitchen below. The man was three sheets to the wind on fever fumes, had no idea where he was half the time.

“He doesn’t recognize anyone right now, ma’am,” Fanny whispered.

“Then he may recognize his own mother. Step aside, please.” Mrs. Pendennis shoved past her, determined as a steam engine, Laura sailing right after her without so much as a “thank you for staying up three nights to save my love” kind word, the Major trailing in their wake like a stray pup. Fanny sank onto the hard bench in the passageway and cried, so hard her bonnet lopsided, praying no one would throw her out entirely. She’d have happily died for that daft boy, and these fine ladies treated her like a stray cat that’d wandered in off the street. She sat there for what felt like three whole lifetimes, no one coming out to check on her, until Dr. Goodenough turned up for his second visit of the day, nearly tripping over her huddled on the doormat.

“Why, little nurse! How’s our patient? Got any rest into him?” the kind man asked, patting her shoulder.

“Go ask the people who stole my job,” Fanny mumbled, nodding at the closed bedroom door.

“Off to bed with you, my poor girl,” he said. “You’ll end up in a fever yourself next, mark my words.”

“Oh no, please, don’t send me away! I love him so,” Fanny wailed, dropping to her knees and clinging to his hand so tight her knuckles went white. The doctor’s spectacles fogged up entirely, he was that moved, he nearly stepped on her own boot lace.

“Nonsense, you silly thing,” he said, swiping a tear off his cheek under the pretense of adjusting his glasses. “Of course you can see him. But first, I’ve got jobs for you.”

Mrs. Pendennis meanwhile was settled like a sentry in the biggest armchair next to Pen’s bed, her watch sat on the table next to his medicine bottles, her Bible open in her lap (she never travelled an inch without it). The very first thing she’d done when she walked in was grab Fanny’s bonnet and shawl off the dresser, fling both out into the study like they were lumps of toxic waste, lock the bedroom door behind her, and stake a claim on her sick son that no one was getting past. She’d been terrified he wouldn’t know her, but the delirious Pen spotted her and beamed, waved, and rambled on like they were both back home at Fairoaks having a picnic on the lawn. Laura could hear him laughing out in the hall, and every cackle felt like a little stab to the heart— so it was true, then, he’d gotten tangled up with this servant girl, the man she’d half planned to marry was raving through his fever and probably going to die any minute, unrepentant as could be.

Everyone in that little flat was so tense the air could’ve been sliced with a butter knife, until Dr. Goodenough walked in like a ray of very practical, lancet-carrying sunshine. There’s no one in the world people cling to harder than the family doctor when someone’s deathly ill. We’ve all held our breath waiting for his carriage wheels to clatter down the street, hung on every single one of his words, nearly kissed his boots if he dared smile and say the patient might pull through. A good doctor gets treated like a temporary god of life and death, and no one dares argue with him when he says it’s time for a bleed.

After quick pleasantries with the new arrivals, he examined Pen, confirmed the fever was spiking dangerously high, told the ashen-faced mother there was no reason to panic just yet, the lad was young and strong enough to pull through, then dragged the Major off into Warrington’s empty bedroom for a private chat. The case was critical: if they didn’t bring the fever down fast, Pen would be dead before sundown. “And what about that other young woman loitering in the passage?” the Major asked, winking. “The little nurse girl. Got a glimpse of her on the way in, begad, she was uncommonly pretty.” The doctor snickered, even in the shadow of death the silliest little jokes still pop up to lighten the gloom for two seconds. He had a plan.

He headed back out to the study, scribbled two quick notes, grabbed Fanny’s abandoned bonnet and shawl, and found her still on that bench in the passage. “Quick, run this note to the surgeon on the Strand, tell him to come at once with his lancet, then pop round to my Hanover Square house, give the other note to my man Harbottle, wait there for the prescription to be made up. It’ll take him a while to mix, don’t rush.”

Poor Fanny trundled off, none the wiser, and Dr. Goodenough made sure no one would ever let her set foot back in Pen’s chambers as a nurse, what with Mrs. Pendennis ready to chase her down the street with a broom. For three days after that, you could spot her little sad face hovering around the staircase, buttonholing the apothecary’s boy, the laundress, even the doctor himself every time he left Pen’s rooms to beg for updates. On the third day, Goodenough’s fancy carriage pulled up right outside Shepherd’s Inn, and he trundled straight up to Costigan’s little garret where Fanny was lying sick in bed from all her worry, and told her the fever had broken, Pen was going to live.

Captain Costigan spotted the fancy green-liveried carriage from his window, tipped his hat so hard he nearly knocked it off his head. “Foine pair of horses that is, bedad!” he yelled at old Mr. Bows next to him. “That’s the good docthor that saved the little girl’s life, he is! I’ll salute that carriage every time I see it from now on like he’s the Lord Lieutenant himself, I will.”

Mrs. Pendennis’s gratitude to Dr. Goodenough knew no bounds. He laughed off every attempt to pay him a fee, he wouldn’t take a penny from the widow of a fellow medical man, so she decided she’d send him the pride of the Fairoaks household: a silly massive silver-gilt vase, covered in carvings of Hippocrates, Hygeia, King Bladud and a whole wreath of snakes, presented to her late husband back in Bath by Lady Elizabeth Firebrace when her son recovered from scarlet fever. It was the ugliest, most over-the-top piece of Victorian art ever made, and she thought it was worth more than the crown jewels. The only favour he asked she refused to grant: to think a single kind, charitable thought about poor Fanny. The second Goodenough even mentioned the girl’s name, Mrs. Pendennis’s sweet soft face went so cold you could chill a whole bottle of claret on it. There’s no medicine on earth, no poppy syrup, no mesmerism, no fancy new hydropathy cure, that can fix that particular streak of maternal rivalry in a lady once it’s set in, and Goodenough gave up asking before long.

People who nitpick novels and ask how on earth a tiny three-room Temple bachelor flat could fit Pen, his mom, Laura, the country maid Martha, a hospital nurse, the laundress, the Major, his valet, Pen’s errand boy and about six other hangers on need not worry their pretty little heads. Everyone in the whole of the Temple was out of town for the summer, and the second floor chambers belonging to Percy Sibwright, that foppish Camford graduate who collected shiny boots and French romance novels, were entirely empty. The laundress who worked for Sibwright was pals with Pen’s laundress, so they broke in and claimed Sibwright’s little brass chintz-curtained bedroom for Laura, who would have been the most honoured guest Sibwright could ever have dreamed of. That silly dandy had a whole museum of pomade and bear’s grease pots, a wall covered in sad pictures of fancy fictional ladies pining away, and his shelves were full of unread law books and very very read French novels. Years later, Laura would blush and laugh and admit to her husband that while she was staying there, she’d dug Percy’s legal wig out of its japanned box, plopped it on her head, and preened at his mirror for a full minute. If Percy had walked in then? He’d have written twelve sonnets on the spot, proposed that very evening, and had the wig framed as a holy relic in his drawing room. You don’t do that sort of silly prank if the person you’re caring for is at death’s door, after all— which means Pen was well on the mend.

Sure enough, the fever broke for good a day or two later. The blisters, the lancet, the bitter potions all did their work, and Pen woke up after a sixteen hour long sleep and yelled for a slice of bread and butter, so hungry he’d have fought a street urchin for a crumb. The joys of convalescent hunger are the best joys there are, no one over thirty gets that feral ravenous feeling ever again, alas. No sooner had the happy news spread than Warrington showed up, tramping all the way back from Norfolk after getting Bows’s letter about his friend’s illness, carpet bag in one hand, cigar in the other, smoke billowing out behind him like a little steam train. He let himself in with his old key, and found the Major half dozing in an armchair, Laura reading him Shakespeare in that soft sweet voice of hers. Warrington was so flustered he dropped his cigar on the doormat (it singed the edge, no one noticed), dropped his hat too, pumped the Major’s hand until his arm ached, then took Laura’s hand and stared at her like he’d just spotted an angel in the middle of his messy bachelor flat.

Just then, Pen’s bedroom door creaked open very quietly, Mrs. Pendennis poked her head out, put one finger to her lips, and shushed them all, like they were a bunch of rowdy schoolboys disturbing her convalescent prince.

CHAPTER LIV. Convalescence (or, The Fever That Cured Pen Of Falling For Porters’ Daughters)

Look, we’ve got a deeply embarrassing fact about our boy Arthur Pendennis to spill here, and no, we can’t skip it—you signed up for his unvarnished memoirs, you get the messy bits too. There he was, flat on his back with raging fever, half delirious and head over heels mooning over Fanny Bolton, the porter’s daughter. The doctors bleed him, blister him, shave half his pretty dandy curls off, pump him full of every vile tonic in the apothecary’s cupboard… and lo and behold, when the physical fever broke, the love fever vanished too. Pen woke up one morning, stared at the ceiling, and realized he was no more smitten with Fanny than you or I are smitten with the guy who sells us morning sausages.

He snickered into his pillow at that second unexpected cure, and immediately did his usual weird little self-autopsy, poking at his dead passion like a kid dissecting a road beetle. What on earth had possessed him to be so hot and bothered over her? It wasn’t her wit, she couldn’t tell a sonnet from a shopping list. It wasn’t her breeding, she’d grown up in a porter’s lodge by the Temple gate. It wasn’t even her looks—half the girls selling flowers on the Strand were prettier. The whole silly passion had grown out of Pen’s own overheated brain, not out of anything special about Fanny. She was exactly the same nice, plain little thing she’d always been. His fever-addled eyes had just seen a goddess where there was a perfectly ordinary girl, and now his eyes were back to normal. He felt nothing but gentle goodwill towards her, same as he’d feel towards a stray cat that wandered into his rooms, and he sure as hell didn’t want to see her again. The best part? He hadn’t done anything terribly stupid when he was out of his mind with that misplaced passion, so he didn’t even have to torture himself with guilt. It was like he’d wandered right up to the edge of a cliff when he was delirious, then woken up safe in bed, and he was half relieved, half mortified that he’d ever nearly fallen off.

But while Pen was busy patting himself on the back for escaping the silliest fling of his life, his mom Helen was at his bedside every second, radiating soft, tired love that made Pen feel as small and safe as he’d been when he was seven years old and scraped his knee at Fairoaks. She never left his side, ready to fetch him any silly little thing he asked for at 3 a.m., as if tending to his every whim was the only joy she needed in the whole world.

Pen had misty, half-forgotten snippets of memory of Fanny being there to nurse him through the worst of the delirium, but they were so blurry he couldn’t tell them apart from the weird fever dreams where he’d ridden a giant hedgehog to the moon. Since he’d never bothered to mention his little Fanny crush to his mom before, obviously he couldn’t sit her down now and explain “hey mom, that girl you saw tending me when I was sick? I was wild for her two weeks ago and now I don’t care, no big deal.” That tiny little lack of quick chat? It landed everyone involved in weeks of total unnecessary pain. Helen had walked in, seen Fanny tending her feverish boy, and immediately jumped to the absolute worst possible conclusion. The poor woman was convinced Pen had done something terrible and dishonorable to the girl, that his physical fever was nothing compared to the moral stain he’d come home with. She bit down on that fear every single day, forcing a bright smile while her heart ached, because she couldn’t bear to confront him about it when he was still too weak to sit up.

Over in Boulogne, Captain Shandon was reading the latest issue of the *Pall Mall Gazette* with his morning brandy, and snickered to his wife Mary that Jack Finucane’s goofy, soft-handed editor bits were gone. “That’s Warrington’s work now, mark my words. I know that man’s whip crack anywhere. Bludyer writes like a butcher with a cleaver, hacking the subject to pieces. Warrington? He slices a man neat, straight down the back, draws blood every single line he writes.” Mary Shandon swatted his arm and told him not to be so gruesome, that Warrington was a perfect gentleman who’d given their kids sugar lumps last time he was in town. Shandon shrugged, poured himself another drink, and said Warrington was kind to kids but a savage to anyone dumb enough to argue with him, and that they’d all be back slogging away at newspaper work soon enough, much to his annoyance.

Sure enough, Warrington had stepped in to cover every single one of Pen’s newspaper shifts, working like a man possessed. He wrote theatre reviews, literary takedowns, snarky little society notes, all with that signature sharp, brutal wit of his. But whenever he stopped by to check on Pen’s family, he wouldn’t shut up about how graceful and light and elegant Pen’s writing was, the sweetest over-praise any friend could ever hand out. “The country’s full of numbskulls who don’t know what good style is,” he told Helen, “I bred that boy’s writing myself, and I’m as proud of him as you are. Yeah, he’s a little vain, a little selfish, spends way too much on silk cravats, but he’s honest, he’s loyal, he wouldn’t hurt a fly.” Helen sighed deep, Laura’s chest felt tight with quiet affection, and both of them loved Warrington even more for loving their Pen.

Even Major Pendennis, who never gushed about anyone unless they were a member of the aristocracy, was singing Warrington’s praises at the top of his lungs. “Every inch a gentleman, my dear! Suffolk Warringtons, baronets back to Charles the First! His old dad was Sir Miles, friend of the Prince of Wales, couldn’t turn out anything less than perfect. That man’s got talent enough to climb straight to the top of everything, if he ever bothered to put his mind to it.”

Laura blushed bright pink when he said that, because she’d spent the last three days staring at Warrington’s dark, brooding eyes and decided on no evidence whatsoever that he’d been left at the altar by a cruel heiress and was now doomed to be a tragic lonely hero forever. She caught herself daydreaming about his sad secret backstory and immediately felt her cheeks heat up so hot she could have fried an egg on them.

Warrington rented cheap chambers right around the corner in Flag Court, so after he finished bashing out three newspaper articles before noon, he’d wander over to Pen’s place for the sunny autumn evenings, and more than once he offered Laura his arm for a stroll in Temple Gardens. When Laura asked Helen if that was all right, the Major practically bounced out of his chair. “Of course it’s all right! Everyone walks there! There’s beadles! No one will think twice about it!” Helen couldn’t argue after that, especially since Laura came back from the walks bright-cheeked and laughing, far happier than she’d been since they got the telegram about Pen’s fever.

You see, Helen and Laura had already had a proper cry together on the carriage ride up from Fairoaks. When they’d heard Pen was at death’s door, Laura had refused to stay behind, admitted through sobs to Helen that she’d been head over heels in love with Pen for years, that even if he’d done something so stupid they could never marry, she still deserved to be there to nurse him. The two of them cried so hard on the road they soaked three handkerchiefs each, and that shared grief made all their old little arguments melt away.

Poor Fanny Bolton, meanwhile, never stood a chance against those two ladies’ combined moral judgment. Women are unforgiving as hanging judges when they think another girl has messed around with a man who belongs to one of their own, right? The narrator isn’t even pretending to argue with that—we’re all fully on team “sew the bad girl up in a sack and toss her in the Bosphorus” over here. But come on, ladies, at least make 100% sure you’ve got the right girl in the sack before you heave it over the side, okay? We don’t want to execute an innocent porter’s daughter by accident.

The Major, for his part, was 100% behind Warrington and Laura hanging out as much as possible. If there was an art exhibition on, he insisted Warrington take her. If Warrington had suggested taking her all the way to Vauxhall Gardens for fireworks, the old man would have waved them off without a second thought. The two of them were perfect company for each other, anyway: Warrington had never spent time around a girl as sweet and unworldly as Laura, and Laura had never met a man as sharp, funny, and unpretentious as Warrington. His rough, no-fuss energy was such a nice change from Pen’s dandified, bored little sneer, that it made Pen’s usual spoiled, languid self look even sillier than usual. The women of Fairoaks had spoiled Pen rotten, after all, coddled him so much that he was bored of every little homage they paid him.

Wait a minute, though—if Pen was that much of a spoiled, lazy little hero-wannabe, why did Laura love him so much? Because what else was she supposed to do? She spent 12 years at Fairoaks with only Dr. Portman and that boring old army guy Captain Glanders for company, Pen was the only remotely charming man she’d ever seen, she’d been hearing his mom sing his praises since she was five years old, and it’s not like anyone falls in love with a portrait in the Royal Academy or a drawing in the Illustrated London News. You fall in love with the person you’re next to, the one who shares their jam tart with you, the one who climbs up a tree to save a baby bird instead of stealing its nest, the one who gives his last shilling to the old beggar woman on the common. You get attached to that image of him in your head, and you hold onto it tight for years, even when you meet far more interesting people later on.

Those quiet autumn evenings in Lamb Court were so cozy, none of them realized how fast the time was flying. The Major was later telling everyone at his club that September in empty London was “doosid pleasant, begad.” He wheezed up the three flights of black stone stairs every afternoon, bringing little fancy jellies and clear soups he’d bullied the famous chef at Bays’s coffee house into making for the invalid. When the doctor said Pen could have a little sherry, the Major showed up with a case of priceless Amontillado that the Marquis of Steyne had given him, a gift straight from King Ferdinand of Spain. Helen and Laura took one tiny sip and thought it tasted like bitter, nutty old dishwater, but Pen perked right up, Warrington declared it the best sherry he’d ever tasted, and they all made silly speeches toasting the Major and Lord Steyne and the entire British aristocracy, right as Doctor Goodenough walked in for his free evening visit and found them all cackling around Pen’s armchair.

Percy Sibwright, who lived in the chambers below, wrote them a flowery note saying they could use all his stuff whenever they wanted, his carpets, his bed, his piano, whatever. That piano was a wobbly little thing that Sibwright played very badly, and Laura sat down at it one evening, blushing bright pink, and started singing old country ballads from home in her lovely rich contralto. Warrington couldn’t tell “God Save The King” from a sea shanty, but he sat there completely rapt, staring at her like she was the greatest musician who’d ever lived.

If you’d looked up at their windows that evening, you’d have seen a pale little girl in a plain black bonnet standing under the lamp-post, Fanny Bolton, lingering there to listen to the music drifting out. No one noticed her. When Pen’s bedtime rolled around, the singing stopped, they led him off to his bedroom, and then the rest of them played cards or backgammon, while Laura stitched a pair of soft worsted slippers that could have been for any one of the three men in the room, all three of whom would have fought over who got them.

Just before ten, the chimes of St. Clement’s Church started pealing out their psalm tune, Martha the maid from Fairoaks got out her bed candles, the Major yelped “Good heavens, is it that late already?” and everyone said their goodnights. Martha locked the front door behind the two men, and bragged later that if any thief had tried to break in, she’d have grabbed that fancy old Damascus scimitar Sibwright brought back from his Levant travels, the one he wore to a fancy dress party at Gloucester Square, and hacked the intruder to bits. Good night, sweet Laura, sleep well. Nobody’s breaking in here, not when you’ve got half the Temple wrapped around your little finger.

Good Helen Pendennis, ever since her son Arthur came staggering back from the brink of a fever-induced delirium, had executed a full, no-nonsense domestic coup over every square inch of his life. She had claimed his drawers, his closets, every half-buttoned shirt and laddered stocking within them, and naturally, the random letters tucked in amongst his socks and cravats—someone had to sort those out, obviously, when the poor boy was too wobbly to stand, let alone answer post. She had been nursing a very specific, unspoken anxiety about the dreadful Fanny Bolton business the whole time, too, too polite to badger her recovering son about it but fretting so hard over it at 2 a.m. she could barely sleep.

The woman even unscrewed the brass front-door knocker off Arthur’s Temple chambers, arguing that the postman’s thundering double rap would jolt the patient right out of his much-needed rest. She screened every single piece of mail that came through the door, no exceptions: angry bootmakers chasing payment for dancing boots Pen swore he’d bought for a single party, hatters begging him to settle his bill before Saturday, the whole gamut of reminders that Arthur was the sort of nice careless boy who forgot money existed until someone waved a receipt in his face. Helen, thrifty to the point of near-parsimony, had a little nest egg stashed away, and she gleefully poured chunks of it into wiping out his tiny pile of debts. After all, any boy who gets his mom to sort his mail and pay his bills while he recovers is already winning at life, and there’s no truer test of a man’s unburdened conscience than how happy he is to hear the postman knock. The good folks cheer for the rat-tat; the rascals dive under the sofa cushions. Helen was only being extra kind, after all, sparing Pen all that tedious hassle while he healed.

There was no damning evidence to be found in his drawers, though, no clear answers about the Fanny Bolton mess. So Helen dragged her brother-in-law Major Pendennis off for a private huddle one sunny day at Richmond, while Pen and Warrington loitered on the terrace bench watching stray dogs wander past. She spilled all her jumbled terrors to him, holding back a small handful of her most mortifying worries, the way every spendthrift hides a few bills from their parents and every society wife omits a couple of silk gowns from her dressmaker’s accounts.

When she asked the Major what on earth to do about this horrid, dreadful affair, and if he knew anything about it, the old gentleman screwed his face up so tight you couldn’t tell if he was suppressing a cackle or had just tasted a very sour plum, squinted at her out of his tiny little eyes, then dropped his gaze back to the carpet. “My dear good creature, I know nothing about it, and I’ve no desire to. If you ask my opinion? You’d best know nothing about it either. Young men will be young men, begad, and if you think our boy is some sort of Jo—”

“Pray spare me that talk,” Helen cut in, standing so straight she looked like a teetotal church steeple.

“My dear, I didn’t start this conversation, if you’ll permit me to say,” the Major replied, bowing so blandly you’d never guess he’d got up to ten times worse than anything Fanny Bolton could possibly be involved in back in his army days.

“I can’t bear to hear such a dreadful sin spoken of so lightly,” the widow said, tears pricking the corners of her eyes. “I can’t stand the thought my boy would do something so shameful. I almost wish he’d died before he ever acted so out of honour. My little Arthur, who was always so sweet and good as a small child! How could he fall so low as to—to—”

“As to flirt with a nice little working-class girl?” the Major snickered. “Egad, if every mother in England broke their heart over that, we’d have no mothers left! Don’t cry, my dear, I hate seeing a woman cry, never could stand it. How do we even know anything serious happened? Has Arthur said a word about it?”

“His silence proves it!” Helen sobbed into her handkerchief.

“Nonsense. There are subjects a young man simply cannot bring himself to blab to his mamma about, you know,” the Major hinted, leaning back like a man who knew every single one of those subjects by heart.

“She wrote to him!” Helen gasped from behind her cambric mask.

“What, before he got sick? Nothing more natural.”

“No—after,” the poor lady wheezed. “I don’t think it came before, that is, I—”

“Only since. And I suppose you took charge of his post while he was too ill to read it, didn’t you?”

“I am the unhappiest mother in the whole world!” Helen wailed.

“The unhappiest because your son is a grown man and not a hermit?” the Major huffed. “Careful, my dear. If you’ve been hiding letters from him, you might do far more damage than the little trumpery drama you’re panicking over. Arthur’s got a stubborn, proud streak—you go rifling his mail and hiding notes from him, and you’ll drive a wedge between the two of you that you’ll regret for the rest of your life, mark my words.”

“There was only one letter!” Helen burst out. “Just a tiny one, only a few words. Look—how can you speak so lightly of this?”

The Major almost burst out laughing when she said “only a tiny one”, he was so tickled he could barely speak, even while he felt real pity for the poor distraught widow in front of him. Their moral worldviews were about as different as a temperance tract and a club bar bill, after all.

“I’d recommend you,” he said, trying to sound serious, “seal that letter back up—those cheap wafer seals are a doddle to fake—and tuck it in with the rest of his post, give it to him when he asks for his old correspondence. If the seal won’t stick? Say you mistook it for a tradesman’s bill, simple as that.”

“I cannot lie to my son,” Helen said, all rigid piety. She’d hidden that letter in her bag two days before they left the Temple, after watching the post slot for it every single day since Pen fell ill, rummaging through all his other letters to make sure she got her hands on that exact one. She pulled it out and passed it to the Major.

“Arthur Pendennis, Esq.,” he read, squinting at the wobbly, scrawled handwriting, a small smirk on his face. “No, no, I won’t read the rest. You’ve already told me it’s just bad-spelt prayers for his health and a note begging to see him? Well, that’s no sin. And since you’re asking for advice? My man Morgan dug around a little, and Doctor Goodenough looked into it too. Turns out this girl was absolutely smitten with Arthur, he paid for her trip to Vauxhall Gardens—Morgan heard that from that Irish chap Flanagan, the laundress’s acquaintance. The girl’s dad’s a violent old drunk who beats her mum, who swears up and down her daughter’s completely innocent to her husband, but then told Goodenough Arthur treated her baby terribly. Whole thing’s still a total mystery. Do you want me to badger Pen until he tells me the whole truth? He’s as honourable a lad as ever lived, he’d spill it immediately.”

“Honour?” Helen scoffed, bitter as a very sour lemon. “Oh brother, what sort of honour are you talking about? If my boy’s guilty, he has to marry her. I’d get down on my knees and beg him to do it.”

“Good God, are you mad?” the Major squawked so loud the sparrows on the terrace took off in a panic. He suddenly remembered exactly how stubborn Arthur was: if his mamma begged him to marry the girl out of honour, the fool would absolutely do it, just for the dramatic principle of the thing. “My dear sister, have you lost your senses?” he said, softening his tone after a dramatic pause. “What right do we have to assume anything untoward happened between them? Look at this letter—she’s sad at home, her dad’s mean, she says she was his nurse, all the spelling’s wrong, that’s it. That’s not evidence of anything except a silly little girl pining after him. She didn’t even step foot in his rooms until he was so delirious he couldn’t tell his own laundress apart from the Archbishop of Canterbury, Mrs. Flanagan told Morgan that herself. She turned up with old Mr. Bows, who was kind enough to come drag me out of Stillbrook that night, actually—never even paid his cab fare, come to think of it, what a nice old chap. There’s nothing to this story at all.”

“You really think so? Thank Heaven, thank God!” Helen cried, jumping up. “I’m going right now to ask Arthur, look at him over there on the terrace with Mr. Warrington, talking to those little kids. He always loved children. He’s innocent, he must be! Let me go ask him.”

The Major’s private opinion was very different, of course—he was judging Arthur by what he himself would have done at 22, after all. If she runs and asks him, he’ll blab the whole stupid truth, and all our plans are ruined, he thought. He made one last desperate effort to stop her.

“My dear good soul,” he said, patting her hand, “think for a second. He never told you about this affair, right? What right do you have to dig it up unprompted? You say you believe he’s a man of honour—why are you doubting that honour over nothing? Who’s even accusing him? Some anonymous busybody, no one’s brought any actual specific charges. If something terrible had happened, the girl’s parents would be banging on our door screaming for compensation, wouldn’t they? You don’t have to entertain some random anonymous rumour, and Pen doesn’t have to prove he’s innocent of it. At that rate, you might as well make him marry that gin-soaked old Irish laundress Mrs. Flanagan, just because she popped in to change his bed sheets when he was sick.”

Helen burst out laughing through her tears, and the Major knew he’d won.

“Marry Mrs. Flanagan, begad!” he went on, tapping her thin little hand. “No. The boy’s told you nothing, you know nothing, he’s innocent, obviously. Now what’s our next move? Suppose he did happen to be a little sweet on this girl? We’ll stick him out at Fairoaks all by his lonesome, with nothing to do all day but moon over that little crush, and a silly little infatuation will turn into a full-blown tragic romance before you can blink. Lonely country houses are the worst places in the world for that sort of nonsense, nothing to distract a fellow from his own moping. We need to occupy him! Amuse him! Take him abroad! He’s only ever been to Paris for a long weekend lark, he’s never done proper travelling. Goodenough says he barely escaped death with that fever, so he’s going to need careful nursing—you can come and watch over him, of course, bring Miss Laura Bell along, and I’ll invite Warrington too. Arthur’s absolutely besotted with Warrington, can’t stand to be apart from him. He’s one of the best young fellows I ever met, family’s old as the hills, perfect company.”

“Does Mr. Warrington know anything about this affair?” Helen asked. “He was away for two months before it all happened, Pen wrote me that much.”

“Not a word! I pumped him myself, never heard a single mention of it, I swear to you,” the Major yelped, suddenly alarmed at the thought of Warrington spilling the beans. “And my dear, you’d best not say a word to him about it either, it’s far too delicate and painful a subject.”

The simple widow squeezed her brother-in-law’s hand. “Thank you, brother. You’ve been so kind, you’ve given me so much peace. I’m going to my room to rest and thank God my boy is innocent. He is innocent, isn’t he?”

“Yes, my dearest creature, absolutely,” the old man said, kissing her hand, so touched by her tender innocence he was almost softhearted. He watched her walk away, fondness warring with amused exasperation in his chest. “Innocent!” he muttered to himself. “I’d swear that until my face turned black rather than make that poor good soul sad.”

Victory secured, the old warrior flopped down on the sofa, draped his yellow silk handkerchief over his face, and settled in for a cozy little nap, snoring so steadily it sounded like a very rickety bagpipe solo. Down on the terrace, the two young men dawdled away the sunny hours, Pen chattering nonstop about his brilliant plans for a new novel and even a brand new tragedy, the lines of which he started declaiming at the top of his lungs while Warrington snickered and pretended to be deeply impressed by his dramatic deliveries.

The Major’s nap got interrupted by Laura Bell walking in. She’d been visiting her old friend Lady Rockminster, who had rented a summer villa nearby, and the old lady had sent over armfuls of grapes, partridges, and other little treats for Arthur the moment she heard he was sick. She’d tried to convince Laura to come stay with her, but Laura wouldn’t leave Helen, who’d run herself so ragged nursing Arthur that Doctor Goodenough had ended up prescribing rest for her too.

The Major jumped awake immediately, ever the gallant old charmer. “Wherever did you find those roses blooming on your cheeks?” he trilled. “I can’t think of a more delightful way to wake up from a nap than the sight of you!” Laura had plenty of good humour and common sense, which made her rather enjoy teasing the old club habitué about his worldliness and silly stories about aristocrats, but today she was in no mood to joke. She’d just been with Helen, she said, and the poor woman looked completely worn out, she was very worried about her health. The Major hinted that Arthur’s illness and other recent anxieties had worn her down, and Laura blushed bright red, catching the allusion, but said nothing. The old diplomat pitched his perfect plan: they’d all go abroad for the autumn, to a quiet little spa town on the Rhine, where Helen could rest and recover, and Arthur could shake off any leftover silly infatuations and become a new man. Naturally Laura wouldn’t leave her dear Helen, of course not, she agreed instantly.

Helen, meanwhile, had sat in her room for an hour turning the whole plan over in her head, and was now as giddy as a schoolboy who’s just opened a book of travel adventures. The farther they went the better, she thought, somewhere so far away not even a sad little letter from Fanny could find them. She fumbled open her desk, pulled out her bank book, and counted all her little savings. If they needed more, she could sell her diamond cross, or borrow a little extra from Laura. “Let’s go, let’s go as soon as he can stand the journey,” she thought, praying Doctor Goodenough would hurry up and give them permission to quit England.

The good doctor drove over to dine with them that very day. “If you keep agitating yourself like this,” he scolded Helen, “you’re going to end up the one laid up in bed, then Laura will have to nurse you, then she’ll get sick too, and I’ll be running back and forth here for free so often my wife will divorce me. Mrs. Goodenough is already jealous of all of you, swears I’m half in love with my patients. Get out of the country as soon as you can, so I can have a little peace at home.”

When they told Arthur the plan to go abroad, he was over the moon with excitement. He immediately decided to start growing a moustache that very second, to get his mouth all trained for perfect French and German pronunciation, only to be mortified when the new hair came in a very bright, very noticeable shade of red. He’d been dreading the thought of three boring months at Fairoaks, listening to old Portman’s long sermons and the local squire’s never-ending Peninsular War stories, with none of the Claverings around for company. “I want to get out of the country,” he confessed to Warrington. “While you were away I ran into a very stupid temptation, and I’m so glad my fever put a stop to it before I made a total fool of myself.” He spilled the whole messy Vauxhall story to his friend, who listened very gravely.

“Not only would you have made your whole life miserable,” Warrington said, “it would have absolutely ruined that poor girl’s life too. And think of the pain it would have caused your mother and all your friends.”

“Not a word to my mother, I beg you!” Pen yelped in alarm. “A scandal like that would kill her, I truly believe it. And the best trick for dodging that sort of danger, I’ve figured out, is not to stand around facing it. Turn your back and run for the hills!”

“Were you very much smitten with her?” Warrington asked.

“Hm!” Pen said, affecting the world-weary air of a veteran rake. “She dropped all her h’s, but she was a dear little girl.”

All you poor silly sweet young maidens out there, take note! If you could hear the man you fancy bragging about you over cigars and coffee in his club, passing your crumpled little love notes around to all his friends to snicker at, you’d never pick up a pen to write him a secret note again. There’s a sort of misdeed that isn’t even complete until the rogue brags about it afterwards, and the man who betrays your honour once will absolutely betray your secret too.

“It’s easy to fall when the temptation’s there, and hard to fight it,” Warrington said gloomily. “And you’re right. The very best thing to do when that sort of danger looms is turn your back and run.”

They got back to talking about the travel plans, and Arthur begged Warrington to come along, insisting he’d have no fun at all without his best friend there. But George shook his head. He couldn’t go, he had engagements, the truth was he simply didn’t have the money to travel, end of story.

Pen was gutted, and told his mother, who was very sorry, but agreed War

Helen Pendennis would sooner have let Pen eat three full pickled onions before breakfast than let him so much as think about little Fanny Bolton, post-fever. Not a single word about the girl ever crossed her lips after her chat with the Major, and she acted so thoroughly like Fanny had been erased from the face of the Earth you could be forgiven for assuming she’d waved a magic broomstick and swept her right out of Clavering. All the same, she kept Pen under a tighter watch than a theatre manager over a drunk leading man, claiming his health was too fragile to leave her sight for even five minutes, and insisting he didn’t have to waste his strength answering letters, ever.

Poor Pen, still wobbly as a new-born lamb, scanned every piece of post that landed on the breakfast table like it was a secret spy dispatch, half-convinced any day he’d spot Fanny’s little wobbly handwriting on an envelope. He was never sure if he was more relieved or annoyed when no such letter turned up. We all know how it goes, don’t we? When your little “romance startup” is clearly going under, you don’t want to immediately hang the “closed for business” sign. You pawn the fancy candlesticks, drag out the sad partner meetings, put off telling all your friends you’re bankrupt, just to avoid the humiliation of a sudden total collapse. Pen was indignant, frankly: could Fanny really just walk away without a single “wait, come back”? Not even a little wave over her shoulder? Turns out most people who sink on those romantic trips just get a little wet, flail a bit, and scramble back to shore dry enough, and anyone who knows Arthur Pendennis knows he was never the sort to drown over a girl, no matter how sweet she was.

Helen couldn’t very well lock up George Warrington too, though. She couldn’t stop him trotting off to London on business whenever he liked, and once she caught herself hoping he’d just stay there permanently, she scolded herself for being selfish — after all, George had stuck by her boy through the worst of the fever, so she treated him like honorary family, all soft sad smiles and extra jam at tea. But the morning he set off for town, she knew exactly what he was up to: he wasn’t going to the market for turnips, he was off digging up news of Fanny for Pen.

Pen had already spilled the whole messy Fanny saga to George, mind. He was so grateful he’d dodged the massive, life-ruining bullet of eloping with her he could have kissed Warrington, but he still insisted he just wanted to say a proper “God bless you” and send her off well, no hard feelings. He spoke so seriously, his voice all wobbly over his toast, that George started to panic his mate wasn’t nearly as over the girl as he claimed. “The smartest thing a fool like us can do when temptation’s hanging about is run as fast as we can,” George told him glumly. “I didn’t do that once, and look at the mess I turned out.” Pen immediately perked up, nosy as ever, begging for the full story. “Some dumb mistake I made when I was your age shattered all my chances at happiness before I turned twenty,” Warrington said. “I’ll tell you the full tale someday, but not now. Just take the warning, kid — don’t go hitting that same rock I wrecked my whole ship on.”

Now, over at the Back Kitchen pub, our favorite over-enthusiastic med student Huxter was having the time of his life. He’d already turned old Irish Captain Costigan into the pub’s main headline act, all for the low, low price of sixpence’ worth of brandy and water. Huxter could mimic literally anything, from a hammy tragic actor overacting Hamlet to a cock crowing on a dunghill to a cork popping out of a stout bottle, and he’d turned getting Costigan sloshed into an art form. Give the old man a sip of the good stuff, and he’d spin you yarns for hours: how he brawled with a jeweler in Phoenix Park, how he once had a private audience with the Duke of York, how he was practically best friends with every nobleman in the country back in the old days, and how his ungrateful daughter Emily had left him to drink alone now, woe is him. The pub landlord was delighted: all the young med students and clerks poured in just to listen to Costigan’s nonsense, spending money left and right, so he pretended not to notice the old man was three sheets to the wind by 2pm most days. Costigan was such a happy, harmless old drunk the regulars even started making up wild stories about where he lived, since he’d never tell anyone his address. Huxter started the silliest rumor of all: he said the old man slept in a watch box by the Bank of England, or tucked into the side of the Duke of York’s column.

Then one bright September morning, after Huxter had spent the whole night dancing at Vauxhall and was nursing his hangover over a twopenny coffee at a Covent Garden stall, he spotted Costigan reeling down Henrietta Street, with a troop of ragged little street urchins hooting after him. The old boy was too far gone to care what the kids thought, too far gone to even get annoyed when the cabmen jeered at him, too far gone to notice he was heading straight for a stray slice of orange peel someone had dropped on the pavement.

Down he went, flat on his back, with a thud that shook the cobblestones.

Huxter sprinted over to help him up. Costigan was too woozy to remember where he lived at first, trying to wave Huxter off and pretend he could make it home on his own, but he was lurching so bad the kid insisted on escorting him, half-carrying the old fellow past Drury Lane’s gin shops and costume warehouses until they stumbled through the gates of Shepherd’s Inn. He dragged Costigan up his rickety staircase, helped him out of his boots and trousers, and spotted the big, raw scrape on his shin from the fall. Turns out old drunks’ cuts don’t heal very fast: the leg swelled up, got all inflamed, and Costigan was stuck in bed feverish and miserable for days.

Huxter immediately volunteered to be his personal doctor, showing up two or three times a day to mess around, tell jokes, and make sure no one gave the old boy too much whisky (to Costigan’s great annoyance). He quickly made friends with everyone in the inn: the porter Bolton, his wife, the Bolton’s little kids, and of course Fanny, who was helping nurse the old General back to health. Fanny was a shadow of her old self by that point, pale and quiet, dropping tears straight into Costigan’s gruel half the time, staring at the postman every time he walked through the gate waiting for a letter from Pen that never, ever came. Huxter brought the kids brandy balls and silly masks to scare each other with, made Fanny laugh once or twice even through her gloom, and Mrs. Bolton, who thought stuck-up old Pen was a cruel stuck-up prat who’d tricked her poor girl, spilled her whole biased version of the story, making Pen sound like the worst cad to ever walk London. Huxter, who still remembered Pen sneering at him like he was something he stepped in on the street, was all ready to hop on a coach to Clavering and punch the convalescent’s lights out, until old Bows the musician pulled him aside and told him the real tea: none of it was Pen’s fault, Costigan was the one who’d messed up the whole meeting, Pen had been nothing but decent, and Helen was the one who’d turfed Fanny out of the sick room. Huxter, decent sort he was, immediately felt like a total git, and called off his planned brawl.

All the same, he kept hanging around the inn trying to cheer Fanny up, which made sour old Bows seethe with jealousy. “Of course it happens again,” he moped to himself, bitter as pickled limes. “Every single time I start to care about anyone, some younger, nicer bloke comes along to steal their attention. I’ve been playing second fiddle my whole sixty years of life, why did I ever think that was going to change?” Fanny, for her part, was so wrapped up in her own sad thoughts she barely noticed Huxter’s kind attentions at all — she was convinced Bows was hiding letters from Pen from her, and treated him so coldly the poor old musician’s life was more miserable than ever.

That was exactly when George Warrington turned up at the Shepherd’s Inn gate, sent by Pen as official, low-key envoy. He asked for Bows by name, and the musician met him at the top of the stairs, surprised to see him. They shook hands, and Warrington passed on Pen’s thanks for Bows running all the way to the country to fetch the Major when Pen was half-dead with the fever. Bows just sneered back at him, all sarcasm. “Lucky him, having friends to run after him. The General up there only cares about his whisky bottle. I know exactly why you’re here, though. It’s Fanny, isn’t it? You lot just sent that poor girl packing out of Pen’s sick room after she sat up three days and three nights watching him rave through the fever, and now you’ve waltzed back to see what you can do with her? My compliments to your fancy lady Mrs. Pendennis, by the way — an apothecary’s daughter who acts so grand? She deserves a double-gilded pestle and mortar stuck to the side of her carriage, that’s how posh her behaviour was.”

Worthy old Major Pendennis fulfilled his promise to Warrington exactly far enough to pat his own conscience on the back and tick the "good deed" box, and far enough to dupe poor Helen into believing Arthur’s silly little fling with the sweet, overlooked gatekeeper’s daughter Fanny was well and truly kaput—no risk of him dragging the family name through the mud by eloping and spending the rest of his days polishing her bootlaces for her. Pen himself, once the sting to his overgrown vanity faded, breathed a massive sigh of relief too: turns out Fanny wasn’t going to waste away pining for him, and there were no awkward, lingering consequences to his dumb little brief misadventure.
With that weight off everyone’s chests, the whole crew set off on their long-planned Continental jaunt. Arthur Pendennis, rentier, travelling with his mamma Mrs. Pendennis and her ward Miss Bell, plus George Warrington, gentleman of leisure, 32 years old, 6 feet tall (very English height, that), face completely unremarkable, dark hair, dark beard, all duly noted down on the absurdly formal French passport they grabbed from the Belgian consul in Dover. They hopped the ferry to Ostend, then trundled slowly through Bruges and Ghent, gawking at every cobblestone before they headed for Brussels and the Rhine.
We won’t bore you with the same old tourist trip every other English traveller wrote a 3-volume memoir about. You already know Laura’s glee at wandering those quiet ancient cities for the first time, you can imagine Helen, our sweet straight-laced country widow, nearly having a conniption every five minutes. Barefoot friars shambling down the high street? Saints with little gold crowns on their heads propped up on church altars, while passers-by bowed down to them in what Helen swore was a direct violation of the 10 Commandments? Priests dripping in fancy silk robes skulking in dark confession booths? Theatres open and people dancing on *Sundays*? She was so bewildered and scandalized she nearly kept her prayer book glued to her chest at all times, and the second the lads stumbled back from their evening walk, Laura would hurriedly put down the psalms she’d been reading to calm Helen’s frayed nerves.
Pen’s recent brush with death had rattled Helen something terrible. She’d sit staring at him for hours, heart sore that her little boy, the one who used to run to her every five minutes to gush about his new marbles or tattle on the kid who called him a wimp, now had whole secret corners of his heart she wasn’t allowed near. Laura, watching her mope, once patted her hand and said “If Pen had loved me the way you wanted, I’d have got him—but I’d have lost you, and I like you loving me best. Men haven’t got a clue how to love the way we do, do they? They’ve got tax bills, tailors’ invoices, boring dinner parties to think about, their heads are stuffed full of total nonsense. A woman? Her whole day revolves around you. Your man’s a stiff oak standing there with his hands stuffed in his breeches pockets, not even noticing the ivy clinging tight around his trunk, smothering him in affection.” Helen sighed and agreed, and honestly? It’s a truism as old as time itself.
Old Pendennis only saw the crew off at the water’s edge, waved them off the ferry, dumped the whole responsibility of the trip on Warrington, then trundled off to stay at the fancy country house of his pal the Marquis of Steyne. He figured he’d more than earned a holiday after nursing Pen through his fever, and even if all the best partridges were already shot, the pheasants were still flying around Stillbrook ready to be picked off. The house party was stuffed full of royal dukes, fancy foreign diplomats, famous politicians, and just generally amusing people—Major Pendennis nearly preened his whole feathered chest off when he saw his name printed in the *Morning Post* alongside all the other distinguished guests.
He was the most useful man at that entire party, full stop. He’d entertain the lads on shooting trips and in the smoking room with raunchy little old anecdotes that had them cackling so hard their cigars fell out of their mouths. He’d escort new arrivals around the park, show them the best view of the mansion, point out the exact spot in the woods where old Lord Lynx caught Sir Phelim O’Neal on his knees before his ladyship, and knew exactly how many servants ate in the housekeeper’s parlour and how many crammed into the main servants’ hall. He had a little friendly snark about everyone, and a warm greeting for every single person he met. He deserved every minute of that holiday, and he was quietly chuffed he’d dumped the task of looking after Helen and Laura on Warrington—who, for his part, was only too happy to do it.
Warrington spoke perfect German, volunteered to give Laura lessons (she jumped at the chance, while Pen was far too lazy to crack open a textbook), acted as unofficial courier, hauled all their luggage on and off boats and carriages, sorted all the money, hunted down the English church for the two ladies on Sundays, walked Helen’s donkey for her on her evening excursions, fetched her a copy of *Galignani* when she was bored, and found the coziest shaded bench under the lime trees for her to sit on while the Kursaal band played after dinner. A dozen foppish, whiskery Prussian and French gambling dandies kept casting longing looks at pretty, fresh-faced Laura, dying to ask her to waltz, but she only stepped foot in the ballroom once or twice, when Pen deigned to dance with her. Warrington, for all his gruff charm, had never learned to waltz, and every time he watched Laura spin past with some other fellow he’d kick himself for not taking lessons as a lad. He’d trudge back to his own lodgings every night lonelier and grumpier than the last, staring at the crow’s feet around his eyes in the mirror, and the little silver streaks popping up in his dark beard, and seething with jealousy at every smooth-cheeked young buck prancing around the bath resort in his tight waistcoat and shiny varnished boots.
Poor Helen saw exactly what was happening. She realized why Major Pendennis had been so insistent Warrington come along on the trip, her little plan to marry Laura off to Pen was falling apart right in front of her face, Pen didn’t even seem to notice what was going on, and the fancy healing waters of Rosenbad did absolutely nothing for her frayed nerves. Pen, meanwhile, was bouncing back faster than a rubber ball: he slept 12 solid hours out of every 24, ate meals so big the waiter’s eyes nearly popped out, and two months after they arrived he was back to his full pre-illness weight and strength.
