Fifteen days after they settled in at Rosenbad, a letter turned up from Major Pendennis saying he was on his way, and sure enough, a couple days later he waddled into town with his loyal valet Morgan in tow, wearing a ridiculous youthful travelling suit that made him look like a lad half his age from the back—until he started walking, and you noticed his ancient knees creaking like an unoiled chair, and he was hobbling around in his tiny pinched varnished boots. He spent the whole night poring over the guest list at the resort, chuffed to bits that half his aristocrat acquaintances were there, and immediately started making plans to introduce Pen to a German Grand Duchess, a Russian Princess, and an English Marquis. Pen, who’d always been a sucker for fancy polite society, was absolutely thrilled.
That very evening he grabbed Pen’s arm and waddled straight into the Kursaal gambling halls, bet a napoleon or two at the Trente-et-quarante table, bragging the whole time about how his old pal the Marquis of Steyne once lost 18 thousand pounds in a single sitting and didn’t even blink. “That’s what I call a proper English gentleman, Pen!” he crowed. He dragged Pen around pointing out all the scandalous Russian princesses and disgraced society ladies loitering by the tables, spilling every bit of sordid gossip he knew about their pasts, and Pen nearly laughed himself sick at his old uncle’s glee. Helen, who’d stumbled through the gambling hall once before, had been so horrified by the croupiers calling out *Rouge gagne!* and *Couleur perd!* that she’d dragged Pen out and made him swear on his honour he’d never touch the gambling tables—and to Pen’s credit, he kept that promise.
The Major’s arrival immediately put a damper on three people at once: Laura, who thought he was a lying old toad, Warrington, who treated him with frosty contempt, and Helen, who was terrified he’d mess up the last remaining shred of her plan to get Pen and Laura together. None of them knew the Major had accidentally brought a ticking time bomb all the way from London with him.
The next morning Pen dutifully trundled over to the Major’s fancy hotel suite to pay his respects, found the room perfectly tidied by Morgan—hats brushed, coats laid out, maps and passports and a well-thumbed prayer book (the Major never travelled without one, thought every self-respecting English gentleman had to show face at the local English church, same as he had to call on the ambassador) all lined up perfectly in place. Morgan handed him a stack of post that had been forwarded from his London chambers: a few copies of the *Pall Mall Gazette*, and an envelope of letters.
One of the letters was scrawled in wobbly handwriting, addressed “Arther Pendennis, Esquire”. Pen’s face went bright red the second he saw it, and he read it with a sharp little twist in his chest. It was Fanny Bolton. She’d showed up at his London chambers while he was sick, found he’d left for Germany without a single word to her, no answer to her letter begging for just one kind note, none of the books he’d promised her before he fell ill. She said the people who’d found her at his bedside while he was delirious had turned her away without a word, she thought she’d die of the heartache, but Doctor Goodenough had taken care of her. She’d even kept a tiny lock of his hair from when they’d cut it all off while he was feverish, and she just wanted to know if she could keep it, if no one would force her to hand it back. She forgave everyone, she said, and she’d pray for him for the rest of her life.
When the Major waddled out of the bedroom, fresh from his bath, he found Pen standing there with the letter in his hand, face dark with rage. “What’s got you glum, my boy? Duns chasing you?” he asked, already knowing full well what the problem was.
Pen rounded on him. “Did you have anything to do with this? Did you send Fanny away from my chambers? Intercept her letters? Who dared treat her like garbage? Was it you?”
The Major huffed and puffed, indignant. “I never tamper with any man’s letters, and I don’t answer rude questions! I showed up at your rooms to look after you when you were at death’s door, and this is the thanks I get? Disgraceful!”
Pen apologized quickly for snapping, then pressed him again, and the Major swore up and down he never even spoke a word to Fanny, never sent her away.
“Then it was my mother!” Pen yelled, grabbing his hat. “I will never forgive whoever did this. She was supposed to be her defender, not her enemy! I’m going to go sort this out right now!”
The Major screamed for him to stop, but Pen was already out the door, striding down the street as fast as his legs could carry him back to their lodgings. The Major sighed, waved Morgan over to pour him a coffee, and peered out the window at Pen’s retreating back. “Poor Helen. Oh hell, all the fat’s in the fire now. This is gonna be one hell of a row.”
When Pen burst through their front door, only Warrington was in the drawing room, waiting to escort Helen and Laura to the Sunday service at the little English church. Pen was so furious he couldn’t wait a single second to rant. He flung Fanny’s letter across the table at Warrington. “Look at that! She nursed me when no one else would, they treated that poor little girl like a stray dog, kept all her letters from me, treated me like a snot-nosed kid! My own mother did this.”
“Hey, that’s your mother you’re talking about,” Warrington tried to reason with him.
“That makes it worse!” Pen snapped. “She should be on her knees apologizing to that girl! She gave me everything she had, and this is how we repay her?”
“Hush!” Warrington hissed, nodding at the closed bedroom door. “They can hear you in the next room.”

Our poor widow Helen Pendennis, ably assisted by her trusty Fairoaks factotum Martha – who’d spent the last fortnight snickering at all the weird German household quirks the boys had picked up on their continent jaunt, and side-eyeing their habit of stowing half-smoked pipes in every teacup and mantel nook – laid out what she considered a veritable feast in honour of Major Pendennis’s arrival. Trouble was, only the Major, Pen, and Warrington turned up to tuck in: Helen sent word she was too peaky to make it down to dinner, and Laura stayed glued to her side for moral support. The Major held court the whole meal, regaling everyone with third-hand anecdotes about his old White’s club cronies and how he’d once won 300 quid off a duke at whist, and didn’t notice – or chose not to notice – that Pen was scowling so hard he could’ve curdled the claret, and Warrington was staring into his port like it had personally insulted his entire family tree.

It wasn’t until the sun was dipping below the hedgerows that Helen and Laura shuffled into the parlour, Helen leaning all her weight on the girl’s arm, backlit by the waning dusk so Pen couldn’t see how deathly pale and tear-streaked her face was. She tottered straight over to her son, whom she hadn’t laid eyes on all day, draped her thin, trembling arms over his shoulders, and kissed him soft as a prayer. Laura immediately stepped back to the far side of the room to give them space. Pen noticed his mother’s whole frame was shaking like a leaf, her hand cold as a gravestone when she pressed it to his forehead, fussing over him like he was seven years old again and home from school with a fever.

Somehow, that display of abject misery only made 20-something idiot Pen’s temper flare harder. He barely bothered to return the kiss, and met her pleading gaze with a face like flint. *She’s playing the martyr*, he fumed to himself, *persecuting me just so she can look like the poor long-suffering mother*.
“You look dreadful, my love,” Helen said, voice wobbly. “I hate seeing you pull that face.” She staggered over to the sofa, still clinging to his passive hand like he was a life raft.
“I’ve had a devil of a lot to annoy me lately, mother,” Pen huffed, chest heaving so hard Helen’s own heart started hammering so fast she went half faint, too scared to speak.
Everyone else in the room froze, holding their breath. It was obvious the storm was about to blow the windows in.
“I got letters from London today,” Pen went on, not noticing his mom was about to keel over. “One of ’em’s given me worse pain than I’ve ever had in my whole life. Says my old letters were intercepted and stolen from me – that a young girl who cared for me more than anyone, who nursed me through my fever, got treated cruelly by – by you, mother.”
“For God’s sake, stop,” Warrington barked, jumping up. “Can’t you see she’s ill?”
“Let him speak,” Helen whispered, so quiet they almost didn’t hear her.
“Let him speak and kill her, why don’t you?” Laura shrieked, rushing to throw an arm around Helen. “Go on then, sir. Watch her drop dead right in front of you.”
That only made Pen angrier, even as his own soft heart was screaming at him that he was being a brute. “You’re the cruel one, all of you! You pin all this mess on me, hurl your wicked accusations, your stupid doubts, hound the people who actually love me, who’d brave anything for me, just because they weren’t born with a fancy name and a fancy estate like you! You want to know what I’m going to do now I know what you’ve done? I’m going straight back to that poor girl you threw out of my house, ask her to come live with me. I don’t care about your stupid pride, your stupid petty suspicions that insult both her and me.”
“Wait a second, Pen, you can’t mean –” Helen started, eyes wide, hands outstretched, but Laura clamped a hand over her mouth to hush her. Helen didn’t fight it, she was too desperate to hear the rest of what he had to say. “Go on, Arthur, go on,” she mumbled, half-slumped against the sofa cushions like she was about to pass out.
“By Gad, I say he *won’t* go on! I won’t stand for it, by Gad!” the Major exploded, jumping to his feet, face beet red. “If you want to insult your own mother, drag the good name of Pendennis through the mud by tying yourself to some low-born kitchen maid after everything we’ve done for you? Fine! Go do it, by Gad! But I wash my hands of you, sir, wash my hands of you! I’m an old man, I haven’t got long left on this earth. I come from one of the oldest, most honourable families in all of England, by Gad! I’d hoped before I kicked the bucket that the boy I doted on, looked after, nursed through every scrap of trouble, would do something to make sure our name stayed untarnished! But if you won’t, dammy, I say amen! By G–, my father and my brother Jack were the proudest men in all the kingdom, I never thought I’d live to see this disgrace attached to my name – never – and – and I’m ashamed to say you’re Arthur Pendennis!” The old boy’s voice cracked into a sob, the second time in all Pen’s life he’d seen tears on those wrinkled old cheeks.
That sob hit Pen like a punch to the gut, his anger vanishing instantly. He stopped pacing the room like a caged tiger, and everyone went quiet for a minute, the sky outside turning properly dark. Then Warrington’s deep rumbling voice cut through the dim, and every head turned to look at him.
“Might I tell you all a little story about myself?” he said, a little unsteady. “You’ve all been so kind to me, Mrs Pendennis, and Laura – I hope you don’t mind me calling you that – Pen and I have been thick as thieves for years, and I’ve wanted to tell you this for ages, only it’s a sad old tale, and involves someone else’s secret. But it might do Pen some good to hear it, and stop all of you tearing yourselves to pieces over this terrible misunderstanding. Mind if I go on?”
“Please do,” Helen said, though she was barely listening, her head swimming with that glorious new hope that Pen wasn’t actually about to run off and ruin his life.
Warrington poured himself a full bumper of port, downed the whole thing in one go, and started talking. “You all see me for what I am, right? A man who couldn’t care less about getting ahead in the world, couldn’t give a fig for reputation, living in a garret on pocket change even though I’ve got a good name and wits enough to make something of myself if I bothered. But I don’t bother. I’ll probably die up there all alone, in that garret. I sealed my own fate when I was barely out of boyhood. You know why I took such a shine to Pen the second we met at Oxbridge? The boys from our college were gossiping about that little early adventure he had with the Chatteris actress, the one that Major Pendennis stepped in to stop before it landed him married at nineteen to a woman ten years older who had nothing in common with him. What sort of a life would that have been for him? Misery, plain and simple. When he was yelling just now about threatening to do the same thing, that was just anger talking – perfectly understandable anger, mind you, after he acted so generous and honourable through this whole mess, and all he got back was suspicion and a pile of unfair treatment of a perfectly innocent girl who’s done all of us a good turn.”
Helen was already half out of her seat, giddy with relief. “Oh, I’m not tired at all – go on, go on!”
“I liked him for that, you see,” Warrington went on, glancing over at Laura with a little half smile. “I like a man daft enough to fall head over heels for someone, against all good sense. That’s why we became friends. He’s been the best companion a lonely old wretch like me ever had. I’m not complaining, mind you – up in my garret, with the flowers you all left me, my old books, and my pipe as a permanent wife, I’m pretty content. Only every now and then I catch myself envying other blokes, the ones with good careers, or the ones who get to have the thing fate and my own stupid choices stole from me – the love of a woman, or a child.” A soft little sigh drifted across the dark room, and a hand reached out for his, only to yank back immediately. Proper Victorian ladies, you see, are trained to blush at the very hint of unscripted kindness, so modesty kicked in, that spontaneous friendly gesture got stuffed back behind propriety, and Warrington just laughed softly and kept going. “My fate is all my own fault, and it wasn’t just miserable for me, it hurt other people too. Before I even went to college, when I was eighteen, I got tangled up in an adventure of my own. No one stepped in to save me the way the Major saved Pen. I was at a private tutor’s house, and like Pen, I got it in my head I was in love with a woman far below my station, several years older than me. I don’t blame you if you’re shocked, Laura –”
“I’m not shocked at all,” Laura said, and this time she reached out and laced her fingers through his, firm and steady, because she’d already guessed his secret from little hints he’d dropped months before.
“She was a yeoman’s daughter, lived on a farm nearby,” Warrington said, voice wobbly. “Her parents knew who my father was, and buttered me up with every cheap trick and flattery under the sun, to drag me into their trap. To give her credit, she never cared a fig for me – her family threatened and bullied her into it. I was young and daft, I wanted to believe I was in love, so I fell for it. What do you think that marriage was like? I found out soon enough I was hitched to a woman who couldn’t understand a single thing I cared about, whose dullness drove me up the wall so fast I started loathe to even come home. After months of sneaking around, hiding the marriage, I found letters that proved her heart belonged to some farm lad from her own village the whole time. When my father died, I paid off all my college debts, and sank every last penny I had left into an annuity for her and her family – on the strict condition they hid themselves away and never used my last name. They’ve kept the deal so far, but they’d break it tomorrow if someone offered them twice the money. If I ever made a name for myself, she’d have turned up out of nowhere to claim a share of it. So at twenty years old, I was stuck – hopeless, ruined, no way out. I was a stupid boy got conned by a bunch of greedy peasants, and it’s only lately I’ve figured out how hard it is to actually forgive ’em. There’s your moral, Pen – be careful who you marry, outside your own class. I think I could’ve had a better life than this, but it’s what I made, so here I am, happy to sit back and watch the rest of you get your happy endings, and not be too bitter about it.”
“By Gad, sir!” the Major cackled, already back in top form, his tears long dried. “I fully intended you to marry our Laura here, you know!”
“And by Gad, you old fool,” Warrington laughed, “you owe me a thousand pounds for that very joke.”
“A thousand? It was only a five-pound pony, you rascal!” the Major said, completely confused, making everyone snort.
Helen was so giddy with relief she jumped clean off the sofa, grabbed Warrington’s hands, and kissed both of them. “God bless you – God bless you forever, Mr. Warrington!” She ran straight over to Pen, and collapsed into his arms.
“I’m innocent, mother,” Pen said, holding her tight, all the anger gone, overflowing with tenderness. “You wronged me, my dearest, dearest mom.”
“Oh yes, my child, I wronged you, thank God, I wronged you!” Helen whispered, crying happy tears. “Come with me, Arthur – not here. I need to ask my boy to forgive me – and ask God to forgive me too, and bless you, and love you, my son.”
He led her, still shaky, into her bedroom, and shut the door behind them. The other three sat quiet, smiling through their tears, watching them go. For the rest of his life, Pen remembered that night: the moon rising through the window, lighting up his mother’s pale sweet face, their long soft talk where he told her every last detail of how he’d fought his temptation to run off with Fanny, how the threat he’d yelled earlier had just been stupid anger talking. Helen said he had every right to be cross with her, that she’d been proud and cruel, that she wanted him to go see Fanny, give her a little present, write to her and tell her no hard feelings. At nine o’clock, Helen reminded him of when he was a little boy, how she’d come up to his bedroom every night at that hour to hear him say the Lord’s Prayer. Pen knelt down at her feet, and sobbed through the old familiar prayer he’d not said in years. When he got to the last line, Helen’s head fell onto his shoulder, her arms wrapped tight around him, and they whispered “for ever and ever, amen” together.
A quarter of an hour later, Pen yelled “Laura! Laura!” at the top of his lungs. She ran into the room instantly, and found Pen still on his knees, holding Helen’s limp hand. Her head was tilted back, her face as white as a sheet. “Help, Laura, help!” he shrieked. “She fainted – she’s –”
Laura screamed, and dropped to her knees beside Helen. The noise brought Warrington, the Major, and all the servants rushing into the room. The kind, gentle woman was gone. Her last feeling on earth had been pure, uncomplicated joy. No more aches, no more doubts, no more grief, no more worries for her boy. Her last heartbeat was love, her last breath a blessing.
The small, grieving group arranged her funeral quickly, and laid her to rest next to her husband in the old Clavering church, the same one she’d prayed in every Sunday for decades. Laura went to stay with Dr Portman for a little while, and he read the funeral service over her, sobbing so hard he could barely get the words out, while a tiny handful of villagers who’d known her stood around the grave. Not many people in the neighbourhood cared to remember her, after that. The cottagers she’d spent years giving food and money to muttered a few kind words, then gossiped about how she’d died of a bad heart. The Clavering housewives spent three afternoons speculating about how much property she’d left, and wondering if Arthur would rent Fairoaks out or move in, all of them betting he’d blow through the whole inheritance in two years flat. By the next market day, almost everyone had forgotten she ever existed. Only three or four people who truly loved her kept her memory alive. The biggest gap she left was in Laura’s heart, who’d loved her more than anyone else in the world. “I’m glad she got to give me her blessing before she went,” Warrington told Pen later. Pen himself could barely believe how much love his mother had held for him, and spent the rest of his life trying to live up to it.
All of Helen’s papers were perfectly organised, her little fortune passed straight to Pen without a single hassle. There was a note in her own handwriting, a prayer asking God to let her die peacefully in the arms of her son – exactly how it happened. Pen and Laura went through all her things together, making sure every little last legacy was sent where she’d wanted it to go: the fancy vase she’d promised Dr Goodenough was shipped off the next week, her favourite silver coffee pot went to Dr Portman, the diamond ring set with her hair was given to Warrington. When Laura first went back to Helen’s empty bedroom, she broke down crying, looking at the old prayer cushion, the vanity chair, the mirror that would never again reflect her sweet sad face. Pen found her a little while later, brought her downstairs, gave her a glass of sherry, and told her “Nothing in your room will ever be moved, Laura. It’s always yours. It’s my sister’s room, forever.”
They found a sealed packet of old letters in Helen’s desk, marked “Letters from Laura’s father”, which Pen handed straight over to her. For the first time, Laura fully understood how deeply Helen had loved her own father, how tenderly she’d cherished that memory all those years.
Pen remembered one last wish of his mother’s that Laura didn’t know about: the gift for Fanny Bolton. He wrote her a kind letter, sent it enclosed to old Mr Bows, asking him to read it before he gave it to Fanny. He thanked her for nursing him through his fever, told her his mother’s last wish was that she’d accept a little sum of money, the interest paid to her every year until she married. It wasn’t a fortune, but it was enough to make Fanny’s life a little easier. Her parents were thrilled, her father saying Pen was a perfect gentleman. Old grumpy Bows just growled that plastering a broken heart with a banknote was the cheapest sort of sympathy, and poor Fanny knew perfectly well that the letter was a gentle, final farewell.

It was Derby Day at Epsom, that great national British holiday where every Londoner who owns a hat, a pair of boots, and at least a vague ability to lie about knowing anything about thoroughbreds drags themselves out to the Downs to gamble, eat too much cold pie, and gawk at anyone richer than themselves. Naturally, half the cast of this little history showed up, all jostling for a spot in the sun.

Wedged in a perfectly respectable open carriage hauled there by two very patient nags was Mrs. Bungay of Paternoster Row, decked out in so many frills, feathers, and brooches that Solomon in all his glory would have taken one look and gone home to borrow a fan. Next to her huddled meek little Mrs. Shandon, who’d somehow gotten stuck with Mrs. Bungay as her ride-or-die bestie and hadn’t had the heart to break the friendship off yet. Mr. Bungay himself had already stuffed himself full of three helpings of cold ham, and was now careening wildly at the stick-the-stall pop-guns a few feet away, sweat dripping down his shiny bald pate like he was trying to water the grass with his forehead. Shandon was shambling around among the gipsies and beer sellers like he was hoping someone would slip him a free pint, and Finucane hovered dutifully by the two ladies, fending off random passersby while letting the regular publishing house crowd pop over to say hello.

One of those visitors was Mr. Archer, who bowed so low he nearly knocked his own top hat off, and proceeded to give Mrs. Bungay a running commentary of every “important” person on the course. “Over yonder’s the Prime Minister,” he announced, “He just told me to put a tenner on Borax, but between you and me, Munmeer’s got it in the bag.” Then he pointed out a dozen “dukes and grandees” that were mostly just random blokes in top hats, and leaned in like he was sharing top-secret state gossip. “Look up in the Grand Stand! That’s the Chinese Ambassador and his mandarin crew—Fou-choo-foo brought me letters of introduction from the Governor General of India, who’s my absolute oldest chum. I wined and dined that ambassador so often he kept a set of chopsticks at my house permanently. But he brought his own cook with him, and would you believe it? One day I was out, the ambassador was in our back garden gorging on gooseberries (the Chinese are mad for gooseberries, apparently) and that monster of a cook spotted my wife’s tiny Blenheim spaniel—you know, the one the Duke of Marlborough gave us, the one whose ancestor’s life Mrs. Archer’s great-great-grandpa saved at Malplaquet? He seized poor little Fido, cut his throat, skinned him, and served him up stuffed with forcemeat as the second course.”

“Lawks!” gasped Mrs. Bungay, her eyes nearly popping out of her head.
“You can imagine my wife’s meltdown when she found poor Fido’s skin in the back yard right after we’d all had a bite of the dish! She refused to speak to the ambassador ever again, and he’s never dared show up for dinner since. The Lord Mayor loved it, though! Swore it tasted just like duck, especially with a side of green peas.”
“You don’t say!” Mrs. Bungay shrieked, now fully convinced Archer was the most important man in the entire country.
“Fact, cross my heart. And look at that lady in blue sat next to the ambassador? That’s Lady Flamingo, they say she’s going to marry him and move back to Peking. She’s already squeezing her feet into tiny little shoes to make them dainty, but she’ll never get them small enough. My wife’s got the smallest foot in all of England, wears shoes made for a six-year-old, but that’s nothing next to a Chinese lady’s tiny feet, Mrs. Bungay!”

“Hey, Mr. Archer,” Mrs. Bungay cut in, squinting at a carriage a little ways off. “Who’s that with Mr. Pendennis? He and Mr. Warrington popped over a minute ago. That Mr. Pendennis is so stuck-up, but he’s earned it, right? Everyone says he hangs out only with top-shelf company. Did he inherit a huge fortune? I notice he’s still all in black, poor thing.”
“Eighteen hundred a year in land, twenty-two and a half thousand in three-and-a-half per cents, give or take a quid,” Archer rattled off like he was reciting the back of his hand.
“Law! You know literally everything, Mr. A!” the Paternoster Row lady gasped.
“Only because I handled poor Mrs. Pendennis’s will,” Archer waved modesty aside. “Major Pendennis hardly does a thing without calling me in, and we tied up all the property nice and tight so Arthur can’t go blowing it all on silly frivolities. —How d’you do, my lord? You know that gentleman there, ladies? You’ve read his speeches in Parliament! That’s Lord Rochester.”
“Lord fiddlesticks!” Finucane yelled from the back of the carriage. “That’s Tom Staples from the Morning Advertiser, you fool.”
“Is it?” Archer didn’t even flinch. “My eyesight’s terrible, honestly, I could’ve sworn it was Rochester. And that chap with the double opera glasses? That’s Lord John! The tall bloke next to him? Sir James, obviously.”
“Yeh, you know ’em because you spot their names in the newspaper,” Finucane grumbled.
“I know them because they call me their dear personal friend,” Archer sniffed. “Look at the Duke of Hampshire! The perfect old English gentleman! He’s never missed a Derby. ‘Archer,’ he told me just yesterday, ‘I’ve been to sixty-five Derbies! First time I came I was seven years old, on a piebald pony with my dad, the Prince of Wales, and Colonel Hanger. I only missed two: one when I had measles at Eton, and one the year of Waterloo, when I was off in Flanders with my great friend Wellington.’”

“Wait, that yellow carriage with the pink and yellow parasols that Mr. Pendennis is talking to, full of random gents?” Mrs. Bungay asked. “Who’s that?”
“That’s Lady Clavering of Clavering Park, her estate’s right next to Pendennis’s family home. The little lad hanging off the box is her son and heir, and he’s three sheets to the wind, the little scamp. The young lady is Miss Amory, her daughter from her first marriage, and she’s got it so bad for Arthur Pendennis it’s practically leaking off her. But I hear he’s got his heart set on someone else. You know young Foker, the brewer heir? He was so heartbroken when Miss Amory turned him down he nearly hanged himself, his valet cut him down just in the nick of time, and now he’s off abroad with a minder to keep him out of trouble.”
“Bless his heart, what a romantic!” Mrs. Bungay sighed. “Who’d’ve thought, when he turned up quiet and demure to our dinners three or four years ago, he’d be out meeting royalty and getting his name printed in every society guest list?”
“I introduced him around loads when he first moved to town,” Archer said, preening, “And his uncle Major Pendennis did the rest. Hang on—Cobden’s here! Of all people! I’ve got to go say hello. Goodbye, Mrs. Bungay! Mrs. Shandon! Lovely chatting!”

An hour earlier, on the other side of the course, a beat-up old stagecoach had rattled into view, its roof crammed full of shabby, loud, overexcited blokes stamping and yelling as the Derby race careened across the grass, watched by thousands upon thousands of screaming people. This was Wheeler’s drag, from the Harlequin’s Head pub in Bow Street, ferrying a whole crew of rowdy lads who’d stuffed a slap-up feast in the boot for the day. As the horses flashed past, every last one of them bellowed the name of the nag they’d bet their last shilling on: “The Cornet!” “Muffineer for the win!” “Blue sleeves! Blue sleeves!” “Yellow cap! YELLOW CAP!” The whole crowd shrieked through that delicious, torturous minute before the winner’s number went up, and when the flapping signal confirmed Podasokus had taken first place, one guy on the roof jumped so high he nearly fell right off, like he was a homing pigeon determined to fly back to London with the good news.

He didn’t make it more than an inch off the boards, thudding back down so hard the rotting old coach roof creaked and cracked under him. “HOORAY!” he howled. “Podasokus is the winner! Supper for ten, Wheeler, mate! Everyone’s invited, and to hell with the bill.”
The rest of the shabby dandies on the coach cheered and clapped him on the back, whispering to each other that the Colonel was going to walk away with fifteen hundred quid, and got the odds from a bookie who couldn’t welch on him. Every single one of them immediately started side-eyeing each other, terrified some other bloke would drag the Colonel off to a quiet corner and borrow a tenner off him before they could get in their own borrow request. The poor guy couldn’t even go to the privy the whole rest of the afternoon, everyone clung to him like glue.

Over on another patch of the Downs, there was a far more modest vehicle: cab No. 2002, that had trundled over from the Strand stand carrying one gentleman and two ladies. One of the girls was perched on the cab’s box, munching lobster salad and sipping bitter ale with her mum and their friend, so fresh and rosy-cheeked that half the fancy dandies who’d been hanging around the rich people’s carriages abandoned all the fancy society ladies just to gawk at her. Her cheeks flushed pink like summer rose petals whenever she laughed, which was a lot—her mum’s cheeks were bright red too, but that was a permanent mottled shade that got deeper the more pale ale and brandy-and-water she drank, until she looked as pink as the lobster they’d just eaten.

The gentleman with them, Mr. Sam Huxter, was in top form, telling jokes so loud and silly the whole road could hear them. On the drive over, he’d yelled good-natured “chaff” at every passing carriage, from the fancy drags full of grumpy guardsmen to the humble donkey cart where the dustman was taking his girlfriend to the races. He’d fired silly little jokes into every cottage window he passed, at lines of grinning schoolgirls, groups of cheering urchins hanging over school railings, even old maiden ladies who looked like they never laughed in their lives. The pretty girl in the pink-ribbonbonnet and her lobster-loving mum both agreed that when Sam was in high spirits, no one could touch him. He’d stuffed the cab full of stupid prizes he’d won at the pop-gun stalls: pin cushions, wooden apples, tobacco boxes, Jack-in-the-boxes, tiny toy soldiers. He’d even brought a gipsy over to tell the girls’ fortunes, and the only damp spot on the whole happy day was when the gipsy told the pretty young thing to watch out for a fair-faced man who’d done her wrong, that she’d been very ill once, but a true dark-haired man would always stand by her.

The girl blushed so hard she looked like a cherry, while her mum and Sam exchanged very significant little grins. Probably the gipsy said the exact same lines to a hundred different carriages that day.

Making his way through the crowd to people-watch as he usually did, Arthur Pendennis suddenly rounded a corner and bumped straight into cab 2002. When the pretty girl spotted him, she gasped and went pale. Her mum went even redder than usual, and the previously cheerful Sam Huxter scowled so hard his eyebrows met, his fists curling tight in his coat pockets, glaring daggers from Fanny Bolton (who the reader has probably already guessed the pretty girl is) straight at Arthur.

Arthur frowned back, his face all serious and disapproving, like he was Mrs. Lynx the neighbourhood busybody catching two people having a perfectly innocent chat and immediately assuming the worst. No one could blame him for his prudish little reaction, honestly. Fanny’s heart started hammering, Mrs. Bolton started talking a mile a minute to defuse the awkwardness. “Lor! Mr. Pendennis, I’m that glad to see you! Don’t he look well, Fanny? We were just talking about you not two seconds ago! If this is the famous Epsom races everyone raves about, I’d be happy if I never saw it again, for all I care. How’s Major Pendennis? And that lovely Mr. Warrington who did Fanny such a kindness ages back? She’s never forgotten it, not once! That tall bloke nearly bashed his whole head off our lodge door, remember Fanny? How he knocked his head—”

While Mrs. Bolton rambled, a thousand old memories flashed through Fanny’s head: the days she thought she’d die of loving Arthur, the lonely sick weeks, the quiet shame, and the slow, soft comfort Sam had given her after it all. There he was, standing right in front of her: all dandified, wearing black crape on his white hat, jet buttons down his shirtfront, a pink carnation in his lapel that some other girl probably gave him, tight lavender gloves sewn with black mourning thread, a tiny little cane that couldn’t possibly have been used for anything harder than prodding a footman. Sam meanwhile had no gloves on, wore giant scuffed Blucher boots, reeked of tobacco, and definitely looked like a good hose-down would do him a world of good. Fanny’s eyes drifted over every inch of Arthur, from the little pale ring on his forehead left by his hat, to the trinkets on his watch chain, to the shiny neat little boots that looked nothing like Sam’s scuffed high-lows. After she gave his gloved hand a tiny wobbly shake, the only words she could get out were: “This is Mr. Samuel Huxter—you two met before, I think. Sam, you know Mr. Pendennis. Would you—would you like a little refreshment?”

Those quiet little words melted every last bit of suspicion off Arthur’s face, and probably took a weight of old guilt off his chest too. The stuffy frown fell away, replaced by a good-natured grin and a little amused twinkle in his eye. “I’m parched,” he said, “I’d be delighted to drink to your health, Fanny. And I hope Mr. Huxter will forgive me for being horribly rude the last time we met—when I was so sick and out of my mind I barely knew what I was saying.” He held out his gloved hand to Sam in a clear peace offering.

Sam’s tight, hot fist had no choice but to creep out of his coat pocket, and when he wrapped it around Arthur’s fancy glove, he left big black smudges all over the lavender kid. He noticed it immediately, and had to fight the urge to clench his fist and punch that stupid grinning dandy right in the jaw, right there in front of the whole race course, to prove he was the better man.

Arthur, completely unphased, poured himself a glass of lukewarm frothy beer, said it was the most delicious drink he’d ever tasted, and drank it happily to their health. While he was chatting away all friendly and charming, a young lady in dove-grey silk with a pink-lined white parasol and impossibly tiny dove-grey boots walked past him, leaning on the arm of a tall stalwart man with a military moustache. The young lady clenched her little fist, gave Arthur a mischievous glare out of the corner of her eye, and the moustached man burst out laughing, tipping his hat to Fanny and her mum. Fanny’s eyes followed the pretty dove-grey girl for a second, then she turned right back to Sam with the softest, sweetest, most innocent smile in the world.
“Isn’t she beautiful?” Fanny said. “What a lovely dress! Did you see how tiny her little hands are, Sam?”
“That was Captain Strong,” Mrs. Bolton put in. “I wonder who that young woman was?”
“Neighbour of mine from the country,” Arthur said. “Miss Amory, Lady Clavering’s daughter. You saw Sir Francis Clavering all the time when you lived in Shepherd’s Inn, Mrs. Bolton.”
Fanny’s head immediately spun out a full three-volume romance about the pair: grand love, tragic betrayal, a fancy wedding at St. George’s Hanover Square, a broken hearted heroine, and poor Sam was definitely not the hero of that little daydream. Sam meanwhile had lit the rankest, cheapest Cuban cigar you could buy, and was puffing it so hard the smoke was practically billowing right under Fanny’s nose.

The second Arthur Pendennis had said his goodbyes and wandered off, the sun suddenly looked less bright to Sam, the sky less blue, the pop-gun stalls completely uninteresting, and the bitter beer tasted like hot garbage. He had a pocket full of peas and a pea-shooter he’d brought to mess around with on the drive home, but he completely forgot they existed until a random bloke fired a whole volley of them right in his sad face on the way back to London. Sam swore a little in surprise, then burst out into a savage, bitter laugh.

But Fanny was absolute perfection the whole ride home, cooing and snuggling up to him, laughing all her pretty little laughs, gushing over the silly Jack-in-the-boxes and tiny toy soldiers he’d won her, thanking him over and over. When they got back to the house, Sam was still moping, about to give her a cold, stiff goodbye, when Fanny burst into tears and called him a horrible unkind thing. That was all it took. Sam grabbed her in his arms, swearing she was an angel, that he was a stupid jealous brute, that he had no right to hate Pendennis, begging her to say once more that she—

Well, what did she say? The rest of the question and Fanny’s answer were whispered so close their mouths were nearly touching, no one else could possibly hear a word. Mrs. Bolton just huffed and said, “Come off it, Mr. H, none of that nonsense now. And I think

A full year’s slipped past since that silly little episode we recounted a few chapters back, as you’ve no doubt noticed if you haven’t been napping through the book. Arthur’s funereal all-black mourning coat is soon getting swapped out for a very dashing blue cavalry uniform, and the rest of his person has seen far nicer upgrades: the ridiculous ill-fitting wig he had to hide his post-fever thinning hair under has been tossed in the back of a wardrobe, and his own slightly scanty hair is finally out in public, no longer making him look like a dodgy elderly clerk skulking to a pawn shop. He even had the grand honour of trotting up to Buckingham Palace as a Cornet of the Clavering Yeomanry Cavalry, presented to the King himself by none other than the Marquis of Steyne.

His old uncle the Major basically badgered him into that, by the way. The old gentleman flatly refused to let a year go by without his nephew properly “qualifying as a gentleman” by doing the whole court presentation bit. He thought Arthur ought to belong to clubs far fancier than the rowdy, beer-soaked Megatherium, and he’s been announcing to every single club bore, dinner party guest and random stranger he meets that he’s bitterly disappointed Arthur’s inheritance only comes to fifteen hundred a year, not the fancy fortune he’d daydreamed about snagging for the lad.

On Arthur’s little 1500 a year income, he’s suddenly the most popular bloke on the edge of polite society. His publishers stop treating him like a forgetful intern and start bowing and scraping every time he drops off a manuscript. Mamas with marriageable daughters don’t exactly shove their prettiest, most ambitious girls at him – those ones are all gunning for earls with 10,000 acre estates and private race tracks, duh – but the mamas stuck with the plain ones, the ones with a slightly lopsided shoulder or a habit of reciting amateur poetry mid-roast dinner? They’re tripping over their own silk skirts to get Arthur to propose. One Mrs. Hawxby even forced her daughter Beatrice, who fancied herself a literary genius, to take all her billiard lessons exclusively from Arthur, and refused to ride in any pony cart other than his, until the Major showed up unannounced at her country house party, dragged Arthur out by the scruff of his neck before she could lock them in the linen closet to force a wedding. She still yells about Arthur “leading her innocent daughter on” at every village tea party, for the record.

The Major wanted Arthur to spend Christmas back at Clavering Hall, but the lad couldn’t bear it. Clavering was too close to old Fairoaks, and every corner of the place was stuffed full of too many sad memories of his late mum, so he skipped the trip entirely.

We haven’t checked in on the Claverings in a hot minute, and poor things, they’ve had an absolutely garbage twelve months. Lady Clavering, the sweetest soul who ever spilled gravy on a duchess’s dress and mispronounced every French menu item, has had her famously good temper tested to breaking point by nonstop family drama. “I’d rather eat a plain boiled turnip for dessert, Strong, than that fancy pineapple and all those expensive Muscatel grapes Sir Francis keeps ordering,” she’d confide to her loyal friend, “if only I got ten minutes of quiet to eat it in. I was far happier back when I was a widow, before all this stupid money landed on my lap!”

Their big London social launch was a flop worse than an overbaked pancake. No one liked them, no one gave them credit for the endless fancy dinners they hosted, and everyone gossiped behind their backs nonstop. “Sir Francis Clavering? Total deadbeat,” all the high society snobs said. “Gambles, cheats at cards, runs away from any fight, and his whole household reeks of tacky new money. Who even is that Lady Clavering, with her dropped h’s and random Calcutta fortune? Her daughter Blanche prances around acting like she’s a Parisian aristocrat when she was raised in a boarding school in Bengal! All their friends are shifty bookies and drunk ex-army men!”

The great Lady Rockminster, who’d originally promised to sponsor Lady Clavering into the highest circles, dumped her faster than a hot potato after three parties. All the fancy great ladies refused to bring their daughters to Clavering parties, the young men who did turn up got drunk, ate all the canapes, and acted like they were crashing a cheap pub night. Poor Lady Clavering ended up inviting what she called “the canal set” – nice, unpretentious folks who don’t care about your great-great-grandfather’s title – to her parlour, because none of the top-tier ton would come. The canal set was way more fun, in fairness, but nothing stings worse than high society ditching you first, rather than you ditching them.

We all thought Harry Foker, that silly rich brewery heir, would be the one loyal friend who stuck around. But his huge, ridiculous crush on Blanche Amory that he thought was private somehow leaked all over town, got back to his soft doting mother, and then landed on the desk of his bald, no-nonsense father who ran the Foker brewery. The two of them had a screaming match so loud the neighbours three houses down could hear, and Harry got banished from England for a full year. His dad gave him an ultimatum: come back in 12 months and marry his boring, rich cousin, or you get 300 pounds a year allowance and never see me, your mum, or the brewery ever again. Poor Harry dragged himself off to Europe, so miserable that even the glitzy Paris boulevards looked drab and the famous Italian sun felt like it was drizzling.

That same year, Sir Francis Clavering blew seven thousand pounds on the Derby. For all you turf fans out there, that’s the ridiculous year a random horse called Podasokus – named after some random warrior from the Iliad by a pretentious young lord who thought no one else had read Homer – won the race, while every single so-called “expert” bet on a favourite called Borax that finished so far back no one even saw it cross the line. Sir Francis, who’d been hanging around the shadiest bookies in town bragging he had “top secret inside information”, bet the whole seven grand on the favourite. Poor Lady Clavering had already paid off his card debts a dozen times, listened to a hundred of his oaths promising he’d never gamble again, bailed him out of trouble with horse dealers and money lenders over and over, and now she was on the hook for that massive bill.

Major Pendennis, who’d already nosed his way into every corner of the Clavering household and knew exactly where all their family skeletons were locked up (including the ugly old story of Lady Clavering’s first husband Amory, back in Calcutta) had kept all those secrets quiet this whole time, and let Lady Clavering know he’d never spill them. He told her straight the only reason he’d been such a loyal family friend was that he’d been keen to arrange a mutually beneficial marriage between Arthur and Blanche. “Sentiment’s all well and good, my dear lady,” he’d said, “but Arthur’s a smart, ambitious lad who needs a leg up. What’s the point of Sir Francis’s useless Parliament seat? He never shows up to the house, never says a single word there. Everyone at Oxbridge said Arthur was a brilliant orator. Give him that seat, and he’ll make a name for himself so grand your grandkids will be proud. Love marriages? Rubbish! People elope to Gretna Green over moonlit gazes, then two years later they’re fighting over the dinner menu and one of them runs off to Switzerland with a new lover. The only sensible union is two people who agree to scratch each other’s backs.”

Lady Clavering blubbered that she thought Arthur ought to marry the nice, gentle Laura instead, who everyone adored, but the Major just laughed. “Marry on 500 pounds a year and sentiment alone? My good soul, you’ve gone mad!”

He gave Blanche the exact same speech later, and she thought it was perfectly reasonable. Arthur was perfectly nice, after all, until someone richer came along to catch her eye.

Arthur, for his part, wandered through that spring half-convinced his old fiery romantic days were over. He couldn’t believe he was the same lad who’d had that grand all-consuming passion years ago, and last year was running around like a loon over that actress Fanny. Now he just hung around the Clavering house, was polite to Blanche, didn’t even formally court her, and everyone just sort of accepted he was part of the furniture there.

We don’t really change all that much as we get older, you know. We just stop hiding the traits we’ve always had. The wild passionate teen turns into the jaded, laid-back middle aged bloke who shrugs at empty sentiment. The ambitious guy climbing the social ladder, even when he’s got all the medals and the crowds cheering, still has a quiet little secret ache for the days he was a small kid playing in the garden. The poor wretch shipwrecked and clinging to a floating spar in the middle of the ocean is thinking the exact same thing. No matter where life lands you, deep down, you’re still that same small kid you remember being, before the whole messy voyage of adulthood even started.

Our soft-hearted, famously wealthy “Begum” Lady Clavering was so incandescent with rage at her husband Sir Francis’s latest spectacular act of Derby-day gambling idiocy that she swore she’d never shell out a single penny to cover his honour debts ever again, packed her overnight bag on the spot, and declared she was dumping the useless man to face his furious bookies all on his own.

The cowardly Baronet didn’t even dare come home that night. He hid out at a grubby little Blackfriars Road inn under the boneheaded fake alias “Mr. Francis”, as if that would fool anyone. The hotel boots who schlepped his carpet-bag back to Grosvenor Place snitched immediately to the footman setting the breakfast table, who spilled the tea to the entire servants’ hall, who passed the gossip to Lady Clavering’s right-hand housekeeper Susan Bonner, who relayed the full ridiculous story to her mistress. Within 24 hours, every coachman in London knew Sir Francis was skulking in a low-end inn, and the whole crowd at Tattersall’s was laying side bets on how soon he’d bolt for the Levant to escape his debts.

The next day the pile of post on Sir Francis’s empty study desk reached new, legendary heights: his fired French chef sent his overdue bill straight to Lady Clavering, the fancy mercers, the celebrated milliner Madame Crinoline, and her daughter Miss Amory all dropped their equally massive long-overdue accounts on her too. When Strong (the man whose entire job description was digging Sir Francis out of self-inflicted holes) turned up that afternoon, he found the stack of alarming bills sitting there and sighed so hard his moustache drooped.

Susan Bonner cornered him immediately, even angrier than her mistress. “We won’t pay a farthing of his messes, not if I have any say!” she ranted. “You go right back to that skulking sneak of a Sir Francis, hiding in a cheap pub too coward to face his own wife like a grown man! We bailed him out of jail! Got him a seat in Parliament! Gave him a fancy town house, a fancy country house, fancy horses to ride and fancy dinners to eat! All the clothes on his back are ours! We’re done. We’ll give him a tiny allowance just so he doesn’t starve in a ditch, then we’re leaving him high and dry. That’s my lady’s orders, if she’s got any sense left.”

When Lady Clavering called Strong up to her parlour, he was already mentally running through the dozen tearful speeches that had convinced her to forgive her husband’s nonsense a dozen times before. He laid it on thick: he’d found Sir Francis holed up in that hotel room, sobbing into a bottle of Curacoa, with a straight razor left out on the table like he was even considering doing the world a favour.

Lady Clavering just laughed bitterly. “That spineless worm won’t hurt a hair on his own stupid head,” she said. “Not as long as there’s a shilling of mine he can steal to fund his next dumb bet. His life’s perfectly safe, you can count on that.”
“Pah, he’s worse than her first husband!” Bonner huffed. “At least that one was a wild devil with the courage to back up his own nonsense! This guy will promise you the sun, moon, and stars, then cheat you out of your pin money the very next week.”

Strong spotted the crack immediately: Lady Clavering had already offhandedly mentioned selling her diamonds to pay his debts, which meant she was already halfway to relenting. “You can’t let your little boy Frank grow up with a bankrupt, disgraced dad hanging over his head, can you?” he pleaded. “Tie him down to every ridiculous promise you want, I’ll sign as witness, he won’t dare break ‘em this time. He was blubbering to me that he never meant to bet at all, the whole racing ring tricked him, it’s changed him forever!”
“Sure,” Bonner scoffed. “He’ll break that promise faster than you can light a cigar.”

When Strong trundled back to the Blackfriars hotel, he found Sir Francis still hunched over his Curacoa bottle snivelling, and laid down the law: “If you break your word to her this time? I spill every single rotten secret you’ve been hiding, and you’ll be begging for pennies outside Westminster Abbey by next week. And I’m not the only one who knows your dirty laundry. Old Major Pendennis recognised you the second he stumbled drunk through your front door that night.”
Sir Francis shrieked so loud the hotel walls shook. “Damn him! I’ll kill him!”
“Sure you will,” Strong said, rolling his eyes. “You’re too coward to even pay your valet the two years’ back wages you owe him.” The Baronet proceeded to have a full, dramatic meltdown, wailing that he was the most miserable man in all England, he’d run off to a forest in the middle of nowhere and hang himself from a tree, and sobbed so hard his wig nearly slid off.

That Derby-day “bad wind that blows no one any good” turned out to be a very lucky break for Colonel Altamont, one of Strong’s roommates back at their Shepherd’s Inn chambers. He’d taken 30-to-1 odds against the winning race horse, and on settlement day he walked away with a fat stack of 50-pound notes. Immediately every grifter in London who hung around the Harlequin’s Head pub crawled out of the woodwork to beg him to invest in their dumb schemes: a sunken treasure ship in the Gulf of Mexico stuffed with 380 thousand dollars, worthless Welsh mine shares you could buy for “an old song”, a high-risk tobacco smuggling racket, a satirical magazine that only needed 200 quid to make you a thousand a year, and “perfectly safe” private bills that paid 50% interest, guaranteed by a very specific guy they swore was totally not a con artist. Altamont, for once, didn’t fall for a single one of them. He buttoned his cash deep in his coat pocket, bolted home, locked the front door, and let Strong chase all the hucksters away. Strong only asked for a 20-pound cut out of the winnings for himself, which he swore he’d pay back the second he could.

Altamont was shockingly generous when he had spare cash. He bought his servant Grady a brand new fancy livery, handed poor old drunk Costigan a five-pound note that made him blubber for three whole minutes before he ran straight to the nearest pub, grabbed two bright, eye-searing shawls from Regent Street for their landlady Mrs. Bolton and her daughter Fanny, and even sent Miss Amory an anonymous birthday parcel full of the most hideous gifts ever seen: a brass-inlaid writing desk, a set of garish amethyst jewellery, a musical snuff box, two years-old forgotten “Keepsake” gift annuals, and two neon-bright gown fabrics so loud they hurt your eyes, that made Miss Amory laugh so hard she nearly spilled her tea. He also went wild at a cheap Cheapside auction, buying a plated dinner centrepiece, a set of fancy candlesticks, and a bagatelle board for the regular raucous dinner parties they hosted back at the inn.

Back at Grosvenor Place, Lady Clavering had gutted their over-the-top fancy household after paying off all Sir Francis’s debts, a public scandal so humiliating that everyone in high society thought even less of the Baronet than they already had before. She upped and left for Tunbridge Wells in a huff, refusing to even speak to her reprobate husband ever again. Sir Francis was stuck in London, no staff that respected him, his valet stealing his dress shirts and velvet waistcoat to go dancing at Vauxhall, and every one of his old rich friends snickering at him behind his back. The only place he was happy was the free-flowing dinner parties at Shepherd’s Inn, where they called him “Sir Francis” like he was actually important, let him tell his terrible old jokes, and warble his wobbly little French ballads after Strong sang his rowdy drinking songs and Costigan blubbered out his Irish folk ditties. He whined nonstop to Altamont that his wife wouldn’t even trust him with a single silver teaspoon, locked all the family plate up in the bank “for safekeeping”, and all his servants openly laughed at him behind his back.

Altamont, meanwhile, was drinking more and more, bragging nonstop about his very obviously made-up adventures: he’d fought an overseer in the Australian bush with nothing but an axe, he’d met Napoleon on St Helena, he’d snuck out of a New Zealand port at night to escape his local native wife who paddled after the ship in a canoe screaming. Strong warned him repeatedly not to mess with the spineless Baronet, that the coward might snap and do something stupid if pushed too far, but Altamont just cackled and said he could wrap that useless sack of flour around his little finger.

His chance came when Strong ran out one day on an errand for Sir Francis, and left the two of them alone. The Baronet was at his absolute lowest, moaning that no one gave him enough pocket money to even pay the cab waiting outside the chambers, he’d pawned his wife’s fancy drawing room clock, his buhl inkstand, and her gold duck-head paperweight for a measly 15 shillings and sixpence, and he was so embarrassed he wanted to die. He begged Altamont for cash, first 20 quid, then upping it to a 25-pound “pony”, swearing he’d pay it back no questions asked in six months, and would do absolutely anything Altamont wanted to get his hands on the money. Exactly what Altamont had been waiting for.

Not long after Colonel Altamont hit the Epsom races and walked away with enough cash to keep him in brandy and fine waistcoats for months, the very same gentleman finally booked that foreign jaunt he’d been rambling about for weeks. The gossip columnist who loiters down at London Bridge for the sole purpose of waving off rich people leaving the country duly noted the passenger manifest of the *Soho* bound for Antwerp one Saturday: it featured “Sir Robert Hodge, his lady, their three Miss Hodge’s, Mr. Serjeant Kewsy and his wife and daughter, Colonel Altamont, Major Coddy, and several other persons of distinction.”

The Colonel travelled like he owned the whole Channel, too. He turned up in a velvet-lined tailored travelling getup that cost more than a farm labourer made in a year, chugged brandy and water nonstop the whole crossing without turning green and puking over the rail like every other snobby passenger on board, and had his loyal Irish ex-legionary valet trailing after him to tote his bags, light his cigars, and fetch him extra biscuits whenever he grunted. The same valet had been waiting on both Altamont and Captain Strong back in their messy Shepherd’s Inn digs for months, so he was well used to their nonsense.

Chevalier Strong threw Altamont a raucous send-off dinner at Blackwall, along with a couple of random drinking buddies, all of whom toasted the Colonel’s health over and over, entirely at Altamont’s generous expense. Halfway through the feast, Altamont slammed a fat wallet down on the table and shoved a twenty-pound note at his mate. “Strong old boy,” he sloshed, “you need a little extra cash right now? Name your sum, I’m your man. You’ve always been a good bloke to me, and a twenty-pound note more or less doesn’t even register on my bank balance these days.”

Strong turned him down flat, claiming he was totally flush – well, flush enough that he couldn’t pay back the last loan Altamont gave him, but still had enough stashed to keep the lights on for months. The two parted on surprisingly friendly terms, no hard feelings at all. Strong found himself wondering later, as he trundled back to the inn, had all that new money actually made Altamont a better, nicer man, or was he just easier on the eyes now that he wasn’t permanently skint? He decided it was a little of both, and besides: even a former transported convict like Altamont was ten times the gentleman that stuck-up useless Baronet Sir Francis Clavering was. Altamont had pluck! He’d stick up for a mate, and punch an enemy in the face if he had to. Clavering? That coward would run and hide behind a lady’s petticoat before he faced a bar bill, let alone a brawl. What even got Altamont in trouble in the first place? He’d been a little wild once, and signed his father-in-law’s name on a bit of paper. Half the toffs in the West End had done worse and still waltzed around with their noses in the air, no shame at all.

Strong immediately felt a little stupid for lying about being flush, though. He was so broke he couldn’t even afford to hire the valet he’d gotten used to, which meant now the formerly portly, dignified Chevalier was down in his scullery every morning, polishing his own boots and broiling his own mutton chops to a cinder. The Shepherd’s Inn digs that had felt crowded and chaotic with Altamont around were now so quiet they were depressing. He even started daydreaming about hopping a boat to Spain to go visit his ex-wife Juanita, the fiery Spanish lady from Vittoria who’d only lasted a few months of marriage before she brained him with a dinner dish and went back to her family. He was properly “down on his luck”, as he’d put it – the exact kind of dark patch all great heroes go through, right? Like Marius stuck in the mud at Minturnae, or Bonnie Prince Charlie hiding in the Highlands, or Napoleon hanging out right before Elba. No great man avoids the occasional burnt mutton chop dry spell.

Clavering, for his part, was absolutely no use at all for getting extra cash. The twenty-five quid he’d cadged off Altamont had vanished faster than a pub landlord’s missing change. He’d run off with a crowd of blokes who loved illegal bare-knuckle fights, dodged the police, landed in Essex, and bet all his cash on a cabman called Dick, who was winning thirteen whole rounds before he accidentally punched his opponent right in the windpipe and killed him dead. “It’s always my bloody luck, Strong!” Clavering wailed, throwing his hat on the floor. “The odds were three to one on my guy, I thought that thirty quid was already in my pocket! Now I owe my valet Lightfoot fourteen quid that he lent me, and the little blackguard keeps dunning me for it! I’m this close to begging my lady for a handout, c’mon Ned, if you can think up some way to wangle fifty quid out of her, I’ll give you half, I swear on my honour!”

Strong shut him down instantly, stone-faced. He’d given his word of honour he would never help Clavering pull another money trick on his long-suffering wife, and he’d black his own boots every single day for the rest of his life before he broke that promise. He even threatened to tip Lady Clavering off the second he got wind Clavering was planning to lie to her about bills.

Clavering reacted exactly like you’d expect: he screamed, he cried, he cursed up a storm, said death was his only possible escape, begged his “dearest old Ned, best friend in the whole world” not to betray him… then the second he was out on the Shepherd’s Inn stairs, he was spitting and swearing that Strong was the worst traitor, villain, blackguard and coward who ever walked the earth, and he hoped Ned rotted in hell, but not before Clavering got to take his revenge out on him first.

Right as he was storming out, Clavering nearly bowled over young Mr. Samuel Huxter, the medical student from Clavering town who was always popping in to visit Strong. Sam had grown up poaching walnuts in Clavering Park, watching Sir Francis drive through the high street with four horses and powdered footmen, and he thought the Baronet was the grandest, poshest old English gentleman who ever lived. He stumbled through an introduction, blushing so hard his ears turned pink, mentioning how his dad had treated Sir Francis’s gamekeeper when his gun blew up and took off three fingers. Clavering, for once, was affable enough, and Sam left walking on air. Back at his digs in Bartholomew’s, he wouldn’t stop gushing about Sir Francis to his mates, and told his girlfriend Fanny over at the lodge that the Baronet was the perfect example of a thoroughbred old country gentleman. Fanny just cackled. She thought Sir Francis was horrid. “He’s wicked! And cheap! And mean!” she insisted. “Mum lent him seven shillings once, and it took us three whole months to get it back! He even borrowed half a sovereign off you yesterday, dummy!”

“Say ‘her’ not ‘er’, ‘borrow’ not ‘borrer’, Fanny love,” Sam corrected, being the grammar nerd he was.

“Fine, her, borrow, actually – you still a stoopid,” Fanny teased, yanking his messy hair and patting his chin, and Sam was so smitten he would’ve sat through a hundred grammar lessons from her right then and there. Her mum Mrs. Bolton just rolled her eyes in the corner, wiping a dish.

You’ve probably thought about this, right, fancy rich reader? All that respect everyone lavishes on you in your old age – the servants bowing, the tenants doffing their caps, everyone hanging on your every garrulous old story? The second you kick the bucket, all of it moves straight to your heir. They’ll drink your best aged wine, get all the cheers and the deference, and everyone will stop being sad you’re gone by Tuesday. You can’t expect people to grieve you forever before they start enjoying all your stuff. It’s the way of the world.

And speaking of the world, no one should think Arthur Pendennis is being disrespectful to his dead mum now that his mourning band’s faded a little and he’s got a new white hat. That sainted lady’s memory is locked in the most sacred part of his heart, and every time he’s tempted to do something stupid or mean, he can hear her soft voice telling him not to. He and Warrington sit up late after everyone leaves, talking about her, and the rest of the world can snark all it wants – that kind of love for a good woman doesn’t just vanish. But the second Major Pendennis or any other random mate walks in, they both shut up instantly and start gossiping about the opera or the latest Parliament debate, like every other normal bloke.

Major Pendennis, for his part, was furious that his nephew wasn’t parading around like a proper rich landed gentleman now he’d come into his property. He kept hounding Pen to get fancy new quarters, buy a flash showy cabriolet, and stop living in that “horrible garret” in the Temple. Pen laughed him off. “Uncle, I’m so absent-minded I’d crash the cab straight into a bus, or drive the horse’s head right through a lady’s carriage window. You don’t want me looking like a doddery apothecary behind the reins, do you?” Then he teased his uncle about how his own dad, the doctor, was dead proud when he first bought his little gig, and the Major turned bright red, sputtering that of course the old family was ancient and noble, no one needed to go around bragging that the former head of the house was a doctor.

Pen eventually compromised: he moved down one flight to the second floor in Lamb Court, left Warrington in his old garret, and they still shared the same servant, the same dinners, and the same horse, just like two medieval Knights Templar bunking together. Those young adult friendships are the best ones, right? Your heart’s still soft enough to let someone all the way in, unlike later on when you’ve shaken a thousand casual hands and you can barely even be bothered to learn a new acquaintance’s last name.

Warrington, the grumpy old cynic, absolutely hated the Clavering crowd. He’d met them during that disastrous London season, and told Pen straight up they were all bad news. “Those two rakes hanging over Lady Clavering’s carriage in the Park the other day, leering at Blanche under her bonnet? No proper mother would ever let her daughter within ten feet of those blokes, let alone invite them to dinner.”

Pen leapt to Lady Clavering’s defence, the “Begum” as everyone called her. “She’s the sweetest, most innocent woman alive! She’s never heard a single bad story about any of those rakes, you old grump.”

“Would you let Laura Bell hang around those losers?” Warrington snapped, his face turning red. “That woman’s not a lady, Pen, no matter how good her dinners are. It’s not her fault she never learned refinement, but that doesn’t mean we have to hang out with her.”

Pen got a little sulky right back. “Why are we being such snobs? She’s kinder to every single person she meets than half the fancy duchesses with perfect grammar. She pays her awful husband’s debts, spoils her son, gives you the best dinners she can afford. She’s never done a single bad thing to anyone.”

Warrington growled, “Your laundress is nice too, but you don’t invite her over for three courses and silver plates. Are you telling me the best thing in life is good claret and a fancy dinner? I’d rather eat raw turnips and sleep in a hollow tree than degrade myself that much!”

Pen laughed at him. “Oh please, you say that every time we finish one of those dinners, then you go home and smoke a pipe and tell me it was the best beef you’ve ever eaten. I don’t pretend to be best friends with the Claverings! If someone invites me over and gives me a good meal, I show up, I’m polite, and we part ways without any drama. I don’t owe them my eternal soul, I owe them the polite fake friendliness everyone gives each other in society. If I only hung out with my real friends, you’re the only bloke I’d ever see.”

“Ugh,” Warrington said, sadly shaking his head. “You’re your uncle’s pupil through and through. You’re a total worldling.”

“Damn right,” Pen shrugged. “Why would I pretend the world’s something it’s not? I’m not some twenty-year-old revolutionary with a half-baked plan to reform all of humanity. Those guys all end up selling out the second they get a cushy government job, anyway. You think Thiers or Guizot were screaming for change when they got into power? They ended up compromising just like everyone else. No one’s strong enough to march faster than the whole rest of the world.”

Warrington snickered and poured himself another glass of wine. “If you keep talking like that, Mr. Right Honourable Pendennis, they’ll make you First Lord of the Treasury and Chancellor of the Exchequer by the end of the week.”

CHAPTER LXIII. Which accounts perhaps for Chapter LXI. (Don’t pretend you remembered what that one was about, we barely did.)

Major Pendennis, that seasoned professional meddler who’d built a side career of digging through gentry families’ dirty laundry for the benefit of his nephew Pen, had just finished compiling his Clavering family dossier and had to pause mid-sip of his Madeira to wheeze. The idea of foisting Miss Amory on Pen as a wife, he realised, came with two very alarming bonus fathers-in-law no guardian in their right mind would wish on a ward. The pair were such walking disasters they almost cancelled each other out, sure — but one of them was the fugitive convict Altamont, who’d hacked down a prison guard mid-transit and would be hoisted straight up the gallows the second he set foot within a British magistrate’s line of sight. Even if he stayed hidden, he could wave that secret over Sir Francis Clavering’s head forever, making the hapless Baronet his personal doormat for pocket change for the rest of time.

Worse, the Begum (Lady Clavering, the poor rich sucker who’d bailed her husband out of debt a dozen times already) was on track to burn through every last penny of her fortune on this irredeemable gambling addict. By the time the dust settled, Miss Amory wouldn’t bring Pen a fancy annual income and a safe seat in Parliament — she’d bring him just herself, and a family tree with a giant red scrawled note next to her last male relative’s name that read *sus. per coll.* (hang him, basically).

Scheming old Pendennis was already turning over a third, better plan in his head, and you’ll see how it all shook out if you stick through the very awkward conversation he ended up having with Sir Francis Clavering, Member for Clavering, a man whose greatest life achievement was avoiding all of his responsibilities.

It’s a universal truth: when a guy is drowning in debt, he vanishes from all his usual fancy haunts faster than a rat off a sinking ship. I’ve known a Pall Mall dandy who vanished from every club and park promenade, only to turn up pink-faced and chowing down on whitebait at an 18-penny ordinary in Billingsgate, covered in fish grease. I knew a wit so deep in debt he once sent me an address for a pub called the Fox Under The Hill, tucked down a dark, smelly Strand archway that looked like it was a front for pickpockets. These men might technically have a house, but they’re never in it; they might have an address for letters, but only a total fool would turn up there expecting to see them. Only their very most trusted cronies know their hiding spots.

Which is exactly what happened to Sir Francis Clavering after he’d had a screaming match with his wife and burned through the last of her goodwill. His butler Lightfoot complained to his mate Morgan that Clavering hadn’t been home before 3am in weeks, pretended to be fast asleep when he brought the morning water jug up, and snuck out the back door the second Lightfoot’s back was turned. He was also dodging his old mate Chevalier Strong, who kept yelling at him about unpaid bills — he was basically running from his own conscience, like the rest of us who try out a new coffee shop just to avoid texting our landlord back.

So the Major, who was determined to corner Clavering for his own little scheme, had to chase him for days like a guy hunting a feral cat that stole his sandwich. First he sent Clavering a very innocent-sounding dinner invite to Greenwich, to “meet a few friends” — the friends were just him, but Clavering got suspicious, bailed last minute, and left the Major to eat an entire platter of whitebait alone while seagulls stared at him through the window. Next he wrote asking for ten minutes of Clavering’s time, arranged a meeting *precisely* 4 o’clock the next day at Bays’s club (the Baronet underlined the “precisely” three times, like that would make it less obvious he was going to ghost) — but 4 o’clock rolled around, and no Clavering was anywhere to be found. The man avoided Pendennis like he owed him 200 quid, not zero.

The very same day Strong had publicly blessed Clavering’s face then cursed him behind his back, Clavering had already broken the sworn, Bible-backed oath he’d made to his wife’s advisors to never sign another bill. He’d scribbled his fancy baronet signature on a bit of stamped paper, handed it to his mate Mr. Moss Abrams, and told him to date it two months prior, so he could pretend he’d forgotten all about it when he did his last debt settlement with the Begum. Strong heard all the tea over a cigar at Santiago’s tobacco shop, the back parlor where every bill discounter and minor aristocrat hung out to swap gossip.

“He’s at it again,” Santiago told him, wiping ash off his counter. “Moss dragged him in here ten minutes ago, sent my boy out for a stamp. That’s a 50 pound bill, mark my words. He’ll be drinking the profits in a gambling den before sunset.” A man who signs six-month “promise to pay” slips for pocket change should know the whole of London’s shadiest characters know his signature by heart, at this point.

Santiago’s shop was two steps from St James’s Street, just down the road from the Major’s Bury Street lodgings. The Major was strolling daintily home when he saw Strong striding towards him, mustachios flapping, reeking of cigar smoke. “Ugh, these young cads and their smoke,” the Major thought. “Every fellow with a mustache and a cigar is a low-class type — oh, it’s Mr. Strong. Hope you’re well?” He gave him a stuffy dignified bow, and was about to fumble his key in the lock to escape, when Strong stopped him and held out a hand. The Major shook it with exactly two very ungracious, cold fingers.

“Still in town, Mr. Strong? Hope you’re well,” the Major said, in the tone of a king addressing a street sweeper, since he’d spent 60 years in fancy company and assumed speaking to a non-noble was doing them a favour.

“My news is bad, sir,” Strong grumbled. “It’s about the Tunbridge Wells crew. Clavering’s at his old tricks again.”

“Good heavens, do come in!” the Major said, suddenly very interested, and dragged him up to his drawing room. Strong ranted for ten full minutes about Clavering’s total lack of loyalty. “That man swore on his knees to his wife, rang the bell for a Bible, cursed his own soul to hell if he ever signed another bill — and he’s at it again! There’s even a bill with both our names on it due next week, sir. He begged me not to mention it, I let him, and half the money was mine, for heaven’s sake! I don’t care if they chase me for it, I’m used to it — but it’s Lady Clavering that makes me mad. That sweet woman bailed him out of debtor’s prison a dozen times, and he’s throwing it all away on bill discounters, bare-knuckle boxers, any random rascal that’ll slip him a fiver. Would you believe he’s even been taking money from Altamont? You know who I mean.”

The Major’s face went perfectly blank. “Altamont? That tipsy fellow who turned up to Clavering’s dinner once? Who even is that, Mr. Strong?”

Strong stared at him, so surprised he almost dropped his cigar. “If you don’t know, I sure don’t.”

“To be frank,” the Major said, purring, “I have my suspicions — mind, just suspicions — that our friend Clavering is exactly as loose a fish as any man in London, and he’s definitely got a few skeletons in his closet he doesn’t want anyone digging up. This Altamont fellow almost certainly knows one of those skeletons, and he’s milking Clavering for every penny over it. Half the best families in England are paying hush money like that, these days. Their private business isn’t mine. I’m only involved for Lady Clavering and her daughter’s sake, they’re charming creatures. But say, is this Altamont in town, then?”

Strong stood up, grabbed his hat, completely offended by the Major’s slippery, patronizing act. “I don’t think I’m obliged to tell you where he is, Major Pendennis,” he huffed.

The Major’s whole demeanour flipped instantly, from haughty to chuckling, matey know-it-all. “Ah, Captain Strong, you’re being clever too, and right you are! Walls have ears, my friend, no use firing off your mouth about secrets that don’t do anyone any good. I’m the same — I won’t know a single thing I don’t have good reason to know, and that’s what any sensible man does. And we’re totally on the same page that Clavering needs to be reined in, before he ruins his whole family.”

“Of course,” Strong said, drily, still not buying the act.

“Wonderful! Delighted we agree. So glad I ran into you today. Good evening, Morgan, show Captain Strong out!”

Strong left more confused than he’d arrived, and the Major’s valet Morgan was equally perplexed — he’d been lingering by the door for the whole second half of the conversation, eavesdropping like the professional he was, and half of what he’d heard made zero sense.

The next time he ran into Lightfoot at the valets’ secret gossip club, Morgan asked, “Who’s this Altamont bloke? You know what he and Strong are up to?”

“Strong does Clavering’s odd jobs, signs his bills for him, the usual kite-flying racket that needs three people to work,” Lightfoot shrugged. “Altamont won a pile of money betting on the Derby. I just wish Clavering could get a big windfall so he pays me the 14 quid he owes me.”

“You think the Begum will pay his debts again?” Morgan said. “Find that out for me, and I’ll slip you a nice bonus, mate.”
