Major Pendennis joked all the time that Morgan was way richer than he was, and he wasn’t wrong. The quiet, sneaky valet had spent years hoarding cash, schmoozing with other fancy families’ valets to learn their masters’ secrets. When Pen inherited his fortune, Morgan had even sidled up to him and offered to lend him 50 or 100 quid if he was short before his rents came in, like a tiny, very polite loan shark. Pen thought it was the funniest joke he’d ever heard, the idea of Morgan the valet being a capitalist, and turned him down laughing before telling his uncle the story.

The Major laughed so hard he had to sit down. “That rascal’s been getting rich for decades. He owns the house I’m renting right now, for God’s sake! He bought up all of Clavering’s old bills after he found out the Begum was bailing him out, and made a killing. Sly as a fox, that Morgan.”

The day after Strong’s visit, the Major cornered Morgan to ask him about the Clavering bills. “I hear you’re investing money these days, Morgan,” he said.

“Blast that Mr. Arthur for blabbing,” Morgan thought. Out loud he said, “Thank you, sir, no complaints about my post.”

“Good man, glad you’re doing well. Don’t go and sink your savings into a public house, though, that’s a fool’s game.”
*Public house?* Morgan thought. *Me? This old fool! I’ll be sitting in Parliament before I die, mark my words.* “No, sir, no plans for that. My savings are all put out safely, sir.”
“You do a little bit of bill discounting, eh?”
“Very little, sir — if you’ll pardon my asking, sir, do you think Sir Francis Clavering’s paper is still any good? Will Lady Clavering pay them off anymore, sir?”
“You’ve already been buying his bills, have you?”
“A little bit, sir,” Morgan said, dropping his eyes all demure. “Got access to the family news through your good self, sir, thought I might as well make a profit as anyone else. I’d be ever so grateful if you could tell me if the Begum will pay out again.”

The Major was so shocked he almost dropped his teacup — he felt like he’d just found out his cat was secretly a millionaire. But he respected success, even when it was under his own nose, and suddenly found himself a little bit intimidated by his valet. “Well, Morgan, I shan’t ask how much you’ve made. If Lady Clavering asks me what to do, I’ll tell her not to pay another penny — though I fear she’s too soft, and will anyway. I heard there’s a new bill out with that Mr. Moss Abrams, for 50 quid. Can you find out the details, and tell me where I can corner Clavering?”

“Of course, sir, right away,” Morgan said, bowing and slipping out of the room like the quiet shadow he was.

The next morning Morgan came back with the intel: the bill was for 50 pounds, and Clavering would be camped in the back parlour of the Wheel of Fortune Tavern at 1 o’clock sharp that day.

The Major showed up right on time, pushed open the door of the back parlour, and found Sir Francis slouched in a Windsor chair, surrounded by overflowing spittoons, prints of bare-knuckle boxers and trotting racehorses, and a beer-stained copy of Bell’s Life in London that reeked of old tobacco. He looked up, saw the Major, and groaned.

“So it’s you, old boy?” he said, disappointed, because he’d been expecting Moss Abrams to show up with his cash.

“Good day, Sir Francis,” the Major said, smiling like a shark. “I wanted a word, so I followed you here.”

Clavering’s face immediately turned sulky. “What’s your problem, Pendennis? Why are you hounding me all over London, sticking your nose in my business? I never borrowed a penny from you! I never did you any harm! If my wife has got a problem with me, she can send her lawyers. I don’t want you meddling, and I won’t have it.”

After a disastrous London season full of screaming matches with her husband, Lady Clavering beat a hasty retreat to a cozy little villa tucked on the edge of Tunbridge Wells’ most postcard-perfect common. Her daughter Blanche came along, obviously, and holiday-bound 13-year-old Frank Clavering traipsed back too, whose favorite home pastime for the first 48 hours was picking petty fights with his stepsister Blanche. That little hobby died fast, though, once he discovered Tunbridge had cricket, rental horses, and a whole crew of other 13-year-old chaos gremlins who turned up at the Begum’s villa every single day. The lot of them gorged on tarts, snuck way too much champagne, raced ponies across the lawn until Lady Clavering nearly fainted from fright, and smoked cheap cigars until the dining room reeked so bad Blanche threatened to camp out in the garden shed. She did not care for the company of 13-year-old gentlemen, thank you very much.

Blanche, for her part, was the sort of girl who got restless if the wallpaper stayed the same longer than 72 hours, so the country novelty hit just right. For a whole week she’d have happily pretended to live in a cottage on bread and cheese, for the aesthetic! For a single night, she’d even have volunteered to camp in a dungeon on bread and water, for the drama! So the move to Tunbridge was no hardship at all. She wandered the woods, scribbled passable sketches of gnarled trees and farmhouses, hoarded French novels like they were being outlawed, drove into town at the first whiff of any conjurer, mediocre local ball, or third-rate touring musician, napped 14 hours a day, bickered with her mother and Frank before breakfast, wandered into the village school to coo at the pupils and pick fights with the schoolmarm, then snapped at the kids and laughed at the teacher ten minutes later. And of course, she was at the tiny local church every Sunday: a precious little Anglo-Norman gem that was practically brand new, decked out in stained glass, carved saint heads, gilded scripture quotes, and unreserved open pews.

Blanche immediately set to stitching a hyper-accurate high-church altar cover, and completely bamboozled the local vicar, our old friend Smirke, Pen’s former tutor and the man who’d once tried to court Helen Pendennis. He’d gotten over that rejection by marrying a nice young lady his Clapham-raised mother picked out for him, and after his mother passed away, his church views got steadily more and more dramatic. He cut the collars off his coats, grew his hair down past his shoulders, ditched his dorky little forehead curl and the prized fancy cravat he’d once been very proud of, stopped wearing any necktie at all, skipped dinner every Friday, read the Roman Hours, and announced he was ready to hear confessions in the vestry. The local dissenting pastor Muffin and old church’s Mr. Simeon Knight spent weeks yelling that he was a secret, dangerous Jesuit working directly for the Pope. The chapel of ease he’d built was paid for entirely by his mother’s Clapham inheritance—goodness only knows what that strait-laced old lady would have said if she saw a table being called an altar, candlesticks sitting on it, or letters dated for the Feast of Saint Whozit.

But Smirke was the most harmless creature alive, no secret Pope postbags ever showed up at his door. At first he thought Blanche was the most pious, talented, charming soul he’d ever met, and when Blanche spent two full hours locked in the vestry chatting with him, his wife Belinda paced up and down the tiny two-grave churchyard daydreaming of adding a third, or running off to a convent to become a nun. It took Smirke a few weeks to wise up: he noticed Blanche threw a screaming fit at the school, lost her temper, and rapped little Polly Rucker’s knuckles very hard. The second Blanche stopped fawning over him for a few days, he sighed, blushed, and admitted to Belinda that he’d been tricked, all her piety was just a fun new hobby for her. Belinda launched herself straight into his arms, no more convent plans, no more gravestone daydreams, and he cooed at her that there was no other woman half as precious as her. Blanche, for her part, forgot both of them existed the second she walked out the church gate.

That stage of indifference hadn’t hit yet when Arthur Pendennis turned up to spend a few days visiting the Begum. Smirke was still fully convinced Blanche was a literal angel, and gushed to Pen for hours about how much better she was than even the opera he’d snuck off to see once (the same one he’d gone to with Helen and Pen back at Chatteris, which he still felt guilty about). Pen found Smirke’s goofy, sincere high-church schtick absolutely delightful, and all his over-the-top praise of Blanche made Pen look at his old acquaintance with very favorable new eyes.

Blanche was absolutely thrilled to see Pen, too: he was fresh from London, full of all the latest gossip, and could rattle off that beloved London small talk none of the stuffy locals understood. The first night he arrived, he made Blanche laugh so hard through dinner she barely had time to pick a fight with anyone. She even hugged and kissed her mother out of nowhere, shocking the honest Begum half out of her wits. When bedtime rolled around, Blanche sighed the prettiest, most dramatic “Déjà!” possible, squeezed Pen’s hand so tight it left a mark, and Pen wandered out to his window to smoke a cigar, convinced that Blanche had gotten immeasurably nicer and sweeter.

“Ooh, heavenly!” a voice trilled out the window next to his, Blanche’s, from behind a clematis-covered casement. Pen snort-laughed, and asked her not to snitch about him smoking. “Go on! I adore the smell!” Blanche called back. “Heavenly night! Heavenly, heavenly moon! But I have to shut my window now, can’t chat with you out here, the locals would have fits about les moeurs. How ridiculous those old decency rules are, aren’t they? Adieu!”

The next day they wandered the fields together, gossiping like old friends about their younger days, Blanche getting all soft and sentimental, gushing about Laura like she was her long-lost sister, begging Pen to bring her down to Tunbridge for visits. The day after that, they rambled through the woods to Penshurst to see the famous old park and hall, and Smirke rambled to Pen even more about Blanche’s supposed perfect piety. Pen spotted her sitting in the schoolhouse “instructing the children,” and fully bought that she was the sweetest, most unworldly, unspoiled girl alive.

“Do you really like the country this much?” he asked her, as they walked the path back.

“I never want to see that horrible smoky London face ever again,” Blanche sighed. “Good thoughts grow here in the quiet woods, like those little delicate flowers that wilt the second you stick them on a London balcony. But… heigho.”

“What’s that sigh for, Blanche?”

“Never mind. I wish you’d never come here,” she sniffled, pulling out her best new collection of sad verses, Mes Soupirs. “Not because I don’t want you—because I don’t want to be sad when you leave.” And then the tears started, Mes Larmes making a very dramatic encore.

What’s a young man supposed to do when a pretty girl cries in a summer wood, surrounded by roses and dewy grass and wildflowers? Two very cynical, seen-it-all London rakes, who’d both weaseled their way out of a dozen worse flirtations, suddenly conned themselves completely. For a whole glowing afternoon, they were no longer jaded city social climbers. They were just simple, sweet Phyllis and Corydon, madly in love under the trees. It’s no wonder almost no single person leaves a week of country walks still unmarried. The place is a total romance scam, and it works every single time.

Arthur Pendennis, who normally rambled to his mate Warrington so freely he’d even admit to accidentally reciting bad love poetry to a barmaid once, was suddenly being as tight-lipped as a tax collector when it came to Blanche Amory. He’d chatter for hours about his old tutor Smirke’s weird little Anglo-Norman church, his new Roman holiday plans, and his very bizarre wife, but the second anyone asked how his visit to the Tunbridge Wells villa went? Suddenly he was the king of vague non-answers. “Oh, she’s a clever, nice little thing, could make a decent wife if someone pointed her in the right direction, but marriage? Me? Nah, my romance days are done, I’m perfectly happy drinking bad punch at the Temple and going to the theater with you, mate.”

Of course, the second he got back to his Lamb Court rooms, little satin envelopes would start appearing in his post, scrawled in the neatest tiny handwriting ever, sealed with a fancy cypher that spelt out B.A. if you squinted hard enough. Warrington never pried, so he never noticed Pen spending three hours crafting gallant replies full of town gossip, sharp little jokes, and probably some very flowery verses right back at Blanche’s own clunky little love rhymes. Turns out those very same verses Pen was scribbling in reply to Blanche later ended up published in *Roseleaves*, the fancy annual edited by Lady Violet Lebas, full of Pinkney’s over-the-top portraits of aristocratic ladies—like Pen had just decided to repurpose his flirty notes as highbrow literature, no shame whatsoever.

Old Major Pendennis caught him scribbling one of these sappy effusions at the club while waiting for his dinner, and immediately leaned over to pat his nephew’s shoulder like he was handing down top-secret state secrets. “Verses are all well and good, lad, same with friendly letters to old country pals—just don’t go and commit yourself, yeah? I never wrote a single letter in my whole life that could get me trapped into anything, and demmy, I’ve got decades of experience messing about with women. Let me tell you the horror stories: young Spoony once wrote a gushy poem to widow Naylor, next thing he knows her brother the Colonel is banging on his door demanding he marry her, and she was old enough to be his mother! Then there was Hopwood of the Blues, who kept all Miss Louisa Salter’s old letters, pulled them out the second she got engaged to Sir John Bird, and the whole wedding fell apart, and she ended up marrying some girl from Lyme Regis. Absolute chaos if you don’t watch your step.”

Pen laughed, blushed a little, and swore he’d be careful. Which he absolutely was—he’d already learned his lesson with the Fotheringay, after all, no way he was getting roped into anything without an easy escape route. He knew if he told Warrington any of this, his mate would call him a coward, say only liars and cowards court a woman with a pre-planned exit door behind them, so he just never brought it up. He knew he was being a slippery rascal, and he didn’t want to get lectured about it.

Over in Shepherd’s Inn, poor Captain Strong was having a far worse time than Pen, all because he’d been too much of a gentleman to take Altamont’s generous cash gift when his friend headed off for a gambling holiday on the continent. He’d regretted that decision within 24 hours. Why turn down a perfectly good pouch of cash from a mate who’d won it fair and square at the races, just because it was a little morally murky? Now all his tradesmen had gotten wind that he and Sir Francis Clavering had dissolved their partnership, and suddenly every single bill he’d put off for two years was being shoved under his door at once. Tailors, bootmakers, pastry chefs who’d catered his fancy dinner parties, all were camped out on his stairs, yelling about owed money. Worse, there were sneaky little lawyer’s clerks skulking around the Inn, with writs in their pockets ready to drag him straight to debtor’s prison if they caught him stepping out his door.

Strong, no fool, barricaded himself in his chambers immediately. He’d been a soldier, after all—this was just a silly civilian siege, he could handle it. He’d peek through the curtain over his letter slot to watch furious duns bang on his door and storm off yelling, and he’d even set up a secret early warning system with his pals: Huxter and Fanny Bolton had taught Fanny’s tiny little sisters to do a very specific, ridiculous yodel that meant “duns are hiding around the corner, don’t come out.” They’d bring him porter and bread and cold meat to keep his garrison fed, so he didn’t starve to death behind his locked door.

He also had a top secret escape route, that only him and Altamont had known about: his kitchen window was two feet away from Bows and Costigan’s spare room window next door, connected by a wobbly old lead gutter pipe. He’d snuck over that pipe dozens of times when the duns were lined up at his front door, waltz out the neighbor’s front step, go eat a full roast dinner at the Albion, catch a play, and slip back home at midnight, leaving all the sheriff’s officers very confused as to where he’d vanished to. He waited weeks for Clavering to send him even a fiver to help him out, but that useless coward didn’t reply to a single one of his letters. Strong was this close to giving up entirely, when who should stumble back into his life but Altamont, fresh from Baden-Baden, flush with hundreds of pounds he’d won at the roulette tables.

Altamont paid off every single one of Strong’s debts that same afternoon, dragged Pen out to Richmond for a celebratory feast, and ordered a magnum of the best claret the moment they sat down. “Cheers to the lads who bail their mates out when they’re properly stuck!” he yelled, blushing a little, before he rambled on for an hour about his brand new 100% infallible gambling system. No way it could lose! He just needed a little more capital, and Strong to come along with him to man the table, since Strong was far calmer than Altamont when he was drunk on luck. He even made everyone toast little Fanny Bolton, who’d lent Strong her entire life savings of two pounds when he was this close to getting dragged off to prison.

Pen told his uncle the Major about running into the pair a couple days later, and the Major immediately acted like he’d never heard the name Altamont in his entire life. “Never met the bloke in my life,” he said, deadpan, “best you steer well clear of him, Arthur.” Then he went on to explain that the Clavering parliamentary seat he’d been offered was totally above board, no ethical issues at all—every big name in government was doing the exact same thing, no reason to be a prude about it. Pen didn’t exactly buy it, but he wanted to believe it, so he did.

When he next headed down to Tunbridge Wells to visit Blanche, he told her the whole ridiculous story of Strong’s siege, his pipe escape route, Fanny lending him that two pounds, and how Fanny was now happily engaged to Huxter the apothecary’s assistant. He made a big dramatic show of sighing over how sad it was that pretty girls’ grand first loves always ended with them marrying some boring bloke who smelled like cough syrup, which made Blanche immediately squint at him and tease him that he was obviously just jealous that Huxter had stolen his old admirer.

For once, Pen couldn’t even argue. He was jealous! How dare someone as plain, unfunny, and unremarkable as Huxter get the girl who’d once pined after him? The two of them went back and forth, sniping at each other about their failed old romances, neither one willing to admit they actually liked each other, neither one willing to say anything so sappy as a love confession. “Why should we pretend to be the heroes and heroines of silly novels?” Pen said, shrugging. “I won’t lie and call you the most perfect woman in the world, if you don’t lie and tell me I’m the grandest gentleman alive. We’re both clever, both good fun to hang out with. That’s more than most couples get, right?”

Blanche curtsied, rolled her eyes, and smiled. “Merci, Monsieur the Misanthrope. At least you’re honest. Even if you are the most heartless creature I’ve ever met.”

It was a very slippery, very cynical sort of temptation, all right—one that neither of them was quite sure they wanted to say no to.

The great drafty mausoleum that was Clavering Park had been pretty dismal back in the days when its bankrupt owner was hiding out as a refugee abroad, but somehow it managed to be even more joyless now that Sir Francis Clavering was actually living in it. 90% of the mansion was boarded up and locked tight, and the baronet camped out in just a handful of ground-floor rooms, waited on by his housekeeper and a part-time helper from the lodge gate, who cooked half the game he wasted entire dreary mornings wandering the grounds shooting. His valet Lightfoot had abandoned him for a post in Lady Clavering’s household, and as Pen learned in a gushing note from Mr Smirke who officiated the whole thing, the clever lad had followed through on his plan to marry Mrs Bonner, Lady Clavering’s head maid. The mature, thrifty Bonner had fallen head over heels for the young man’s charm, handed over all her life savings, and the pair of them were dead set on running the Clavering Arms pub as soon as they finished their current jobs at quarter day. Pen graciously promised he’d host his victory election dinner there the second Sir Francis stepped down and handed the borough seat over to him, so naturally when Pen showed up for his September visit, courtesy of his uncle who could get Clavering to agree to literally anything, the baronet was over the moon. Finally, a companion to end his loneliness! And probably a guy who’d lend him a bit of spare cash!

Pen handed over that very desirable cash just two days after he arrived, and no sooner was the money in Sir Francis’s pocket than he suddenly remembered he had urgent business in Chatteris and all the fancy nearby watering holes the shire had to offer. Off he trotted to sort his “affairs” – which, for anyone who knew him, meant binging at the county races and the local billiard halls for days on end. Pen didn’t mind being left alone at all; he had stacks of books, a fat cigar, and a pile of blank paper to keep him occupied through the long nights, and in all honesty he’d found three days of Sir Francis’s sloshed rambling unbearable enough that he’d practically jumped at the chance to hand over the loan just to send the man packing.

What’s more, he had work to do: he needed to win over the Clavering townsfolk and all the borough voters, and he was extra motivated to shake off the old reputation he’d earned as a snooty, sulky young dandy ten years prior. His mischievous sense of humor made the whole game a blast. The usually reserved, quiet Pen suddenly turned into Captain Strong’s carbon copy: loud, jovial, ready to laugh at literally anyone’s joke, shaking hands so hard and warmly you’d swear he’d known every farmer and tradesman his whole life. He showed up for market day and the farmers’ lunch at the pub, schmoozed every person who would talk to him, and behaved exactly like a perfectly seasoned politician – the exact sort of upstanding high-birth gentleman who acts like your best friend the second he wants something from your vote. It’s a funny little quirk of human nature, isn’t it? We all know that over-the-top charm is fake, cheap as counterfeit coin, that the flattery costs them nothing to hand out to every single voter – but we all take it anyway, rather than turn down free nice things.

Pen popped in on the new liberal Clavering Athenaeum the progressive lot had built specifically to spite the stuffy old upper-class reading room that banned tradesmen and once nearly refused to even let copies of the *Edinburgh Review* cross its threshold. The good Doctor Portman was off at Harrogate treating his gout with his whole family, which Pen pretended to be devastated about in a very polite, heartfelt letter begging for the old reverend’s future advice – but privately he was thrilled, because it meant he could cozy up to the opposition preacher Mr Simcoe, the two cloth-factory partners from Chatteris, and the local Independent minister all with zero pushback. He invited the younger cloth-factory partner over for a casual dinner at the park, dropped off hares and partridges at the Simcoes’ house and made a big show of begging to read Simcoe’s latest sermon, and even faked a little mild upset stomach so he could go pay a visit to local apothecary Mr Huxter, who was over the moon to get a fancy young gentleman as a patient. Old Pendennis would have been so proud of his star pupil, Pen cackled to himself, and his little wins had him in a wicked, excellent mood.

But when he wandered home late one night after chairing an Athenaeum meeting, crossed the old familiar bridge over the rushing Brawl, and spotted his old childhood home Fairoaks peeking through the trees, all that glee drained right out of him. He could see a faint light glowing in the upstairs bedroom window where his mother had sat up for hours worrying, praying, and waiting for him when he was a boy. He averted his eyes fast, like the light was the old lady’s ghost watching him, disappointed in all the silly politician games he was playing. The midnight toll from the town clock jolted him out of his sad reverie, and he trundled back through the lodge gates, under the rustling lime tree arcade back to the park.

The next afternoon he called on Fairoaks’ new tenant, Captain Stokes of the Bombay Artillery, who gave him a full tour of the grounds, bragged about the new duck pond he’d dug in the back garden, and begged Pen and his wife to come over for dinner as soon as they had time. Pen admitted straight up he’d been putting the visit off for two weeks because he couldn’t bear to see the place he’d grown up in full of strangers. The captain got it, no questions asked, and soon his wife came down in her best dress, surrounded by their kids, who were clambering all over the old worn armchair that used to belong to Pen’s dad – the chair Pen never would have dared climb in as a kid, not even if you’d offered him a king’s ransom. When Stokes’ daughter sat down at the old piano to play, Pen didn’t listen to a single note she played; all he could hear was Laura singing, and his mom tapping her foot along behind her in the old days. The dinner that followed was dreadfully awkward and dull, until the local barber doubling as a part-time waiter Hodson dropped a whole plate right over Pen’s shoulder. Squire Hobnell cackled that Hodson’s hands were obviously slippery with bear grease, that he’s always dropping crockery, and Pen burst out laughing so hard the whole table loosened up. For the rest of the night everyone took turns making terrible, unfunny jokes about making Hodson cut the hare for the second course, and every single guest thought it was the funniest bit of banter they’d ever heard in their lives.

Meanwhile, back in London, Blanche Amory was in town from her Tunbridge villa on a big shopping trip, chaperoned by none other than Mrs Bonner, who was gearing up to quit service, marry Lightfoot, and become landlady of the Clavering Arms. The old nurse wanted to buy all her favorite loved ones goodbye presents before she left, so she dragged Blanche along to help pick stuff out. She bought Blanche a massive fancy Bible, and Blanche talked her into buying an equally huge copy of Johnson’s Dictionary for her mom, both of them very pleased with how thoughtful those gifts were. Bonner also stocked up on bargains of linen for the pub, and picked out a bright red and yellow neck handkerchief that was very obviously meant for Lightfoot – who was already dripping with cheap pins, rings, shirt studs, and chains Bonner had lavished on him, despite the 25 year age gap between them.

When they were done in the shop, Blanche smiled sweetly at the shop boy and asked him to point them the way to Shepherd’s Inn. Bonner was confused the second they stepped out the door. “Shepherd’s Inn? What on earth do you want there, Miss Blanche? Captain Strong lives there, are you going to pay him a visit?” Blanche said no, she wanted to go thank that nice Fanny Bolton girl who’d nursed Pen back from the brink of death the year before, and she’d even dug out a whole pile of her old dresses to give her as a thank you. Bonner immediately started mentally staffing her future pub with the ugliest, most hunchbacked 70-year-old barmaids imaginable, the kind that could never steal her sweet Lightfoot away, and mumbled something about not liking pretty young girls hanging around single men.

Blanche, all dolled up in her frills, shot-silk dress, fancy mantle, and parasol, floated into Shepherd’s Inn like Queen Victoria making a royal charity tour, making the scrubbing woman at the lodge gape. She asked for Fanny Bolton, only to be told Fanny had popped up to Captain Strong’s rooms, so off they went up the stairs. Loitering in the courtyard, old swaggering Captain Costigan caught a sight of Blanche peeking out from under her bonnet and whistled to himself: “Begor, that’s a foine young gyurll, heading up to Sthrong and Altamont’s rooms. They do always have a foine line of visitors up them stairs.” A second later he heard bloodcurdling shrieks coming from the second floor, and he charged up the stairs as fast as his old wobbly legs could carry him, banged so hard on Strong’s door the whole frame shook. Strong cracked the door half an inch, growled “Go to hell, Cos!” and slammed it right in his face, leaving the old soldier stomping back down the stairs muttering about duels and how he’d get his revenge for that insult.

What he didn’t know, and you lucky readers do, was this: Blanche’s long-presumed-dead father Amory, who was now going by the name Altamont, had arranged to meet a pair of actresses from a local theatre at the chambers that exact same afternoon, to take them on a day trip down the river to Greenwich. His servant Grady saw two ladies walk through the door, assumed they were the thespians, and ushered them straight inside without a single question. The two fancy bouquets sitting on the table were for the actresses, and Blanche was happily sniffing the roses, peeking behind the curtains, cooing over the prints and the Clavering estate map on the wall, when a roar boomed out of the back bedroom: “Grady! My coat!”

Mrs Bonner froze, that voice was all too familiar. The bedroom door flew open, and out stepped Amory in his shirtsleeves, no coat, very confused to see two women he didn’t recognize. Mrs Bonner shrieked so loud the windows rattled, screamed his name “Amory! Amory!” and fainted dead away right in her chair. He stared for a second, then rushed over to Blanche, grabbed her, and planted a big smacker on her cheek. “That’s right, Betsy, it’s me! Don’t tell your mom I’m alive, it’s a big secret, everyone thinks I’m dead! Look how pretty you’ve grown, come on, give your old dad a kiss!” Blanche screamed too, over and over, those are the exact shrieks Costigan heard wafting down the stairs. Strong came rushing in the second he heard the noise, saw their faces, understood immediately what had happened, and sent Grady down to stand in the courtyard and lie to everyone who knocked that no one was home, the whole day trip party had already left hours ago. When Costigan later buttonholed Grady to ask what all the screaming was about, Grady just shrugged. “Oh, it was some woman. And if you ask me, women are the cause of almost every bit of mischief that ever happens in the whole world.” Blanche never got to visit Fanny Bolton that day, and the two actresses never got their fancy Greenwich river trip, either.

Back in his home shire, Arthur Pendennis was deep in his self-serving little parliamentary schemes, schmoozing farmers, practicing his campaign smile, and daydreaming of the fancy desk he’d have in the House of Commons, when a messenger rode in with news: Lady Rockminster and her entire chaotic household had touched down at nearby Baymouth, and they’d brought Laura with them.

Pen immediately broke out in a cold sweat. Laura, his late mother’s favorite, the girl who could spot a half-truth faster than a farm dog spots a dropped sausage, was the one person on the entire planet he actually cared about impressing. She was his mom’s last bequest to him, after all—he was supposed to be her patron, her protector, the cool cousin she could brag about. How the hell was he supposed to explain that he was angling for a seat in Parliament and an engagement to the wealthy, performative Blanche Amory, just to chase a pile of cash and a silly little title of prestige? He was half-convinced both his secret scheme and Blanche herself would melt under Laura’s calm, unimpressed stare, like cheap wax held over a candle. So he sat down to write her a letter, full of flowery protestations of love, silly jokes, and playful snark, and halfway through scribbling it he had to pause and go, *Oh no. I’m acting like a total rogue and a hypocrite. She’s gonna see right through this.*

Why was a simple country girl making this polished, worldly, well-educated gentleman quake in his boots like a truant schoolboy staring down the headmaster? Because all his fancy social tricks, his snarky comebacks, all his clever diplomacy, were useless against her pure, unvarnished honesty. He couldn’t bring himself to tell that good, honest soul the full truth of what a sellout he was being. The whole ride from Clavering to Baymouth, he felt exactly like a kid who forgot to study for his arithmetic test and is about to walk into the classroom—because face it, truth is the scariest headmaster of all, he always has the answer key, and he never cuts you slack.

Laura, for her part, had thrived way more than anyone expected in the year she’d spent as Lady Rockminster’s companion. Most girls raised in Helen Pendennis’s smothering, all-consuming love would’ve crumpled the second they were tossed out into big, indifferent society. Helen thought the whole world revolved around her two kids, fussed over Laura if she so much as had a mild headache like no human being had ever had a sore head before, and hovered over her every waking second—then she was gone, and Laura was left alone in a household full of strangers. There were snitty servants sometimes, rude distant relatives of Lady Rockminster’s who made her feel like an intruder at family dinners, and days where no one even noticed she was sad. But instead of moping, she just thought, *Hey, there are thousands of governesses and paid companions way worse off than me. My lot’s infinitely better than most!* and smiled her way through every awkward moment.

Turns out that uncomplicated, warm, unassuming cheer is a superpower. Within a year, literally everyone in Lady Rockminster’s household was wrapped around her finger. The old lady’s personal maid, who’d survived 40 years of daily yelling, clawing, and snark from her employer, and had run off 15 previous companions, fought a battle with Laura’s own maid Martha over who got to nurse Laura through a fever in Paris, when Lady Rockminster herself wouldn’t go near the sickbed for fear of catching it. The fancy chef Grandjean nearly poisoned her with 17 different rich delicacies the second she started recovering, and cried happy tears when she ate her first plain slice of chicken. The Swiss butler sang her praises in every mangled European language he knew, the coachman fought other families’ drivers for the best parking spots to give her a smooth ride, the 12-year-old page boy sobbed when he heard she was ill, and the two giant, stoic footmen Calverley and Coldstream—men who would’ve watched the entire house burn down without even blinking to preserve their dignity—got the page boy absolutely sloshed at a local wine shop to celebrate her getting better. Even Lady Diana Pynsent, who’d hated Laura on sight, started gushing to everyone that Lady Rockminster had hit the companion jackpot. None of this was flattery or clever scheming, mind you—Laura was just nice, and that was enough.

Pen rolled up to the house half-expecting to find Laura tucked away in a back closet, treated like a lowly skivvy, but before he could even ring the bell she came thundering down the stairs and threw her arms around him right in front of Calverley and Coldstream. Those two didn’t so much as twitch. If Laura had whipped out a pair of scissors and snipped Pen’s head off mid-hug, they’d have calmly fetched a dustpan and acted like nothing out of the ordinary had happened.

Pen stared at her and nearly dropped his hat. When did his quiet little cousin turn into this glowing, rosy-cheeked, impossibly pretty girl? Her eyes were bright, she was clearly thriving, and when she smiled at him he felt so guilty and soft he could barely speak. “You’re way too good to me, Laura—sister,” he mumbled. “I don’t deserve this.”

“Mamma left you to me, remember?” she said, brushing a stray hair off his forehead with a quick kiss. “We had a pact before we parted, remember? You come find me when you’re in trouble, or when you’re wildly happy. Which is it right now? Are you giddy about running for Parliament? I can’t wait to watch you panic through your first speech!”

Pen blurted, “Wait—you already know about the election plan?”

“Know it? The entire county knows it!” she laughed. “Lady Rockminster’s doctor was chattering about it at breakfast this morning. The Chatteris paper’s gonna print it tomorrow, I bet. Everyone’s saying Sir Francis Clavering is gonna step down just to hand you the seat. And that you’re betrothed to the beautiful young Miss Blanche Amory—”

“Even that?” Pen yelped.

“Even that!” Laura said, grinning. “Tout se sait, as the French say. Blanche sent me the sweetest possible letter, she rants about how clever and wonderful you are nonstop. When’s the wedding? Can I come live with you two afterwards? I’m a fantastic housekeeper now, I even took cooking lessons from Grandjean in Paris!”

“Everything I have is yours, Laura,” Pen said. But for some stupid reason, he felt weirdly stabby jealous at the thought of some hypothetical random guy who might one day come along and marry Laura, and take her away from them. He was half-convinced she’d be mad about his plans for Blanche, but she was just happy he was happy, and that made him unreasonably peeved for no reason he could name.

Laura rattled on about how she’d take singing lessons with the money he’d sent her, learn to dance, be the resident aunt to his future kids, and did a perfect, over-the-top Parisian curtsey for him right as Lady Rockminster stomped into the room. She gave Pen exactly one single finger to shake, and he bowed so clumsily he nearly toppled over.

“So you’re getting married and running for Parliament, you little scamp,” the old lady barked before he could even say hello. “I wanted Sir Francis to give that seat to my grandson! Why the hell didn’t he give it to my grandson? And I hope Miss Amory comes with a mountain of money, because I wouldn’t touch that silly little affected thing with a ten-foot pole without a huge dowry.”

Pen winced, “Sir Francis is just tired of Parliament, that’s all. The rest of the story’s still very much up in the air.”

“I still can’t wrap my head around why you’d pick that lumpy little faker over our Laura,” the old lady went on, unimpressed. “You young men are all cowards and money-grubbers! Back in my day we ran off with the people we liked and had silly romantic elopements, now all of you only marry for cash. I asked the three embassy attaches in Paris why none of them proposed to Laura, and they all laughed and said she didn’t have enough money! Disgusting. Where’s your big friend Warrington, the grumpy one? Why doesn’t he marry her instead?”

Pen got huffy. “Did you even bother to ask Laura if she wanted to marry some random attaché you picked out for her?”

“Laura’s got barely any money, she needs to get married soon, someone’s gotta find her a match!” the dowager huffed back, very pleased with her own logic. “Laura, dear, I was just telling your cousin all the young men today are utterly romance-less selfish losers, and he’s exactly as bad as the rest.”

Laura popped back in the room, grinning, and grabbed Pen’s hand. “Quit trying to marry me off to random bachelors, grandma. Arthur’s gonna have Blanche, and I’m gonna come live with them, and I’ll make sure every bachelor who shows up to their dinner party gets grilled about why he hasn’t married me yet. Fair’s fair.”

Pen’s conscience heaved a huge sigh of relief—Laura hadn’t yelled at him, hadn’t called him out for being a sellout, she was just happy to see him. Lady Rockminster insisted he come over for dinner every single night he didn’t have other plans, pointing out that her cook was one of the best in the country, and being around Laura would knock all that selfish, self-obsessed edge off him. He got the cheap wine on normal nights, and a tiny glass of champagne only if he was very amusing and didn’t disturb her post-dinner nap.

Pen spent almost every free minute he had down at Baymouth, walking and riding with Laura, talking for hours. Now that he was very publicly attached to Blanche, all the awkward unspoken tension between them was gone, and they fell right back into being the best of friends. They visited their mom Helen’s grave in the Clavering churchyard one quiet afternoon, rode past the old Fairoaks cottage where they’d grown up, and Pen hopped off his horse to pluck a sprig of honeysuckle from the old porch for Laura. As they trotted back across the downs, Pen got all mopey and dramatic, complaining that life was a stupid joke, that men were fools who traded real happiness for useless little bits of fame and pocket change. “Everyone’s selfish,” he grumbled. “The whole world’s selfish. The only exceptions are people like you, who shine so bright you make everyone else look extra grubby.”

Laura tucked the honeysuckle into her dress and smiled. “Stop being a drama queen, Arthur. You weren’t being selfish when you climbed off your horse to get me that flower. If you think all this parliamentary fame is so vain and stupid, why chase it? Just bring Blanche home to Fairoaks, write nice kind books, do good for the people who live around you. We could be happy here, no fancy speeches required.”

Any fool who’s ever popped into the Wheel of Fortune public-house—where you’ll recall Mr. James Morgan’s “Confidentials” valet club met, and where Sir Francis Clavering had that delightfully awkward chat with Major Pendennis—knows its ground floor has three guest rooms besides the landlady’s snug little bar. One’s a regular parlour open to all and sundry; another is reserved for gents in livery who spend hours swapping the most embarrassing secrets of their posh employers; the third, with a chipped “PRIVATE” sign tacked to the door, belongs exclusively to the Confidentials, where Morgan and his mate Lightfoot were core members.

That quiet little shadow Morgan had crept along and listened to every word Strong and Major Pendennis had chattered about in the Major’s own lodgings, stockpiling gossip to chew over for weeks. His curiosity piqued so bad he followed the Major back to the Wheel of Fortune later, tucked himself into a little nook in the Confidentials room while Pendennis and Clavering yelled at each other in the next parlour. That nook had acoustics so good you could hear someone crack a nut in the next apartment, so every shouty, heated line of the conversation reached his ears perfectly, confirming every sneaking suspicion he’d already formed.

“Wait a minute, he recognised Altamont straight away back in Sydney, did he? Clavering’s no more properly married to his Lady than I am the Archbishop of Canterbury! Altamont’s the real husband, and he’s a convict to boot! Young Arthur’s gonna waltz into Parliament, and that old rascal Pendennis promised not to snitch. Good grief, that old fox is slipperier than an eel in a rain barrel! No wonder he’s dead set on marrying Blanche off to Arthur—she’s got a hundred thousand quid easy, and she’ll hand her new hubby a Parliament seat on a silver platter too!” A physiognomist would’ve paid good money to gawk at Morgan’s face when this bombshell clicked fully into place. “If society didn’t have these daft hang-ups about age and valets, dammit James Morgan, you could marry her yourself,” he muttered, winking at his reflection in the pub mirror.

He couldn’t marry Blanche and her fortune, sure—but he could line his own pockets with this secret easy. Everyone tangled up in it would pay through the nose to keep it quiet. Sir Francis would cough up to avoid total ruin, Altamont would pay to not get dragged back to the colonies and hanged, that stuck-up prat Arthur would pay to stop the world knowing he’d bought a Parliament seat by trafficking a convict’s secret. Lady Clavering? She’d pay if she wanted to dump Clavering, she’d pay double to protect her son Arthur. And Blanche would definitely fork over cash to get the fortune she was cheated out of. “Dammit,” Morgan grinned to himself, “these cards are so good I’m set for life. It’ll be a regular annuity. Every last one of them will have to keep paying me off. With the cash I’ve already saved I can quit valeting, fire my old boss, turn proper gentleman, and hire my own damn servant.” For a man planning such grand schemes, he showed incredible self-restraint: he still polished the Major’s boots to a mirror shine, brought his early morning tea up exactly at 7 a.m., and took all the old gent’s swears and snide remarks with a perfectly blank, obsequious face. No one looking at him hefting the Major’s trunks or helping serve at posh country house dinners would guess he was richer than his own employer, and holding half the neighbourhood’s most explosive secrets in his waistcoat pocket. He was a legend among valets: the younger blokes called him a boring old fogey with no ideas, but every last one of them prayed to die as well off as Morgan Pendennis.

Major Pendennis, for his part, spent the autumn traipsing between the country houses of his remaining friends. If the Duke was off in France and the Marquis up in Scotland, he’d condescend to crash with plain old Sir John or the local squire. Truth be told, his reputation was on the wane: most of his old Regency mates were dead, their kids and grandkids had never heard of his wild Prince and Poins anecdotes, and didn’t care a fig for them if they had. He’d walk past old familiar London doors and realise no one was inviting him in for banquets anymore, and get all melancholy, convinced the young folks were laughing at him behind his back. “The breed’s gone!” he grumbled to himself. “No more grand old courtly manners. Old Lord Castlewood used to roll into London with four post-chaises and sixteen horses, the whole North Road lined up to gawk at his procession! The new Lord Castlewood crams five bagmen into a railway carriage and sneaks off from the station smoking a cheap cigar in a beat-up brougham. The old Lady Lorraine wore diamonds the size of walnuts and rouge so bright it outshone a streetlamp, and all the wits bowed at her feet. The new one dresses like a governess in a plain black gown, won’t stop yammering about astronomy and labouring classes, and creeps to church at 8 a.m. like she’s hiding from the law. The guardsmen these days sprawl over sofas, smoke tobacco that stinks up the whole turnip field, and don’t respect nothing—they’re all useless cotton spinners and prissy young parsons with their hair plastered down their foreheads. My day’s over,” the old man sighed, and he wasn’t half wrong.

After a pretty rubbish autumn full of bad shooting, grumpy young hosts, and no chance to play his beloved whist, Pendennis trundled back to London at the miserable end of October, when the fog rolls in and all the lawyers drag themselves back to town. Everyone knows that vibe: the overloaded cabs rattling through dun grey evenings, dumping sleepy kids and sunburnt holidaymakers back into cold dark houses, the stack of bills waiting on the study desk, the printer’s boy loitering in the passage, that bloke Smith who turns up out of nowhere to collect that one tiny bill you forgot all about. Pendennis didn’t have any bills to stress over, and no wife or kids waiting to soothe him when he got home, but he was still in a foul mood. He’d had two bouts of gout at his last country house, walked for miles over ploughed fields that ached his old bones to bits, left his umbrella on the train, and he’d been taking all that frustration out on Morgan for days.

Bays’s Club wasn’t any better: it had just been repainted, reeking of varnish so strong it made his eyes water, and some careless painter had streaked a thick line of white paint right down the back of his favourite fur-collared coat. The dinner was terrible, and the three most boring old gits in London had decided to sit right near him: old Hawkshaw hacking up a lung nonstop, old Colonel Gripley hogging every single newspaper in the room, and Jawkins the bore who wouldn’t stop reciting every single inn bill he’d paid on his recent European tour. Then the club waiter trod right on his gouty toe while bringing his coffee. The Furies really do hunt in packs: they chased Pendennis all the way from home to the club, and all the way back.

While he was out, Morgan had planted himself in the landlady Mrs. Brixham’s parlour, guzzling hot brandy and water and ranting about how badly the Major had treated him all week. Mrs. Brixham, a poor clergyman’s widow, was his total slave: he’d bought the lease on her house, held all her furniture as collateral for a tiny loan she’d taken out five years prior, and could throw her son, an insurance office clerk, in jail whenever he felt like it. Morgan made her fetch his bootjack, his slippers, and refill his glass over and over, drinking her own liquor for free, three full tumblers deep, when the Major trundled back home and rang the bell for his valet. Morgan swore savagely under his breath, finished his last full glass, and dragged himself up to answer.

The Major yelled at him for being slow, none the wiser that the red-faced valet was three sheets to the wind. He knelt down to pull off Pendennis’s boots, and as the old gent yelled “Confound you, mind that strap! Don’t wrench my foot off,” Morgan was muttering under his breath that he’d love to wring the old man’s neck and drown him in the foot bath by the fire. When he got the boots off, he leaned close to help Pendennis out of his coat, and the old man caught a whiff of brandy strong enough to knock over a horse. “You drunken beast,” he snapped, “you reek of liquor.”

That was the final straw. Morgan snapped, throwing every last bit of obsequious behaviour out the window. “Drunk am I? A beast am I? You infernal old miscreant! D’you think I’m gonna put up with your snooty old arrogance forever, you grinning old baboon? Come on, if you’re such a man, fight me!”

The Major grabbed a knife off the side table. “Step one inch closer and I’ll stick this in you. Get out of my sight, send for your wages in the morning, and never show your face here again. You’ve been getting too big for your boots for months, you’re too rich to be a valet now. Get out.”

Morgan laughed a nasty, sneering laugh, and plopped himself down in a chair like he owned the place. “And where d’you think you are, old man? This house is mine. Every stick of furniture in it belongs to me, except your old rags, your shower bath, and your silly wig box. I worked hard and bought the whole lease. I’ve served you loyally for twelve years, and all you ever do is call me a beast and a dog? No more of that. I quit. You don’t get to boss me around anymore. We ain’t done talking, you and me, not me, not your stuck-up nephew Arthur, and not the whole Clavering family. You’ll see.”

The Major was so shocked he cooled off instantly, like someone had dumped a bucket of freezing water over his head. He tried to shoo Morgan out of the room, but the valet sneered, “You’ll get sick of seeing me long before this is over,” and trundled out, leaving the old gent to sit by the fire fuming. He ranted to himself about ungrateful servants, and then grumbled that for all his cheek, Morgan was actually the best valet he’d ever had: he made a boot polish better than any sold in shops, whipped up perfect mutton broth, and looked after him like a nurse when he was sick. How on earth was he gonna replace the man?

He’d just taken off his wig, popped on his little night handkerchief, and settled in by the fire to grumble some more, when there was a tiny, pitiful knock at the door. It was Mrs. Brixham the landlady, looking half in tears. “God bless my soul, Mrs. Brixham!” the Major yelped, embarrassed that the widow was seeing him in his silly bedtime getup. “It’s very late. About Morgan, I suppose? I can’t take him back, I’m sorry. He’s gone too far.”

“Oh sir,” the poor woman wailed, “I know. He’s ruined me. I borrowed a tiny little sum off him five years ago, I’ve paid it back ten times over, but every single thing I own is his now. My son’s at his mercy. He’s a dreadful man.”

The Major blinked, and sighed. “Dash it, that’s terrible news. But there’s no help for it. I’ve got to leave your house tomorrow too, can’t stay under the same roof as that villain. No way around it.”

Bright and early the next morning, Morgan the valet swung open the Major’s bedroom shutters with a perfectly grave, respectful face—you’d never guess they’d had a screaming falling out 12 hours prior—hauling in hot water, fancy grooming tools, and the old man’s crispest dressing gown like nothing had ever happened.
“Ugh, it’s you, is it?” grumbled the Major from under his covers. “Don’t get ideas, I’m not taking you back.”
Mr. James Morgan drew himself up so stiff he nearly creaked, full of the kind of dignity only a long-serving upper-servant who runs a secret side loan-shark ring can pull off. “I wouldn’t dream of coming back, Major Pendennis, sir! I’m done serving you, or any man. But since you’re still camped out in my house, I figured I’d make sure you didn’t look a mess while you’re here.”
He went through the whole tedious old valet routine one last time, laying out the Major’s silver razor case like he was arranging a state funeral wreath, then leaned in with the kind of solemnity reserved for announcing a duchess has run off with a stable boy. “Figured you’d need a temporary new valet while you hunt for a permanent one, so I rounded up a chap last night. Lived in all the best families, I can vouch for him.”
“Truly? You are far too polite,” the Major snickered, because he knew exactly what Morgan had been up to the night before: he’d hotfooted it straight to the Wheel of Fortune, the valets’ own private club where they traded gossip, pilfered cufflinks, and job leads, and found Frosch, a courier fresh back from dragging young Lord Cubley around the Continent who was currently at loose ends. Morgan had spun him a whole dramatic tale about blowing up his own job and quitting service forever, so Frosch jumped at the temp gig.
“Er, he’s worked for Lord De La Pole! He was with young Lord Cubley on his grand tour, and he doesn’t want to go up to Fitzurse Castle because his chest can’t handle Scottish cold, so he’s free as a bird,” Morgan huffed, getting tetchy because he could tell the Major was ribbing him.
“Marvelous, send him in, he’ll do perfectly,” the Major said. “Morgan, would you be so kind as to—”
“Show him all your little quirks and routines, sir, of course,” Morgan said. “Breakfast here or at the club, Major?”
“Here, thanks, then we’ll sort out our little accounts.”
“Right you are, sir.”
“Now please leave the room.”
Morgan stomped off, seething. The Major’s over-the-top politeness was way more infuriating than any insult he could have yelled. While the Major faffed about doing his toilet, we’ll leave him to it, no peeking.

After breakfast, the Major and new guy Frosch set to work packing. The Major was the kind of neat old bachelor who never accumulated junk, so he didn’t have 30 years of random memorabilia to sift through. His entire collection of books and art amounted to his late mom’s Bible, a road atlas, Pen’s calf-bound novel, every volume of the Duke of Wellington’s dispatches, plus a handful of prints of Wellington, the other generals he’d served under in India, and random portraits of British kings and queens. He could be packed and ready to move in two hours flat, and the same old crates he’d arrived with 15 years prior were still rotting in the attic, perfect to hold everything. The overworked “Slavey” maid dragged them down, dusted them off under Morgan’s glowering supervision. Morgan hadn’t said a word about the blackmail he’d threatened the night before, but he looked so menacing poor widow Mrs. Brixham was trembling in her kitchen waiting for the axe to fall.
The Major prodded along with his cane, overseeing Frosch packing everything up, while the maid burned all the old useless papers the Major didn’t want hanging around. Soon every closet, every drawer was empty, except his desk, waiting for Morgan to bring over the final bill.

Morgan swanned in with his ledger. “Want that new valet out of the room, sir, I need to talk private.”
The Major sent Frosch off to hail two cabs and wait downstairs. Morgan made sure he was gone, then pulled out his books, which were totally easy to settle up. He pocketed the Major’s cheque, scribbled his signature with a fancy flourish, and puffed out his chest. “Now that our accounts are squared away, Major Pendennis, I reckon we can talk man to man. I’ve got some very valuable information, see.”
“Oh? What sort of information?” the Major asked, already guessing.
“I know of a so-called marriage that’s not a real marriage! A baronet who’s as good as unmarried, whose wife was already hitched to someone else before she married him! And you know all about that, sir!”
The Major snorted, looking down his nose. “Ah, that explains why you’ve been acting so weird. You were lurking at the keyhole at that public house the other night, weren’t you? Should’ve checked for eavesdroppers before I went in.”
“Hey, I’m allowed to get information same as you!” Morgan squawked. “A poor servant can have a lucky break same as a fancy gentleman, right? Don’t pull that aristocrat haughty rubbish on me, it’s all nonsense! I’m an Englishman, I’m as good as you are!”
“Cut to the chase, man,” the Major said, all regal. “What’s this got to do with me?”
“What’s it got to do with your nephew Pen? His seat in Parliament? The whole mess with suborning people over this bigamy secret? Why should you lot be the only ones making money off it? I want half, Major! Make it worth my while, and I’ll zip my lips forever. 500 quid a year, first quarter 125 quid right now, hand it over. Your chequebook’s right there on the desk.”
“And this,” the Major growled, yanking open the desk drawer to pull out the old double-barrelled pistol that had belonged to his old Indian army commander, “you scoundrel. One more word and I’ll shoot you like a rabid dog. Kneel down and say your prayers, you lying coward, I swear I’ll blow your brains out!”
Morgan screamed so loud the windows rattled: “MURDER! POLICE!” and made a flying leap for the open window, right above a copper who happened to be walking his beat.
To Morgan’s absolute shock, the Major wheeled his heavy table out of the way, walked over to the other open window, and waved at the copper. “C’mon up here, officer!” he called, then planted himself in front of the door. “You pathetic little sneak,” he snickered at Morgan. “That pistol hasn’t been loaded in 15 years. You’d know that if you weren’t such a coward.”
The copper trundled up, followed by a terrified Mrs. Brixham and the Slavey maid, who’d been holding back a crowd of nosy passersby trying to crane their necks to see the drama. The Major launched straight into his story: “This drunk rogue I sacked last night tried to assault me! I picked up that old unloaded pistol to defend myself, and he screamed for help before I even pointed it at him. I’m charging him with stealing my property—search his trunks, he’s got piles of my stuff stashed there.”
Morgan was losing his mind. “You gave ’em to me! The velvet cloak you haven’t worn in three years! The old waistcoats! The fancy shirts! The opera glass I was gonna put back, I swear!”
“Confessed he’s a thief, right in front of you,” the Major said calmly. He knew Morgan had all sorts of embarrassing garbage stashed in his own trunks too: the dodgy snuffboxes, fake jewelry, bad cigars and cheap French prints he peddled to spendthrift young aristocrats as part of his side hustle. If this went to the station, his whole secret racket would get exposed, and his reputation as a respectable valet would be totally ruined. He looked like he was about to faint.
The Major paused for a second, glancing at poor Mrs. Brixham’s terrified face. If he dragged Morgan off to jail, the desperate man might blurt out all the bigamy secrets they were all trying to keep quiet, so no point pushing him too far. “Hold on, officer,” he said. “I’ll sort this out with him alone for a minute. Wait outside, will you?”
The copper stepped out. Morgan whispered, “Thank you, sir, thank you so much.”
“You thought you had the winning hand, didn’t you?” the Major cackled. “You lost, mate. I found out your little side gig a week ago, by the way! Young De Boots from the Blues recognised you peddling your dodgy goods at the barracks, one part cash, one part eau de cologne, one part cheap French prints. I never missed any of those old shirts and trinkets, I didn’t care! But I saw a perfect chance to take you down, and I did.”
“What do you want from me?” Morgan whimpered.
“Simple. You’ve been bleeding poor Mrs. Brixham dry for five years. You lent her 60 quid, she and her son the insurance clerk paid you back 250 quid total, and you still hold a bill of sale over all her furniture for another 120 quid. You’re gonna burn that bill right now, and give her her note of hand back.”
“No! That’s my property!” Morgan yelled.
“OFFICER!” the Major shouted, turning to the door.
“Fine! Fine, I’ll give it back!” Morgan yelped.
The Major made him sit down at the desk and write out a whole humiliating confession, signed and dated, saying he’d been wildly overpaid in his 15 years of service, had made his fortune running loans and dodgy goods, stole all the Major’s old trinkets, and any lies he told about the Major or his family were totally untrustworthy. Morgan fumed the whole time, but he had no choice but to sign it, and get the copper and Mrs. Brixham to witness it.
Half an hour later, all the Major’s trunks were loaded into the cabs, he bade a grateful Mrs. Brixham a quick goodbye, and strolled off to his temporary rooms at Cox’s Hotel in Jermyn Street, just a few minutes’ walk away. Morgan glowered at him from the parlour window, steaming mad, unable to do a single thing about it.

A few hours later, who should turn up at the door but Arthur Pendennis, our very own hero. “My uncle in, Morgan?” he asked, already amused to see the valet loitering on the steps smoking a cigar—something the strict old Major would never have allowed while he was living there.
“Major Pendennis is out, sir,” Morgan said, bowing stiffly but not bothering to take his cap off. “He moved out today, sir, and I’m no longer in his service. He’s staying temporarily at Cox’s Hotel in Jermyn Street, sir.” He paused, a weird glint in his eye. “Are you in town for a while, sir? Staying at your chambers? I’d be ever so grateful if you could spare a quarter of an hour of your time, I’ve got some important business to discuss that concerns you.”
Arthur assumed the man was begging for a reference to get a new job, so he said fine, wandered inside the dining room, and had no idea he was about to get an earful of every chaotic secret Morgan had dug up.

Our mate Arthur Pendennis rolled into London that very same day for a flying 48-hour visit, first dumping a crew of deeply boring fellow travellers he’d escorted up from the West Country at their hotel, then hightailing it straight to his Lamb Court chambers. That drab old Temple building only gets about 12 minutes of direct sunshine a day total, but its residents never complain too hard: total freedom from overbearing landladies and nosy neighbours is more than a fair swap for a bit of shade, and the lot of them grumble nonstop while lazing about as happily as cats in a sunbeam.

Pen’s harried landlady greeted him with the news that Warrington was home too, so Arthur bolted up the stairs two at a time, and sure enough found his oldest friend propped at his desk wreathed in pipe smoke thick enough to hang a hat on, half-buried under a pile of newspapers and quarterly reviews. Their greeting was peak unhinged British male stoicism: no dramatic hugs, no loud sobs over the months they’d spent apart. “Well, young one!” Warrington grunted, pausing to fish his pipe out of his mouth. “How are you, old boy?” Pen replied, sticking out a hand.

You’d never see two Frenchmen pull that: they’d be clinging to each other’s shoulders shrieking *“Mon cher Arthur! Mon coeur!”* loud enough to rattle the street lamps. A pair of German pals would have dotted six perfumed kisses on each other’s moustaches before they even said hello. But two proper English blokes? They’d give that exact same flat, no-frills greeting even if they’d just dragged each other out of a burning building the night before. By next week they won’t even shake hands, just nod at each other across the breakfast table. They’d both empty their wallets for each other, scream down anyone who dared badmouth the other, and write 10 pages of gushing letters when they’re apart – but in person? They’d die before they looked sappy. Hardman, the bloke who’s never felt a genuine friendly emotion in his entire life, loves bragging “See? We Britons aren’t all over-dramatic like those confounded continentals” like that’s a personality win.

“Been to Switzerland, then?” Pen asked.
“Aye,” Warrington said. “Couldn’t find a single half-decent bit of tobacco the whole trip, until I hit Strasburg and stocked up on caporal.” The man had seen the most jaw-dropping alpine peaks and sunlit Italian lakes that would make any normal person cry, but he’d rather die than gush about it out loud. He’d ramble about those sights late at night after half a bottle of claret, and stuff all that giddy wonder into his travel essays later when no one’s watching, but for now he mumbles through his whole itinerary: Switzerland, North Italy, the Tyrol, Vienna, Dresden, down the Rhine, like all of it was a massive boring chore he’d rather not talk about.

Pen then took his turn updating his pal: he’d been stuck in the countryside for months, bored out of his skull on the parliamentary canvassing trail, counting cracks in the village pub ceiling just to pass the time. Next he’s off to some party near Tunbridge Wells full of people who will do nothing but ramble on about turnip yields and gout for three days straight. It’s physically painful for any Englishman to admit he’s having fun, after all.

“Speaking of that seat you’re gunning for,” Warrington said, “is that all sorted?”
“100%,” Pen grinned. “As soon as Parliament sits and they issue a new writ, old Clavering retires and I slip right into his spot.”
“Who are you going to pretend to be to win votes, then?” Warrington teased. “Liberal Conservative? Loyal government man? The lone champion of the working man?”
Pen waved him off. “Oh, politics are all the same these days, aren’t they? I don’t own enough land to be a protectionist even if I tried. I’ll mostly vote with the government, but push ahead of them on the sanitation and colonisation stuff I’ve been cramming from blue books all vacation – stop snickering, you old cynic. I’m no Moses, I’m not about to haul a bunch of holy commandments down a mountain to give to the masses. I don’t have the vanity, or the hypocrisy, to pretend I’m a grand world reformer. I promise you, George, I won’t tell outright lies. If there’s a good speech to give in the Commons I’ll give it, if there’s a good bill to back I’ll back it, if there’s a decent government job going I’ll take it and be chuffed with my luck. But I won’t kiss up to some fancy lord any more than I’ll kiss up to a rowdy mob. That’s all my politics are, no tricks.”

Warrington snickered, then leaned back in his chair. “And after ambition? Love, I suppose? You gonna divorce me and the chambers to get hitched?”
Pen sighed so loud the pipe smoke rippled across the room. “I s’pose. Blanche’s good fun, sings, doesn’t yell if you smoke in the drawing room. She’s got a decent fortune, no exact numbers yet, but my uncle swears her mum the Begum is gonna dump a massive wad of cash on us when we tie the knot. And I think she’s dead keen on me.”
“Meaning you’re happy to take her caresses and her money?”
“Come on, we both agreed life’s just a transaction, isn’t it?” Pen shrugged. “I’m not pretending I’m madly head over heels, I told her exactly what my feelings are, and we’re engaged. Her letters – especially the ones to Laura – get softer every week, you can tell she’s falling harder. Mine’ve been plain as day, no fancy love poems, no grand vows, just treating the whole thing as done, no point rushing or dragging it out.”

Warrington went quiet for a second. “How’s Laura, then?”
Pen looked him dead in the eye, and his voice went soft, wobbly like he was about to crack. “Laura, George – she’s the best, noblest, sweetest girl the sun ever shone on. I’m an idiot, I only just figured it out, now it’s too late. I had an angel right under my nose for years and I let her slip away. Who doesn’t lose the best prize life ever offers them, right? Who gets to the grave without having to give up something they loved most just to pay Fate’s fine? I’m not the only one, am I, old boy?”
Warrington just nodded. He was thinking of his own lost Helen, the girl whose old handwriting in a bible and a glass that once held a posy of her flowers were all he had left of her. Neither of them said a word for a good five minutes, both lost in their own dumb sad memories.

Pen broke the silence first, saying he had to go track down his uncle, the Major, who’d been firing off grumpy letters nonstop lately. “Said he’s getting old, wants to see me in Parliament, settled down with a kid to carry the Pendennis name before he kicks the bucket,” Pen said.
Warrington snickered. “That old heathen’s got a good heart, though. He cares about something that’s not buttoned up inside his own waistcoat, at least – you, and the whole Pendennis line. He’ll die happy knowing the family’s multiplying, then get carried off to be buried under St James’s Church right by Piccadilly, where he can hear the carriage traffic even six feet under. Very edifying ending.”

Then Pen rambled on about Blanche’s chaotic family: her dad was an Indiaman lieutenant who drowned between Calcutta and Sydney, her mum the Begum has had the worst luck with husbands ever, and her current stepdad Sir Francis Clavering is the most useless, roguish baronet to ever pretend to be a respectable county gentleman.
“Funny you mention Clavering,” Warrington cut in. “I ran into Chevalier Strong at Baden a few weeks back, parading around all covered in his fancy medals. He told me he’s had a massive falling out with Clavering, said he’s a total utter scoundrel. That Bloundell bloke who taught you to cheat at cards back at Oxbridge was with him too – years have turned him into a way more polished, professional scammer than he was when we were students. And the king of the whole casino was this Colonel Altamont, who apparently breaks the bank every single night like he’s got a direct hotline to the devil.”

Pen hopped up to grab his hat, then stopped at his own downstairs chambers and started dousing himself in eau de Cologne, spritzing so hard he was basically fumigating his hair and whiskers.
“What the hell?” Warrington grumbled. “You weren’t even smoking. Did my pipe offend you that much?”
“Got to call on some ladies, and eat dinner with them later,” Pen said, preening as he slipped on a crisp new shirt, perfectly tied neckcloth, fresh gloves, and boots so polished you could see your own reflection in them. Warrington, meanwhile, was still wearing his beat-up old work shirt, frayed at the collar from months of his coarse blue stubble rubbing against it, and scuffed thick work boots.
“Go on, then, you fancy buck,” Warrington laughed. “I love walking around with you – makes me feel like I’ve got a fresh rose stuck in my buttonhole. You never turn your nose up at scruffy old me, do you?”
Pen was about to protest, when Warrington added quietly, “If you write to Laura, tell her God bless her from me, will you?”

Pen burst out cackling so loud he nearly dropped his cologne bottle. “You daft sod – I’m going to dinner with her right now. I drove her and Lady Rockminster up from the country this very day, we stopped overnight at Bath. You can come too! The old lady won’t mind, she never stops talking about you.”
Warrington made a big show of saying he had a urgent article to finish, hemmed and hawed for all of 10 seconds before caving, and the two of them trundled off to the Jermyn Street hotel in very high spirits. Laura’s face lit up when she saw them, her soft hand warm and welcoming in theirs, and there was half an hour still to go before dinner.
“Off you go now, Mr Pendennis,” old Lady Rockminster ordered him, waving a gnarled finger. “Go visit your uncle, and do not – I repeat, do not – bring that old Major back to dinner. I’ve heard his story about the time he met the Duke of York 47 times already. Mr Warrington can stay here and chat with us, you’ve bored us both silly solid for two whole days straight.”
Pen obeyed at once, grinning, and trotted off towards his uncle’s Bury Street lodgings, already mentally rehearsing all his good news to make the old man chuffed.

Arthur stumbled back to Lady Rockminster’s dinner table looking like he’d just run into a ghost that owed him money, and the old battleaxe was already mid-rant about tardy young people having zero respect for good roast beef. But Laura spotted her cousin’s face was paler than a badly set blancmange, cut her imperious patroness off mid-scold, and demanded in a panicky whisper if he’d come down with some terrible wasting disease. Arthur just chugged an entire bumper of sherry like it was cheap pub tap water, hissed that he had the wildest story he’d ever heard but would not spill it in front of footmen who’d run straight to the town’s gossip mill before dessert.

He fidgeted so hard for the rest of the meal that he tramped right on tiny Fido the lapdog’s paws, sent his saucer of gravy flying, and made Lady Rockminster snap that he was the only person she’d ever met who could turn a perfectly good dinner into a bouncy castle test run. By the time the fruit platter came out, she’d had enough. “This meal is drier than a three-day-old biscuit,” she announced, tossing her head like a very posh swan. “It’s obvious you two want to whisper about boring secret business, so I’m off for my nap. Don’t bother bringing tea later, I won’t touch it. Good night, Mr. Warrington, do come back when there’s no scandal hanging over the table.” And she glided out of the room with all the dignity of a queen who’d just caught her footman stealing her jam.

Warrington was already half out his chair to say goodbye when Arthur called him back, wide-eyed. “Don’t go, George. I need a neutral third party who won’t either sob or throw a teacup when I tell you this, I haven’t the faintest clue how to act.”
Laura’s heart jumped so hard she almost spilled her wine. “It’s Blanche, isn’t it?” she blurted, cheeks flushing so bright she matched the port on the table.
“Worse,” Arthur said. “When I popped over to my uncle’s old lodgings earlier, Morgan – that servant who’s been with him for 20 years skimming every spare shilling off his master’s side table – told me he quit that morning, bought the whole house, and is now basically a secret-holding millionaire. Do you know what he had to blackmail me with? Blanche’s dad is not dead. The guy everyone thought drowned in New South Wales? He’s alive and kicking. That means Lady Clavering’s marriage to Sir Clavering is 100% fake.”
“Wait, so Blanche is technically her grandfather’s heir?” Warrington said, frowning.
“Maybe, but her dad’s an escaped convict,” Arthur groaned. “Clavering knows it, my uncle knows it, and the old rascal was waving that secret over Clavering’s head to force him to hand over his parliamentary seat to me, no questions asked.”
“Blanche doesn’t have a clue, does she?” Laura said, voice tight. “Or poor Lady Clavering?”
“Not a bit,” Pen said. “Blanche thought her dad was a ship’s captain who drowned. Lady Clavering told me once she was so relieved when she heard he was dead, she cried for a whole hour – said it was the worst kind of shame to be happy your husband kicked the bucket. But now this louse Morgan has the whole secret, and he tried to bribe me 10 minutes ago with a private annuity if I helped him keep it quiet. He thought I was in on the whole blackmail scheme! He threatened me! As if our family – our poor mom’s son – would ever trade on that poor lady’s misfortune for a crummy seat in Parliament.”
Laura grabbed his hand and kissed it immediately. “I never thought that for a second, Arthur.”
“Damn right you didn’t,” Warrington rumbled, his deep voice a little wobbly. “Arthur Pendennis doesn’t marry a convict’s daughter and sit in Parliament like he works for the prison board. You should break the engagement, Pen. Don’t explain a single thing, say family reasons make it impossible. It’s far better Blanche and her mom think you’re a lying cad than they ever learn their whole life was a fraud.” He paused, and he couldn’t quite bring himself to look Laura in the face. He was already waving away his last tiny sliver of hope that the engagement might fall apart, he knew it.
But Laura was already shaking her head, going back to Arthur’s side to wrap her arm around him. “Her dad’s crimes aren’t her fault, Arthur,” she said, fast and bright and pale as a sheet. “Would you abandon her if you were already married, just because something that’s not her fault went wrong? You promised her when she had a good name, you can’t leave her when she’s fallen on hard times. Our mom would never have done that.” She buried her face on his shoulder, and Pen sobbed that she was the best person who ever lived, and he’d do whatever she thought was right.
“Amen,” Warrington groaned, head in his hands. “She’s right. That girl never does a single wrong thing.”
Laura wiped her eyes, smiled, and patted the table. “Right. Now we’re all going to have tea.”
Before anyone could pour a single cuppa, though, a servant popped in to say Major Pendennis was back at the hotel and waiting very impatiently for his nephew. Laura gave Arthur a very pointed look that screamed *Don’t be an idiot, stand your ground, be nice to your old uncle but don’t let him bully you into a life of blackmail* before she trundled off to bed. Warrington grumbled about the bad timing, he’d been looking forward to that tea, and wandered off to his club, where a bunch of loud drunks were yelling about horse races, bad opera girls, and how the committee was the most tyrannical group of thieves alive. He sat there smoking his cigar, carrying their huge sacred secret around like a heavy backpack, and thought it was very funny that every single one of those yelling idiots almost certainly had a weird, private secret they’d never tell a soul.

Arthur’s anger was bubbling up to boiling point by the time he reached his uncle’s hotel room. The old Major was hunched over the fire, sniffling, grumbling that his new German servant was a total lout who couldn’t even hand him his dressing gown right, and cursing Morgan for being the ungrateful thief who’d stolen all his secrets and quit. “He got drunk last night, challenged me to a duel!” the old man whined, coughing so hard his whole body shook. “I could have stabbed him!
