That rascal’s got ten thousand pounds now, mark my words, but I wish he’d stuck around till I kicked the bucket. He knew exactly how I liked my tea made. Speaking of – hey, Arthur, I’m getting very old. I found my poor mom’s old bible in my books today, I want you to have it. I hope you’ll find it when I’m under the sod, yeah?”
The old man’s wobbly kindness almost deflated all Arthur’s anger. He knew he was about to smash every single last one of the old guy’s air castles to smithereens.
“Hey, I still want to see you give your first speech in the Times!” the Major cackled. “‘Unaccustomed as I am to public speaking’! And then you can buy the old family estate out in the west, become a baronet, rise right up the social ladder like a rocket!”
Arthur’s heart sank. He had to pop that balloon. “Uncle. I went to your old lodgings earlier. I talked to Morgan.”
The Major’s face flushed bright red immediately. Oh no, he thought, the cat’s fully out of the bag.
“He told me that story about Blanche’s father,” Arthur said grimly.
“Awkward, that,” the Major said, rubbing his nose very fast. “I’ve known about that little thing for a while, yeah. But Amory can’t show his face! The second he does, he’s either dead or shipped back to the hulks for life. We’ve got him completely trapped, it’s no trouble at all! Miss Amory’s a perfectly nice girl with fifty thousand pounds, Clavering likes you, you get his seat in Parliament! What could be simpler?”
“Simple?” Arthur burst out. “I’d rather go move into the prison hulks with my future father-in-law than profit off this blackmail scheme! I’d rather steal the silver spoons off the table than take a parliamentary seat that’s a bribe for keeping quiet about their secret! You almost made me prostitute my honour for a petty title and a pile of cash! I’m not going to do it.”
The old man shrieked so loud the windowpanes rattled. “WHO’S GOING TO TAKE IT AWAY FROM YOU? Clavering will give you every penny of that eighty thousand pounds, I’ll make him!”
“No,” Arthur said. “I’m going to keep my promise to Blanche, because she didn’t do anything wrong to deserve being dumped over her father’s crimes. But I’m not taking Clavering’s free seat. I’m not taking a single extra penny beyond the original settlement that was already written down for Blanche. We’re not doing any more blackmail.”
The Major’s knees gave out, he sank right down on the floor, grabbing Arthur’s hands and weeping. “Arthur! Your dad was the proudest man alive, he cared so much about the family’s honour! I’ve always been so fond of you! Don’t throw this chance away, it’s the making of you! I’m begging you, on my knees, don’t do this.”
Arthur almost cried too, hauling the old man up. “Fine. I’ll marry her. But I’m not taking any of the bribes. Don’t ask me to.”
The old man wiped his tears, coughing so hard he could barely talk. “Ring the bell for me. I said everything I had to say. I’m a broken old idiot. Shakespeare was right, Cardinal Wolsey was right, if I’d served God half as hard as I served you, I wouldn’t be on my knees begging my own nephew to take a fancy to a parliamentary seat. Good night. Don’t bother coming to see me tomorrow.”
His hand was cold and clammy when Arthur shook it. It looked like the fight had aged him ten years. The next day, he stayed in bed, and refused to let Arthur through the door at all.

Arthur Pendennis trundled over to his best mate George Warrington’s chambers the next morning swaddled in his most faded, coffee-stained dressing gown, as was his custom—all fired up to spill every last detail of his chaotic late-night chat with his uncle, and beg George to talk him off whatever dumb impulsive ledge he’d been teetering on that week.

The only soul he found in the familiar, book-strewn rooms was Mrs. Flanagan, their laundress, who was clucking over a pile of crumpled manuscript pages like a mother hen guarding stolen potatoes. “Arrived at six this morning I did, sir, and there was the dear gentleman slouched over his desk, one candle burned clean down to a puddle of tallow on his dog-eared college Plato, and his bed hadn’t been slept in at all! Said he had work to finish, grabbed his carpet bag ten minutes after, and was off to his brother’s place in Suffolk before the baker even delivered the crusty rolls.”

Which was true enough: George had bailed on his club the second the post-midnight poker brawl got too loud to bear, trundled home, and pulled a full all-nighter cranking out book reviews and sharp satire so he didn’t have to sit and think about the heartache sitting heavy in his chest. The next week’s quarterly review ran his piece, full of snark and clever little references, and all the posh London bookworms cooed over how clever the author was—no one had a clue half the lines were written through puffy red eyes, his desk chair pulled so close to the window the cold November dawn was practically breathing on his neck. Freelancers the world over know that vibe, right? You’re sniffling over a broken heart, or a sick kid, or the landlord banging on the door for rent, and you just keep scribbling, and no one reading your work ever guesses you were one second away from flinging your inkwell across the room.

The table was a mess, as usual: pens stuck in congealed ink, half a eaten scone, crumpled drafts torn to bits, a stick of sealing wax chewed almost in two from George’s nervous habits. Pen flopped into his empty friend’s chair, and noticed the gap on the bookshelf right next to that beat-up Plato: Helen’s Bible was gone. Oh. He got it immediately.

“Dear, dumb, wonderful old George,” Pen mumbled, scrubbing at his eyes so hard he saw stars. Who else was half as good? Half as brave? No wonder—well, of course she loved him. The man was a walking gentleman, no performative nonsense, no silly posturing. Pen resolved right there that he and Laura would be George’s honorary siblings for the rest of their lives, no more, no less: they’d look out for him, send him Christmas cake, never let him be lonely. Then he knocked over the half-full inkwell on the desk, and Mrs. Flanagan shooed him out of the room calling him a great big clumsy lummox.

Back at his own place, Pen spotted a crumpled note stuffed in his door letterbox, in George’s messy scrawl:
> Dear Pen,
> You’ll be halfway through your bacon and eggs by the time you read this, I’m already trundling north. My two cents on the business we chatted about on J Street? I’d be the world’s most awkward third wheel if I stuck around, so I’m off to camp out at my brother’s for Christmas, maybe even go further up to Norfolk to shoot partridges. Don’t be daft, don’t get yourself in trouble.
> Vale,
> P.S. Give my very best to your cousin Laura.

Well, that was that. George was gone, and Mrs. Flanagan had claimed his empty rooms as her personal territory to hang her drying laundry.

Pen trotted over to call on his uncle the next day, only to be told the old man was too under the weather to see visitors, so naturally he popped over to Lady Rockminster’s apartments, where the 70-something dowager’s eyes lit up like she’d just been handed the juiciest piece of town gossip. “Where’s Bluebeard? That George fellow! Bring him to dinner tonight at once!”

“Bluebeard’s done a runner to Suffolk,” Pen said, and passed her George’s little note. Laura took it, glanced at it, didn’t meet Pen’s eye, and handed it right back before wandering over to the window. Pen immediately launched into a dramatic 10 minute eulogy about how George was the finest man alive, so earnest Lady Rockminster blinked at him. “Well I never,” she said, blunt as a brick. “I didn’t think you had it in you to care that much about anyone who wasn’t you or your own silly drama.”

A few days later, Pen was wandering through Waterloo Place, heading to the hotel where Laura was staying, when he spotted a familiar face stepping out of Gimcrack’s fancy jeweller’s: Harry Foker, his old school chum, decked out head to toe in full deep mourning, his brougham, his coachman, even his horse all wearing little black armbands. The late Mr. Foker had finally kicked the bucket, and Harry was now the owner of the massive Foker & Co brewery, 15 thousand a year, and the entire Logwood estate. A shopman trailed behind him carrying armfuls of parcels.

“Foker! You old rascal!” Pen yelled, waving. Harry jumped like he’d been caught stealing a bottle of his own beer, held out a gloved little hand that had three inches of snowy cuff peeking out of the black sleeve, and looked like he was about to bolt. His other hand was clamped around a tiny morocco jeweller’s case like it held top-secret government documents.

“Le Roi est mort! Vive le Roi!” Pen teased. Harry blushed bright red all the way to his ears. “Ah! Yes! Thank you! Very busy! Goodbye!” he yelped, scrambled into his black brougham, and sat hunched behind the coachman like a little black cloud of misery the whole time he drove off.

Pen snickered to himself, strolling off. Classic Harry. The old brewery bosses are all bowing and scraping to him now, the draymen are cheering their new boss like he’s a royal, the bankers are all being extra polite because he’s now the one signing the cheques. Lucky there was never any money nonsense between him and Laura, right? He said as much to her later.

Laura’s eyes immediately filled with tears. “Arthur! Why would you even say that? You’d never have been tempted by anything so mean! You make me blush for you when you pretend you’re that selfish.”

They bickered back and forth gently, Pen being his usual over-skeptical self, rambling on about that annoying little familiar named But that follows every cynic around—tapping you on the shoulder in church, hanging over your wedding pillow, hiding behind your deathbed curtain, going “but what if it’s all garbage?”—until Lady Rockminster swept into the room after finishing her three-hour elaborate toilette that involved two maids, three bottles of lavender water, and a ridiculous amount of lace.

“What on earth are you two yammering about?” she huffed. “Pen, you’re over here so often the doorman thinks you live in the lobby.”

Pen explained he’d just run into the newly wealthy Harry Foker, which made Lady Rockminster’s eyes go wide with glee—she had top-tier tea to spill. “Oh! That boy’s my cousin! You haven’t heard the news? Lady Ann Rosherville, the girl he’s been engaged to for years? The one everyone thought was going to marry him for the 15 thousand a year? She snuck out last week after breakfast, ran off to the little church on their estate, and eloped with her father’s chaplain. Red-haired widower, two little snot-nosed kids. Lord Rosherville is absolutely apoplectic. He’s tried to make Harry marry one of his other daughters, but one’s covered in smallpox scars and the other’s 38 years old and collects taxidermied hedgehogs. The whole ton will be cackling about this for months.”

Pen’s jaw dropped. Oh! That’s what Harry was buying the fancy jewellery for, wasn’t it? He wasn’t sneaking trinkets for some opera dancer, he was about to go propose to someone else, the poor lovestruck idiot.

Laura, meanwhile, had suddenly gotten very weirdly urgent about getting Pen’s wedding to Blanche sorted as fast as humanly possible. She wrote him three notes a day, begging him to speed up the plans, offered to go stay with Blanche down in Tunbridge Wells the second Lady Rockminster let her leave, even defied the old dowager for the first time in her whole life to insist she go—so desperate to get Pen married off you’d think there was a fire under her skirts.

A whole flurry of fake-sweet notes passed between Blanche and Laura:
*Oh dearest Laura, how horribly unfortunate! The spare bedrooms are full! That dreadful Mrs Planter decided to come down and catch a cold that won’t let her leave for another week, so there’s nowhere for you to stay!*
*Dearest Blanche! That’s such bad luck! Don’t worry, I’ll wait, no rush at all! Oh by the way, Pen is such a wonderful, kind man, I can promise you that with my life!*
*Dearest Laura! What on earth is in this completely wild, weird, reserved letter I just got from A.P.?! What’s going on?! There’s a mystery here and I need answers!*

And the answers? They’re all in the wild, unexpected letter Arthur Pendennis had just sent her, of course.

“Dearest Blanche,” Arthur Pen penned, mid a very chaotic post-catastrophe hangover, “you’re forever spinning grand dramatic romances out of real life, aren’t you? Well, congrats! You’ve just landed a starring role in one, and it’s not the fun part where the heroine sashays into her father’s royal palace, shows her new husband all the gold and crown jewels, and coos ‘all this is ours.’ It’s the part where you wake up to find the prince you married is actually penniless Claude Melnotte, or you walk in just as your husband Alnaschar has kicked his entire fortune of porcelain bowls all over the cobblestones. Quick side note: that Alnaschar bloke wasn’t even married, for the record—he hadn’t even popped the question to the Vizier’s daughter before he smashed his whole investment to smithereens.

“So here’s your choice: laugh me off, dump the broke guy, and go chase the fancy rich husband you were daydreaming about, or be the Lady of Lyons and stick around with the penniless fool? I’ll do my absolute best to make it worth your while, I promise. But let’s be real, there’s no secret fortune waiting for us. Our Cinderella carriage vanished at midnight, our Belgravia townhouse was spirited away by a very unimpressed evil genie, and I’m no more a Member of Parliament than I am a bishop in the House of Lords or a duke prancing around with a fancy garter on his knee. We’ll scrape by, writing silly stories no one remembers after a month, me dragging my feet through law school, if I’m very very lucky (and work very very hard, which we both know I’m bad at) maybe we’ll score a random colonial post one day and you can be an Indian judge’s lady. I’m gonna buy back the *Pall Mall Gazette* too, Shandon’s old partners are sick of running it and will sell it for pennies. Warrington will run the show for me, we’ll get Finucane the sub-editor on board, and the three of us will churn out respectable journalism and pay our rent on time. We can get four nice rooms above the newspaper offices in Catherine Street, Strand, plus a kitchen and a little garret for Laura—our very own live-in saint, basically. Or if you hate the Strand, we could get a tiny house in Waterloo Road! It’s very charming, you just have to pay a halfpenny toll every time you cross the bridge. Our hypothetical future kids can go to King’s College, that’s nice, right? Does all this sound like a joke?

“Blanche, it’s not a joke. I’m as sober as I’ve ever been, and I’m laying all my cards on the table. We’ve already won enough to afford a cab ride when we’re too tired to walk, and never have to skip the omnibus on a rainy day. That’s it. Is that enough for you, my dainty little fancy-lady? I genuinely don’t know if you can hack the boring, humdrum life I’m offering. If you say ‘yes Arthur, I’ll stick with you through thick and thin and cheer you on no matter what,’ I’ll be the luckiest guy alive, and I’ll do everything I can to make you happy. If you’d rather chase the grander life you deserve? I won’t stand in your way. I’ll just wave from the crowd when you ride past in your fancy chariot to your first court presentation, like I saw Lady Mirabel do last season—her husband was dripping with medals and ribbons, the coachman had more roses pinned to his coat than the entire royal garden. Which do you want? The gilded chariot, or a quiet walk home where you have to mend your husband’s holes in his socks?

“I can’t tell you exactly what blew up all my prospects these last 24 hours, not yet anyway, but it’s all irreversible, like Alnaschar’s shattered porcelain. I already told Sir Francis Clavering I can’t take that empty Parliament seat anymore, and I won’t touch a penny of your fortune beyond the tiny sum you’ve had since your grandpa died. None of this is anyone’s fault, but it’s all done, no do-overs. I’m not moping about it—no point crying over smashed teacups. I’ll be happy enough if you are. Warrington and Laura both know the whole messy secret, and they’re 100% in my corner, so we’ve got two of the best people alive on our side. Who cares if some random lord doesn’t invite us to his fancy ball, right?”

Blanche wrote back two letters: one for Laura, one for Pen himself, and her reply was so flowery and dramatic Pen stared at it for ten full minutes trying to work out if she’d said yes or no. It was covered in her fancy scented ink, gilt-edged, every line oozing over-the-top romantic wailing. “You don’t love me right!” she raved. “You treat my whole heart like it’s common dross, not the precious diamonds of the deepest caverns of my soul! I’ve cried buckets over this letter! I prayed, I stayed up all night, my whole dream of being your everything is fading like a wilted flower! You’re so cold! You’d let me go without even a fight! Is love just disappointment for everyone, and I was a fool to think I’d be the exception? I’ll write more when my brain stops spinning. Tell Laura to come visit as soon as we get back from the country. Bless you forever, Arthur! B.”

Pen cackled sardonically, twiddling the musky letter in his hand like it was a very silly riddle. Fortune had well and truly dumped him, and he kind of deserved it, honestly. He was still grinning to himself, turning the letter over and over, when his servant popped in to say a Mr. Samuel Huxter was here on very urgent business. If Pen had peeked out into the hall he’d have seen his old med school acquaintance sucking his walking stick like it owed him money, rolling his eyes so hard they almost got stuck, looking like he was about to confess to arson.

“Send him in!” Pen said, highly amused, and Sam shuffled in, looking more hangdog than ever.

“Take a seat, Mr. Huxter,” Pen said, putting on his fanciest posh voice. “What can I do for you?”

“I don’t wanna talk in front of the flunkey—before the man,” Sam yelped, so the servant ducked out. “I’m in a right old mess.”

“Oh dear?”

“Fanny sent me to you,” the young surgeon mumbled.

“Fanny? Is she all right? I’ve been meaning to visit, I’ve just been drowning in work since I got back to London.”

“Heard all about you from my dad and Jack Hobnell,” Sam cut in, blushing. “Congrats on the Parliament seat, and the posh lady you’re gonna marry. Fanny says congrats too.”

“Ah, well, there’s many a slip between cup and lip, who even knows who’ll end up MP for Clavering next session?”

“You’ve got my dad wrapped around your little finger,” Sam pushed on. “You got him that fancy gig tending to Clavering Park, he was over the moon when you called him in. Hobnell wrote me all about it. You think you could talk him round for me, Mr. Pendennis?”

“Talk him round about what?”

“I done it,” Sam said, making a very weird guilty face.

“Wait a second—you didn’t hurt that sweet little Fanny, did you?” Pen shot up, furious.

“Nah, nothing like that—we eloped! We got married five weeks ago last Thursday. And my dad’s gonna have a conniption fit, he was supposed to take me into the family surgery as partner once I passed my exams, we were gonna be ‘Huxter and Son’ for goodness sake. But I had to go and do it, and he’s already writing to say he’s coming up to London tomorrow to pick up some medical supplies, and then the whole thing’s gonna blow up in my face.”

“When did all this happen?” Pen asked, not exactly thrilled that a girl who’d once had a little crush on him had moved on, but not that mad about it either.

“Two days after Miss Amory showed up at Shepherd’s Inn,” Sam said. “I was there tending to old Cos’s bad leg, ran into Strong who said some lady had a fit of hysterics in his rooms. Got up there, and it was Blanche’s old housekeeper, kicking and screaming like a banshee. Strong, Altamont, Blanche all there too, Blanche as pale as a sheet, Altamont stomping around furious. Whole lot of drama, we got the old lady calmed down and sent her home in a cab. Next day I popped round to their place in Grosvenor Place to check everyone was okay, but they’d already bolted without saying thank you. Then two days later… well, me and Fanny got hitched.”

Oh! Pen thought, a cold little jolt hitting him. Blanche knew that secret about her dad a whole month ago. That’s why she wrote that waffly letter today. She doesn’t want to spill her father’s secret, so she’s making up a fake excuse to break off our engagement so no one gets hurt. She’s the most generous girl alive!

“You know who Altamont is, right?” Sam asked, breaking the silence. “Me and Fanny have been gossiping about it for ages, we swear he’s Mrs. Lightfoot’s dead first husband come back from the grave, just as she’s remarried. And—uh,” Sam scowled, jealousy still gnawing at him even though he was married now, “Fanny still talks about you sometimes, y’know.”

“Enough about that,” Pen said, cutting him off. “Let’s sort your mess out. Congratulations on the marriage, honestly. Fanny’s the sweetest, kindest girl alive, she deserves a good husband, and you seem like a solid bloke. What do you need me to do?”

“She said you’d talk my dad and my mum round,” Sam said, shaking Pen’s hand gratefully. “Mum’s dead proud of being a clergyman’s daughter, she’s gonna throw a fit when she finds out Fanny’s not posh. But she’s a Huxter now, right? She’ll fit right in once she’s had a bit of practice in society. You should hear her sing and play piano! Old Bows taught her. And she’s a total natural flirt, no way around it. Even my best man Jack Linton from med school, he’ll stop by and sit there staring at her and she’ll bat her eyes at him without even trying. Bows says if you put 20 men in a room with her, she won’t rest till every single one of them is hanging off her every word.”

“Get her mum to stay with her, that’ll settle her down,” Pen laughed.

“Nah, her mum’s gotta keep working the porter’s lodge at Clavering, she can’t come live with us. Can’t have the neighbours thinking I’m related to the lodge keeper, it’d ruin my professional standing,” Sam said, stroking his chin like he was a very important dignitary. Pen was trying not to snort when there was another knock at the door, and the servant announced Mr. Bows.

The old grumpy music teacher shuffled in, his bald head glowing in the sun, face pink, hands shaking a little as he shook Pen’s hand. He coughed, wiped his face on his ragged cotton handkerchief, and plopped down with his hands on his knees. Pen looked at him, and felt a sharp little twinge of sympathy. This old guy had poured all his heart and his talent out at some woman’s feet, and she’d trampled all over it, same as half the people he knew. Sam winked at Bows, then winked at Pen like ‘ain’t this daft old sod a fool, head over heels for my wife?’

“Well, we lost, Bows,” Pen said, nodding at Sam. “Here’s the lucky winner.”

“Lucky winner’s right,” Bows said dryly.

“You just came from my place, didn’t you?” Sam said.

“Yep, Fanny sent me after you. She said you’d be an idiot and try to barge in here and offend Mr. Pendennis, and you’d forget to ask him to come over and visit her. Knew you’d never deliver that message, but I would.”

Sam went bright red and swore under his breath. Pen cackled, this whole absurd scene was exactly the kind of chaotic fun he needed. “Of course I’ll come pay Mrs. Huxter a visit,” he said, grinning.

“Her place is over the baker’s in Charterhouse Lane, right hand side coming from St. John’s Street, you know Smithfield?” Bows said, no mercy for Sam’s pride. “Old Doctor Johnson used to walk that street with holes in his shoes, carrying bundles of penny-a-line articles for the magazines. You fancy literary types have it easy now, riding around in cabs, wearing yellow kid gloves.”

“Old friend, I’ve watched a hundred brilliant, good guys flop, and a hundred total frauds get all the prizes, I’m not the least bit puffed up about my own luck,” Pen said, serious for a second. “You’re every bit as good as me, you know that. You’re just mad at fortune for messing with you, that’s all.”

“Fair,” Bows mumbled, wiping his bald forehead. “Most of us get grumpy when we’re talking about our own bad luck. Hey, Sam? You wanna run home right now? Fanny’s got Jack Linton and Bob Blades, two of your med school mates, over visiting. They turned up right after you left, so she sent you off on this errand to get you out of the house for a bit. They’ll stay there gawking at her for another two hours easy.”

Sam shot up so fast he almost knocked his chair over, stuffed his stick in his coat pocket and grabbed his hat. “You’ll talk my dad round, right, Pen? You’ll come visit us soon?”

“Only if you patch me up for free the next time I get sick at Fairoaks, you silly sod,” Pen laughed. “I’ll help you, no problem.”

As soon as Sam had bolted out the door, Bows plopped back down in the chair. “There he goes. Every word I said was true, that little flirt. She’s got that whole crowd of lads wrapped around her finger already. If no men are around she’ll even cozen the old German baker downstairs into giving her free buns, or chat up the crossing sweep. You asking if she’s actually fond of Sam? Yeah, weirdly enough, she is. She decided she wanted him, and wouldn’t rest till she married him. Slipped out of the lodge one day, got the banns read, ran off to Gravesend with him, left a note for me to break the news to her mum. Her mum knew the whole time, of course, just pretended she didn’t. Now I’m an old fool hanging around their tiny flat, watching her mend Sam’s shirts and sing while she cooks his dinner. I even lent ’em three quid to get by till his dad forgives him. I can’t stand to see her sad, even when she’s making a total fool of me.”

After Bows left, Pen grabbed Blanche’s confusing letter and trotted off to find his favourite person to dump all his problems on: Laura Bell. It was absurd how much Pen, who usually thought he was the cleverest man alive, now ran to Laura for every decision, even what colour waistcoat to wear. The old lady Laura lived with was highly amused by it, of course.

He handed Blanche’s letter over to Laura, and asked her to decode it. Laura read it over, her face going all serious.

“Arthur, it seems to me like Blanche is being very artful here,” she said quietly.

“Making sure she can dump me if she wants to, no hard feelings?” Pen asked.

“I’m afraid so. That’s a bad sign for your future happiness, Arthur, after you were so honest and open with her. Wait—wait a second, I almost hate to say this,” Laura said, blushing bright red, “I think she knows the secret about her father. She didn’t even ask you why you gave up the Parliament seat that would have made you so rich and successful. She’s not confused about why things changed, not at all.”

“And if that means she’s making up an excuse to break off the engagement, and I’m free?” Pen burst out, grinning. “Then will the best, sweetest woman in the world—”

“You are not free yet, Arthur,” Laura said calmly, smiling a little. “You still belong to someone else. And I think we both know that for now, that’s how it has to be.”

The train zipped Arthur off to Tunbridge far faster than he’d bargained for, though he had more than enough time on the 20-minute journey to marathon every bad life choice he’d ever made, like scrolling a folder labeled *Total Disaster Moments* at 2 a.m.
“Great, this is it,” he moped to himself. “All that romance, ambition, big fancy dreams? Down the drain. When I told my mom no to marrying Laura, I turned down an actual literal angel. And then when I caved to my uncle’s begging and chased Blanche, the parliamentary seat, and all that fat cash? Turns out the cash vanished, and I’m left holding a wife-to-be and zero of the nice stuff I agreed to settle for instead of a real heart. My old uncle doesn’t even get why I refuse to take that dodgy fortune, he’s madder than a wet hen that I’m being such a prude. I’m never pleased, I never make anyone else happy either. What’s Blanche even gonna get out of marrying a guy this much of a flop? Even if I wrote a bestseller, I’d be the first person to make fun of my own book. If I became a big lawyer, I’d have to bully witnesses and lie for a living, that’s not exactly a life to brag about.”
He glanced around the carriage. Across from him, a priest hadn’t lifted his nose from his prayer book the whole trip, except when they went through the Reigate tunnel where he couldn’t see a thing. Next to the priest, a grumpy old man was giving him the most unhinged over-the-top hater stare over his newspaper, clearly daydreaming about burning all clergymen at the stake. “Those guys don’t have any doubts,” Pen thought. “They’re just marching along totally sure they’re right. Lucky sods.”
“Care to borrow the paper, mate?” the stout man holding the hateful anti-clergy journal offered. Pen thanked him, took it, and proceeded to not read a single full sentence, too busy spiraling in his own head.
At last he pulled himself out of his moping, decided he was gonna be the world’s most honest, devoted boyfriend to Blanche, start fresh, stop being a sad sack, be a good husband and a good dad someday, raise kids with an honest name no fancy nonsense required. By the time the train pulled into Tunbridge, he was practically bouncing, carpet bag in hand, ready to face his fortune.
A hack cab zoomed him straight to Lady Clavering’s house, and he rehearsed his big noble speech the whole ride: *Blanche, I don’t get your last letter, but I’m here, I’m ready to marry you right this second, no fortune required, I’ll love you forever, the lot of it*.
He skidded up to the front door, where a servant he’d never seen before stared at him like he was a random vagrant who’d wandered in off the street, and didn’t even reach for his carpet bag. “Her Ladyship’s not at home, sir,” he said.
“I’m Mr. Pendennis,” Arthur said. “Where’s Lightfoot the usual butler?”
“Lightfoot got sacked,” the guy said. “My orders are-”
“I can hear Miss Amory singing in the drawing room,” Pen interrupted, shoving past him. “Take my bag to a spare room, yeah?”
He waltzed right into the drawing room, where Blanche was warbling a super dramatic sad love ballad at the top of her lungs. Little Frank Clavering was half-asleep on the sofa not caring a jot about the music, and right next to Blanche at the piano, gawking at her like she’d invented ice cream, was Harry Foker.
Everyone jumped. The music stopped mid-note. Frank woke up, looked at all three of them, went “Oh! This is a right old mess!” and sniggered so hard he bolted out of the room before anyone could say a word.
Right on the piano, next to Blanche’s elbow, was the exact same purple leather jewelry box Pen had seen Foker carrying outside a jeweler’s three days prior. It was open, displaying the gaudiest snake bracelet you ever saw, with a ruby the size of a marble for a head and diamonds all down the tail.
“Uh… hey Pendennis,” Foker said, turning so red his ears matched the ruby on the bracelet. Blanche squawked, shoved her handkerchief over the bracelet like it was a dirty magazine a parent just walked in on, and tried to play polite. “How is dearest Laura?” she stammered.
Pen couldn’t even be mad. He burst out cackling so loud the chandelier rattled.
“Oh this is the secret, is it?” he wheezed. “Don’t hide, Foker you daft loyal thing. I’d never get between you and Blanche, not when you’re this smitten, not when you’ve got 15 thousand a year to spoil her rotten.”
“It’s not the money!” Blanche declared, striking a dramatic noble pose. “It’s not rank, it’s not gold! It’s his constancy, his big faithful loving heart that I care about! I once thought I loved *you*, Arthur, I truly did, but you were always so cold and heartless, you trifled with my poor maiden heart and threw my affection back in my face! I explained all of it to Harry, he knows the whole story of how cruel you were to me.”
“Every last bit of it,” Foker confirmed, nodding so hard his hat almost fell off.
Pen just shrugged. “Fair enough. I’m not gonna argue. I came down here ready to make you an honest offer, but clearly you’re far happier here. I hope you two have a great time, Foker, you deserve every bit of happiness for being this loyal. Shake my hand, mate, yeah? You gonna tell everyone I’m a total cad?”
“I think you’re a-” Foker started to splutter, before Blanche cut him off.
“Henry! No fighting!” she cried, staring up at the chandelier like it was sending her angelic blessings. “We must forgive him! Arthur is like a brother to me! We grew up together! Arthur, I forgive you with all my heart, for making me realize what real love is! Shake hands, you two, be friends!”
Foker was so flattered he was sniffling. “Oh my gosh you’re an actual angel. I’d kiss the ground if she told me to, I love her that much. C’mon Pendennis, shake, it’s fine, if she forgives you I forgive you too. I love anyone she says is okay.”
Blanche let him kiss her hand, all seraphic and soft, hiding her eyes behind her handkerchief like she was weeping happy tears.
Then Frank Clavering wandered back in. “Oi, Pendennis, your cabbie’s been waiting forever, he drank all the beer the kitchen gave him and he’s threatening to leave without you if you don’t pay him.”
“I’ll go right now,” Pen said, ready to bolt for the hills.
“Wait, one private word,” Blanche said, shooing Foker and Frank out to the hall to smoke a cigar.
When they were alone, she dropped the whole angel act immediately. “You’re gonna run right back and marry Laura, aren’t you? You never loved me. You’re such an idiot, Arthur. You gave up that parliamentary seat, all that power, all that fancy status, over a stupid silly scruple? Why wouldn’t you take the money that’s rightfully mine? I know all about my dad, by the way. I found out ages ago. It’s no big deal. I’m gonna marry Harry, I’ll wait until after the wedding to tell him about my dad, no one’s gonna spill the secret, right? You won’t tell him, will you? I need excitement, I need big feelings, and Harry gives them to me. You’re too boring, you’re over all your ambitions already, you’d never make me feel special in society.”
Pen stared at her, suddenly realizing every single emotion she’d ever shown him was fake. Her passion, her anger, her sadness, all of it was just a cheap little performance she pulled out whenever it suited her, flaring bright for five minutes before fizzling out completely.
He waved goodbye, walked out to the cab, and paid the driver, snickering to himself. Man, he really should have bought that return ticket before he got on the train. This whole trip was the dumbest wild goose chase he’d ever gone on.

Pen paced the Tunbridge station platform so hard he was this close to grinding a permanent foot-shaped trench into the gravel, fuming so hot he could’ve melted the arriving train tracks. The half-hour wait for the London express dragged on for what felt like six full hours of sitting through a bad church sermon, but eventually the wheels screeched, the train trundled off, and the London gaslights bobbed into view through the smog. A man who’d forgotten his carpetbag under the seat came barrelling out of the carriage, threw the first handful of spare change he found in his pocket at a hansom cabbie, and yelped “Drive to Jermyn Street like the devil’s nipping at your heels!” The cabbie tipped his own hat so hard it nearly slid off in gratitude for the absurdly generous tip, and Pen went sprinting up the hotel stairs to Lady Rockminster’s apartments like the building was on fire and the only bucket of water was sitting in her drawing room.

Laura was there alone by the lamp, half-reading a book, her face pale as a unbuttered scone from three days of fretting. She looked up the second Pen burst through the door, and didn’t even get the words “what, you’re back so soon?” all the way out before Pen charged over, grabbed her trembling outstretched hand before she could even stand up straight, and dropped to his knees right there on the carpet like he was performing a stage melodrama for a full house.
“I saw Blanche!” he blurted, breathless. “She’s properly engaged to Harry Foker—she dumped me flat. And—and… what do you say, Laura?”
No grand speech needed. Her hand squeezed his so hard her knuckles went white, her eyes lit up like someone had just flipped a switch inside them, and her wobbly, smiling lips said more than three volumes of flowery poetry ever could. Pen buried his face in her lap and sobbed that they ought to get his old mother Helen’s blessing for the match, and it felt like those old soft arms were already wrapped around him.

That’s exactly when Lady Rockminster trundled in from her post-dinner nap, her maid Beck trailing behind holding her snuff box, and the first words out of her mouth were: “Upon my word, young people!” Beck stood behind her gawking so hard her eyes almost fell out of her head, and who could blame her? They’d just walked in on a very kneeling, very chuffed-looking Pen, still clutching Laura’s hand like it was a winning lottery ticket.
“Get up off the carpet, you daft boy,” the old lady huffed. “Beck, get out of here, what do you think you’re doing, poking your nose in two people’s business?”
Pen shot to his feet grinning like he’d just won the village fair’s biggest pig, and announced “She’s consoling me after my great tragic heartbreak over Blanche, ma’am!”
“Kissing her hand like you’re a prince in a pantomime, that’s what you’re doing,” Lady Rockminster sniffed. “I don’t know what you two will get up to next.”
Pen responded by kissing *her* hand instead, and launched into the full silly story: “Blanche ditched me for Harry Foker! I walked in on them while he was draped over her feet warbling love songs to her, they’ve been secret for ten whole days. He used old Mrs. Planter’s rheumatism as an excuse to hang around the house and keep Laura out! The bloke’s a proper gentleman, too—he already promised the Logwood living to Lady Ann’s husband, got her a diamond necklace so big it weighs a ton, and bolted straight to Blanche the second he found out his dad’s stupid attempt to marry him off to a duke’s daughter fell through.”
“Oh, so you couldn’t land the girl you were chasing, so you settled for the next nice one hanging around? Is that the trick, sir?” the old lady asked, one eyebrow raised.
“He’s not settling,” Laura cut in, flushing bright pink. “He’s being honest.”
Pen nodded so hard his hair flopped in his eyes. “I know I don’t deserve half of how good she is. If being around the sweetest, purest creature on the planet doesn’t knock all the stupid nonsense out of me, nothing will.”
Lady Rockminster grunted, but her crinkly old eyes were soft. “Hm. It’s all very well. Though I still would’ve preferred Bluebeard for you two, at least he had a properly dramatic backstory.”

To steer the conversation away from the mushy stuff that was making them all a little teary, Pen suddenly remembered the whole mess with Sam Huxter and Fanny Bolton, the drama he’d completely forgotten the second he’d seen Blanche and Harry together. He told them the whole ridiculous story: how Sam had eloped with Fanny and made her his wife, and now he was hiding out in their tiny lodgings terrified his dad, the well-respected Clavering doctor, was going to turn up in London and flay him alive. He made extra sure to emphasize that Fanny was a terrible little coquette who flirted with every man that so much as breathed in her direction, very clearly hinting “see? That little old nonsense with her years back was all her fault, I was perfectly innocent, just a helpless man caught in the path of her wiles!”
Laura laughed and squeezed his hand. “If you did make a silly mistake, you repented for it ages ago. And besides, I’ve no right to scold you anyway— I was once daft enough to have a silly little crush on Warrington myself, remember?”
“Hmph,” Lady Rockminster grumbled again. “Still say Bluebeard would’ve been more fun.”

The pair were so giddy and full of the joy of being newly betrothed that they immediately decided they had to go visit the Huxters right now, at 11 o’clock at night, which earned them a snort from the old countess. “You stupid things, they’re already in their nightcaps eating pickles by the fire, go see them tomorrow first thing. Good night, you two.”

The next morning, Laura turned up bright and early with her maid Martha, and the three of them trundled over to Fanny and Sam’s tiny Charterhouse Lane lodgings. Fanny was bouncing off the walls, all flustered, calling Pen her “guardian” ever since he’d left her that little bequest ages back, absolutely shaking in her boots at the thought of old Mr. Huxter turning up and disowning Sam, leaving them to sell bootlaces on the street for the rest of their lives. Ten minutes after they left, Sam’s lawyer pals Linton and Blades popped by, smelled pipe tobacco all the way down the stairs, spotted the countess’s coronet on the carriage outside, and marvelled at what a high-and-mighty swell their old mate Pen was now, considering his dad was just a country sawbones.

On the drive back through Fleet Street, stuck in traffic at Temple Bar, they ran straight into Sam, who was white as a sheet. His dad had arrived, holed up at the Somerset Coffee House, made a fortune on the new local railway line shares, and was in a shockingly good mood—but Sam was too much of a coward to tell him he’d eloped and gotten married. Pen hatched the dumbest, most perfect scheme on the spot: they’d tell old Huxter that Lady Rockminster had come down with a sudden mystery ailment and specifically requested *him* as her doctor, butter the old man up so much he was floating on cloud nine, then spring the news of the marriage on him when he was too pleased with himself to throw a fit.

Old Mr. Huxter could barely believe his luck when Pen walked in and told him a real, actual countess wanted him as her personal physician. He tripped over his own medical bag getting out the door, climbed straight into the coroneted carriage with Laura, who spent the whole ride gushing about how he was the most skilled doctor in the whole south of England. Lady Rockminster laid it on even thicker when they got back to her rooms, going on about how her usual doctor was out of town and she’d heard nothing but praise for his genius. The old softy prescribed her a dose of peppermint, ammonia, and orange flower water, and spent the whole rest of the day bragging to Sam that he was now personal physician to a peeress of the realm.

By the next morning, he was so chuffed you couldn’t wipe the grin off his face. Pen sat him down, went on and on about how wonderful Sam was, how young men in London got up to all sorts of terrible trouble if they weren’t married young, and the old doctor nodded along like a bobblehead the whole time. When Pen finally dropped the bomb that Sam had already secretly married a sweet, pretty little girl who the whole Rockminster household adored, the old man could barely get his “God bless my soul!” out before Pen yanked the door open, and Sam and Fanny stumbled in to kneel at his feet, sniffling and laughing at the same time. Fanny looked up at him with big watery eyes, and the old man didn’t stand a chance. He sighed, patted her cheek, kissed the little tear-streaked thing on the forehead, and said “Alright then, get up. What’s your name, my dear?”
Fanny wiped her nose on her sleeve, grinned, and said “Fanny, papa.”
The comedy was done. No fights, no ruined fortunes, just two new happy couples, a grumpy old countess who was definitely going to tease all her friends about tricking the country doctor into being her fake patient, and enough madeira to go around for everyone.

Our whole ragtag cast is a full month older than we last left them, and by some wacky unwritten small-town story law, nearly every single one of them has piled back to the dinky little shire town where we first made their acquaintance.

Frederic Lightfoot, formerly head waiter and majordomo to Sir Francis Clavering, baronet of Clavering Park, has taken out a full-page ad in the local paper to inform the nobility, gentry, and random drunk farmers of ———shire that he’s taken over the well-worn, famously cozy Clavering Arms hotel. He promises he’s spruced the whole place up to peak comfort, there’s perfect stabling for the Dumplingbeare hounds’ riders, a brand new billiard room, and cellars stocked with the fanciest wines and spirits money could buy, no expense spared. Commercial travelers are promised fair prices and no silly hidden surcharges, per his manifesto that swears he’s fully aligned with the “economical spirit of the times.”

And honestly? The old pub does look shockingly lively these days. The Clavering family crest over the gateway’s been repainted so bright it practically glows, the coffee room windows are all shiny and new draped in Christmas holly, and the local magistrates have been loitering in the old assembly room for petty sessions. The farmers’ weekly dinner is bigger than ever, everyone losing their minds over Mrs. Lightfoot’s cooking — her curries and mulligatawny soup are so famous that Major Stokes, Captain Glanders, and half the local gentry have been coming back for seconds (and thirds) at private dinners and the big Clavering Institute gala that opened the town’s new reading room. The whole shebang was chaired by Sir Francis Clavering himself, with the respected Dr. Portman at his side, while the vice chair spot was filled ably by Mr. Barker, the ribbon factory magnate who’s also running the new Clavering and Chatteris Great Western Railway branch that’s set to open next year, construction crews clattering away on the tracks as we speak.

The local paper — the *Chatteris Champion, Clavering Agriculturist, and Baymouth Fisherman*, famed for its unshakable principles, fierce loyalty to British oak trees, and very reasonable ad rates — dropped a bombshell scoop no one saw coming: “Our talented townsman Arthur Pendennis has dropped his plans to run for local parliament, on account of a very interesting upcoming personal milestone. Rumour also whispers that if Sir Francis Clavering’s ailing health forces him to step down from his parliamentary seat, he’ll hand the spot over to a young aristocrat of colossal fortune, soon to wed a lovely accomplished lady very closely related to the Clavering Park household. Lady Clavering and Miss Amory have arrived at the Park for Christmas, and a swarm of top-shelf nobility are set to visit for very special New Year’s festivities.”

If you’re a half-awake reader, you’ve already pieced together what went down in our little narrative break. Lady Rockminster grumbled a little when she heard Laura Bell had picked Arthur Pendennis over her absurdly rich “Bluebeard” candidate, but anyone who knows the old lady’s soft spots knew she was secretly thrilled Laura was about to do the correct young lady thing and marry. She told her maid Beck the news that very night, and Beck — who’d already been getting full daily updates from Martha at Fairoaks for weeks — faked the most convincing shocked delighted gasp you ever heard. The two did the math on Pen and Laura’s combined incomes: the railway was paying out a fat sum for a chunk of Pen’s family land, Laura had her own tidy little fortune, they’d be perfectly comfortable. Then Lady Rockminster hatched a plan: she’d nudge her nephew, who ran the Tape and Sealing Wax Office, to give Pen a nice cushy government sinecure, and she’d pop over to visit Major Pendennis the next day to make sure he got on board with the whole match, and agreed to leave his fortune to Pen and Laura’s kids. “That stubborn old bachelor won’t dare say no to me,” she muttered to herself over her bedtime tea.

At one o’clock sharp, the countess turned up at the Major’s rooms, and you can bet he bowed so low he nearly toppled over to receive such a distinguished guest. He wasn’t fully surprised by the news she brought — Pen had woken up that very day, remembered his uncle existed for the first time in weeks, and dashed off a quick note to fill him in:
> Dear Uncle,
> Any silly little fight we had is totally over now. I popped over to Tunbridge Wells yesterday and found the prize we were both arguing over had already been snatched up. Miss Amory dumped me without a single pang for Harry Foker, and his whopping 15 thousand a year. I walked in on them mid-snog, for crying out loud, and left him to it.
> Also, Tatham wrote to say the railway company bought three of my fields at Fairoaks for an absurdly high price. I’ll tell you all the rest when we meet.
> Your affectionate A.P.

“I think I already have an inkling of what you came to tell me, your ladyship,” the Major said, all courtly smiles. “How kind of you to go out of your way to bring me the news! It was a silly little arrangement with Miss Amory, all things considered — I wanted it once, but now I can’t say I’m sorry it fell through.”
“Good,” Lady Rockminster said, very matter-of-fact. “Now we need to get him a proper wife. That Laura Bell girl, of course.”
It took the Major all of two seconds to catch on, and the negotiation was over before he even had time to put up a token fight. Who was he to argue when a countess swore Laura Bell was a paragon, too good for the likes of Pen by a thousand miles, fit to marry a king? The second she finished that speech he decided he’d been a lifelong admirer of Laura Bell too, no notes. Ten minutes later, Pen and Laura turned up at his door, Laura blushing so bright she looked like a Christmas berry hanging off Pen’s arm, and the old Major shook their hands with so much genuine emotion he nearly spilled his sherry all over his waistcoat.

Back at Clavering Park, Lady Clavering was practically bouncing off the walls with glee. Blanche was no longer mooning over Pen and moping about her prospects, she was engaged to Harry Foker, who was so smitten he was begging to cut the engagement as short as possible. The whole household was in a perfect good mood: Sir Francis got back in his wife’s good graces, came back to live at the Park, and was suddenly flush enough to hang out at the billiard rooms and gambling halls like he was 20 again. When Pen and the Major turned up for dinner at their London house one evening, who should greet them with a perfectly straight face, offering them their choice of sweet or dry champagne, but the very same James Morgan we all know and side-eye? Chevalier Strong was there too, regaling the table with wild stories about his recent travels abroad.

He pulled Pen aside as soon as he could. “Lady Clavering invited me, but that Morgan bloke looked like he was about to turn black with rage when I walked in. He’s up to no good, mark my words. I’ll slip out first, wait for you and the Major by Hyde Park Gate.”
When the Major was leaving, Morgan helped him on with his coat, muttering something about taking a temporary job with the Clavering family.
“I’ve got a very interesting document of yours in my files, Mr. Morgan,” the Major said dryly.
“Feel free to show it to Sir Francis, I don’t care,” Morgan said, eyes down, voice cold. “I owe you for all your ‘kindness’, Major, and I’ll pay you back for it one day.”
Pen caught that line, and the look of pure hatred on Morgan’s face, and immediately yelled that he’d forgotten his handkerchief, sprinting back up the stairs to the drawing room. Foker was still lingering by Blanche, totally smitten, so Pen shot Blanche a very pointed look that she immediately understood. She ran after him when he turned to leave, shutting the drawing room door behind her.
“Have you told Harry?” Pen hissed. “Morgan knows all of her father’s old secrets.”
“I know,” she said, her usual bright smile faltering.
“Have you told Harry?”
“No, no,” she said, panicking. “You won’t tell him, will you?”
“Morgan will, if you don’t get there first,” Pen said.
“No he won’t,” Blanche said, pale all of a sudden. “I made a deal with him. Just wait until after the wedding — just wait a few weeks, I’m so wretched.”
Pen begged her over and over to tell Foker that very night, that he’d forgive absolutely anything, but Blanche just pressed a little note into his hand for Laura, told him to tell her to meet her at Madame Crinoline’s dress shop at 3pm the next day so they could drive in the park, then sashayed back into the drawing room singing, playing a bright brilliant trill on her piano just as Morgan came padding up the carpeted stairs.

Pen told his uncle everything when he got back outside.
“Begad, what’s to be done?” the Major said, shuddering. “Nothing, that’s what. We’re out of this mess, thank the good lord, leave it to the people it concerns. Don’t you dare meddle, Arthur — that girl plays her own cards, and you’d be a fool to get tangled up in it again.”
They found Strong lurking a few doors away, smoking a cigar. He laughed when he heard the whole story. “I’m an old soldier, I know when to keep my mouth shut. That Morgan villain’s been snooping around our rooms for two months, pumping old Costigan for information, getting him drunk to fish out where Altamont’s hiding. He bribed the inn porter to tell him when we got back, then waltzed his way into the Claverings’ service using all that blackmail material. He’s going to make a fortune off it, the rat.”
“Where’s Altamont now?” Pen asked.
“Boulogne,” Strong said. “I left him there, warned him not to come back. We had a massive fight, I thrashed him within an inch of his life at Baden after he tried to run off and marry that fake baroness widow, the *Barone de la Cruche-Cassee* — translates to the Cracked Jug, for crying out loud. He got so insolent I had no choice but to hit him. He never even challenged me to a duel, apologized the next day, said I was right to wallop him. I pawned every single thing I owned to pay off his debts, then went to the roulette tables with my last ten florins. I won big, thank god — and that’s it, no more gambling for me. I’m going into the wine business now! My wife’s relations live in Cadiz, I’m importing sherry and acorn-fed Toboso hams. Here’s my card, Strong and Co., sole agents for the Duke of Garbanzos’ famous Diamond Manzanilla. Come over and taste them, I guarantee you’ll love it. I’m going to make a fortune this time, no mistakes.”

By the time we circle back to Clavering, the Foker and Blanche wedding is only days away. The brand new Foker family carriage is parked in the Clavering Arms coach house, the coachman handing out free pints to every local who stops by to gawk at it. The dressmaker Madame Fribsby is sewing matching gowns for all the local tenant daughters who are going to act as a little chorus of bridesmaids, and the whole town is talking about the massive feasts, the ox roasting, the bell peals, and the grand party planned at Clavering Park for the big day.
Harry Foker himself is holding court at the Clavering Arms bar, handing out free drinks to everyone who wanders in, deliriously happy. “The whole tenantry’s going to turn up, we’ll slaughter the ox, drain every tankard, my father in law will cry and give us his blessing on the porch, it’s going to be perfect! Huxter, my man, you and your new wife are invited, all drinks on me — Lightfoot, give him everything your cellar has to offer!”
The only problem? Young Mr. Lightfoot, the new innkeeper, has full run of the fancy new cellar, and no self control. He’s been tippling from dawn to dusk, reeling around the yard sloshed, and his poor wife is already miserable, watching him waste their expensive new stock on drunk local farmers.
Morgan keeps popping over from the Park to hang around the bar, sneering at poor tipsy Lightfoot like he’s enjoying every second of the mess. Foker, for all his good humor, can’t shake the creeps the butler gives him. “I don’t trust that bloke for a second,” he confides to Mrs. Lightfoot. “He looks like he’s measuring me for a coffin every time he glances over. Blanche says I have to hire him as my personal head servant, but I don’t know — there’s something off about the guy. No I do not like thee, Dr. Fell, and I can’t tell you why!”
And under all the bright tinsel, the holly, the wedding plans, and the free flowing booze, every single one of them has a little bit of secret trouble hiding under the surface — even the gleefully happy Harry Foker, who has no idea what chaos Morgan is about to unleash right before his very perfect wedding day.
